I had just wrapped my final studies at Oxford, lingering in that soft, unmoored lull between the end of my student days and the full weight of assuming formal management of my inherited family estate. My father died when I was barely old enough to hold a clear memory of his face; my mother followed him to the grave a year later, leaving me as nearly alone in the world as a man could be without being utterly adrift. I knew almost nothing of my long line of ancestors, save that a great many of them had been quiet recluses, their lives devoted entirely to books and research. I had inherited that quirk myself: while I dabbled in the physical sciences in a desultory, curious sort of way, I was far more drawn to the strange, glowing overlaps I kept catching between different branches of knowledge—the half-glimpsed parallels between hard physical hypotheses and the foggy, vivid metaphysical daydreams I kept slipping into when I let my focus wander. I had a bad habit, too, of turning those flickering half-notions into full theories long before I had proof to back them, but there is no need to dwell on my small peculiarities of mind.

The old house, like the family that had lived in it for centuries, was thick with history, but no detailed description of its layout matters to the story I have to tell. What matters most is the library: its collection predates the printing press, growing and shifting with every generation’s changing tastes and obsessions, until it spread like a slow, hungry shadow. Over decades of renovations and additions, it swallowed one ground-floor room after another until it occupied nearly the whole of that level. Its main chamber was vast, walls lined with bookshelves that rose nearly to the carved plaster ceiling, and the overflow rooms that branched off it were all odd, mismatched sizes, connected by every sort of passage imaginable: regular oak doors, open stone arches, short winding hallways, three steps down to a sunken nook of medieval herbals, two steps up to a low-ceilinged closet of occult tracts. I spent nearly all my free time in the great main room, reading old science as eagerly as new, for nothing fascinated me more than tracing the slow, fumbling path of human curiosity as it groped through the dark of what we did not yet know. Ptolemy, Dante, the two Bacons, and Boyle felt more vivid, more real to me than Darwin or Maxwell—they were the ones at the very front of the charge, breaking through the edge of total ignorance.

It was a gloomy August day, the rain streaking the library windows for hours on end, until just before sunset the clouds split open right in front of the sinking sun, spilling a flood of deep crimson light straight across the room. I had been sitting with my back to the window, nose buried in a 17th century chemistry text, and I rose to look out. The tall fountain at the center of the lawn caught that red light in its rising spray, turning every drop into flying embers. When I turned back to my chair, that same gilded, bloody glow caught the only painting in the whole vast room: a portrait set in a small, sunken niche carved straight into the bookshelves. I knew it was one of my ancestors, but I had never once wondered why it hung here, all alone, instead of in the formal portrait gallery with the rest of the family likenesses. That direct sunset light woke the painting to life; for the first time, I did not just see the brushstrokes, I felt the man’s eyes meet mine across the room, like he had been waiting for me to look for years.

My eyes still swimming with that reflected red light, I glanced idly toward the far end of the great reading room—and there was a tall, lean figure, stretching a hand up to reach a volume on the highest shelf. The next instant, the dimmer dusk of the far corner corrected my sight, and no one was there. I told myself my optic nerves had been tricked by the afterimage of the sun on the portrait, shrugged, and went back to my book. I would have forgotten the whole fleeting oddity entirely, if I had not needed to pull a specific rare alchemical treatise from the shelf a moment later. The spot where it should have stood was empty, bare wood where its spine should have been. The second I noticed the gap, I remembered: that was the exact spot I had seen the phantom figure reaching. I searched every nearby shelf for half an hour, but the book was gone. The next morning, though, there it sat, right back in its usual spot on the shelf, perfectly aligned with the rest. There was no one else in the house who cared anything for that kind of obscure, old text.

Three days later, a far stranger thing happened. Set into one wall was a low, narrow door leading to a locked closet that held some of the oldest, rarest volumes in the whole collection. Some long-dead ancestor had the whimsical idea of gluing fake book spines to the front of the thick, heavy door, turning it into a harmless, clever prank: the titles on the sham spines were either silly jokes, or names of books that had been lost centuries before. I had always loved that trick door. To make the illusion even more perfect, some old workman had wedged a thin, partial volume into the top of one of the fake shelves, cutting a diagonal chunk out of it so its limp vellum binding jutted out past the fake spines, its open corner peeking out far enough to show the handwritten parchment pages inside.

I looked up from my reading that afternoon, and the half-manuscript was gone.

I was angrier than the small, trivial value of the scrap of book justified, and rang the bell for the butler. He had served my family his whole life, more faithful than any servant I could have asked for, but he turned deathly pale the second I asked about the missing volume, and swore he had no idea what had happened to it. I believed his word easily—but I could tell he was holding something back, something he had been told never to speak of.

Later that same afternoon, I was reading again, pausing to puzzle over a dense passage, my eyes wandering idly over the far side of the room. That was when I saw it: the back of a slender, stooped old man in a long, dark wool coat, shiny from decades of wear, gliding straight through that trick prank door as if it was made of mist, vanishing into the closet behind. I sprinted across the room so fast I nearly tripped over a footstool, wrenched the heavy door open, and stared into the small closet. It had no other entrance, no windows, no hiding places. I was alone.

I told myself I was having another hallucination, that the long quiet days alone in the library were making my mind play tricks. But a few minutes later, I glanced up to reassure myself I was truly by myself—and nearly screamed. The half-vellum manuscript was back in its spot on the fake shelf. I lunged for it, pulled as hard as I could, and it would not budge an inch, wedged as firmly as it had always been.

I was completely bewildered. I rang the bell for the butler again, told him every last thing I had seen, and he spilled every secret he had been keeping for decades. He had hoped the old gentleman would stay forgotten, he said, that no one but me would ever catch a glimpse of him. No one had ever dared mention the figure in the library in my grandfather’s hearing: the old man had sworn he would fire any servant who so much as breathed a word of it, calling the whole thing a silly ghost story maids made up to have an excuse to run into the footmen’s arms. Not a single maid in living memory had ever claimed to see the apparition, but one footman had run off in the middle of the night without a word, too terrified to stay.

An old crone who lived at the edge of the village had told him the legend, he said. The figure people saw was Mr. Raven, the librarian who had served Sir Upward—the very man whose portrait hung sunken in the bookshelf niche across the room. Sir Upward had been obsessed not just with regular books, but with strange, forbidden, terrible texts, and Raven had encouraged him every step of the way. Hundreds of years ago, the pair of them vanished without a trace, no body ever found, no note ever left. No one had seen Sir Upward again after that, but Mr. Raven had kept showing up in the library, wandering the shelves, at random intervals ever since. The old woman swore he was not alive, not in any way that made sense for a human man, but that he had never truly left the library he had guarded his whole life.

“I hope his visit was a friendly one, sir,” the butler finished, his face tight with unease.

I told him I had no objection at all to Mr. Raven wandering the shelves as he pleased, but that he must say nothing of the ghost to the rest of the staff, to keep them from panicking. I asked him if he had ever seen that half-vellum manuscript loose or moved, and he shook his head. He walked over to the trick door, wrapped his fingers around the vellum corner, and pulled as hard as he could. It did not move a single inch.

Nearly a full week drifted by without a single strange incident to ruffle the quiet of my inherited old manor house. I’d returned to the property barely a month prior, and that half-lost, half-trapped manuscript fragment I’d found tucked in the back of the library closet had lingered in my thoughts every day since. I’d prodded, pried, and tugged at it a dozen different ways, but I could never figure out what invisible force was holding it fast, leaving it half-stuck in the dark of the shelves. The closet’s stale, cold air had also left me uneasy for weeks, worrying the moisture and rot might be eating away at the hundreds of uncared for books stored inside, and one afternoon that vague idle intention to give them all a full thorough sort snapped into sharp, unshakable resolve. I was just pushing myself up out of my worn leather armchair to start the work, when I caught a flicker of movement out of the corner of my eye: the old librarian, Raven, gliding away from the closet door toward the far end of the library.

To call him a man feels almost too generous. What I saw was little more than a shadowy impression: a slight, stooping figure draped in a shabby tailcoat that dragged almost to his heels, the fabric splitting a little as he moved to reveal stick-thin legs stretched in taut black stockings, and oversize feet slipping silently in wide, flopping slipper-like shoes. I never doubted for a second he was something real, even if he looked like a smudge of old ink bleached from a hundred-year-old page, and I set off after him at once.

He drifted out of the library, across the marble expanse of the entrance hall, and straight to the foot of the great grand oak staircase, then climbed all the way up to the first floor where all the unused, formal reception rooms sat gathering dust. I followed close at his heels, and he never so much as glanced back as he slipped past all those closed drawing room doors, down a wide paneled corridor, and to the base of a narrower, little-used service stair leading up to the second floor. The second I stepped onto the landing at the top, I realized I was in a stretch of the house I’d never even seen a hint of before. I’d had no siblings to drag me on silly childhood romps that would make every nook and cranny of a big house feel familiar; I’d been barely seven years old when my guardian had pulled me away from the manor, and I’d never stepped foot inside again until I’d come back to claim my inheritance.

We wound through twisting, low-ceiling passages until we reached a door at the foot of a rickety winding wooden stair, and Raven glided up it first. Every single step creaked and groaned loud enough to echo when I put my weight on it, but I never heard a single sound come from my shadowy guide. Somewhere halfway up the stair, I lost sight of him entirely. When I reached the top, he was nowhere to be found in the sprawling main garret, its massive hewn oak beams arching overhead, long dark vistas stretching out between piles of discarded old furniture, the gloom only thinned by a handful of cobweb-choked windows and small, dusky skylights. I stared out across the huge empty space with a weird, giddy mix of awe and delight: every inch of this unexplored garret was mine. Dead center of the room sat a rough, unpainted plank enclosure, its door hanging slightly ajar. Assuming Raven must have slipped inside there, I pushed the door open and stepped through.

The tiny chamber was full of light, but the hollow, still light of a place that had sat abandoned for decades. It felt dull, disconsolate, like the very walls were sad they’d been forgotten and rendered useless. A few slanted sunbeams cut through the cloud of dust motes I’d stirred up entering, landing full on a tall, narrow, old-fashioned mirror, its glass face filmed over with layers of dust. Its frame was carved from solid ebony, and perched right on its top edge was a sculpted black eagle, wings spread wide, a thin golden chain clamped in its beak with a polished black obsidian ball hanging from its end.

I’d been staring idly at the frame rather than the glass itself, when I suddenly registered that the mirror wasn’t reflecting anything at all: not the plank chamber around me, not my own body standing right in front of it. I could have sworn I saw the far wall of the chamber behind me melt like soft wax, and for a split second I wondered if I’d mistaken a sheet of protective glass over a priceless hidden painting for a mirror. But what stretched out before me was no painted image. I was looking at a wild, wind-scoured broken heath. Desolate low hills, strange and unrecognizable, rose in the middle distance, a jagged far-off range of mountain tops stretched across the horizon, and the closest stretch of the view was flat, melancholy moorland, carpeted in dark purple heather.

I’m short-sighted, so I stepped closer to squint at the texture of a weathered half-buried stone in the immediate foreground, and that’s when I spotted him: a huge, ancient raven, feathers the rich iridescent purple-black of polished jet, streaked here and there with softening streaks of gray from great age, hopping slow and solemn toward me, bobbing his head as if rooting for worms in the turf. I didn’t even stop to feel surprised at seeing a living bird inside what I’d thought was a painting, so I took another step forward to get a better look. My boot caught hard on something solid — no doubt the thick ebony lip of the mirror’s frame — I stumbled, and the next second I was standing nose to beak with the great bird, cold wind tangling my hair, the sharp peaty scent of heath in my throat. There was no glass, no garret, no old manor house. I was standing out in the open, on a houseless, endless moor.

I twisted around to stare at the space behind me, and found nothing solid at all: all was a smudged, soft haze where no line separated fog from heath, cloud from crag, no horizon anchored the world to anything I recognized. I told myself it was a trick of the light, that touch would set my scattered senses right, so I spread my arms and wandered this way and that, palms brushing at the empty air, hoping to bump into a tree trunk, a wall, a stone—something, anything to prove I was still in the world I knew. My search turned up nothing.

On instinct, I turned to the only living thing nearby: the raven, standing a few paces off, watching me with a weirdly precise mix of quiet respect and unmistakeable amusement. The absurdity of looking to a bird for help hit me for a second, and I spun away again, reeling in bewilderment sharpened with cold fear. Had I stumbled past the edge of every physical and psychological rule our world ran on? Could any ordinary person step out of the bounds of order at any random moment, and get left at the mercy of things that followed no laws at all? But I could still see the raven, still feel the springy heather under my boots, still hear wind sussurating through the low stems around me.

“How did I get here?” I said out loud, without meaning to.
“You came through the door,” a harsh, odd croaky voice answered right away.

I spun around, scanned every inch of the haze, and found no human shape anywhere. A cold terror seized me that I was slipping into madness—would I never be able to trust my own senses, my own memory ever again? The very next second I realized the voice had come from the raven: his head was tilted up at me, waiting, his beak parted just a little. There was no sun in the sky, but the bird cast a shadow sharp as spilled ink, and the shadow seemed to hang to him, not the ground, like it was a part of his body.

I beg the reader to bear with me here, because I am trying to describe something no words were built for. I was in a world—if you can even call it a world, a set of conditions, a shape of existence—so little like the rules of the life we all take for granted as the only real one, that every phrase I can reach for is no more than a blurry sketch of what I actually lived. I already fear I have taken on an impossible task: trying to write down things that no existing speech is shaped to hold, because they live in the spaces between what we know to name. I catch myself retyping sentences, trying to find truer words, only to feel the memory of the place slipping away from me the way a dream dissolves the second you wake, the familiar shapes melting one after another until you can no longer tell what they ever were.

I reasoned that any creature that could speak to me deserved at least the same courtesy I would give a human, maybe more, for the rarity of it. His voice was rough around the edges from a lifetime of croaking, but it was not unkind, even if his answers gave me no clarity.
“I never came through any door,” I told him.
“I saw you step through it with my own ancient eyes!” the raven asserted, firm but not rude.
“I never saw a door at all!” I protested.
“Of course you didn’t,” he replied. “All the doors you’ve seen so far—and you haven’t seen many—are doors you walk *in* through. That one you came through is a door *out*. The strangest thing you will learn soon enough is that the more doors you walk out of, the farther you go in.”

“Just tell me where I am, please.”
“I can’t. You don’t know the first thing about whereness. The only way to figure out where you are is to start making yourself at home here.”
“How am I supposed to do that when every single thing around me is alien?”
“Do something. Anything. The sooner you start, the better—because until you stop being a stranger here, getting out will be just as impossible as getting in was easy.”
“I stumbled in by accident. Once I find the way out, I will never come back.”
“You stumbled in, and you might stumble out again. Whether you got in unfortunately or not is something you will have to find out for yourself.”

“Do you leave this place often?”
“I go out whenever I please, but not for long. Your world is half-baked. It’s childish, and it’s absurdly proud of itself, and it’s not nearly grown enough for an old raven like me. At your service, of course.”
“Does that mean I’m wrong to assume a man is inherently better than a bird?”
“That all depends on the man and the bird. We don’t waste our time making broad generalizations. We judge one by one.” He paused, tilting his head. “I think it’s my turn to ask a question now.”
“Of course you have every right,” I said. “You earned it by being able to speak at all.”

“Good answer,” he croaked. “Tell me who you are. If you happen to know.”
“Of course I know who I am,” I scoffed, but the words died in my throat. I could not give him a single answer that held weight. I could not dredge up the name I went by back in my house, could not prove I was not someone else, could not even argue that I was not my own father, or my own fool wandering lost in a delusion. The name I had lived with my whole life meant nothing here, and I could not bring myself to care what it had been. I fell silent, and I knew that was the wisest choice I could make: there was no point trying to lie to a creature that saw past the accidents of surface life straight to the core of what a being *was*.

“Look at me,” the raven said, and turned his back.
In that instant, I knew him. He was no bird. He was a tall, stooped, thin man in a long black tailcoat, the very shape of the librarian my family had employed for decades. When he turned back to face me, he was a raven again.
“I have seen you before,” I said, feeling foolish more than shocked.
“How can you say that when you only saw my back? You have never even seen your own back, let alone the whole of yourself. So who am I?”
“Forgive me,” I said. “You were our old family librarian. I don’t know what else to call you.”
“Why apologize? You thought I was a raven, and that meant you acknowledged I existed. That is all anyone can ever ask of their fellow beings. As a thank you, I will give you a lesson: no one can ever call themself, truly, until they first know that they *are*, and then learn what their own self even is. Right now, nobody is fully themself, and the ‘self’ people cling to is nobody at all. You won’t understand all of it today, but you will understand what you need to. You stumbled into this realm far too soon, but you still have to make it your home. Home is the only place that lets you walk out and in as you please. Every other place only lets you go one way.”

He turned to walk away, and the next time I blinked, he was the librarian again, no shapeshift, just like he had tucked his raven shadow under his arm and carried it off with him. I stared after him until he vanished, no trace of him left in the haze, though I could not tell if he had walked out of sight or dissolved into the heather. I stood there, reeling, wondering if I was dead and did not know it, if this was what people called the afterlife, and I would wander forever looking for a place that was mine. How was I supposed to make this alien place home? The raven said I had to *do* something, but what? I was nobody, now, no name, no proof I existed at all.

I turned and followed the path the raven had taken, and soon spotted a stand of tall, slender pine trees. The sharp, resinous scent hit me long before I reached them, and I hurried toward the wood, plunging into its dim green twilight. Between two trunks, I saw something shimmering: no color, just a translucent, thrumming haze, like the hot air that rises off sun-scorched asphalt at midday, vibrating like a plucked violin string. The closer I walked, the less clear it grew, and when I stood right in front of it, I could not see it at all—only the pine trees on the other side looked soft, unsteady, like they would melt if I touched them. I tried to step between the two trunks, and a sharp static jolt ran through my whole body. I stumbled, and fell.

When I got to my feet, I was standing in my own garret. The rough wooden wall was right in front of me. I turned, and there was my mirror, the carved black eagle perched on its top rail as if it had landed there only seconds before.

A blind terror seized me, and I ran. The wide empty stretches of the garret behind me looked uncanny, like they had been waiting for hundreds of years. The thing they had been waiting for had arrived now, and they were waiting for something new. A shudder wracked me as I stumbled down the winding stair: the whole house felt strange to me now. I was sure something would leap out at me from the shadows behind my back. I darted down the spiral steps, slammed into the wall, fell, picked myself up and ran again. I got lost on the next floor, looping through the same three passages twice before I finally found the main stairwell. By the time I stumbled into the library, I was gasping for breath.

I swore I would never climb that terrible top stair again. The garret hung over the whole house, heavy as a living thing, threatening to crush me out of existence. It was the brooding brain of the building, full of invisible, alien dwellers that could step through into any room at any time. I was not safe anywhere in this house. I would sell it, I told myself. I would move to the Alps, build a tiny single-story wooden cottage on a bare crag, no garret, no top floor, guarded by nothing but old stone peaks that could only ever bury me under rockfall, not under a haze of unnameable things. I knew the whole thought was ridiculous, that there was a faint, sardonic humor running through my own panic—until I heard a raven’s croak echo in the back of my head.

If I did not even know the whole of my own house, what was to stop my own brain from making all of this up? Could I prove what my mind would generate next week, next month, next year? What hid in the core of my own thoughts? Was I even *there* at all, behind my own eyes? Who was I?

I could not answer that question any better now than I could when the raven had asked it out in the heath. I jumped to my feet, ran across the library to the masked door where the tattered, half-stuck manuscript jutted out from the shelf of books, and dropped to my knees. I pulled it out as far as it would go, but I could see nothing. I lit a taper, leaned in like I was peering into the jaws of something reluctant to open, and saw that the manuscript was verse. Left halves of lines glinted on the left page, right halves on the right. There was no way to pull the book far enough out to see a single full line, no way to guess what the poem was about. But the half-words, the broken shreds of verse, woke a feeling in me I had never known before: the kind of uncanny, aching pull you get from a half-remembered dream, a few bars of a half-heard song, a whiff of a scent you cannot place, the ghost of an idea that you know was once yours, if only you could reach it. I copied down the longest of the fragments, tried to finish the broken lines myself, and failed utterly. All I got for my effort was bone-deep weariness, and I fell asleep the second I climbed into bed, and slept without dreaming at all.

When I woke up the next morning, all the cold, clawing terror of the empty garret was gone.

The sun blazed sharp and gilded over the lawn, but a prickle of premonition told me the fine spell would not hold. I twisted the milky sapphire heirloom on my finger, squinting at the faint, starlike core trapped in its cloudy blue depths: it was far dimmer, far more blurred than I had ever seen it.
I pushed back my breakfast chair and crossed to the window to glance again at the odd standing stone at the edge of my property. Heavy rain had lashed the manor overnight, and a thrush was perched on a patch of moss, hammering stubbornly at a snail shell to crack it open. I twisted my ring further to catch the full sun on its star core, when a glossy, keen black eye blinked directly back at me from the gem’s milky mist.
The shock jolted the ring right out of my hand. When I fumbled to pick it up, the eye was gone.
At that exact second the sun winked out, swallowed whole by a roiling dark vapour. In less than a minute the entire sky was clogged with storm clouds, the air turned thick and sultry, and a gust of wind slammed the window curtain against my shoulder. Lightning seared the hedgerows, one single, sharp thunderclap rattled the china on my breakfast table, and then the rain fell in solid, torrential sheets, blurring every line of the landscape beyond the glass.
I left the window propped open, leaning against the frame to watch the downpour, when I spotted a raven striding across the soaked grass toward me. His gait was slow, solemn, not a single feather ruffled, and he moved through the deluge like it did not exist at all. I recognised him at once from my earlier strange encounters, and congratulated myself I was on the ground floor with no winding staircase to tempt me after him. Even so, a quiet, unshakable certainty settled over me that if I let my guard slip, something would shift that I could never undo.
He drew close, bowed so deep his beak nearly brushed the grass, then launched in a sudden winged leap straight to the windowsill. He stepped over the ledge, hopped down onto my floor, and walked purposefully toward the back of the house. I followed, half expecting him to head for the library or the stairs, already resolved I would not set a single foot on that upward path after him. But he turned not toward the library or the landing, but to the tiny, seldom-used side door that opened onto an overgrown grass nook wedged between two sprawling wings of my old rambling manor. I hurried to pull the door open for him.
He stepped out onto the creeper-draped porch, and stood staring out at the rain, which fell like a vast, thin silver cataract. I lingered in the doorway behind him. A second flash of lightning split the sky, followed by a long, rolling peal of distant thunder. He tilted his head back over his shoulder to glance at me, as if to say *You hear that?*, then swivelled his gaze back to the storm, his posture almost human in its quiet approval.
The gesture was so uncannily familiar that I spoke before I thought. “Fine weather for the worms, Mr. Raven!”
“Fine, indeed,” he rumbled in that rough, croaky voice I had already learned to recognise. “The ground’s soft enough for them to burrow in and out easy. Must be grand times on the steppes of Uranus right now! I’d wager it’s pouring there too. It’s rained solid for a whole week.”
“Why would that be grand?” I asked.
“Every creature there is a burrower,” he explained. “Just like your field mice and moles here. That whole world’s still young, deep in its evolutionary spring. You’d think a beast heaving up out of the mud was a hairy elephant, or some long-lost deinotherium — though none of them match anything that’s ever lived on Earth. The first time I saw a dry-bog-serpent wallow out of the mud, all thick mane and great glowing eyes, I nearly jumped out of my own feathers. But the shower’s nearly over. It’ll stop the second after the next thunderclap.”
A flash burst as he spoke, and half a minute later the thunder boomed. The rain ceased instantly, as if someone had turned off a tap.
“Now it’s time we go,” the raven said, stepping to the edge of the porch.
“Go where?” I asked.
“Where we have to go,” he said. “You didn’t truly think you were home, did you? I told you no one can come and go as they please until they find the real home.”
“I don’t want to go anywhere,” I said.
“That doesn’t change much, not even a little,” he croaked. “This way.”
“I’m perfectly content right where I am.”
“You think you are. But you aren’t. Come along.” He hopped down onto the soaked grass, and turned to wait for me.
“I won’t leave the house today,” I said, stubborn.
“Then just step into the garden,” he bargained.
I sighed, and gave in, stepping out past him onto the lawn. The sun broke through the clouds, and every raindrop on the grass glinted like scattered diamond.
“You’ll wet your feet,” I warned him.
“And mire my beak,” he answered, and plunged that black, sharp beak deep into the soft turf, yanking out a fat, wriggling red worm. He tossed it high in the air.
The worm unfurled great, iridescent wings of red and black, and soared straight up into the sky.
“Tut tut,” I laughed. “Worms don’t turn into butterflies, Mr. Raven.”
“Never mind. It’ll do for once,” he croaked. “I’m not much of a reader these days. I’m sexton at a graveyard — a cemetery, more properly — somewhere far past the edges of where you think the world ends. Dig things up sometimes, when there’s nothing to bury.”
“You can’t go digging things up and making them fly off,” I said. “No creature should ever forget where it came from.”
“Why not?” he asked.
“Because they’ll grow proud. They’ll stop recognising their superiors.”
I did not know then that I was making a perfect fool of myself.
“Where do worms come from, anyway?” he asked, tilting his head like he was genuinely curious.
“Why the earth, obviously,” I said.
“Last, sure,” he answered. “But they didn’t come from the earth first. The earth will never get them all back, either.” He nodded up at the sky.
I looked up, and saw nothing but a small dark cloud, its edges glowing red like it was lit by sunset — impossible, the day was barely half done.
“The sun can’t be setting already,” I gasped.
“It isn’t,” he said. “That red glow belongs to the worm we just let fly.”
“See? That’s what happens when you make creatures forget their origins!” I said, heated.
“Only if forgetting lets them rise higher,” he said. “I’m not making them lose their roots. I’m teaching them to find them. You wouldn’t have the whole sky full of worms, anyway. That’s just the sexton’s work. If the rest of the clergy understood half that much, the worlds would be a far better place.”
He drove his beak into the turf a second time, pulled out another wriggling worm, and tossed it. It spread its own pair of glowing wings and soared away.
I turned back toward the house, and a cry of dismay tore out of my throat. I had sworn not ten minutes earlier I would never leave it. Now I was standing in the middle of a dark, fragrant pine forest, with no sign of my manor anywhere.
“What right do you have to drag me here against my will, Mr. Raven?” I snapped, stung. “Am I not a free agent?”
“A man is exactly as free as he chooses to make himself. Not an atom more,” the raven answered. “No one can force a choice on a full individual. But you are only just beginning to become one. So I can.”
I stared around at the endless pines, searching for a glint of the familiar, any path back to the life I knew. How could I ever call that house home again, when every door, every window opened out into somewhere else, and even my garden was no longer mine to keep inside?
“Maybe that eases it a little,” the raven said, watching my face. “You haven’t left your house. Your house hasn’t left you. It just can’t contain you, not any more. You can’t inhabit it the way you did.”
“I don’t understand you,” I said. “Where am I?”
“The region of seven dimensions,” he said, making a curious low rumble in his throat and flicking his tail feathers. “You’d better follow very closely now. You might step right through someone you can’t see, and hurt them bad.”
“There’s no one here to hurt but you, and half the time I’d happily do it,” I said.
“Not seeing them is the danger,” he said. “You see that big pine tree, thirty yards off to your left?”
“Of course I see it,” I said, testy.
“Ten minutes ago you didn’t. And right now you have no idea where it actually stands. That tree is growing straight up through your kitchen hearth, right out your kitchen chimney.”
I laughed out loud. “Now I know you’re making fun of me!”
“Was I making fun of you when I winked at you out of that sapphire ring of yours, not an hour ago?” he asked.
“You’re just messing with me,” I said, turning away.
“No one can make a fool of you but yourself,” he said. “You make yourself one by refusing to accept what’s true, and then you punish yourself for it by believing what’s completely false. Those hyacinth blooms tangled in the strings of your piano? They’re what make the playing so sweet. Your housekeeper’s niece is sitting right there at the keys, playing Grieg’s Wedding March, right next to that wild rose bush you see growing at the foot of the pine. You just can’t see any of it, not yet.”
I walked over to the rose bush. I could not hear a single note of music, but the scent that rose from its petals was not just rose — it was rose, tangled with the bright, thrumming echo of the march, something stranger and sweeter than any scent I had ever breathed.
“Mr. Raven,” I said, softening. “I’m sorry I was rude. I was just angry, scared. Please show me the way home. I have an appointment with my bailiff. I can’t break faith with my servants.”
“The path back doesn’t go through any door,” he said. “It goes through you. No one can walk that path for you. No one can show it to you.”
There was no arguing, no pleading. Entreaty was useless. But I took a deep breath, and the despair loosened in my chest. It was an adventure. I had never done a single meaningful thing back in my old world, had not left it a single bit better than I found it. But here, I would have to earn my keep, learn new rules, live a new life. At the very least, I would be one of the rare few to know two whole worlds.
“There’s no rush here,” the raven said, watching my face. “We don’t run by clocks. But the sooner you start the work you were meant to do, the easier it goes. I’ll take you to meet my wife.”
“Thank you,” I said. “Lead on.”
He hopped off through the pine needles, his black wings barely brushing the misty air, and I followed.

I followed the raven deep into the pine forest, the air thick with resin that clung to my throat like the breath of a half-asleep god. Neither of us spoke for long stretches, wrapped in the sacred, velvet gloom that swallowed sound whole until not even a footstep could echo. The trees swelled larger as we walked, trunks gnarled and scarred with centuries of unrecorded weather, their boughs curving into strange, whimsical shapes as if they had forgotten how to be ordinary pine a hundred lifetimes prior.

At last the forest thinned, its ranks breaking apart to reveal the wind-scoured stretch of open heath beyond. “You see that hawthorn?” my guide said, his ebony, iridescent beak jabbing toward the tree line.
I squinted. Where the wood faded into heath edge, there stood a shape that looked less like a plant than a hunched ancient man, his head frosted pure white as if dusted with snow out of season. “I see a gnarled old elder, with a great white head,” I corrected.
“Look again,” he rumbled. “It is a hawthorn.”
“It is certainly an ancient hawthorn,” I frowned, “but this is no time of year for it to bloom. There is no spring here.”
“The season for the hawthorn to blossom,” he replied, “is when the hawthorn blossoms. That tree grows in the ruins of the old church on your home farm. You were going to give your bailiff directions for that churchyard the morning the thunder struck, were you not?”
The memory hit me sharp and bright. “I was going to tell him to let it run wild, turn every inch of it into a tangle of rose bushes, and ensure the plough never touches within three yards of the graves.”

“Listen.” The raven went still, as if holding his breath, his feathers flattening against his body.
I did as he said. At first I thought I caught the sigh of a distant, melodic wind, then something softer: the ghost of hymns sung so long ago they had dissolved into pure, wordless joy. I could not be certain I heard anything at all, until the note of it hummed in my bones.
“They still gather there,” the raven said.
“Who? And what do they do?” I asked.
“Some of the congregation that once knelt in that old church, they still return to the ruins,” he said. “I do not think they will be able to stay much longer, though.”
“What draws them back now?”
“They lend each other the weight of their company to work their thoughts free, to hatch the feelings that cannot unfold in a busy, loud world. They talk soft, they sing old songs, and then the great, shared thought they have tended floats out of all their chests together, like a tall ship slipping out of a river at the highest high tide, bound for open sea.”
“Do they pray, too, alongside their singing?”
“No. They have learned every heart speaks to the divine best in its own quiet, unshared silence. Some people never stop praying, even when they think they are doing nothing at all.--Look! There goes one!”

He snapped his beak upward, pointing into the empty blue sky. A snow-white pigeon was ascending, beating its wings faster and faster, climbing the rungs of an invisible, ethereal stair I could almost see shimmering beneath the sunlight. Light burst off its feathers in trembling, rippling waves.
“I see a pigeon,” I said.
“Of course you see a pigeon,” the raven huffed. “Because the pigeon is there. But I see a prayer, flying straight home. I wonder what heart gave birth to it? Someone in my cemetery must have finally woken up.”
“How can a pigeon be a prayer?” I protested. “I understand it makes a lovely symbol, of course, but a live, breathing bird that steps straight out of a human heart?”
“It must puzzle you! It cannot fail to, with how little of the worlds beyond your own you have seen.”
“A prayer is a thought! Something spiritual!” I pressed.
“That is true. But if you understood even a single world outside the small mortal sphere you call home, you would understand your own far better. When a heart is truly, fully alive, it can think living, breathing things. There is one great Heart at the core of all creation, every one of whose thoughts is a strong, joyful creature, even whose dreams are full of new life. Some people lift their prayers heavy as stones off the ground, only to drop them straight back again. Others send their prayers skyward in living shapes, whatever creature matches the shape of the longing in their chest the best. Every living thing was a thought first, once, so they are made to be held and given back by those that think. When you say to that great Thinker ‘This is one of your thoughts, I am holding it clear and bright in my own heart now’ -- that is prayer. A word from one small heart, back to the big Heart that made all of them. --Look, there is another!”

This time he pointed down, toward the foot of a weathered granite boulder half-buried in heath. I knelt. There was a tiny flower growing there I had never seen the like of, its shape soft and trusting, its colour a pale, blushing rose, its scent sweet as a new dawn that had never been seen before, that tasted like both a forgotten memory and a brand new world. It looked almost like an anemone, its center glowing the soft, rich gold of honey.
“That is a prayer-flower,” the raven said.
“I have never seen a flower like that in my life,” I breathed.
“There is no other exactly like it. No two prayer-flowers ever grow the same,” he said.
“How do you know that is what it is?” I asked.
“By the look of it. The expression of it, in the curve of every petal. I cannot explain more than that. If you know it, you know it. If you do not, no list of facts will make you.”
“Can you not teach me how to spot them for myself?” I said.
“I could not. And if I could, what good would it do you? You would not know it for the thing itself, through your own eyes. Why bother learning the name of a thing if you never learn to see the thing itself? It is no one’s job but your own to pry your eyes open. But make no mistake: the whole business of the universe is to make a fool of you, until you finally admit you are one -- and then, at last, you can start to be wise.”

I stared down at the small, glowing flower, and I knew. It was unlike any bloom I had ever encountered in my old world, so strange and alive I could almost feel the quiet, gentle longing that had grown it, thrumming soft in its petals. A great, humbling awe washed over me, as I realized I was not looking at a weed. I was looking at a shadow of a prayer, made tangible, rooted in the dirt.

We had tramped for uncounted miles across rocky moorland where brittle dry heath and crumbly ash-gray moss crunched under every step, when I spied a tiny cottage huddled on the very edge of the far horizon. The sun had not yet set, but it was wrapped thick in a veil of murky gray cloud, leaking no warmth. The whole moor looked as if it had never once felt the touch of summer heat, and the wind bit through my coat like it blew straight off some hidden realm where it was midnight forever.
“Here we are at last!” croaked the raven, shaking feathers ruffled flat by the gale. “What an endless trudge! In half the time I could have flown all the way to Paradise to visit my cousin—you remember him, the one who never came back to Noah’s ark! Dear me, the cold’s so sharp it might as well be midwinter!”
“Winter?” I stared at him, bewildered. “It feels barely half a day since we left my front door back home!”
“That’s because we’re traveling faster than the rules of your world allow,” he cawed. “Back there you can’t yank up that heavy plumb line you call gravitation and let the whole world spin right under your boots! But look—there’s my wife’s house. She’s gracious enough to let me share it, and we call it the sexton’s cottage!”
“But where is your churchyard?” I frowned, scanning the empty moor, no headstones, no fence, no stone steeple in sight. “Where do you dig your graves?”
The raven stretched his long neck, held his beak perfectly horizontal, and slowly pivoted full circle across every point of the compass, not uttering a word. My eyes followed the slow sweep of his beak, and suddenly the truth hit me: there was no church, no carved stone markers, no fenced plots. *All of it* was a churchyard. Every stretch of wind-scoured heath, every dip and rise of the moor, was his cemetery. He was sexton of every acre his eyes fell on: lord of all the souls laid aside. I was standing in the burial ground of the whole universe, its borders nothing but the low, starless gray horizon. I had left spring, summer, autumn, and every last scrap of sunshine far behind me, and stepped straight into the waiting winter I had long dreaded. But I was wrong. The days of this strange realm stretched long enough to hold every season at once. Winter slept through the long dark, wrapped in a winding sheet of ice; Spring woke at dawn with a soft, childlike smile; Summer blazed at noon in all her lush, dazzling glory; old Autumn crept slow across the fading afternoons, and breathed his last the moment the ghostly, fog-choked night drew near.
As we drew within a few yards of the cottage door, the cloud-shrouded sun plunged down the steep western slope, and sank completely out of sight before we crossed the last stretch of heath. The very same instant, a cold that felt almost solid wrapped itself around my bones, and I stumbled over the threshold fighting free of its clutches, as if I had just dragged myself out of a frozen lake. The wind rose to a howl on the moor and hurled itself at the door, and I barely managed to slam it shut and bolt it behind me.
All at once it was dead still. A single beeswax candle guttered on a plain deal table in the middle of the room, and the first thing I spotted was what I thought was an upright coffin lid propped against the far wall. It swung open on silent hinges: it was a door, and a woman stepped through. She was clad head to toe in white, as bright and unmarked as fresh fallen snow, and her face was as pale as her dress—but it was not the cold white of snow. It hummed with quiet, steady warmth. Her features were so perfectly formed I could not find a flaw in them, but her eyes made me forget every other detail of her face. Every scrap of life in her whole body was gathered there, concentrated into glowing light. It might have been the hush of coming death that made her skin luminous, but her eyes held enough life for a thousand generations—large, dark, and growing deeper in shadow the longer I stared. A whole night sky was condensed in each pupil, every star pricking bright through the blackness, ringed by an iris of soft eternal twilight. No mortal eye could have been made so: those eyes must have come fresh from the palms of God’s own hands. Her still, perfect face was primeval, crafted at the dawn of time; those living eyes were being made new, every single second.
“Here’s Mr. Vane, wife!” said the raven.
“He is welcome,” she answered, in a low, rich, honeyed voice that sounded like buried harp strings thrumming. It held treasures of immortal sound I could not name. I stared at her, completely unable to speak.
“I knew you’d be glad to see him,” the raven added. She stayed still by the door she had stepped through, and did not move an inch closer.
“Will he sleep?” she asked.
“I fear not,” the raven replied. “He is not weary enough, not heavy laden with the weight of his deeds.”
“Then why did you bring him?”
“I am afraid it may have been a little precipitate,” he admitted.
“I do not quite understand you,” I cut in, a sharp uneasy foreboding pricking at my chest, even as I clung to a vague, faint hope of escape. “Surely a man must finish a full day’s work before he rests!” My heart fluttered as I stared at her pale white face. She held my gaze in silence. “Let me go home first,” I went on, “and come back once I have found something, made something, invented or discovered some work of my own!”
“He has not yet learned that the day begins with sleep,” the woman said, turning to her husband. “Tell him he must rest before he can do anything at all.”
“Men get so proud of the things they have already done,” the raven put in, “that they tuck themselves right up inside their own past and fall asleep, like a chicken crawling back into the eggshell it just hatched out of.”
His words pulled my gaze away from the woman, and the raven’s feathers vanished. Standing there was the librarian from my own house, that lean elderly man in his frayed rusty black coat, long jacket tails sweeping his calves. I had only ever seen his back before—now I saw his face for the first time. It was so thin I could trace the clean line of his skull under the pale skin, recalling all the graves his sexton work must have made him familiar with. But no face I had ever seen felt more alive, no gaze sharper or kinder than the one in his pale blue eyes, which held a faint soft haze as if he had wept a thousand tears.
“You knew I was not a raven,” he said, smiling.
“I knew you were Mr. Raven,” I laughed back, “but I truly thought you were a bird too!”
“What made you think that?”
“You looked exactly like a raven, and I saw you dig worms out of the dirt with your beak.”
“Then what did I do?”
“Toss them up in the air.”
“Then?”
“They sprouted wings and turned into butterflies, and flew away.”
“Did you ever see an ordinary raven do that? I told you I was a sexton!”
“Does a sexton toss worms in the air and turn them into butterflies?”
“Yes.”
“I never saw a sexton do that!”
“You saw *me* do it!” He grinned. “I’m still your house’s librarian, you know. I was never dismissed, and I never gave up the post. Now I’m librarian here too.”
“But you just told me you were sexton here!”
“So I am. It’s nearly the same profession. If you are not a true sexton, every book is just a dead body, and a whole library is nothing but a catacomb.”
“You have me all bewildered,” I said.
“That’s the whole point,” he said, and fell silent for a long moment. The woman stood as still as a marble statue, silent, by the door I had mistaken for a coffin lid.
“On some occasions,” the sexton said at last, “it’s far more convenient to let one’s bird-self take the front of things. Every human being, as you ought to know, has a beast-self, and a bird-self, and a silly fish-self, ay, and a slithering serpent-self too—something it takes a great deal of crushing to kill. In truth, you also have a tree-self and a crystal-self, and I can’t even count all the rest—all of them must learn to move in harmony. You can always tell what kind of a person someone is by the creature that slips to the front most often.”
He turned back to his wife. “We have traveled a very long way, wife. We are in want of something to eat and drink.”
“You know, husband,” she answered, “we can only give to the one who asks for it out loud.” She turned her radiant, star-filled eyes full on my face.
“Please give me something to eat, Mrs. Raven,” I said, “and something to drink, whatever you like, to quench my thirst.”
“Your thirst is not deep enough yet for the water that will truly quench it,” she replied, “but what I can give you, I will gladly.” She crossed to a cupboard set into the wall, brought out crusty warm bread and dark, rich wine, and set them on the table. We sat down to the perfect simple meal, and as I ate and drank, the bread and wine sank deeper than just satisfying my hunger. All my anxiety, all the sharp cold of the moor, vanished, and soft bright expectation took its place. All at once I was so sleepy I could barely keep my eyes open, and I felt for the first time how bone-deep tired I was from the journey.
“I have earned neither food nor sleep, Mrs. Raven,” I said, “but you gave me the food freely, and now I hope you will give me the sleep too, for I need it terribly.”
“Sleep is too fine a thing ever to be earned,” the sexton said. “It must be given, and accepted. It is a necessity. But it would be perilous to use this house as a halfway inn, stopping just for one night’s repose before heading on again.”
A scrawny, wild little black cat jumped onto his lap as he spoke. He patted it slow, the way you pat a fretful child to lull it to sleep, but the motion looked exactly like patting soft, warm turf down over a new grave, gentle, with a quiet lullaby hummed under his breath. “This is one of Mara’s kittens,” he said to his wife. “Give it a little something to eat, and put it outside. Mara will be looking for it.”
The woman took the cat from him gently, gave it a crumb of bread, and stepped out the door, closing it soft behind her.
“How then am I to make use of your hospitality?” I asked.
“By accepting it fully,” he answered.
“I do not understand.”
“In this house, no one wakes of their own accord.”
“Why?”
“Because no one anywhere ever wakes of their own accord. You can no more wake yourself up than you can make yourself from nothing.”
“Then perhaps you or Mrs. Raven would kindly wake me when it is time?” I said, a new vague foreboding coiling in my gut.
“We cannot.”
“How dare I then go to sleep?” I cried, afraid.
“If you would have the rest this house offers, you must stop troubling yourself about waking. You must go to sleep heartily, altogether, no half-wakeful fretting holding you back.”
My soul sank in my chest. The sexton sat looking me straight in the face, his eyes soft and gentle, seeming to say *Will you not trust me?* I held his gaze, and nodded. “I will.”
“Then come,” he said, rising to his feet. “I will show you your couch.”
As we stood up, the woman stepped back into the room. She picked up the guttering candle, turned toward the inner door I had first taken for a coffin lid, and led the way. I followed close behind her, the sexton stepping right after me, and we walked into the dim dark beyond.

The air hit me the moment I crossed the threshold, sharp and still as the interior of an ice-house, crystallizing the edge of my coat collar and stinging the tip of my nose. The heavy door clicked shut behind us with no echo, as if the space itself swallowed every sound before it could travel. The sexton muttered something to his wife, and she turned to face us— and the change that had come over her stole my breath. It was as if the blazing splendour of her eyes had overflowed their bounds, sinking deep into every line of her face until she glowed with a loveliness that outstripped even Beatrice’s image blooming in the white rose of the redeemed. Eternal, unshakable life streamed from her skin like unbroken, gentle lightning, and even her hands shone with milky radiance, every curved knuckle gleaming like polished moonstone. Her beauty was so overwhelming I could barely look at her, and I exhaled in relief when she turned that unearthly light away from me.

The candle’s flame stretched only a few feet ahead of us, thin and wavering, and at first I could make out almost nothing of the room around us. Presently, though, its glow caught a faint glimmer raised just above the stone floor. Was that a bed? What living thing could possibly sleep in such mortal, biting cold? No wonder it would never wake of its own accord, I thought. Beyond that first glimmer shone a fainter one, and soon I could see uncertain, pale gleams stretching out on every side.

A few slow paces brought us to the first shape: a human form stretched straight and still under a sheet, its face turned away from the light, so I could not tell if it belonged to man or woman. I soon realized we were walking down an endless aisle of couches, almost every one of them holding a still, sheeted figure laid with its head pointing toward the passage. My throat tightened, and a heavy, silent dread settled over my soul. We wound through aisle after aisle, past couches too numerous to count. Only a handful of them were visible at any one time, but they stretched out on all sides, vanishing into shadow that seemed to stretch to infinity. Was this where I was meant to choose my bed? Must I lie down to sleep among the unwaking, with no one to rouse me? Was this the sexton’s famed library, and were these still sleepers his books? This was no gentle half-way house, this chamber of the half-dead.

“One of the cellars I am placed to watch!” remarked Mr. Raven in a low whisper, as if he feared his voice could rouse even the deepest sleeper. “Much wine is set here to ripen! But it is dark for a stranger,” he added.
“The moon is rising; she will soon be here,” said his wife. Her voice was clear, low, and sweet, ringing with the soft weight of ancient sorrow long since laid to rest.

Even as she spoke, silver light spilled through a narrow opening in the high wall, and a thousand white sheets glowed back to meet her shine. Still, I could not make out the beginning or end of the rows of couches. They stretched on and on, as if they were laid out for every soul in the disparted world to rest upon. Down every narrow, receding aisle, each couch stood alone, holding a single lonely sleeper. I had thought their state was death at first, but soon I saw it was something far deeper, something I had no name for. The moon climbed higher, shining through other openings in the wall, but I could never see enough of the vast space at once to make out its true shape: now it looked like a long, empty cathedral nave, now a huge stone barn repurposed to hold tombs. The moon here was colder than any moon I had ever seen on the frostiest winter night of my old world, casting a bluish, icy gleam over the white sheets and the pallid faces peeking out from beneath them — though it might have been the faces themselves that made the moon feel so cold.

Every sleeper shared the quiet brotherhood of rest, but no two faces bore the same story. Here lay a man who had died — though this was not death, and I had no other word for what I saw — in the full prime of his strength. His dark beard flowed like a liberated stream from the glacier of his frozen, pale countenance, his forehead smooth as polished marble, a faint shadow of pain lingering only at the corners of his lips. On the next couch lay a girl of unearthly beauty. The sadness left on her face by parting had not yet fully melted into perfect peace, but absolute submission softened every placid feature, with no trace of wasting disease, killing care, or grief of heart: if pain had ever touched her, it had long since been charmed asleep, never to wake again. Many of the beautiful lay still there, some of them small children — but I saw no infants among them. The loveliest of all was a stately lady whose white hair was the only marker that she had been old when she first fell asleep. On her face rested not submission, but noble, unshakable acquiescence, a certainty as firm as the foundations of the universe that all was exactly as it should be. On a small handful of faces lingered the almost obliterated scars of old strife, the faint marrings of hopeless loss, the fading shadows of sorrows that had once seemed inconsolable. The aurora of the great new morning had not yet quite melted them away, and every one of those faces seemed to plead, “Pardon me: I died only yesterday!” or “Pardon me: I died but a century ago!” That some had rested here for ages I knew, not only from their unutterable repose, but from some quiet, ancient weight about them for which I had no word or symbol.

At last we came to three empty couches, immediately beyond which lay the form of a beautiful woman just past the prime of life. One of her arms was outside the sheet, her palm turned upward, a dark spot right at its centre. Next to her rested the stalwart figure of a man of middle age. His arm too was out of the sheet, his strong hand half closed as if still clenched around the grip of a sword. I thought he must have been a king who died fighting for the truth.
“Will you hold the candle nearer, wife?” whispered the sexton, bending down to examine the woman’s hand. “It heals well,” he murmured to himself. “The nail found in her nothing to hurt!”

At last I found the courage to speak. “Are they not dead?” I asked softly.
“I cannot answer you,” he replied in a subdued voice. “I almost forget what they mean by DEAD in the old world. If I said a person was dead, my wife would understand one thing, and you would imagine another. This is but one of my treasure vaults,” he went on. “Not all my guests are laid in vaults: out there on the moor they lie thick as the leaves of a forest after the first blast of your winter — thick, let me say rather, as if the great white rose of heaven had shed its petals over it. All night the moon reads their faces, and smiles.”
“But why leave them in the corrupting moonlight?” I asked.
“Our moon,” he answered, “is not like yours — the old cinder of a burnt-out world. Her beams embalm the dead, not corrupt them. You observe that here the sexton lays his dead on the earth; he buries very few under it! In your world he lays huge stones on them, as if to keep them down. I watch for the hour to ring the resurrection-bell, and wake those that are still asleep. Your sexton looks at the clock to know when to ring the dead-alive to church; I hearken for the cock on the spire to crow: ‘AWAKE, THOU THAT SLEEPEST, AND ARISE FROM THE DEAD!’”

I began to conclude the self-styled sexton was in truth an insane parson: the whole thing was too mad to be real. But how was I to escape? I was helpless. In this world of the half-dead, the raven and his wife were the only living souls I had yet encountered. Whither could I turn for help? I was lost in a space larger than imagination; for if here two things, or any parts of them, could occupy the same space, why not twenty or ten thousand? I dared not think further in that direction.
“You seem in your dead to see differences beyond my perception!” I ventured to remark.
“None of those you see,” he answered, “are in truth quite dead yet, and some have but just begun to come alive and die. Others had begun to die, that is to come alive, long before they came to us; and when such are indeed dead, that instant they will wake and leave us. Almost every night some rise and go. But I will not say more, for I find my words only mislead you! This is the couch that has been waiting for you,” he ended, pointing to one of the three empty frames.
“Why just this?” I said, beginning to tremble, stalling for time with parley.
“For reasons which one day you will be glad to know,” he answered.
“Why not know them now?”
“That also you will know when you wake.”
“But these are all dead, and I am alive!” I objected, shuddering.
“Not much,” rejoined the sexton with a smile, “—not nearly enough! Blessed be the true life that the pauses between its throbs are not death!”
“The place is too cold to let one sleep!” I said.
“Do these find it so?” he returned. “They sleep well — or will soon. Of cold they feel not a breath: it heals their wounds. Do not be a coward, Mr. Vane. Turn your back on fear, and your face to whatever may come. Give yourself up to the night, and you will rest indeed. Harm will not come to you, but a good you cannot foreknow.”

The sexton and I stood by the side of the couch, his wife, with the candle in her hand, at the foot of it. Her eyes were full of light, but her face was again of a still whiteness; it was no longer radiant.
“Would they have me make of a charnel-house my bed-chamber?” I cried aloud. “I will not. I will lie abroad on the heath; it cannot be colder there!”
“I have just told you that the dead are there also, ‘Thick as autumnal leaves that strow the brooks In Vallombrosa,’” said the librarian.
“I will NOT,” I cried again; and in the compassing dark, the two figures gleamed out like spectres that waited on the dead. Neither answered me; each stood still and sad, and looked at the other.
“Be of good comfort; we watch the flock of the great shepherd,” said the sexton to his wife. Then he turned to me. “Didst thou not find the air of the place pure and sweet when thou enteredst it?” he asked.
“Yes; but oh, so cold!” I answered.
“Then know,” he returned, and his voice was stern, “that thou who callest thyself alive, hast brought into this chamber the odours of death, and its air will not be wholesome for the sleepers until thou art gone from it!”

They turned and vanished deeper into the great chamber, and I was left alone in the moonlight with thousands of still, quiet forms. I turned to escape. What a long way I found it back through the aisles of the dead! At first I was too angry to be afraid, but as I grew calm, the still shapes loomed terrible at the edge of my vision. At last, with loud, crude offence to the gracious silence, I ran, I fled wildly, and bursting out, flung the door shut behind me. It closed with an awful, perfect silence.

I stood in pitch-darkness. Feeling about me, I found a second door, pulled it open, and was greeted by the dim glow of a familiar lamp. I stood in my own library, hand wrapped around the handle of the masked hidden door. Had I come to myself out of a vision? Or lost myself by stepping back into one? Which was the real — what I now saw, or what I had just ceased to see? Could both be real, interpenetrating yet unmingling? I threw myself on a couch, and fell asleep.

In the library was one small window facing east, through which, at this time of the year, the first rays of the sun shone upon a mirror that cast their reflection straight onto the masked door. When I woke, the beam of light was still there, and it drew my gaze like a magnet. With the sudden, certain feeling that the boundless chamber I had fled lay just behind that door, I sprang to my feet and wrenched it open. The sunlight, like an eager hound released from its leash, shot before me into the closet and pounced upon the gilded edges of a large book.
“What idiot,” I cried, “has put that book on the shelf the wrong way?” But the gilded edges, reflecting the light a second time, flung it onto a half-open nest of drawers tucked in the dark corner, and I spotted the gap in the wood.
“More meddling!” I grumbled, and went to close the drawer. It was stuffed full of old papers, so overfull it would not shut. Taking the topmost sheet out, I perceived that it was written in my father’s hand, a long, dense page. The words my eyes landed on first filled me with sharp, urgent eagerness to read the rest. I carried it to the library, sat down in one of the western windows, and began to read what follows.

Awe clings to every word I am about to set down, heavy as sea fog. The sun hangs gilded high above the rooftops, the sea stretches unbroken and sapphire under its gaze, the same old world pushes grass up through the soil and sends larks darting through the air I have breathed every day since I was a babe. But I know now all that outspread splendour is no more than a painted drop-scene on a stage, and at any moment it could be lifted away to reveal something far more strange, far more beautiful, beneath.

Not long after my father was laid to rest, I sat in the library one drowsy morning. I had been staring listlessly up at the portrait hung between the oak bookshelves, a man whose face I had always known only as that of some distant forgotten ancestor, and idly wishing I could learn even a sliver of the life he had lived. I pulled a random volume from the shelf and began to read, but when I glanced up a minute later, he was there: a thin, pale man in threadbare rusty black, stepping not from the direction of the door, but from the gap between me and that very portrait.

He was sharp-eyed, eager, with a long, prominent nose that made me think at once of the chipped earthen jug my sisters used to keep in the scullery, the one they’d nicknamed Mr. Crow.
“Finding myself in your vicinity, Mr. Vane, I thought I’d take the liberty of calling,” he said, his voice odd but soft, not unpleasant at all. “Your honoured grandfather treated me as a friend, you know—no presumption to say so, I should think, seeing I was his father’s librarian, raised alongside him from childhood.”
It didn’t even strike me then how impossibly old that would make him. “Forgive me, but where do you make your home now, Mr. Crow?” I said.

He smiled, amused. “You almost hit my name right on the head—shows the old Vane family insight. You have seen me before, once, when you were far too small to remember what you’d heard that day.”
“Where was that?”
“Right here in this very room. You could not have been more than four years old.”
I groped for the memory, half-sure I caught a flicker of it at the edge of my mind, and begged him to tell me his true name.
“There is such a thing as holding a memory without recognising it for what it is,” he remarked. “As for my name—you are close enough already—once it was Raven.”
I had heard that name, of course. Wild, half-forgotten tales of a strange, shadowy librarian had drifted down through the family for generations.

“It is so kind of you to call,” I said. “Won’t you sit?”
He sank into the nearest chair at once. “You knew my father then, I presume?”
“I knew him,” he said, a little odd, dry smile tugging at his mouth. “But he had no taste for my acquaintance, and we never once spoke. That man up there, though—” He nodded at the portrait on the wall. “Old Sir Up’ard, that’s what his villagers called him—he was a far closer friend to me than even your grandfather ever became.”
That was when I first began to think the interview was far stranger than any ordinary visitor call. But after all, it was no more odd for this man to remember Sir Upward, a man who had lived three generations back, than it was to claim he had been my great-grandfather’s personal librarian.

“I owe him a great deal,” he went on. “For all I’d read ten times more books than he ever had, his peculiar, focused line of study showed me a relationship between modes of being I never would have found on my own, or learned from anyone else alive back then.
“Would you tell me all of that story?” I asked.
“By all means—at least, as much as I am able. There is no such thing as a secret kept for no reason,” he said, and began to recount the tale.
“Sir Upward kept his personal library in that old closet over there, a hundred handwritten manuscripts or so, for printed books were a new, rare thing back then. One morning I sat in there cataloguing them, when he poked his head around the door and said ‘Come.’ I set down my pen and followed him: across the great hall, down a steep, rough stone stair, and along an underground passage to a small tower he’d just finished building. It was nothing but a winding stone stair and a single room at the very top. The door to that room had a massive, ancient lock, which he undid with the tiniest key I had ever seen in my life.
“I had barely crossed the threshold after him, when my eyes played a trick: he began to dwindle, shrinking smaller and smaller, until suddenly my vision snapped back to clarity, and I saw he was not shrinking at all, but moving away from me at impossible speed. In a minute he was no bigger than a speck on the horizon, with the blue peaks of mountains rising behind him against a sky of paler, softer blue. I knew that country well—for I had travelled back and forth to it dozens of times before, though I’d never found this particular shortcut to it.
“Many years later, long after that tower had crumbled to dust, I taught one of his descendants the same secret Sir Upward taught me. To this day, I still use your house when I want to take the shortest way home. Without your leave, I apologise, I must have earned a right of way through it by now—not from front door to back door, you understand, but from the bottom of it to the top.”

“So you are telling me, Mr. Raven,” I said, “that you walk straight through my house into another world, ignoring all the space that should separate the two?”
“That I walk through it is proof space exists, not that I defy it,” the old librarian replied.
“Please don’t quibble, Mr. Raven,” I said. “You know exactly what I mean.”
“There is a door hidden in your house, one single step through which carries me into a world that is entirely other than this one.”
“Is it a better world?”
“Not all of it. It is so different, in fact, that most of its physical laws, and many of its mental ones, do not work the way they do here. Moral laws, though—those are fundamentally the same, everywhere.”
“You stretch my belief almost to breaking,” I said.
“You think I’m a madman, then?”
“You do not look like one.”
“A liar, then?”
“You have given me no reason to think that.”
“You just don’t believe me?”
“I will step through that door with you right now, if you like. I trust you enough to risk the journey.”
“The blunder all my children make,” he murmured, half to himself. “The only door out is the door in.”

I began to wonder if he was the mad one. He sat silent for a minute, elbow propped on the table, head resting on his hand, his eyes fixed on the spines of the books across the table.
“A book,” he said, louder now, “is a door in—and therefore a door out. I see old Sir Up’ard before my eyes now,” he went on, closing them, his tone softening. “My heart swells with love for him. What world is he in, then?”
“The world of your heart,” I answered. “The idea of him lives there, that’s all.”
“Then there is at least one world your front hall door does not open into, is there not?”
“I will grant you that much. But the things in that world are not real things, you cannot hold them in your hands.”
“Think a little further,” he said. “Did anything ever truly become yours, that you did not first bring into that inner world of thought and feeling? That line of thinking is too far beyond you for now, though. Let me tell you—there are more worlds, and more doors to them, than you could imagine in a hundred years of thinking.”

He stood then, left the library, crossed the hall, and walked straight for the garret stair, moving with the ease of a man who knew every inch of the house better than I did. I followed him, staring at his back: his long dark hair hung straight and glossy down past his shoulders, his wide black coat pooled almost at his heels, his shoes looked far too big for his feet.
Light seeped in around the edges of the great slate roofing slabs up in the garret, showing gaps where no flooring had ever been laid, so we had to step carefully from one exposed joist to the next. Right in the middle of one of those open gaps stood a rough wooden partition, with a plain door set into it. I followed Mr. Raven through it into a small, dim chamber, its walls slanting upwards to meet at the sloped roof above.
“That is the door I told you of,” he said, pointing to a full-length oblong mirror propped against the far wall, standing right on the bare floor.
I stepped in front of it, and saw our two figures dimly reflected in its dusty, tarnished glass. Something about it made my skin prickle, uneasy. It looked old, forgotten, but for all its ordinary appearance, the carved eagle perched on its top edge, wings spread wide, looked somehow menacing.
“As a mirror,” the librarian said, “it has grown dull with age. That does not matter. Its clearness does not depend on its glass—it depends on the light.”
“Light?” I said. “There is hardly any light in here at all!”
He did not answer. Instead he reached up and pulled a small brass chain hanging on the opposite wall. A slow, heavy creak echoed through the room: the entire sloped top of the chamber was turning, revolving slowly. He paused, checked his pocket watch, then pulled the chain again.
“We are perfectly on time,” he said. “It is almost exactly noon.”
The roof creaked and turned for another minute, then he pulled two more chains, one after the other, before returning to the first. A moment later the chamber flooded with light: a perfect patch of noonday sun fell on to a small polished mirror set in the opposite wall, and I could see its reflected ray cutting across the dust motes straight to the big floor mirror. But no reflection came back. The light did not bounce off the glass— it passed right through it, and there was no second patch of sunlight anywhere on the floor of the chamber.
“Where did the sun rays go?” I cried.
“I cannot say,” Mr. Raven answered. “Perhaps they went back to wherever they first came from. I think, now, they belong to a sense we have not even developed yet.”
He started to talk then, about the strange links between mind and matter, between our senses and the hidden qualities of the world, in terms I could barely follow, then moved on to stranger things still I could not understand at all. He spoke of dimensions far more than the three we know, some of them tied to powers that lie dormant inside every human being, that we have not even guessed exist yet. But his words landed on me no better than that sunlight landed on the mirror— I thought, foolishly, that he could not possibly know what he was talking about.

Then, all at once, I realised our reflections were gone from the big mirror. The glass was filled not with our faces, but thick white mist. As I stared, the mist began to clear, and I saw the peaks of a high mountain range slowly rising into view, growing sharper and sharper. The mist vanished entirely a moment later, leaving a wide, windswept heath stretched out before me. Far off on its surface, the figure of a man was moving swiftly away.
I turned to speak to my companion. He was not there beside me.
I looked back at the mirror, and I saw that wide black coat flapping in the wind that blew on that far heath, his dark hair lifting in a gale that did not touch me at all where I stood in the garret.
Terror seized me, and I turned and ran from that dark room as fast as my legs could carry me.

I laid the yellowed manuscript down, breath catching at the quiet, unlooked-for comfort of it: my father, too, had peered behind the veil of that uncanny, unnameable world, had crossed paths with the uncanny Mr. Raven I’d fled only the night before. Then a memory pricked sharp, a gap I’d never once thought to question: no one had ever told me the cause, the smallest circumstance of my father’s death. The realization bloomed cold in my chest—he must have followed Mr. Raven, finally, and never come back.

Shame burned hot in my throat immediately. What unthinkable wonders of life, of death, of the vast, unseeable realms that stretched far past the thin edge of ordinary perception could I have already gathered, if I’d not run like a frightened child? The Ravens were nothing but good, I told myself fiercely. A single night under their roof would never have harmed me! They were strange, yes—but the man’s oddities were stitched from otherworldly faculty, the woman’s strangeness wrapped in a beauty so luminous it made mortal faces look dull as unglazed pottery. And I’d doubted them! Treated them as if they meant me harm, as if they hid some dark design behind their quiet smiles.

The longer I turned over every rude, fearful word and gesture I’d shown them, the more I loathed myself. What was there to fear from the dead who rested in that holy house? To share that soft, unbroken stillness was an honor I’d proven myself far too small and suspicious to deserve. What harm could that sleeping king on his bier, that gentle lady with the faint wound in her palm, ever have done me? I found myself aching for the quiet, stately sweetness of their faces, and broke into weeping.
