I fell onto the couch mid-sob, and dropped off to sleep so fast it was as if the dark of the room had swallowed my consciousness whole. I woke just as abruptly, certain someone had spoken my name. The house was so still it might have been an empty church, hollow with echoes of old prayers. A blackbird trilled bright from the lawn outside, clear as a bell.
“I’ll go back,” I said to the quiet air. “I’ll tell them I’m ashamed. I’ll do anything they ask of me.”

I rose and climbed the stairs straight to the garret, no hesitation in my steps. The wooden chamber was exactly as I’d left it, the tall mirror on the far wall dim, blurring the edges of every object it caught. It was nearly noon now, the sun a little higher in the sky than it had been on my first visit. I’d just have to shift the hood over the skylight a fraction, adjust the mirrors to catch the light right! If only I’d paid attention when Mr. Raven had done it the first time.
I hauled on the chains, tilting the first mirror to spill its beam of sunlight across the surface of the second. When I turned to look, the shapes I’d seen in my first crossing were there, but wavering—shapes of heather and far peaks, trembling like a landscape reflected in a pool ruffled by the first soft breath of wind. I pressed a finger to the glass, and it wouldn’t give an inch. I remembered then that polarized light was likely the trick, so I shifted the mirrors, twisted them, reworked their angles again and again, until by some happy chance the light slotted perfectly between them.

There were the blue mountains, sharp and steady, no tremor to them at all. I stepped forward, and my boots sank straight into soft, springy heather.
All I remembered of the path to the Ravens’ cottage was that we’d passed through a thick pine forest. I tramped past thickets and small stands of fir, over and over convincing myself I recognized some rock or fern, but no great forest stretched before me. By the time the sun hung low, bleeding red across the horizon, the air was sharp with the first bite of coming winter. And then I saw it, a small dark shape lurching toward me across the moor: the raven himself.

I ran to meet him. “Sir,” I said, bowing my head, “I beg your pardon for my rudeness last night. Take me with you now, I’m sorry, I know I don’t deserve it.”
He looked up at me, dark eyes glinting. “Ah,” he said, after a long pause. “My wife did not expect you tonight. She regrets that we ever encouraged you to stay with us last week.”
“Take me to her, please,” I begged, humbled. “Let me tell her how sorry I am.”
“It is no use,” he said. “Your time to rest was not ripe then, or you would never have fled us. It is not ripe now. I cannot show you the way. The dead rest soft under their daisies, all of them curled in the roots of heaven’s flowers—they were so happy at the thought of how you would delight when winter passed, and the soft bird-loud morning came. The moment you turned your back to run, they shivered in their beds. When the great spring of the universe comes—though that is ages off, how many I do not know, and do not care to know—then you might be welcome.”

“Tell me one thing, Mr. Raven,” I said, my throat tight. “Is my father with you? Did you see him after he left our world?”
“Yes. He is with us, fast asleep. That was him you saw the night you fled, his arm slung over the edge of his coverlet, his hand half curled.”
“Why didn’t you tell me? I was that close to him, and I never knew!”
“And so close you turned your back on him,” the raven corrected. “If you had been ready to lie down beside him, you would have known him of your own accord. Old Sir Up’ard, and your great-great-grandfather, both rose and left us long ago. Your great-grandfather has rested with us for many a year—he will soon begin to stir. You saw him last night, too, though you had no way of knowing.”
“Why of course no way?”
“Because he is far closer to waking than you are. No soul that will not first rest can ever wake.”
“I don’t understand a word of that!”
“You turned away, and would not let yourself understand.”

I fell silent, panicking that he would walk away and leave me there. “My grandfather,” I pressed. “Is he with you too?”
“No. He is still in the Evil Wood, fighting the dead.”
“Where is the Evil Wood? I will go find him.”
“You will not find him, but you will not miss the wood. It is the place where those who refuse to sleep wake at night, to hunt their own dead, kill them, and bury them again.”
“I still do not understand.”
“Naturally not. Neither do I understand you. I cannot read your heart, or read your face. When my wife and I cannot understand our children, it is because there is not enough of them yet to be understood. Only God can make sense of foolishness.”

I felt naked, hollow, worthless, as if the moor wind had blown all the substance out of me. “Then please,” I said, “show me the shortest way home. There are more ways than one, I know, I have come by two already.”
“There are many more than two,” he said.
“Tell me how to tell which is the nearest.”
“I cannot,” the raven answered. “You and I use the same words, but they hold different meanings for us. We often cannot tell people what they need to know, because they are busy chasing something else, something they want to know instead, and they would only twist and misunderstand whatever truth we gave them. Home is held soft in the palm of your own hand, there is no use telling you how to reach it. But you will get there. You must get there. You have no choice. Every soul that is not at home is on their way there. You thought the house you fled was your home, the one you ran back to after you left us: if that had truly been your home, you would never have been able to leave it. No one can leave home. And no one ever reaches home without walking the path there themselves.”

“Riddles piled on riddles!” I burst out. “I did not come here to be fed puzzles.”
“No, but you showed up, and the riddles were waiting for you. You are the only real riddle there is. What you call riddles are plain truths, they only seem confusing because you yourself are not yet whole and true.”
“Worse and worse!” I cried.
“And you must answer the riddles,” he went on. “They will keep asking themselves of you, over and over, until you finally understand who you are. The whole universe is a riddle trying to unfold itself, and you are barring your own door tight against it.”
“Have pity,” I said. “Tell me what to do, where I must go.”
“How am I to tell you your own purpose, or the path that leads to it?”
“If I cannot go home, at least point me to other souls like me.”
“I know of none nearby. The beings most like you are that way.” He pointed his beak toward the horizon. All I could see was the setting sun, so bright it blinded me.

“Fine,” I said, bitter. “I feel hardly used. Dragged from what I thought was home, abandoned in a strange world, and no one will tell me where I am or what I am meant to do!”
“You forget,” the raven said, “that the first time I brought you here, and you refused my hospitality, you made it back to what you called home perfectly safe. This time you came here of your own accord. Good night.”
He turned, and began to walk slowly away, his beak tilted toward the heather.

I stood dazed. It was true I’d come of my own will, but hadn’t I come to make amends? My heart ached, my brain was empty of all quest, all purpose, all hope, all desire. I stared after him, half inclined to follow, but knowing it was useless.
Then he pounced, all his weight slamming down onto his beak, and dug furiously at a spot in the heather for several long moments. He fluttered his wings, tossed his head back, and something shot out of his bill, flying high into the air. The sun set the very same instant, the world dipping into dusk fast, but that small something unfurled into soft golden radiance, pulsing toward me like a firefly, but far larger, its glow warm and yellow. It flew over my head. I turned, and followed.

Here I pause my tale, to note that every step of this account is a struggle to put words to things that refuse to be said, to describe events and beings so far outside the bounds of our ordinary mortal world that I can only render how they felt to me, not the things themselves. Even that small task I do with a constant, sharp awareness of failure. I can only ever show one small facet of a reality that has a thousand layered, overlapping meanings, one thin sphere of a truth that unfolds in endless concentric layers. A single object here could be a dozen different things at once, with a quiet, shifting core of identity that changed its shape the moment you thought you’d pinned it down. The language and perceptions of our mortal world are not made to carry this strangeness clearly, to make it make sense even to someone who has walked these moors far longer than I have. I might be fully, unshakably certain I was watching a scene of living, moving activity, and at the very same second, be aware in my bones that I was reading a metaphysical argument written across the sky.

The air thickened to pitch-black ink as the otherworldly winter coiled tight around me, and the fluttering, fire-winged sprite I had trailed for hours blazed brighter, its erratic flight stilling to hover, waiting. The moment I stepped beneath its radiance, it drifted slowly ahead, pausing every few beats of its wings to linger over patches of jutted, half-buried rock. Each time I glanced up it had swelled in size, until at last it cast a long, wavering shadow at my feet. It was no creature I recognized from Earth: half swallow, half giant butterfly, its wings broad and nearly square, strobing every hue of the rainbow with that sharp, darting double-beat of flight no terrestrial bug or bird could match.

I was so transfixed by the riot of unnameable colors spilling off its plumage that I tripped over a low, moss-crusted rock, sprawling hard and stunned. When I dragged my wits back, it hovered an arm’s length above my face, radiating a full, unbroken chord of light streaked with thousands of subtle gradients, tints no human eye had ever registered back on my home planet. I staggered to my feet and pressed on, but I could not tear my gaze from the glowing little wonder long enough to watch my steps, and soon my foot slammed hard into a half-buried stone. Afraid I’d crack my ankle and fall prey to the endless dark, I sank down onto the cold heather just to watch it, and a sharp, hungry longing bloomed hot in my chest: I had to hold it in my hands.

To my wild, disbelieving delight, it started sinking toward me. Slow at first, then faster, growing larger and brighter, like it was pouring the entire universe’s hoard of light right into my waiting palm. I reached out, fingers closing around it — and the second my skin made contact, its light snuffed out entirely. All went pitch black, and a cold, heavy book, its boards splayed wide open, lay dead and inert in my grip. I yelped and hurled it as hard as I could, only to hear it land with a dull thud somewhere in the thick heather. I buried my face in my hands, hunched there in shivering, utter misery.

But the cold bit so deep it started to burn through my coat, and I knew if I stayed still I’d freeze solid before dawn. The moment I lurched to my feet, a faint prickle of light pricked at the edge of my vision. “Did it come back to life?” I gasped, and a stabbing, bright spike of hope went through me. No. It was the thin, sharp edge of an unfamiliar moon, peeking over the perfectly flat horizon. It gave me light, yes — but no guidance. It would not hover, would not wait for my stumbling steps. It only offered me a blind, unknowing choice of paths. It rose full, pale and strange, and I started to pick my way west toward the line of low hills I could just make out breaking the flat dark horizon.

What a night that was, before I ever got there! The moon stared down at me, odd and unblinking, like it knew a secret I didn’t. Its light was icy, but not uncurious, almost pitying, like it was wondering what a human was doing wandering out in this cursed, unlighted waste. It was no moon I’d ever seen hanging over Earth — its face bore a completely unfamiliar pattern of craters, its light a strange silver-blue glow I could not place, as if it reflected a sun that had burned out a million years before. Every time I looked up, it felt like it was staring right at me, full bore. At first I was annoyed, like it was a rude stranger prying into my business, but soon I thought I saw that soft, wondering pity in its gaze: why are you out here, all alone, in the dark? That was the first moment I truly grasped how horrifying it was to be awake, alive, adrift in an endless, uncaring universe. I was. There was no opting out.

As I walked, the heather under my boots gave way to bare, spongy soil, like dry, powdery peat. To my horror, the whole ground heaved under my feet for a split second, then I saw what looked like the ripple of an earthquake snaking ahead of me, faint and shadowy in the low moonlight. It vanished into the distance, but before I could even process what I’d seen, a single wave of the soil rose up and rolled slow toward me. It burst a yard from my feet, and a tawny, tiger-like creature scrambled out, mouth and matted ears crusted with dark mould, its eyes blazing like embers as it charged at me, fangs bared in a soundless snarl. I stood frozen, not feeling fear or courage, just transfixed. It snapped its head down and plunged straight back into the dirt, vanishing like it had never been there.

“The moon’s addling my brain,” I muttered, getting back on my path. “Nothing here is real, it’s all phantoms, the stuff of dreams. I’m walking through a hallucination, a vain show.” I told myself that to keep my panic at bay, never guessing that the strange moon I was mocking was the only thing keeping me safe from the very real horrors I thought were just my imagination. Its light held the monsters paralyzed, or I would have never survived a second step on that shifting, living ground. “I won’t be scared of something that’s only pretend,” I told myself, but my skin crawled at the thought of walking across a sea of dirt where things like that lurked just below the surface.

Then, not two steps ahead of me, the head of a worm started slowly pushing up through the soil. It was as big as a polar bear’s head, looked almost identical, a thick white mane cascading down over its bright red neck. The wriggling, heaving movements as it pulled its massive length out of the earth were so grotesque I couldn’t look away. The second its tail was free, it collapsed, exhausted, flopping weakly to try and burrow back down into the dirt. “Does it feed on the dead?” I wondered. “Is it too weak to hurt the living? If they can smell prey and come all the way out, why aren’t they tearing me apart?” I know now it was the moon’s light that was holding them, locking their muscles, draining their strength.

All that long night, hideous creatures no two alike rose up out of the dirt to threaten me. Some were even more grotesque for their bright, beautiful coloring: one massive serpent was covered head to flickering tail in feathers that glowed every color of a tropical sunset. I got so used to their harmless, frozen snarls that I started making up my own silly monstrosities in my head to pass the time, never suspecting I was only alive because that staring moon was holding them back. Even though its light was only reflected — second, third, hundredth hand — it was still light, enough to chain the evil things down so I could walk safe. If I had known what would happen the second its light vanished from this cursed stretch of ground? That they’d be on me in a heartbeat, no mercy, writhing all over me, every last one of them as deadly as they’d seemed harmless before? I never would have sauntered so carelessly. I watched the moon sink low, slow, solemn, anxious, the only unease I felt being the stupid fear I’d get lost — as if I even had a path to lose.

I was almost at the hills I’d set as my goal, the moon just a hand’s breadth above their skyline, when all the burrowing and wallowing stopped. The soil lay still, bare, silent. That was when I saw a woman, walking slow across the glowing dirt. A thin white mist curled around her, shifting in and out of the shape of a soft white gown, gathering tight to her form then blowing loose again, pushed and pulled by a wind that followed only her steps. She was beautiful, but her face was twisted with equal parts unbearable pride and crushing misery, so much so I could barely believe my eyes. She paced back and forth, over and over, grabbing at the mist trying to wrap it around herself to cover the dark, festering spot on her left side, pressing her hand to it like she was trying to smother the pain welling up from it. Her hair hung all the way down to her ankles, pale gold in the moonlight, and sometimes the wind tangled it so tight with the mist I couldn’t tell where her hair ended and the fog began.

Suddenly she pressed both hands hard to her heart, and crumpled to the ground. The mist drifted up off her, dissolving into the air. I ran for her, but she started writhing in such searing, silent torture that I froze, horrified. A heartbeat later, her legs tore free of her body and slithered away as serpents. Her arms flew off her shoulders, panicking, also serpents. Something bat-like flapped up from what was left of her, and when I blinked, she was gone.

The ground heaved like a storm-tossed sea, and raw, cold terror seized me. I turned and ran for the hills. I was already halfway up the base slope when the moon sank behind the nearest peak, plunging me into total shadow. Behind me rose a long, wailing, sickening cry, like a thousand frustrated, hungry voices all at once — the only sound I’d heard since the dead butterfly-book had thudded into the heather. It shook my bones so hard my heart thudded like a flag in a gale. I glanced over my shoulder, saw dozens of dark shapes bounding toward me, and ran for the crest of the ridge where the moon still spilled light over the edge. It seemed to linger there, just for me, so I could see what I was doing to defend myself.

I scrambled faster, and soon crested the ridge in full moonlight. I could hear the creatures panting right at my heels. The very first one launched itself at me, fangs bared, snarling with greedy hate — and we both stumbled straight into the moon’s silver glow. It flared bright, angry, and the beast fell off me, melting into a shapeless, bodiless splotch of shadow. A surge of strength flooded my veins, and I turned to face the rest. One by one, as they lunged into the light, they dissolved with thin, wailing howls. I swore I saw a faint, knowing smile on that round, pale face hanging above me.

I climbed to the very top of the ridge. Far below, the moon sank fast toward a new, far horizon. The air up here was pure, sharp, warm in my lungs. I climbed down a little way, found a sheltered spot that held the heat, and sat down to wait for the dawn. The moon vanished over the edge of the world, and the dark closed in around me, thick and total once again.

I fell fast asleep the moment my head touched the stone rise, and woke to the first gold spill of sunrise over the horizon. I clambered back to its peak and looked behind me: the hollow I had crossed in silver moonlight lay completely empty, not a footprint, not a rustle of displaced grass, no trace of the uncanny life I had sensed seething beneath its calm surface. Could the wide, seemingly still expanse stretching ahead of me truly swarm with ravenous, devouring creatures hidden from casual sight? I turned my gaze forward, to the path I was fated to travel. It stretched as a vast, barren desert, one single smudge of darker colour far on the horizon that might be some patch of forest. There was no mark anywhere of human or animal presence: no wisp of smoke, no trail of dust, no faint line of ploughed earth or cultivated growth. Not a single cloud drifted in the perfectly clear sky, no thin haze softened the knife-sharp edge of the circular horizon.
I climbed down from the rise and set out toward that distant patch of green. If any living thing could survive in this dead land, it would be there; surely nothing could find purchase on the bare stone this side of the trees. When I stepped onto the plain, I found it stretched as far as the eye could reach: the entire bed of a vanished ancient river, scored by a thousand dried water runs, its rock here flat and worn smooth, there humped and jagged into sharp pinnacles. Not a single drop of moisture lingered in any of the channels. Some held patches of crumbly dry moss, and the faces of some rocks bore lichens so hard they were almost fused to the stone beneath them. The air that must once have rung nonstop with the pleasant noise of falling and running water was now as silent as a tomb.
It took my entire long, blazing day of walking to reach that green patch, which I found was indeed a full-grown forest—though I had not crossed even the tiniest trickle or brook the whole way. All through the glowing noon I was haunted by an aural mirage: the sound of a thousand streams and falls was so loud in my ears I kept squinting for a glimpse of water, only to stare at bare sunbaked rock.
The sun was dipping low toward the horizon when I stepped off the last slab of the dead riverbed and into the trees. Sunk below their crowns, its golden rays slanted between thick pillar-like trunks, spilling into pools of cool, blessed shadow that wrapped around me like a hug. I had expected a stand of pines, but these trees were half familiar and half otherworldly: some bore a passing resemblance to oaks or ash I knew, others were unlike any earth tree I had ever seen. I threw myself down beneath the boughs of a eucalyptus-like tree in full blossom: its flowers grew as hard skull-shaped calyxes, their tops peeling back like lids to spill frothy pale bloom that looked exactly like foaming brain tissue. From under the shadow of its long, curved falchion leaves I stared deep into the forest’s endless receding aisles.
Dusk fell fast after that. The treetops’ branches wove shut above me, closing off the glades and corridors I had been gazing into, and night wrapped tight around the wood. The cold hit sharp and instant, biting through my clothes to settle in my bones. How do I even describe the wild ghostly uncanniness of that night? The tree I leaned against rose tall and straight before branching, but its lower boughs curved so low they looked ready to fold shut around me at any moment, trapping me entirely within their shade. My roving, half-lidded eyes watched the clumpy foliage’s outlines shift, twisting into shapes that were not leaves at all.
A light wind picked up, rocking the boughs of a nearby tree, setting every twig and leaf swaying in rhythm with the whole. The mass of green blurred into a pack of wolves straining against an invisible wizard’s leash, their bodies heaving so hard greyhounds could never have pulled with half that savage desperation. I watched them, my fascination growing as the wind blew harder and their movements felt more and more alive. Further off, a larger, denser clump of foliage resolved into a line of war-horses, their heads and forequarters sticking out as if they were caparisoned and held in stalls no eye could see. Their necks bobbed up and down in impatience, and as the gale grew stronger it broke their steady vertical rhythm into wild side-to-side swaying. Their heads were gaunt and strange, half of them nothing but bare skulls, one with skin stretched tight over its bleached bones, another missing its entire lower jaw, hanging low as if in unutterable weariness, then heaving up every few seconds as if trying to ease a phantom bit in its mouth. Above them, perched upright on the end of a high branch, was the shape of a woman, waving her arms in imperious, demanding gestures.
The clarity of these shapes first surprised me, then made my skin prickle with unease. What if they stopped being tricks of shadow and wind, and overpowered my brain to become real? Then the last of the twilight drained away into total darkness, the wind cut off completely, every uncanny shape vanished into the black, and I fell asleep.
It was still pitch dark when I began to hear a distant, confused rushing noise tangled with faint, far-off cries. The sound swelled and swelled, until a tumult of thousands of gathering beings filled the entire wood. The noise drew nearer on all sides at once, until the spot where I lay was the dead centre of the commotion spreading through every acre of trees. I did not dare move a single hand or foot, terrified I would give away my presence to every hostile thing creeping through the dark.
At last the moon rose over the treeline, spilling pale silver light into the wood. With her very first gleam, the noise exploded into deafening uproar, and I began to make out dim shapes swirling all around me. As she climbed higher and grew brighter, the sounds grew even louder, and the shapes sharpened into clarity. A furious battle was raging all around where I lay hidden. Wild cries, roars of rage, the thunder of first onslaught, the drag of prolonged struggle, tangled together with full, articulate words in my ears. Curses and creeds, snarls and sneers, peals of mocking laughter, sacred names and howls of hate, all poured out in chaotic, overlapping jumble. Skeletons and phantoms fought in the maddest confusion imaginable. Swords swept straight through the misty phantoms, only making them shiver. Maces crashed into skeletons, shattering their bones hideously—but no one fell, no one stopped fighting, as long as two bones remained connected at a joint. Loose bones of men and horses lay scattered in heaps across the ground, and the fighting skeletons crunched over them underfoot. Bone-white gaunt skeleton steeds charged everywhere; on foot or on the backs of misty wind-tossed phantom horses, the indestructible spectres raged, ravened, and raved. Weapons clashed, hooves crushed, while skeleton jaws and phantom throats swelled the uproar with every war cry of every opinion, good or bad, that had ever bred strife, injustice, or cruelty in any world. The holiest words of all were screamed right along with the most hate-fuelled blows. Truths twisted by lies flew through the air on the wind of javelins and flying bone shards. Every few moments a fighter would turn on his own comrades, striking even more wildly, screaming over and over “THE TRUTH! THE TRUTH!” as he fought. I saw one man wheeling in an endless circle, striking out at everyone within reach. Exhausted pairs would sit side by side for a minute to catch their breath, then leap back to fight again with fresh fury. No one stopped to comfort the fallen, no one stepped aside to spare a wounded enemy.
The moon shone on through the whole long night, until the sun began to rise, and all that time I caught sight of that woman floating above the tormented, fighting throng, moving freely back and forth across the battle, one outstretched arm urging the fighters on, the other pressed tight to her side. “Ye are men: slay one another!” she shouted over the roar. I saw her dead glassy eyes, the dark stain on her chest, and recalled the sight of her I had witnessed back in the hollow. That was the battle of the dead, which I saw and heard as I lay hidden under the tree.
Just before sunrise, a soft breeze blew through the forest, and a deep, clear voice called out: “Let the dead bury their dead!” At those words, all the thousands of combatants dropped silent, noiseless, all at once. When the sun peered over the treeline, there was not a single bone to be seen anywhere—only a few scattered withered branches here and there.
I stood up, my limbs stiff from lying so long still, and set off to resume my journey through the quietest wood that had ever grown out of earth. The morning wind had died when the sun rose, and the trees stood perfectly silent. No bird sang, no squirrel, no mouse, no weasel showed itself, not even a belated moth flitted across my path. But as I walked, I kept strict watch over my own mind, not daring to let my gaze linger on any clump of leaves or twisted bough. The whole time I could hear faint distant sounds of mattocks and spades, the crunch of hurtling bones, and I was half convinced that at any moment my eyes might open to sights I did not want to see. Daylight prudence whispered to me that maybe those ten thousand phantoms were only waiting for my own willing fancy to step forward and appear.
In the middle of the afternoon I walked out of the wood entirely, and found a second vast network of dry water courses spread out before me. At first I thought I must have wandered off my intended path, and turned around to retrace my steps—but I soon realized that was not the case, and concluded I had reached another, separate branch of the same ancient dead riverbed. I set off crossing it at once, and was at the bottom of a wide deep channel when the sun set. I sat down to wait for the moon, grew drowsy, and stretched myself out on the dry moss. The very second my head touched the ground, I heard the sound of rushing streams, every kind of sweet, watery noise. That veiled, molten melody of water song lulled me into a dreamless sleep, and when I woke the sun was already high, the whole wrinkled rocky country spread out wide and visible before me. It lay covered in long shadows, striped and mottled like the pelt of some great wild beast. As the sun climbed higher, the shadows shrank, as if the rocks were sucking back all the darkness that had oozed out of them through the night.
Until that moment, I think I had loved my Arab mare and my books more than any living man or woman I had ever known. But now my soul was athirst for the presence of another human, and I even ached to meet the inhabitants of this alien world the raven had described so vaguely, the ones who were closest to my own kind. With a heavy yet hopeful heart, my mind haunted by the quiet doubt that I was even moving in any real direction at all, I kept wearily travelling north-west and by south.

Winding through the sun-dappled, cut-throat channels of the strange dry riverbed, I stumbled on what I first mistook for a small low shrub – a forward picket, I hoped, of a far larger army of vegetation stretching deeper into the wild. I knelt to study it closer, and spotted a single small, unrecognizable fruit dangling from its thinnest branch. Too wary of the alien landscape to risk poisoning, I did not reach for it, never guessing that hundreds of eyes burned bright over the rim of the nearest boulders, all fixed on me, all holding their breath to see if I would take that first bite.

I moved on, encountering one slightly bigger plant after another, until I found myself wading through dense clustered growth of the same kind, and realized they were not shrubs at all, but stunted dwarf trees. By the time I neared the bank of this second fork of the old river, the channels were so choked with them I could barely pick my way across the stretches I could not leap over. Once, a great roar of motion burst from the foliage near me, like thousands of birds erupting from an ivy-choked wall, but when I spun to look, there was nothing to see.

Soon I came upon a stand of full-sized, heavy-fruited trees lining the edge of a wide hollow that had clearly once been a lake basin. A thick forest seemed to spill down from the left ridge to fill the old lake bed: while the trees on the upper slopes were of a dozen varied kinds, every tree in the hollow bore fruit. I wandered a few paces down the soft slope of moss-mingled grass, and dropped my tired body to rest. A little further down, a tiny dwarf tree hung heavy with the rosiest little apples, no bigger than a cherry, its top bough sitting level with my outstretched hand. I plucked one, bit into it, and found it impossibly delicious.

I was already lifting my hand to pluck a second, when a gale of children’s shouting and bright, brook-clear laughter burst out, startling me half out of my skin – not with fear, but joy.
“He likes our apples! He likes our apples! He’s a good giant! He’s a good giant!” hundreds of tiny voices chorused.
“But he’s a giant!” one small, skeptical voice objected.
“He *is* rather big,” another conceded, “but littleness isn’t everything! You won’t avoid growing big and stupid if you don’t take care of yourself, anyway.”

I propped myself up on one elbow and stared. Above me, below me, all across the slope, stood a throng of children of every age: some barely steady on their running legs, others closer to twelve or thirteen, with three or four older teens hanging back in a quiet little knot, far less excitable than the rest. They chattered in giddy groups, argued and teased and contradicted each other like a crowd of grown city-dwellers, but with far more merriment, kinder manners, and sharper sense. I guessed they had seen me reach for a second apple and taken that as proof the first had pleased me, but I could not fathom how that had led them to decide I was good – though I did not blame the one child who had urged caution. I did not speak, afraid I would startle them all away, certain I would learn more by listening than by asking questions. That I could understand every word they said did not strike me as strange, after all: to understand is no more a miracle than to love.

A soft murmur of movement rippled through the crowd, and a bright, roguish little boy with a face like sunshine stepped forward, to press a huge, coarse green apple into my palm. The whole throng fell dead silent, every pair of eyes fixed expectantly on me.
“Eat, good giant,” he said.

I sat up, smiled my thanks, and bit into the fruit – only to fling it as far from me as I could the second the bitter, sour flesh hit my tongue.
A roar of pure delight went up. They swarmed me all at once, clambering over my legs, my arms, my shoulders, kissing my face and my hands, tangling their small fingers in my hair, until I collapsed back into the soft grass, nearly smothered under the weight of the horde of lovely little goblins. “Good giant! We knew you would come! You dear, good strong giant!” they cheered, their babble rising and falling, peals of joy bursting over and over from hundreds of clear small throats.

Then silence fell again, sharp and sudden. The children on top of me scrambled off, the rest backed away, and they tugged at my sleeves to yank me to my feet. Every sweet face that had been crinkled with laughter a moment before was pinched with worry.
“Get up, good giant!” a little girl begged. “Hurry, much hurry! He saw you throw his apple away!”

I was already on my feet before she finished, following the line of her pointing hand up the slope. There on the brow stood a loutish, sullen, ill-favoured fellow a few inches taller than myself. He looked furious, but I saw no reason to fear him at first: he carried no weapon, and then I realized every last one of my small friends had vanished without a trace.

He began to lumber down the slope, and I climbed upward to claim higher, firmer footing. He growled like a feral beast as he turned to face me. When I reached a flat level patch I stopped and waited for him. As he drew near, he held out one hand as if in greeting, but then snapped it back as if to strike, before holding it out again, gesturing pointedly to the spot where I had thrown the green apple. I made a face of open disgust, shook my head to reject his claim to the fruit. He let out a howl of unthinking rage that clearly screamed, *Do you dare say my apple was not fit to eat?*

“Even the best tree can grow one bad apple,” I told him. I do not know if he understood a single word I said, but he lunged forward a step, and I braced for the fight. He waited a beat to attack, though, until a second brute just like him, who had crept up silent behind my back, was close enough to strike – then they both rushed me. I landed a solid blow right to the first one’s face, but the second clubbed me hard across the back of the head, and between the two of them I was overpowered in seconds.

They dragged me through the upper woods to the squalid village where their tribe lived: a huddle of miserable huts cobbled together from fallen branches and loose stones. They threw me inside one, shoved me to the dirt floor, and kicked me until I could barely breathe. A woman stood by watching, her face blank and indifferent. In all the rest of my captivity there, I could barely tell the men apart from the women, their features so blunt and unvaried. More than once I wondered if I had stumbled on some sort of half-sentient fungoid people, gifted with just enough awareness to move, to snarl in anger, and to grasp at what they wanted. Their food – a mess of bitter tubers, acrid bulbs, and coarse unripe fruit – was so repulsive I could barely stand to touch it, and nothing enraged them more than seeing me turn up my nose at their meals. The women cuffed me, the men kicked me, every time I refused to swallow a bite.

I spent that night on the dirt floor, half too sore to move, but I slept deeply, and woke the next morning a little refreshed. They dragged me back down to the valley, tied my ankles to a tree with a long rope, and pressed a flat, sharp-edged stone into my left hand. When I shifted it to my right, they kicked me until I switched it back, then gestured that I was to scrape all the bark off every non-fruiting branch they left within my reach. They kicked me once more for good measure, and walked away.

I set to the dreary work at once, hoping that if I did as they told me placidly, they would grow careless, leave me mostly to myself – so I could watch for my chance to escape. Luck was with me: one of the tiny dwarf apple trees grew right beside my post, and every other minute I plucked one of its small, sweet fruits and ate it, feeling my strength seep back into my sore limbs faster than I could have hoped.

I had only been hacking away at the giants’ assigned labor for a handful of minutes when I heard high, tinkling voices drift through the trees, soft as wind chimes strung with pine resin. Moments later, the Little Ones—as they soon told me they named themselves—came creeping out of copses of knee-high miniature trees that sprung like brushwood between the colossal, gnarled old-growth trunks. Within a minute, scores of them swarmed around my boots, their hair the hue of moss and wildflower petals, no bigger than the length of my thumb.

I made urgent signs that the giants had only just left me, and could not be far off, but they laughed outright, telling me the wind was clean of their stench now. “They are too blind to see us,” they trilled, their laughter pealing like a hundred tiny sheep bells ringing all at once.

“D’you like that rope tied round your ankles?” one little boy asked, tilting his head up at me.
“I want them to think I can’t tear it loose,” I replied.
He cackled so hard he nearly toppled over. “They can scarcely see their own stumpy feet! Walk with tiny shuffling steps, and they’ll never notice the rope’s gone slack.”

The tallest of their girls, the quiet, sharp-eyed one who would soon reveal herself as their de facto matriarch, knelt down to the thick, hemp knot around my ankles. I smiled, assuming her dainty needle-thin fingers could never work the clumsy tangle—but it slipped free in two seconds flat. They bade me sit down on the moss, and fed me fruits so sweet they burst like honey on my tongue, tiny plums and wild berries no bigger than my fingernail. The smallest of them immediately began to play with me in the wildest, giddiest fashion, clambering over my lap, across my shoulders, tugging my hair and playing tag across my forearms, so that I could not possibly have returned to my chopping work if I tried. When the first set of children grew tired, another took their places, the game stretching on until the sun bled crimson over the treetops, and heavy, thudding steps shook the ground under my feet.

The Little Ones scattered in an instant, and I fumbled to re-wrap the loose rope around my ankles, retying it to look as if I had never touched it. “We have to be careful,” the girl who had freed me whispered, the last to linger. “One misstep from one of their horrid fat feet could crush a dozen of us before we even feel it!”
“Can they not perceive you at all?” I asked.
“They might catch a flicker of movement. If they’d found all of us piled on top of you ten minutes ago, they’d have stamped every last one flat. They hate every living thing that isn’t as big and dull as they are—half the time they’re not even properly alive, themselves!” She trilled a note sharp and bright as a wren’s call, and the next blink not a single Little One remained in sight, no sound, no trace, as if they had seeped straight into the moss.

My giant “master,” as he thought of himself, had arrived to drag me home. He wrenched my ankles hard to yank me across the dirt, threw me on the ground outside his hut’s door, retied my feet, gave me a brutal kick to the ribs, and trundled back inside. I could have slipped the rope the second he left, run for the woods and escaped for good—but I did not. I had friends now, bright and winsome and full of such soft, glowing joy that I could not bear to leave them behind, not until I knew every secret they held. “Tomorrow,” I thought, giddy in the dark, “I will see them again.” And from the moment the last snore rumbled from the huts until I fell asleep, I heard their tiny whispers brushing my cheeks, tucking stray leaves out of my hair, watching over me. They never left me entirely alone after that, not for an hour.

I never came to know the giants at all. There was scarcely anything to know. They never grew the least bit friendly, but they were far too stupid to invent new cruelties: most of the time, when they went to kick me, I just caught their calloused foot, twisted it, sent them sprawling in the dirt, and they never renewed their attempt that same day. But the Little Ones? Every day they said or did something that made my chest feel light, something that surprised me. I grew more and more loath to leave them. While I worked, they came and went in constant little bursts, amusing me, chasing away every scrap of the misery and most of the weariness of my monotonous toil. Very soon I loved them more than I could ever put into words. They did not know the book-learning of my own world, but they were wise in the way old trees and hidden streams are wise, and seemed capable of learning anything they set their minds to. I had no bed but bare dirt, but almost every morning I woke curled in a nest of children: one tucked in the crook of my arm, one resting on my chest, the smallest snuggled against my neck, trading turns through the night so no one got left out. I never suffered from the night’s bitter cold, not even a little.

The first thing they did at dawn, and the last before sunset, was bring me more of their delicious wild fruit. One morning, I woke to find myself entirely alone. As my senses cleared, I heard soft, muffled sounds of excitement, and the tall, grave girl their whole community called their mother stepped out from the wood, followed by a whole throng of the Little Ones, all glowing with silent jubilation so they would not wake the giant dozing ten feet from where I lay. She carried a boy-baby swaddled in dandelion down in her arms, the youngest they had ever found. Lona—for that was her name—laid the tiny baby in my arms. He blinked one eye open to stare at me, then closed them again and fell fast asleep.

“He loves you already!” Lona said, her eyes sparkling.
“Where did you find him?” I asked.
“Out in the wood, of course,” she laughed. “That’s where we always find them. Isn’t he a beauty? We’ve been out all night looking for him. Sometimes it takes hours to track them down!”
“How do you know there’s a new baby to find in the first place?”
“I can’t tell,” she said. “Everyone rushes to tell everyone else, but no one ever remembers who said it first. Sometimes I think someone murmurs it in their sleep, and another hears it half-awake. When there’s a baby waiting in the wood, no one stops to ask questions—by the time we think to wonder, we’re already running to find it.”

The more questions I asked, the more odd, gentle truths I pried loose. They did not count their numbers, she told me, it would not “feel smooth,” they would not want to know the exact tally of their small, soft community. Babies never came from anywhere but the wood, fully finished, no messy unspoken bits no one could explain. She squeezed a drop of plum juice to the baby’s lips, and he sucked it down without ever waking, and she glowed with such gentle pride she looked like a tiny sun herself.

But there was one secret they hated to speak of. Some of the Little Ones, Lona said, after they thought they had finished growing, began to get bigger anyway. It was a frightful thing. It meant they were turning into giants. It happened when a Little One stopped caring about sharing, when they got greedy, then lazy, then dull, then cruel. They ate the big sour apples the giants cultivated, the fruit no Little One was ever supposed to touch, and they grew faster every day, until they forgot all the songs, forgot all their friends, forgot how to laugh like sheep bells. They woke one morning as full giants, and never remembered they had ever been small. Blunty, the moody little boy I had seen hoarding an apple nearly as big as his own head, was the latest one. It was already too late for him, Lona said, they had tried everything, but he would not listen.

A few days later, word spread that Blunty had vanished. There were a few grave faces among the older Little Ones, but no one mourned him for long. The next dawn, Lona crept to my side and whispered, “Look—by that quince tree. That’s the giant that was Blunty. Would you have known him?”
“Never,” I said, staring at the lumbering, blank-faced man shoveling apples into his mouth. “But now you say it, I can almost see the little boy through a fog. He looks so stupid.”
“That’s what happens when Little Ones refuse to stay little,” she sighed.

A cold, horrid realization settled over me then. Back in my own world, we called that growing up. We called chasing more bigness, more things, more weight we hoarded for ourselves—wealth—success. I had spent my whole life chasing that sort of bigness, never noticing I was slowly turning myself into a giant, too. I had been given the chance to stay small, stay soft, learn to laugh like the Little Ones forever, and I had almost thrown it all away. Blunty did not even know he had lost something—but I had just begun to understand mine.

For a long, soft stretch of days, I wanted nothing more than to spend the rest of my existence curled in the grass, laughing and chasing fireflies with the Little Ones. But a slow, restless thrum began to build under the surface of that quiet bliss: first the vague, gnawing certainty that I was not meant to laze and be fattened like the sows the brutish local boors kept penned in their styes. Then came the sharper realization that I was standing in a world stitched together by laws no outsider had ever mapped, and it was my duty to learn its shape. If I was ever to repay the gentle kindness the small beings had shown me, I could not stay willfully ignorant of the secrets they were too unworldly to explain.

It could not be natural, I reasoned, for their growth to be arrested forever. No universe could be built so discordantly that loveliness, truth, and purity could only survive if you throttled every natural path of development. Law and life could not be at such bitter odds that perfection required you to break what a creature was born to become. But the Little Ones *were* frozen, stunted by some invisible force I could not name. Lona, their de facto matriarch, looked no older than fifteen, yet had ruled this horde of childlike beings for longer than any of them could recall, all of whom called her mother, obeyed her without question, and loved her more than the sun itself. They had no concept of time, no memory of their own ages—even Lona swore she could not remember ever beginning to exist. Brimming with unnameable, intuitive wisdom and empty of every scrap of book-learned knowledge, she was both their Love and their Law. And the only fear she ever harbored was that her Little Ones would one day wake up and start to grow, and turn into the cruel, stupid “bad giants” that haunted the surrounding valleys.

Their “good giant” could not leave them to fend for themselves, I told myself, but neither could he help them without more understanding of their history, their nature, the thing that was holding them captive in eternal childhood. My presence was already drawing unwanted attention: I was not their protector, they were mine, and my lingering had only put them at greater risk of discovery by their violent neighbors. Knowledge, I knew, made wicked people far worse, but it could only make gentle people better. I ached to teach them to trace numbers in the dirt, to write down the dainty, wordless melodies they hummed at dusk and forgot by dawn. So I resolved to pack my wits and set off wandering, in search of whatever clue could unravel the riddle of their strange, stunted fortunes.

I might have dawdled for weeks more before acting on that plan, if fate had not intervened. One afternoon, as we all worked picking wild berries, I told them I would have left the bad giants’ lands long ago, if not for how deeply I loved the Little Ones. Before I could finish the sentence, they swarmed me as one: scrambling over each other, leaping from the lower boughs of the great oak we sat beneath, dropping onto my head and shoulders until I was half smothered. Three tiny ones clung to my arms, one perched on each shoulder digging her little fingers into my neck, a third stood straight up on the crown of my head, four or five wrapped their arms tight around my calves, and still more climbed and tumbled over the pile of bodies already clinging to me. I was as helpless as a man buried under a slow avalanche of soft, warm lava.

None of them noticed the brute creeping through the treeline until he was almost within arm’s reach. One sharp cry of *“Take care, good giant!”* ripped through the air, and in an instant they were gone: vanishing under the undergrowth like skittish mice, dropping off me like ripe hedgehogs, scurrying up the oak’s trunk like squirrels. The same second, the bad giant rounded the tree, swung a thick gnarled cudgel, and cracked it against the side of my head so hard the world went black.

I woke at midnight. A handful of pale stars hung low above me, waiting for the moon to rise. My head throbbed so badly I could barely lift it, my tongue swollen and heavy with thirst. When I rolled wearily onto my side, my ear pressed to the loam, and I heard the gurgling, gushing sound of a hidden spring buried just below the grass. Before I could move, the whole horde of Little Ones was gathered around me, silent as ghosts, pressing cool, juicy wild berries to my lips one after another until my thirst ebbed away. Only then did I notice the soft, tiny sobs rippling through the air, a sound I had never heard from their carefree throats before. When I tried to sit up, a heap of small warm bodies piled instantly at my back to prop me upright.

“You must go away, good giant,” Lona whispered, kneeling at my side. “The bad giant who hit you has told all his clan you are a wizard, and that we are your familiars. At dawn they will come to hunt you, and trample you to nothing.”

One of the older boys piped up that he had pressed his ear to the brute’s cottage door, and heard him boast that he had found me idling talking to a horde of moles and squirrels, and that the creatures had tried to kill him after he struck me down. They would all come for me before the sun was high, he said, to put an end to my magic.

“I will go at once,” I told them, as the Little Ones hauled me gently to my feet, their small hands surprisingly strong for their size. “And I will come back as soon as I find what it takes to make you bigger, and stronger, so the bad giants can never hurt you again.”

They looked at me, their little faces grave as polished stone. “We don’t want to be bigger. We won’t grow bad giants. We are strong now—you have no idea how strong.”

There was no use pushing the point. I bade them lead the way, and we trundled slowly up the slope of the valley, the small procession winding along the old riverbed. They fed me slices of sweet root and handfuls of fruit the whole way, chattering that I had spilled far too much of my red juice, and I needed to eat enough to make it back. When we reached the lip of the valley, the moon lifted her silver forehead over the horizon, soft and glowing.

“She has come to watch over you,” Lona said. “She will show you the road.”

I asked them what lay ahead. They told me of a far-off great city ruled by a giant-girl queen, though they could not say exactly where it was, how far it stretched, or what her name was—only that she hated the Little Ones, hunted for them across every valley, and longed to kill them all, if only she could track their hidden glen down. When I said I would go to her and ask why she wished them harm, they shrieked in terror, clinging to the hem of my tunic. “No no no! She is an awful bad-giant witch, she will tear you apart!”

They bade me head past the baby forest, toward the smooth green plain where the moon rose, no rocks, no sharp thorns, soft under every step. They stopped walking when the moon hung high overhead, at a fork in the stream, and told me they had never wandered this far from their oak trees before.

“Mind you memorize the path, deep inside your eyes,” Lona said, “so you can find your way back to us.”

“Beware the Cat-Woman that lives in the desert past the plain,” one of the older girls called over her shoulder as the rest turned to leave. “She is horridly ugly, and she scratches anyone who comes near her.”

The smaller children were already running, gamboling back down the path. Lona was the last to stay, holding her swaddled baby up for me to kiss, gazing deep into my eyes, before she whispered, “The Cat-Woman will not hurt *you*,” and turned to vanish into the dark after the rest.

I stood there a long time in the silver moonlight, watching their faint bobbing lanterns recede. Soon their laughter drifted back to me, like the ring of a thousand tiny sheep bells, rippling through the air and echoing off the rocks. The smaller ones chased each other and tumbled as they went, no care in their sweet souls, while Lona walked slowly apart, cradling her baby against her chest.

As I turned to start my solitary journey, my mind turned over and over their strange, gentle ways. Once I had suggested they leave the valley of the bad giants entirely, and come with me to find a safer home, and they had told me that leaving would mean they stopped being themselves. They had no ambition, no greed, no fear of discomfort, no desire for change of any kind. Their only terror was the unknown that waited if they ever started to grow. Could I really call myself their friend, if I forced my own world’s ideas of improvement on them? Might I not warp their soft, perfect innocence, distort and weaken them, in my eagerness to “help”? The role of self-appointed philanthropist was a dangerous one, I realized. If a man truly wanted to do his neighbor good, he must first learn how to do him no harm at all—and he must pull the great wooden beam out of his own eye, before he ever tried to pluck the tiny splinter out of another’s.

I traveled on with the moon as my constant companion. She hung full, as she always did – I had never once seen her crescent or dark in this strange land – and on that night, as she sank low toward the horizon, I could have sworn I caught a soft, secret smile tugging at the edges of her craters. When her lower rim dipped just below the line of the sands, a cottage bloomed clear in the very center of her glowing disc, painted as if by brushstroke against her silver: door and windows stood wide, and her own light poured straight through them. The certainty hit me like a soft hand on my shoulder: I was expected there.

In the next breath the moon winked out, and the cottage vanished with her. The dark came on fast, and the road ahead snaked through a jagged chain of small, shadow-choked ravines. I decided to make camp right where I stood. I stretched out in a soft sandy hollow, supped on the leftover fruit the little Lovers had pressed on me at parting, and fell asleep almost instantly.

I woke abruptly. New constellations blazed overhead, patterns no Earth astronomer had ever mapped, and I lay staring up at them for long minutes before I noticed the figure seated on the rise of ground a little way above me. The shock of realizing I was not alone pricked at my skin. The silhouette was backlit against the alien sky, looming larger than any human ought to be from my vantage low in the hollow. It turned its head, and I saw its back was fully turned to me.

“Will you not come with me?” The voice was sweet, mellow, unmistakably a woman’s.

“Thank you,” I called back, not wanting to seem rude, “but I’m quite comfortable here. Where do you mean to take me? I’ve always liked sleeping under open sky.”

“There is no harm in the air,” she replied. “But the night creatures that roam these wastes are not the sort a man would choose to have sniffing at his blankets while he sleeps.”

“I haven’t been disturbed yet,” I said.

“No. I’ve sat beside you ever since you laid down.”

That took me aback. “That is very kind of you. How did you find me? Why do me such a favour?”

“I saw you,” she answered, still not turning to show me her face, “in the last sliver of the moon’s light before she set. I see very poorly in the day, but perfectly at night. The shadow of my cottage would have hidden you from view, but both its doors were standing wide. I was out wandering the waste, and watched you slip down into this hollow. I did not want to wake you – people get frightened when I creep up on them unannounced. They call me the Cat-woman. It is not my name.”

I remembered the children’s warnings: that she was hideously ugly, that she scratched strangers who crossed her. But her voice was gentle, almost apologetic, and I could not bring myself to fear her. “I will not call you that,” I said. “Please tell me what I ought to call you.”

“When you know me well enough, give me the name that feels like it fits,” she said. “That will tell me what kind of man you are. People rarely give me the right one. It is a good day when they do.”

“Then I suppose you live in that cottage I saw, glowing right in the heart of the moon?”

“I do. I live there alone, save for the guests that find their way to me. It is a humble little place, but I do what I can for them. Sometimes their sleep there is sweeter than any they have known elsewhere.”

Her voice seeped into my bones, and I felt an unexpected stillness settle over me. I climbed to my feet. “I will come with you, madam.”

She rose at once, and led the way without a single glance over her shoulder. I could just make out her outline well enough to follow. She was taller than me, but not nearly as giant as she had looked at first from the hollow. That she refused to turn to face me sparked my curiosity, not fear – her voice rang too true for me to doubt her. But how could I ever find a name that fit a woman I could not see? I tried to match her stride, to slip beside her, but no matter how fast I stepped, she matched my pace and kept a steady easy lead a few feet ahead. After a while a faint prickle of unease crept in. Why was she so determined not to be seen? Extreme ugliness would explain it – she might fear terrifying me. A sudden, cold horror of some inconceivable monstrosity snaked through my chest: was I trailing some unnameable abomination through the dark? I almost regretted agreeing to follow her. The silence stretched too long, thick and unbearable, so I broke it.

“I am trying to find a place I have heard of, but do not know its name,” I said. “Perhaps you can point me the way?”

“Describe it, and I will guide you. The stupid Bags know nothing of such things, and the careless little Lovers forget almost everything.”

“Where do those two peoples live?”

“You just came from their lands,” she said.

“I never heard those names before.”

“Of course you did not. Neither people knows its own true name.”

“How strange,” I said.

“Perhaps. But hardly anyone anywhere knows their own real name. Many a fine proud gentleman would stare in shock if someone called him by the name that actually belongs to him.”

I fell quiet, wondering what my own true name might be.

“You are thinking of yours right now, aren’t you?” she said, as if she could read my thoughts. “But never mind it. It is of no consequence. Your real name is written on your forehead, but right now it whirls around too fast for anyone to read. I will help it steady. Soon it will slow, and settle, I hope, at last.”

That startled me, and I said no more. We had left the ravines far behind, but there was still no sign of her cottage. “The Little Ones – the Lovers – told me of a smooth green country, soft underfoot,” I said.

“Yes?”

“They spoke too of a girl giantess who is queen there somewhere. Is that her land?”

“There is a city in that grassy realm,” she said, “ruled by a woman they call their princess. The city is named Bulika. But she is no girl. She is older than this world, and came here from yours, with a terrible history that has not yet run its course. She is evil, and holds great favour with the Prince of the Power of the Air. The people of Bulika were once simple folk, tilling the soil and pasturing sheep. They welcomed her when she arrived, and she rewarded their kindness by teaching them to dig for diamonds and opals to sell to strangers. She made them abandon their fields and their flocks, and build that city of stone. One day they found a great serpent and killed it, and that drove her to rage. She declared herself their princess, and became a terror to them. Back then, this whole region was called The Land of Waters. All those dry, empty river beds you have crossed were once full of rushing, living torrents, and the valley where the Lovers’ fruit trees grow was a great lake that fed them. But the wicked princess gathered half the waters of the whole land into her lap, sealed them up inside a hollow egg, and carried it away. The rest of the water fled straight underground the moment she was gone, leaving the country as dry and dusty as her own heart. If not for the hidden waters still flowing beneath the sand, every living thing here would have perished long ago. Where there is no surface water, no rain ever falls, and where no rain falls, no springs rise. Ever since, she has ruled Bulika, holding her people in constant fear, doing all she can to stop them from growing in number. And yet they boast of their prosperity, think themselves so very self-satisfied. They are good at bargaining, good at cheating, they stick close together when their interests align, and will betray each other without a second thought the moment those interests clash. They are proud of their cruel princess, and despise everyone they can outwit. They have not the faintest doubt that they are the most honourable nation alive, and each man among them thinks he is better than every other. No one who has not walked their streets can understand how deep their worthlessness runs, how high their vainglory towers.”

“Thank you for telling me,” I said. “Now tell me of the Little Ones, the Lovers. I want more than anything to help them. They are the greatest wonder I have found in this world of wonders.”

“Perhaps you will find answers to that in Bulika. There is an ancient poem locked in the palace library, no one alive there can read it anymore, but it says clearly that after the Lovers pass through great trials and learn their own true name, they will fill the land, and make the giants their slaves.”

“By that time they will have grown a little, I suppose?” I said.

“They will have grown. And yet, I think they will also not have grown. It is possible to grow and not grow at the same time, to grow smaller and bigger all at once – even to grow by means of not growing at all.”

“Your words are very strange, madam,” I said. “But I have heard it said that some words seem to mean less, because they actually hold far more meaning than we can grasp.”

“That is true. Those kinds of words have to be felt to be understood. It would serve the princess of Bulika well if she could hear what the very silence of this land is shouting in her ears all day long. But she is far too clever to ever understand anything that matters.”

“Then when the little Lovers are grown, the water will come back to the land?”

“Not exactly. When they are thirsty enough, they will find the water. And when they have water, they will begin to grow. To grow, they must have water. And deep beneath the sand, it is still flowing.”

“I heard that water twice before,” I said. “Once when I laid down to wait for the moon, and woke to sunlight, once when that cruel bad giant almost killed me. Both times the water’s voices came, and healed me.”

She never turned her head, never broke her steady pace a few feet ahead, but every word she spoke was clear as a bell, and her voice reminded me sharply of the soft healer woman I had met in the house of death. Much of what she said I could not parse, and so cannot now recall, but I had long since forgotten every last flicker of fear I had felt for her. We crossed a wide stretch of soft dune sand before the cottage finally came into view. It sat half sunk in the dunes, its foundations hewn out of solid rock. Its walls were thick, thicker than the old sexton’s cottage I had visited earlier, and its door stood open to us. We stepped straight into a large bare room, with two small, unglazed windows set opposite each other.

My hostess walked straight to the far corner, lifted a long white linen cloth from the floor, and wound it tight around her head and face, covering all her features. Then she shut the second door, trimmed a small horn lantern sitting on the hearth, and turned to face me.

“You are very welcome, Mr. Vane,” she said, calling me by the name I had realized I could not remember just a little while before. “Your entertainment will be scanty. The night is almost spent, but dawn is not yet here, and it is far safer for you to be inside these walls. A little lack of comfort is no great harm.”

“I thank you with all my heart, Mara,” I said, and the name came to my lips unbidden. At once I remembered the old sexton, and the little black cat he had kept by his hearth.

“Some people take me for Lot’s wife, still turned to salt and lamenting over Sodom,” she said. “Some think I am Rachel, weeping for her lost children. But I am neither of those.”

“Mara, may I lie here on your floor until morning?” I asked.

“Up that narrow stair you will find a bed,” she said. “Some who have slept there woke feeling lighter than they ever had, some lay awake all night and slept straight through the next full day. It is not soft, but it is far better than the sand – and there are no hyenas sniffing around its legs.”

The stair was steep and tight, leading straight up to an unceiled garret with one wide, low dormer window. A narrow bed stood under the sloped roof, its white linen coverlet sending a sharp shiver down my spine, so vividly it recalled the couches in that strange chamber of death. On the small table beside it sat a dry loaf and a cup of cold clear water. After months of eating nothing but wild fruit, it looked like a feast.

“I will leave you in the dark,” Mara called up from the bottom of the stair. “This lantern is the only light I have, and there are things I must tend to tonight.”

“It is no trouble at all, thank you,” I called back. “Eating, drinking, lying down and sleeping are all things one can do perfectly well in the dark.”

“Rest in peace,” she said.

I ate the whole loaf, drank every drop of the water, and laid down. The bed was hard, the covers thin, the night cold, and I dreamed I was back in the chamber of death, lying between the great fallen warrior and the lady with the healing wound. I woke deep in the night, hearing faint distant wild animal noises on the wind. “Desert creatures sniffing for me,” I told myself, and was about to roll over and fall back asleep when a rough, deep purr rose outside my window, climbing up into a full, thundering howl. I sprang from the bed to see what kind of beast made that sound.

Outside the cottage door, bathed in the full silver glow of the moon, stood Mara, tall and clad all in white, her back turned to me. She was kneeling, stroking a huge white panther-like creature that purred under her hands, one hand patting its head, the other pointing straight up to the moon hanging half-way up the sky, then drawing a straight line down to the distant horizon. The creature bolted off at impossible speed, straight in the direction she had pointed. I followed it with my eyes for a moment, then looked back for Mara – but she was gone. I had still never seen her face. When I looked for the great cat, I could not tell if the faint white speck far off on the sand was real, or just a trick of the moonlight. What did it mean? What mission had she sent that monstrous cat out to complete? I shuddered, and climbed back into bed. Then a thought hit me: the moon had been setting when I laid down in the sandy hollow, and now a few hours later it was halfway up the sky, full and bright. Everything in this world was unknowable, even the paths of the stars. I learned later this world had a whole host of moons, each bound to different rules, and no mortal could ever map their orbits.

I fell asleep again, and did not wake until full dawn. Downstairs, Mara had left more bread and water waiting for me. The loaf was so large I could only eat half of it. She sat across from me, still fully muffled in her white cloth, and did not speak until I asked her the way to Bulika. She told me to follow the high bank of the dry river bed until it vanished, then veer right until I reached a thick forest. I could spend one night there, but I had to leave the woods facing the rising moon. Keep that same path, she said, until I found a running stream of living water, cross it at a right angle, and walk straight on until the spires of Bulika rose over the horizon.

I thanked her, and mentioned that I had looked out my window in the night, and been astonished to see her great cat messenger understand her signs so perfectly, race off so fast. “If I had a creature like that to guide me,” I said, fishing for what she might tell me of its mission, but she cut me off.

“It is heading straight for Bulika. The shortest possible way.”

“It looked so intelligent.”

“Astarte knows her work well enough to be trusted with it,” she said.

“Do you have many messengers like her?”

“As many as I need.”

“Are they difficult to teach?”

“They need no teaching. They are all of one strange breed, but no two of them are ever the same. Their origin is so natural, you would think it impossible if I told you.”

“Will you tell me how they come to be?”

“A new one was born just last night,” she said. “Out of your own wandering thoughts, while you slept in the sand.”

I laughed. “Everyone in this world seems to love their mysteries,” I thought to myself. “Some stray thought of mine must have caught root, and she gave it that big cat form.”

“Then that creature is mine?” I said.

“Not at all,” she replied. “Nothing is truly yours unless your will helped make it exist.”

I smiled to myself, surprised. A metaphysician too, I thought. “May I take the rest of the loaf with me?” I asked.

“You will not need more food today,” she said.

“But I might need it tomorrow!”

She rose and walked to the door. “It has nothing to do with tomorrow – but take it if you wish.” She held the door open for me. I picked up the leftover bread, but lingered, burning with the desperate wish to see her face. “Must I go then?” I asked.

“No one sleeps in my house two nights in a row,” she said, her voice soft and muffled through the cloth.

“Thank you for your hospitality, farewell,” I said, turning to step across the threshold.

“The time will come when you will have to house with me for many days and many nights,” she murmured sadly.

“I will come willingly,” I said.

“No,” she said. “You will not come willingly.”

I thought to myself that she was right – I never would have chosen to return here a second time of my own free will. But then my heart rebuked me, and before I had fully crossed the line out of the cottage, I turned back.

She was standing in the middle of the room, the loose white linen of her outer clothes pooling at her feet like foaming ocean waves, and the cloth that had wrapped her face lay discarded among them. Her face was as beautiful as a night of unbroken starlight. Her great gray eyes were lifted up to the sky

I pushed to my feet to resume my journey, tramping mile after mile across the sun-scorched, barren plain. My chest ached for a mountain, even a tall jagged rock, whose peak might let me peer past the dismal flatlands and choked river channels to catch a glimmer of hope on the far edge of the world. But what good would even that foresight have done me? What dwells inside a person, not what lies stretched beyond their line of sight, is the true architect of what befalls them: the world’s work upon your soul is the event, not the sights you see. To foresee is not to understand, else the latent prophecy all men carry in their bones would surface far more often than it does.

The sun hung halfway down the western sky when I spotted a rugged rocky slope ahead, but by the time I neared its base that urge to climb had drained out of me, replaced by a overwhelming, bone-deep longing to lie down. The sun was nearly set by then, the air thickening with dusk, and at my feet lay a carpet of moss so soft and vividly green it might have been stitched for a king’s couch. I threw myself down, and weariness began to ebb at once: the moment my head touched the moss, I heard the third time the low rush of many hidden waters below me, weaving broken, ethereal harmonies as they tumbled over the stones of their long-buried channels. It was the loveliest chaos of raw musical material, that subterranean harp sending up gurgles and bell-like drips that tangled into overlapping, clashing melodies held together by a single unshakable refrain. What a masterpiece Händel might have spun from that endless, haunting sound!

As I lay listening, my eyes wandered up the steep rocky face above me, reading the carved mark left by a thousand-years-dead cataract that had once roared down this same slope, feeding the river channels I had followed all the way to its foot. My chest swelled at the thought of that splendid, roaring tumult, waves dancing in wild revelry as they fell, massing their thunder into one great organ roar in the pool below. But the hidden brooks soon lulled me to sleep, their lullabies winding through my dreams.

I woke before the sun cleared the horizon, and scrambled up the rock face at once to see what lay on the other side. To my dismay, there was nothing but a desert of the finest, powdery sand. Not a trace remained of the river that had once plunged down those cliffs: the fine drift had filled its old course level with the rest of the dreary expanse. Glancing back, I could now make out the two branches the old river had split into at the falls: one whose bank I had followed to the foot of this scaur, and the other I had crossed long before to reach the Evil Wood. I could see that wood faint and far off between the two old river beds on the horizon. Before me and to my left, the desert stretched past the edge of my sight, but far to the right the skyline lifted, the rise of land promising the forest my strange hostess had directed me to find.

I sat down to dig in my pocket for the half-loaf of bread I had carried away with me, and only then understood what the hostess had meant when she spoke of it not keeping. The bread had shrunk and hardened to a solid, heavy stone. I tossed it aside, and set out again.

Around noon I reached a scattering of tamarisk and juniper trees, then a handful of stunted firs. Thickets grew denser, firs grew taller, until I found myself in a full pine forest identical to the one where the Little Ones tended their found babies, and I realized I had wandered back to a far stretch of the same woods. But what did place matter, when everywhere felt the same as nowhere? I had not yet done anything in this world to turn any spot into a home, into somewhere that mattered. I was not truly alive yet, only dreaming that I lived, nothing more than a disembodied consciousness with a view of the world. I had been no more real than that in the old world I had left behind, but now I could see the truth of it plain as day. I swore to myself that if I caught even the faintest glint of that enchanted mirror that would send me back to my old life, I would turn miles out of my way to avoid it. Here I might finally learn to be something real, by doing something real. I could not bear the thought of waking up back in my old bed, knowing all my wanderings had amounted to nothing, a dozen half-starts with not a single end to show for them. The Little Ones would face whatever fate was waiting for them, I would never meet that terrible witch, the dead would ripen and rise without me. I would wander forever rather than end up with that empty, hollow wake-up.

I went deeper into the wood, weary, eager to rest somewhere among the trees. The trunks grew massive, spaced in almost perfect geometric order with wide, clear gaps between them. There was barely any undergrowth, and I could see long stretches in every direction. The forest felt like a vast, empty cathedral, solemn, silent, entirely uninhabited. I met no creature on two legs or four all that day, though every so often something swift and something slow would dart across the far space my eyes were fixed on, distant enough that they only amplified the strange sense of vast, empty quiet. I heard a few hidden birds, and saw dozens of butterflies: some painted with impossible, blazing hues, others a pure, dazzling white that seemed to glow in the dappled shade.

I came to a stretch where the pines thinned out, making room for flowering shrubs, and my heart lifted at the sight, hopeful that some dwelling was nearby. I followed the direction where roses grew thicker and thicker, starved for the sight and voice of another living soul, human or otherwise, any being I could even partially understand. What a terrible hell, I thought, to wander forever alone, trapped inside your own existence that never reaches outward, never widens itself by sharing life with another, bound tight by the thin cords of your own petty quirks, an eternal prisoner in the dungeon of your own skull. I was learning fast that it is impossible to even live for yourself alone, unless you are in the company of others — though sadly, in that case, living for yourself becomes far too easy. Evil is only ever a parasite on the tree of goodness, selfishness a leech that can only feed on shared life. Back in my old world, I had the habit of singing to myself when I was alone. Here, not a single crooning murmur ever crossed my lips.
