There I had never bothered to find a close friend; here, the clumsy affection of a half-wit would have felt like the greatest divine gift. “If only I had a dog to love,” I sighed, and stared back in wonder at my old self, who had preferred the company of a book or pen to a real man or woman, who would have chased away the author of a story he was enjoying just so he could get back to the words on the page. I had chosen the dead made of ink, not the living, the thought not the thinker. “Any living person,” I said to myself, “is worth more than the greatest book ever written.” I had neglected my living brothers and sisters back home, and now I was left with nothing but endless empty woods.

The pines thinned further, their trunks growing even larger, soaring like stone columns straining to hold up the sky. More varied trees appeared, the forest growing lusher, until the roses themselves grew into full trees, their blooms blazing with impossible splendour. Suddenly I spotted what looked like a huge castle, but its edges were so indistinct I could not be sure it was anything more than a random arrangement of tree trunks. As I drew nearer, the shape held, but its lines never sharpened, never grew more defined. When I stood right before it, I still could not tell what it was. It was no habitable house or castle, certainly, but it might be a ruin overgrown entirely with ivy and roses — though I could not spot a single fragment of stone wall anywhere through the foliage. Again and again I thought I glimpsed masonry, only for it to dissolve before my eyes. Could the ivy have wrapped itself around a giant edifice and slowly consumed it, its tangled branches retaining the shape of the walls it had devoured? I could make no certain sense of it.

Right before me was a rectangular empty gap, the ghost of a doorway with no door. I stepped through, and found myself in an open space like a giant great hall, its floor carpeted with grass and wild flowers, its walls and ceiling woven entirely of ivy, vine, and climbing roses. There could be no better place to spend the night. I gathered a pile of dry withered leaves, tucked them in a corner, and threw myself down on the makeshift couch. A red sunset flooded the hall, the night was warm, and I lay staring up at the living ceiling, tangled with twigs and foliage, peepholes to the sky far above. My eyes wandered through the web of branches until the sun set, and the dark crept over the world. The red roses turned black, until only the yellow and white blooms were visible, and when they too faded the stars hung through the leaves like living topazes, throbbing and sparkling with a dozen different colours. I was lying under the boughs of a tree straight out of Aladdin’s cave. Then I noticed the canopy was full of little nests, tiny heads peeking out of them every so often to chirp softly before vanishing again. For a little while there was rustling, quiet stirring, the soft little noises of baby birds saying their small bedtime prayers, until the dark deepened, every mother bird tucked her brood tight under her wings, and the whole bird nursery went still, drifting into sleep. A few more soft flutters made me look up: an owl sailed silently across the hall. I only caught a glimpse of it, but I felt the cool soft gust of its wings brushing the air more than once. The mother birds did not stir — they knew it hunted mice, not chicks.

Around midnight I woke completely, roused by a soft, muffled revelry. The noise was not loud, and it was not far off, it was right there, attenuate, thin as mist. My eyes were dazzled at first, and I could see nothing, until slowly they cleared. I was still lying on my pile of dry leaves, in a splendid hall of light. Before me, a crowd of gorgeously dressed men and women in soft, flowing robes danced, none of them able to see me. They wove their dances one after another, acting out the old story of all life: first meetings, burning passions, bitter partings. As a student of Shakespeare, I knew every traditional dance referenced in his plays, and I recognized several immediately: the slow, stately minuet, the solemn pavan, the skipping country hey, the quick, swirling coranto, the leaping lavolta. Their fine clothes were as ancient as the dances themselves. The moon had risen while I slept, shining down through the leafy roof, its light crisscrossed with so many shadows that at first I could barely make out their faces. But there was something very strange about them, and I sat up to get a better look.

Heaven help me, those were no faces. They were bare skulls, gleaming hard bone, jaws stripped of flesh, truncated noses, lipless bared teeth that would never smile again. Some of those teeth flashed white and sharp and murderous, others were fogged with the dark of decay, broken and gapped, stained the colour of the earth they had rotted in for centuries. Worse still, their empty eye sockets were not empty. Every single one held a living, lidless, glowing eye. In those ruined faces, eyes blazed, sparkled, glowed in every colour, every emotion: the proud dark lustrous eye, condescending to everything it rested on, was the most terrifying of all. The soft, languishing, amorous eye was the most repulsive. The dim, sad eyes, the ones that fit most naturally to the bony sockets, were so unbearably mournful they pulled at my heart no matter how much horror I felt at the sight.

I stood up and walked straight among the apparitions, desperate to understand what they were. Were they souls, or were their rhythmic dances nothing more than phantoms of long-dead people? Not a single one of them noticed me, did not break step for an instant. I was not real to them. But how real were they to each other? Surely they saw their companions just as I saw them, each a walking death with living eyes. Did they know how they looked to each other? Had they spent their whole lives using their faces as masks, hiding their true selves behind false expressions to seem what they were not, and so been stripped of their soft flesh forever, condemned to wander faceless in their bone until they repented? How long, I wondered, would they flaunt that facelessness, their eyes trapped in bone? How long would their terrible punishment last? Had they begun to love, to grow wise? Had they yet given in to the shame that had hunted them down? I heard no sound come from their bare mouths, no word spoken. Had they been stripped of speech because they spent their whole lives lying? Their eyes spoke, longing to be understood, but what was it they spoke — truth, or more lies? They seemed to know each other, surely they must have long grown accustomed to judging which skull was beautiful, which plain. When a dancer drifted close to me, they passed straight through my body like I was no more than mist, and I could tell, even without looking, whether it was a man or a woman that had walked through me. Many of the skulls still held their hair, no matter how fine or luxuriant it was, it looked horrid resting bare on the bone of the forehead and temple. Often the outer ear still clung to the bone, a pearl, opal, or diamond glimmering at its tip, nestled under dark brown locks, raven tresses, or pale gold curls, looking obscene and alien against the damp yellow or ivory white naked bone. I glanced down and saw their daintily arched insteps peeking out under their gowns, then glanced up and saw their soft plump shoulders, that narrowed halfway up the neck to the ridged bone of a bare skull.

The music grew wilder, the dance faster and faster. Eyes flared, jewels glittered, casting streaks of colour and fire over the pale grinning skulls that wove through the hall in a tangled, intricate maze of motion, until the dance stopped all at once, every eye turning to the same spot.

In the vine doorway stood a woman, perfect in form, perfect in poise, her skin glowing with warm, living colour, staring down at the company like a goddess on her pedestal. The dancers all froze like they had been cursed, locked into a new death by the sight of that living warmth. Dead things, her scornful glance said, I live.

Then, as if caught all at once by a gust of wind, they turned to their partners and burst back into dance, a new look in their eyes, once lonely, now lit with shared triumph. You too, they seemed to say, will soon be as weak as us. You will soon become exactly like us.

I turned back to the woman, and saw a small dark shadow spreading across her side. She had seen the shift in the dancers’ eyes, she looked down, understood exactly what their glowing eyes were saying. She pressed both her lovely hands to the dark shadow, let out a smothered, terrified cry, and fled.

The birds rustled in their nests, and a flash of pure joy lit every eye in the dancing skulls, when a warm wind roared through the hall, growing stronger as it swept across them, snuffing out every spark of warmth that clung to the apparitions. The low moon still glimmered faint and sickly on the horizon, and the dim, turbid glow of all their eyes gave me more than enough light to see what happened next. Every shape crumbled like a snowman caught in the sun. Flesh peeled off the bone in thin papery flakes, dropping like dirty snow from under their gowns and doublets. The clothes fell to the floor in rags and strips, and the bare, lank white skeletons stepped out of the decaying flesh and fabric, standing alone amid the litter on the flower floor. A faint rattling shiver ran through the whole company, and one by one the glowing eyes in their sockets went dark. The darkness closed around me, thick with the weight of loneliness. For a moment the dry leaves swept rustling all one way, then the wind died, and the owl glided silent through the quiet night.

I had not felt fear, not for a single second. It was true that anyone who crossed into this strange other world had to leave their fear behind, but I could not take any credit for my courage. No deliberate bravery was at work in me — I simply was not afraid. I did not know why I had no cause to fear, nor why I should have been afraid. I was not even afraid of fear, the most dangerous of all dangers. I stepped out of the vine hall back into the wood, ready to resume my journey. A new moon was rising over the treetops, and I turned my face to its light.

I had not gone ten paces through the moss-choked, half-shadowed wood when a lopsided, half-buried shape caught my eye, and I drifted closer to make sense of it. It was a mouldering carriage of forgotten vintage, its gilded flaking, its frame warped with rot but still held upright on pitted, heavy wooden wheels. Flanking the rotting pole, still harnessed in rusted traces, lay the skeletons of two horses; from their grinning, lichen-dusted white skulls, shrivelled leather reins stretched taut up to the bony hand of a skeleton coachman perched stiff on his tattered, frayed hammer-cloth. Both carriage doors had fallen away to splinters long ago, and within the empty frame, two skeletons slouched back in their corners as if frozen mid-sulking journey.

Even as I stared, they stirred awake. There was a sharp, rattling crack of grinding bone, and each launched itself out the empty doorway nearest their perch. One slipped at once and crumpled in a heap of dead leaves. The other wobbled for a long, perilous moment, its entire frame shaking like a stack of dry twigs, then crept with stiff, grinding joints, one hand clamped to the back of the carriage for purchase, over to its fallen companion. Its thin leg bones seemed far too thin to bear its weight, and it nearly toppled twice as it knelt, bony hands fumbling to haul the other upright.

At last the prostrate skeleton heaved itself up to a sitting posture by its own effort, yellowed skull turning side to side as if testing its neck joints. Ignoring the other for a moment, it dragged itself to its feet by clinging to the spokes of the rear carriage wheel, half-hunched, both hands clamped around one loose, wobbly knee joint. The other, still kneeling, contorted its way upright a moment later, and turned its empty eye sockets on its companion.
“Have you hurt yourself, my lord?” Its voice was thin, distant, whistling through the hollows of its ribcage as if blown off course by some spectral wind.
“Yes, I have,” the other rasped back, rougher, his teeth clicking when he snarled. “You did nothing to help me, and this cursed knee is popped clean out of its socket!”
“I did my best, my lord.”
“No doubt, my lady, for it was terrible! I thought I’d never get my feet under me again—But bless my soul, madam! Are you out in your bones?”
She glanced down at her own ribcage, the movement of her skull making a small clinking rattle. “I have nothing else to be out in,” she returned dryly. “And you at least cannot complain! What in all the world does this mean? Am I dreaming?”
“You may be, madam—I won’t argue that—but this throbbing knee of mine forbids me that grateful illusion. Ha! I see now I’ve got nothing to walk on but bones too! Not so unbecoming for a man, I suppose. Though I do hope to goodness these aren’t *my* bones—every single one aches worse than the last, and this loose knee worst of all! The bed must have been damp, and I far too drunk to notice it when I collapsed.”
“Probably, my lord of Cokayne!”
“What? What? You make me wonder if I *am* dreaming, aches and all! How do you know the taunting title my roistering drinking buddies gave me? I don’t remember you! Anyhow, you’ve no right to take such liberties. My name is—am I lord—tut, tut. What do you call me when I’m—I mean, when you’re sober? I can’t think of it right this moment. Why, what *is* my name? I must have been very drunk when I went to bed last! I often am.”
“You came to my bedchambers so rarely, I scarcely know it myself, my lord—but I’ll take your word for that much.”
“I should hope so!”
“—If for nothing else, that is.”
“Hoity toity! I never told you a lie in my life!”
“You never told me anything *but* lies.”
“Upon my honour! Why, I’ve never seen this woman before in my life!”
“You knew me well enough to lie to, my lord.”
“I do seem to half-dream I’ve met you somewhere before, but on my oath, there’s nothing here to tell who you are! Stripped of your fine clothes, who could say who you might not be? One thing I can swear for certain—I never saw you this undressed before! By heaven, I have no recollection of you at all.”
“I’m glad to hear it. My few memories of you are all the less distasteful for that. Good morning, my lord.”
She turned away, hobbling across the leaf litter with a steady clack of bone on stone, then paused a few paces off.
“You are just as heartless as—as—any other woman, madam! Where in this hellish place am I supposed to find my valet? What was the cursed fool’s name I always called him?” He twisted his bare skull side to side on its creaking neck vertebra, still clutching his wobbly knee with both hands.
“I’ll be your valet for once, my lord,” the lady said, turning back to him. “What is it you need? It’s not easy to guess.”
“Tie my leg on properly, of course, you fool! Can’t you see it’s all but falling off? Heigho, my dancing days are done!”
Her empty sockets scanned the underbrush until she found a long, tough strand of fibrous grass, and she knelt to bind the displaced knee joint back into place. When she finished, he tested his weight with two careful, tentative stamps.
“You used to stamp your feet far harder than that, my lord,” she said as she stood.
“Eh? What? Now I look at you again, it seems to me I used to *hate* you—eh?”
“Naturally, my lord. You hated a great many people. Your wife, of course, was chief among them.”
“Ah, I begin, I be-gin to recall. But I must have been somewhere a very long time. I really can’t remember all of it. There! Your damned miserable bit of grass is already stretching thin! We used to get on pretty well together, didn’t we?”
“Not that I recall, my lord. The only happy moments I ever had in your company were scattered across the first week of our marriage.”
“Was that how it was? Ha! Ha! Well, it’s over now, thank goodness!”
“I wish I could believe it! Why were we both sitting in that carriage together? It wakes the worst of fears.”
“I thought we got divorced, my lady!”
“Hardly enough of one. We are still trapped together.”
“A sad truth, but easy enough to fix. This forest looks large enough to lose each other in.”
“I doubt it. I very much doubt it.”
“I’m sorry I can’t think of a compliment to pay you without lying. Judging by your frame and the yellow of your bones, you’ve lived very hard since I last saw you. I can’t surely be quite as naked as your ladyship—beg your pardon, madam! I do hope you’ll take it as a jest in a dream. It doesn’t matter anyhow. Dreaming or waking, it’s all the same, all just mere appearance. You can’t be certain of anything, and that’s as good as knowing nothing at all. Any fool learns that much from life.”
“It taught me what a fool I was to ever love you.”
“You weren’t the only fool to do that! Women used to have a trick of falling in love with me. I’d forgotten you were one of them.”
“I did love you, my lord, a little, once.”
“Ah! That was your mistake, my lady. You should have loved me much, loved me devotedly, loved me savagely, loved me eternally. Then I would have tired of you far sooner, and not hated you so bitterly afterward. But let bygones be bygones! Where *are* we? Locality is the only real question. To be or not to be is not the question at all!”
“We are in the afterlife, I presume.”
“Granted. But which sort, what kind of afterlife? This can’t be hell.”
“It must be hell. There’s still marriage in it. You and I are damned to each other.”
“Then I’m not like Othello, damned to a fair wife! Oh, I still remember my Shakespeare, madam!”
She picked up a sharp, broken fallen branch half-buried in a bush, steadied herself on it, and began to walk away, her skull tossing with every step.
“Give me that stick!” he cried after her. “I want it far more than you do.”
She did not answer.
“You mean to make me beg for it?”
“Not at all, my lord. I mean to keep it,” she replied, picking up her pace, the clack of her joints growing sharper.
He lurched to follow her, but nearly toppled at once: the grass around his knee had snapped. He swore, the sound thin and piping through his empty jaw, and clutched his loose leg again. “Come and tie this up properly right now!”
She turned, her empty sockets fixed on him for a long quiet moment, then took a few steps further away.
“I swear I will not harm you if you come back!” he shouted.
“Swear on, my lord. There’s no one here to believe you. But pray do not lose your temper, or you will shake yourself all to pieces, and I do not know where you will find enough string to tie all your crazy joints back together again.”
She walked back, knelt down at his side—but not before she set the disputed branch out of his reach, but close enough for her to grab. The second she finished retying his knee, he lashed out to grab for her hair, only for his bony fingers to slip uselessly over her smooth, bare skull.
“Disgusting,” he muttered, then clamped his hand hard around her upper arm bone.
“You will break it!” she said, looking up from her knees.
“Then I will break it,” he snarled, and began to twist.
“I will not tie your leg back together the next time it comes loose,” she threatened.
He wrenched at her arm harder, but her bones were in far better condition than his rotting ones. She stretched her other hand out for the branch.
“That’s right, reach me the stick,” he grinned, teeth clicking.
She swung it around in a wide, hard arc, and the bone of his good leg snapped clean through. He fell in a heap, choking on curses that only came out as thin whistling puffs of air. The lady laughed, a dry rattling sound like pebbles shifting in a bag.
“Now you will have to wear splints forever!” she said. “Such old dry bones never knit back together.”
“You devil!” he cried.
“At your service, my lord. Shall I fetch you two wheel spokes for splints? Neat, but heavy, I fear.”
He turned his bone-white face away, did not answer, and lay groaning in the leaves. I marveled he had not shattered entirely when he fell. The lady stood, adjusted her grip on the branch, and walked away—not entirely ungracefully, I thought.
“What can come of this?” I murmured to myself. “These two are far too wretched for any world I could imagine. But this can’t be hell. The Little Ones are here, and the gentle sleepers I met earlier, too. What can this all mean? Can things ever come right for a pair of skeletons?”
“There are words far too big for you and me. *All* is one of them, and *Ever* is another,” said a familiar voice somewhere close by. I looked all around, but could not see the speaker.
“You are not in hell,” it went on. “Neither am I in hell. But those two skeletons *are* in hell.”
Even as the voice finished, I spotted the raven perched on the bough of a beech tree right over my head, feathers black and glossy in the faint filtered sun. The next instant it launched itself from the branch, landed softly on the moss, and shifted at once into that thin old man I had seen in the library, long nose, trailing black coat and all.
“That man was never a gentleman,” he went on, nodding at the two figures in the distance. “Now, in this bony stage of their retrogression, all their pretense of skin and fine manners has fallen away, and their true characters are laid bare for all to see. The woman is less vulgar, and still has a little heart left beating somewhere in that empty ribcage of hers. Now that all the restraints of society are gone, you see them exactly as they are, exactly as they always were.”
“Tell me, Mr. Raven, what will become of them?” I asked.
“We shall see,” he replied. “In their day, they were the handsomest couple at the royal court. Now, even in their dry bones, they still carry the ghost of that old fame as if it were some inalienable possession. Back when they had flesh and fine clothes, they thought themselves rich beyond measure, but now they are already beginning to feel pinched for even a scrap of grass to tie a joint. My lord used to think my lady a useless encumbrance, when he had tired of her beauty and spent every penny of her fortune. Now he needs her to cobble his broken joints back together for him. All these small shifts have roots of hope in them. Besides, they cannot wander far from each other now, and they will meet no other souls like themselves for a very long time. At last they must grow weary of their mutual loathing, and begin to love one another—for love, not hate, is the deepest thing in everything that Love once loved into being.”
“I saw many more souls like them in the great hall an hour ago,” I said.
“Of their kind, but not of their sort,” he answered. “These two are centuries behind the souls you saw last night. The ones you saw are far further along. They can even dress themselves in faint, soft fabric for parts of the night now. By and by they will grow faces, and flesh, and lives of their own. Every grain of truthfulness in them adds a new fibre to their humanity. Nothing but the truth can appear here. And whatever is, must show itself.”
“Are they upheld by this hope?” I asked.
“They are upheld by hope, but they have no idea what that hope is. To understand it is still immeasurably far beyond them,” answered Mr. Raven. His sudden shift from bird to man did not surprise me. I had become like a small child, full of endless wonder at everything, but no longer shocked by anything.
“Did you come here to find me, sir?” I asked.
“Not at all,” he replied. “I have no anxiety about you. Souls like you always find their way back to us.”
“Tell me, please, what am I such as?” I said.
“I cannot make my friend the subject of idle conversation,” he said, with a faint smile.
“But the friend is right here, present with you!” I urged.
“Then I decline even more strongly,” he rejoined.
“But the friend is asking you directly!” I persisted.
“Then most positively I refuse,” he returned.
“Why?”
“Because he and I would be talking of two very different people as if they were one and the same. Your own idea of who you are, and my knowledge of who you really are, are worlds apart.”
The lapels of his coat flared out wide, the long tails lifting, and I thought I was about to watch the full metamorphosis of man to raven unfold before my eyes. But the coat fell closed again around him, and he added, with strange seeming inconsequence: “In this world, never trust a person who has once deceived you. Above all, never do a single thing that such a person asks you to do.”
“I will try to remember,” I answered. “Though I might forget.”
“Then some evil that is good for you will follow, all the same.”
“And if I do remember?”
“Some evil that would not have been good for you will not touch you at all.”
The old man seemed to sink a little into the moss, and the very next instant I saw the raven several yards away, flying low and fast over the underbrush, vanishing into the green dark of the trees.

I walked onward, face turned to the moon, who hung low and unlovely over the treeline, not yet risen high enough to escape the forest’s shadow. She looked dented, scuffed, cast from an old battered disc of copper, dispirited and worn thin, no clouds hovering to keep her company, the surrounding stars burning far too bright to let her shine bright. *Is this never going to end?* her slanted, dim glow seemed to ask. We traveled opposite paths across the sky and through the undergrowth, yet we walked side by side for miles through the trees, her casting that dull, faded silver light, me moving as the live shadow stretched long before her. I barely looked up at her, my eyes fixed on the ground, but I could feel her disconsolate gaze fixed to my back all the while.

A flash of stark white under a sprawling old beech snagged my attention, and I turned toward it. Vague in the leaf-filtered moonlight, it resolved into the shape of a human body as I drew near. “Another skeleton,” I muttered to myself, kneeling to rest a hand on it. It was no skeleton, though—nothing could have been closer to bone without having already turned to dust. It lay curled on its side, cold not the hard, unyielding cold of stone, but the quiet, hollow cold of something that had very recently stopped being alive. The closer I stared, the more I ran my fingers over its frame, the more certain I was that it had to be dead. For one bewildered, silly moment I thought it was one of the wild, whirling nocturnal dancers, some ghostly Cinderella that had lost its way back to its realm and perished in the strange, unforgiving dark of this untamed world.

The body was fully naked, wasted so thin that even in the dappled shadow I could count every rib without touching them. Every bone beneath its skin was outlined sharp and clear, as if it was stretched tight over a frame nothing more substantial than thin elastic leather. Its lips had retracted back to show a full set of bright, unmarred teeth that glinted ghastly through the dim. Its hair was longer than its entire frame, thick, fine to the touch, black as the deepest void. It was the body of a tall woman that must have once moved with effortless grace. How had she ended up here? She could not have wandered this far on her own while already wasting away to nothing. She must have collapsed mid-step, fallen where she stood, and starved where she lay. But how had she grown this gaunt without any sign of decay, any mark of scavengers, any of her clothing torn away? No wild beast would have left her body unmolested, yet there was not a single scratch on her.

I stood up, staring down at her, certain I could not leave her exposed like this, forsaken out in the woods. Reverence would not let me. Even a woman’s clothing deserves respect; I could never leave her uncovered, for any wandering brute or unkind eye to stumble across. It would take years for rain and soil to pull her down into the earth. But the ground beneath me was laced so thick with tangled tree roots it was almost solid, and I had nothing but my bare hands to dig with. At first I thought she could not have been dead for more than a few hours—there was not a single hint of rot or decay anywhere on her. But then, what was even left of her to decay? What if she was not dead at all? The rules of this world were strange, unrecognizable, anything was possible. Even if there was barely a sliver of a chance I could bring her back, I could not bury her before I was absolutely certain she was gone.

I remembered I had stuffed a bunch of ripe wild grapes I’d found by the forest hall doorway in my pocket earlier, a handful still clinging to the stem. Their sweet, juicy pulp might be the only thing that could rouse her, if there was any life left to rouse. Her mouth hung slightly open, and to shift her to a better position I slid my arm under her shoulder—and found the pine needles beneath her still warm. She could not have been dead for long. She *might* still be alive, even if I could not feel a heartbeat, even if I could not see the faintest flicker of breath against my palm. One of her hands was clenched tight, curled around something small I could not make out. I squeezed a drop of grape juice onto her tongue, but there was no swallow, no reaction at all.

I spread a thick layer of dry pine needles and crisp dead leaves across the ground, laid my own outer tunic—still warm from my body—over top of it, lifted her as gently as I could onto the makeshift pallet, then piled my extra clothes and armful after armful of leaves over her to trap what little residual heat she had left, hoping that once the sun rose it would build back up in her. I squeezed another drop of grape juice into her mouth, still no movement. “Doubt’s a terrible thing to build your hopes on,” I told myself, “but it’s an even worse reason to do nothing at all.” Her skin was stretched so tight over her bones I dared not rub her to stimulate circulation, for fear of tearing it. I crawled into the heap of leaves beside her, tucked myself against her, and wrapped my arms around her. I did not have much extra heat left to share, but every bit of it I would give to her to try to bring her back. I spent the rest of the night sleepless, waiting for the sun, her cold seeping into my bones, none of my heat seeming to sink into her at all. I had run away from the soft, beautiful sleepers I’d found laid out on their dim silver couches, just to end up here, holding a possible corpse through the dark. I had turned down a gentle, lovely rest for this awful, sacred duty. Beneath that sad, dimming copper moon I lay with the almost-dead, watching for the dawn.

The dark lightened, the eastern horizon bleeding pale grey, when I spotted it: movement, low to the ground, not far from our hiding spot. A huge, undulating snake slid past me in a straight unswerving line, heading toward something I could not yet see. Not long after that, a roe doe and her calf appeared, moving the same direction, then two bear cubs trailed by three or four tiny scurrying creatures no bigger than squirrels. The light was growing fast, and when a troop of tiny horses trotted past minutes later I could see they were all fully grown, no bigger than the smallest Shetland pony, perfectly formed: some built like miniature cart horses, others like tiny war chargers, racehorses, hunters. Dwarf cattle and palm-sized elephants followed after them, all on the same path. “Where are the Little Ones?” I thought, suddenly anxious. The second I am sure about this woman I will go back and fetch them, bring them to this place. Where were all these creatures going? Was this a great exodus, or just their morning routine? I could not leave her until the sun came up. I rested my hand on her chest, and thought she felt just a little bit warmer than she had the night before. She could have stolen a little of the heat I lost to the cold, sure—but there was no reason to lose that tiny flicker of hope.

The edge of the day’s sky began to glow pink, and the sun peeked over the treetops, bright, innocent, like it had never seen a world like this before. At the sight of that raw, untainted daylight I jumped to my feet, full of sharp, fierce energy, ready to fight death itself to take her from it. I pulled the scrap of cloth I had tucked over her face to keep pine needles out of her eyes and mouth, and stared down at her, half terrified I would find nothing but an empty husk, half praying I would find a living soul. She was still as motionless as the day I found her. In full daylight now I could see how hollow her cheeks were, how sharp every bone jutted under her thin skin, how every line of her teeth was visible beneath her lips. The human body she wore was worn so thin it was almost threadbare—but that did not mean the soul inside was not still nested there, ready to wake, ready to speak. I rearranged the scrap of cloth over her face, laid a handful of leaves over top to shield her from the direct sun, and set off to follow the procession of creatures.

Their track was wide, well trodden for who knew how long, dozens of smaller paths branching in from the forest on both sides to merge and widen it. The trees fell away behind me, and a vast, rolling expanse of vivid green grass stretched all the way to the horizon. A small, full-banked river ran along the edge of the treeline, and the main track led straight to its shores. Bluish mist curled off the water, vanishing the second it drifted up into the air, and on the far side hundreds of tiny grazing animals were feeding, having swum across to reach the fertile plain. The moment I saw the water a wild, new hope bloomed in my chest. I knelt to drink, and the water was hot, stinging my palms, with a faint sharp metallic tang. This was the warmth I had been searching for—the exact thing I needed to rouse her.

I did not even pause to think how strange it was, how alone I was out there. The thought of this woman waking, of no longer being the only living human in this whole wild world, was overwhelming. “No matter who she turns out to be,” I thought, “I will never be lonely again.” I had been such poor company for myself for so long, I had forgotten what real hope felt like. This blessed hot water would chase away the cold that was clinging to her, and drown my desolation for good. She was impossibly light, all delicate bones with barely any flesh between them, so light I could carry her easily on one arm, her head leaning against my shoulder like a sleeping child. I walked as softly as I could, afraid the very wind of my movement might jostle her and snuff that last tiny possible flicker of life out. The water was far too hot to submerge her immediately, the shock could scare any lingering soul right out of her, so I laid her on the bank, dipped my tunic in the warm current, and bathed every inch of her wasted frame slowly, gradually. She was so gaunt, save for her long thick black hair, I could not even guess if she was young or old. Her lids were cracked just barely open, like a crack in the clouds over a dark night, with no sun shining through. The longer I bathed her the more my hope faded, that those bones would never fill out again, that her eyes would never lift to look at me—but I kept going, warming every part of her so no patch grew cold while I tended to another, until at last I dared to step into the river with her, holding her face above the surface and letting the slow warm current wrap all around her. I noticed, but could make no sense of it, that her clenched hand never loosened its hold for even a second.

After ten minutes I lifted her out, dried her off, wrapped her in my clothes, then ran back into the forest for armfuls of leaves to pile over her. The grass and soil were warm to the touch, and when I came back she had barely lost any of the heat the water had given her. I laid more leaves over her, ran back for more, then a third trip, a fourth, until I could safely leave her to go explore the rocky hills I had spotted a short way up the stream. When I reached them, I found the river poured fully formed straight out of a gap at the base of the largest hill, as if it had been rushing down a long hidden stairway inside the rock desperate to get out, finally finding its exit. The stream did not fill the whole opening, so I crept through the crack into a small, sheltered cave, and found that the water rose quiet and steady out of the back of the cave floor like the base of a great stone column, running along a deep narrow channel cut into the rock. I realized right away if I dragged a few long fallen boughs across the channel, wove smaller twigs between them, and piled dry moss and thick leaves over top, I could make a soft warm bed suspended right over the running hot water, that would stay perfectly heated day and night.

I went back for her, carried her into the cave, laid her down on that new warm couch, and when I pried her mouth open to tuck a grape inside, I found I could open it far wider than the day before. The grape sat heavy on her tongue, but I hoped some of its juice was seeping slowly down her throat. Two hours later, she was no longer cold at all. The warmth from the running stream had sunk fully into her frame. It was not the warmth of a living person, not yet—but it was the kind of steady heat that could let a flicker of trapped life hang on, if it was there. I had read stories of people in trances that laid motionless for weeks without moving, without eating, without speaking. For seven whole days and seven whole nights I sat in that cave with her, waking and sleeping in turns, never leaving her unwatched. Every morning I bathed in the hot river myself, and felt like I had eaten a full meal, drunk my fill, enough to sustain me for the whole rest of the day—so every morning I bathed her too, in the same warm water, leaving a fresh grape on her tongue each time. Once a faint discoloration bloomed on her left side that terrified me, making me think decay had finally set in, but it was gone entirely the next sunrise. I ate only the wild grapes and berries I could forage just inside the treeline, and found I barely needed that much at all. I dreamed every single night of a wounded angel that fell from the sky, who could not fly, who stayed with me, who loved me and never wanted to leave—and every time I woke, it was not that glowing angel face I saw, it was her pale, motionless wasted face staring up at the cave ceiling. But Adam himself, waiting for Eve to wake, could not have watched more anxiously than I watched for this woman to open her eyes. Adam had not yet known what it was to be alone, to hunger for another soul. I was a stranger in this world, far from all my people, and I had learned to crave connection so fiercely that if this last shred of possible womanhood faded away, all I would have left inside me was a burning, endless hunger for life. For a little while I even forgot the Little Ones, left safe where I had hidden them. The state of the world did not matter, nothing mattered but that this form beside me might wake, might open her eyes, might look at me. I realized then: a man all alone is never fully a man. He is just a possibility, a need, half finished. No man can be enough for himself. You only grow into your full self, your full individuality, when you are surrounded by other souls. You cannot breathe in an atmosphere made only of yourself. Even if this woman woke and was nothing at all like the angel in my dreams, I would no longer have to wander alone, no longer have to go join the grazing beasts just for the sake of being near something alive. Even with that tiny, almost impossible flicker of hope, I was no longer the poorest creature in this whole wild world. I was a possible man, on the cusp of finally being whole.

I woke one morning from a sleep so deep it felt like I’d been dragged half-submerged across the threshold of another world, a sharp throbbing fire lancing up from my left hand that sent me jolting upright. The back of it was swollen to twice its usual size, stretched tight and shiny, and dead center of the inflamed flesh sat a neat triangular puncture, oozing a faint bead of iridescent blood exactly the shape of a leech’s bite. The swelling ebbed as the hours dragged on, and by dusk the wound was little more than a faint silver scar, almost fully healed. I turned over every moss-caked loose stone in the cave that night, probed every crevice in the rock walls, but found nothing that could have possibly sunk its fangs into me.

Days bled into one another, slow and soft as the warm mineral mist curling off the cave’s running hot stream. The body I guarded never moved, never stirred a single eyelid, but it could not be dead. Not a hint of rot clung to the air around it; the sharp jutting angles of its cheekbones and ribcage were softening, the parchment dryness of its old stretched skin smoothing into something plump and faintly pearlescent. Life had to be there, thrumming slow under that yielding skin – the tide that had ebbed so far out toward the edge of nothingness had finally turned, creeping back to fill the empty shape beneath the white linen I’d draped over her. Twenty times a day I knelt to check for the smallest sign of that return, twenty times I doubted, twenty times I almost despaired… until I pictured her the way I’d found her, crumpled in the moon-dappled forest, and hope would spark bright in my chest again.

Several weeks had slipped by that way, when one night I lay wakeful for hours, the cave’s warm air cloying and heavy on my skin, until I finally rose to step outside for a cooler breeze. The full moon hung low over the treeline, pouring silver light through the cave mouth to dapple the floor, and I paused for one last lingering look at my treasure before I left. “Bliss eternal,” I gasped out loud, my voice cracking, “am I seeing her eyes?”

Great dark orbs, black as the void between stars, luminous not with reflected light but with the excess of their own endless dark, seemed to glow out of the pale, moon-silvered curve of her face. I crept closer, my heart thudding so hard I was sure the noise of it would rouse her, and bent over her – only to feel my stomach drop straight through the floor of the cave. Her lids were still sealed tight. Hope and my own starved, lonely imagination had woven the illusion between them. I collapsed on the cold stone, and wept until my throat was raw.

Then a thought hit me. That tiny, awful crack in her lids I’d spotted the first few days after I’d carried her here, the little gap that had let nothingness peer through? It was gone. She must have opened them for a heartbeat, then fallen back asleep. Or maybe she was awake right now, holding them closed on purpose. Either way, it was life that had shut them, not death. My dread ebbed, and I dropped back onto my pallet, fast asleep in seconds.

That night I was bitten again, and woke burning with a thirst so fierce my tongue felt glued to the roof of my mouth. I searched every inch of the cave the next morning, even probing the depths of the hot stream’s shallows with a stick, and still found nothing. The bite was identical to the first, and by evening it was nearly gone. I concluded some giant, uncanny leech native to the hot waters was sneaking in to feed at night. “But if it’s only after blood,” I told myself, “so long as I’m here, I’ll give it mine before it ever gets near her.”

That same morning, when I peeled a plump sun-ripened grape, plucked out all the seeds, and brushed it against her lips, they twitched in a tiny, clear movement of reception. I knew then, beyond all doubt, that she lived. My hope swelled so big I started planning: she had to have clothes, something soft and easy to slip on, so the moment she was strong enough to stand, she could do it without delay.

I headed out into the forest that afternoon, hunting for a suitable fabric, and soon spotted the tough, fibrous skeletons left behind by fallen prickly pear leaves, woven into fine, strong reticulated mats. I gathered an armful, spread them out in the sun to dry, pulled apart their fine interwoven layers, and started stitching together two loose, flowing garments: one a skirt that hung from the waist, the other a tunic that draped soft over her shoulders, sewn through three layers of the fiber with an aloe spine needle and twisted vine filament.

For a week after that, she showed no new signs of waking, but every day she took the grapes more easily, her form growing rounder, her skin fairer and clearer. But a horrid, creeping doubt would nudge at me sometimes: was this growth not life, but some kind of uncanny fungal bloom? The few grapes I fed her could never account for how fast she was filling out.

Then I was bitten again. The thing began to visit me every three days, without fail. It would sink its fangs into my neck or my upper arm, always just one clean bite, always while I slept, never once venturing out in the daylight even when I napped on purpose. I lay awake for hours on the watch, ears straining, eyes wide in the dark, but I never heard it approach, never saw a glint of it, never even felt the moment its teeth broke my skin. Eventually I stopped trying to catch it. I could feel my strength slipping, feel the blood draining out of me at a dangerous rate, but I didn’t care. Death was retreating from her, life was growing brighter by the day, and soon we would leave this cave together, and I’d recover fast enough once the leech was gone.

I finished the garments, then wove layers of the same fiber into soft, flexible sandals for her. One night I jolted awake, breathless and faint, my head spinning so bad I had to crawl toward the cave mouth to get air – when a soft rustle from her leaf couch froze me mid-movement.

“I caught the vile thing,” said a faint, clear voice, speaking my own mother tongue, so familiar I almost cried out. “I caught it in the very act.”

She was alive. She spoke. I clamped down on my wild, overwhelming joy before it could make me stammer and startle her. “What creature?” I breathed, barely pushing the sound past my lips.

“The thing that was biting you.”

“What was it?”

“A great white leech.”

“How big?” I asked, forcing every muscle in my body to stay still and calm.

“Nearly six feet long, I think,” she answered.

“You may have saved my life! But how could you touch that horrid thing? That was so brave of you!” I cried.

“I did it,” was all she said, and I heard a faint shudder in her tone.

“Where is it? What could you do with a monster that big?”

“I threw it in the river.”

“Then it’ll come back, I’m afraid!”

“I don’t think I could have killed it even if I knew how,” she said. “I heard you moaning, got up to see what was hurting you, and saw that awful thing fastened to your neck. I wrenched it off. But I couldn’t hold it, I could barely heave it away from me. All I heard was it splash into the rushing water.”

“We’ll kill it next time it comes,” I said – but the words barely left my mouth before the world swam, my vision went white, and I pitched forward toward the cave mouth.

I woke at sunrise, the golden light spilling over me. She stood a few paces away, clad in the rough, clumsy fiber clothes I’d stitched, and even in that crude garment, she was grand as a marble caryatid, graceful as a wind-blown pine. I’d seen those glorious eyes the night before, I realized. They’d shone in the dark hours, dark as the primeval void, and now in the sun they glowed brighter than daylight itself. She stood straight as a carved column, gazing at me, her pale face showing no softness, only a quiet, unyielding question.

I scrambled to my feet. “We have to leave,” I said. “The white leech –”

I broke off. A strange, cold little smile flickered across her beautiful face.

“Did you find me there?” she asked, pointing at the pallet where I’d laid her these past months.

“No,” I said. “I carried you there.”

“You carried me?”

“Yes.”

“From where?”

“From the deep forest.”

“What did you do with my clothes? And my jewels?”

“You had none when I found you. Nothing but skin and bone, and your hair.”

“Then why didn’t you leave me there?”

“Because I hoped you weren’t dead.”

“Why would you care?”

“Because I was so lonely. I wanted you to wake, so I wouldn’t be alone anymore.”

“You kept me trapped, enchanted, just to hoard my beauty for yourself,” she said, sharp with proud, icy scorn.

Her words stung, and indignation flared hot in my chest. “There was no beauty left to hoard when I found you,” I said.

“Then why didn’t you just leave me to die?”

“Because you were my own kind.”

“Your kind?” she cried, her tone dripping with utter contempt. “I thought that once, too – but I see I was very much mistaken.”

“I pitied you, that was all.”

“No woman ever deserved more pity, and less of every other feeling,” I shot back.

A look of unutterable pain, mortification, and blazing anger crossed her face. She turned away from me, and went silent. The dark gulfs of her eyes held the depth of a starless night; the light in them had died, snuffed out by her hate for the kindness I’d shown.

“If you’d never roused me, what would you have done?” she asked suddenly, not turning around.

“I would have buried you.”

“It? What ‘it’? You would have buried *this*?” she shrieked, spinning around on me in a white, blinding fury, her arms flung wide, her eyes darting forks of cold, unearthly lightning.

“No, not you,” I said, steady, unflinching – there was no point in lying to a woman like this. “What we’ve brought back to life, these weeks of watching and tending. But if I’d spotted the smallest sign of rot on you, I would have buried you at once, no hesitation.”

“Foolish dog!” she snarled. “I was only in a trance! Samoil, what a cursed fate! Go fetch that savage woman you stole this hideous rag of a dress from.”

“I wove it for you myself,” I said. “It’s ugly, I know, but it was the best I could do.”

She drew herself up to her full, towering height. “How long was I unconscious?” she demanded. “A woman couldn’t sew that much in a single day.”

“Not in twenty, hardly even thirty,” I told her.

“Ha! And how long do you claim I lay there, senseless? Answer me now.”

“I don’t know how long you’d been lying there before I found you, but when I picked you up, you were nothing but skin stretched over bone. That was more than three months ago. Your hair was the only beautiful thing left on you. I cared for it as best I could.”

“My poor hair,” she murmured, and she drew a great, heavy fall of it over her shoulder from behind her. “It’ll take far more than three months of care to make me forgive you for dragging me back to life. I suppose I have to thank you, though I can’t say I feel grateful.”

“There’s no need for thanks, madam,” I said. “I would have done the same for any woman, or any man, for that matter.”

“Why isn’t my hair tangled?” she asked, running her fingers through the silken strands.

“I washed it for you every morning in the hot river’s current. I never could have brought you back to life without those daily baths.”

She flinched hard, a shudder of raw disgust running through her, and stood for a long minute staring at the rushing, warm water. Then she turned back to me, her face set like stone.

“We are going to understand each other clearly,” she said. “You have done me the two worst wrongs anyone could inflict. You forced me back to life, and you shamed me. Neither of those wrongs can I ever pardon.”

She lifted her left hand, and flung it out in a sharp, repelling motion. Something ice-cold, colder than the heart of a frozen glacier, slammed into my forehead. The world went black. When I woke, I was sprawled on the wet cave stone, soaked through, shivering so hard my teeth chattered, alone.

I stumbled to my feet, every nerve frayed, my chest hollow with a shock so deep it took ten full seconds to clear the fog from my eyes. For a beat I could not find her. She was gone. The loneliness that rolled over me was heavier than any storm cloud, the kind that blots out every patch of blue left after the rain that had soaked the moor hours before. The woman I had hauled back gasping from the very lip of the grave—she who had owed me her second breath of life—had fled from my side, and left nothing but empty, searing desolation in her wake.
I could not bear to stand motionless in that awful, gaping solitude for one more heartbeat. Had I wronged her somehow? The thought knifed through me. I had wrenched her out of the quiet rest she’d craved, hadn’t I? The least I could do was spend the rest of my days at her side, helping her carry the weight of that life I’d forced her to take up again.
I spotted her then, striding fast across the sun-dappled grass, putting distance between herself and the silver curve of the river behind us. I darted to the water’s edge for one quick, bracing sip of the restorative current that had helped nurse us both back to strength, then set off after her. The lingering fever from the white leech’s parasitic bite, paired with the sharp blow the other woman had landed on my jaw, had sapped most of my strength earlier—but now every muscle was thrumming with returning vigor, and I kept her in clear sight without straining to catch up.
“Is this really how it ends?” I muttered as I walked, the words winding into a sad, wordless hum in my chest. The image of her eyes, blazing angry and full of unnameable hatred, replayed behind my lids over and over. I could almost parse her resentment: who wouldn’t seethe at being dragged back to a life they’d already said goodbye to? But what else had I done to hurt her? Why did she look at me like I was something filthy? Could even modesty itself bristle at a kindness that had never once crossed a line? What proud woman, who had watched every single one of my actions while she lay unconscious, could ever hold a single shred of grudge against me for dishonoring her? I had touched her as reverently as a father holds his motherless newborn. I had tended every bruise, every fever spike, every trembling moment of her return. Had all that work, all that desperate, gasping hope I’d poured into saving her only bought me ingratitude in return?
“No,” I said to myself, shaking my head hard. “Any creature that beautiful has to have a heart. It’s just buried deep. The deeper it’s hidden, the stronger, the truer it will wake up when it finally unfurls in that dark, beautiful grave of its own making. Waking that heart inside her would be a greater gift than any easy, happy life I could have given her otherwise. It would hand her a higher, nobler kind of existence altogether.”
She was climbing a soft, gentle slope ahead of me, her back straight, her steps steady as someone who knew every inch of the land and exactly where she was going—until I noticed she was pulling further and further away, her pace picking up without any visible effort. I called on the last of my stored strength, and it flooded through me all at once. My veins hummed with light, my body felt almost weightless, ethereal, and I moved like a breeze slipping through grass, eating up the ground between us until I was nearly at her heels. She hadn’t glanced back once. She moved with the unhurried, unstopping grace of a Greek goddess on her way to pull off a miracle, no sign of haste in her movements.
I was three yards from her when she turned on a dime, her grace never faltering for a second, and stood still facing me. There was no flush of exertion on her cheeks, no gasp of breath. Her paleness wasn’t the sickly gray of fatigue—it was the pure, luminous white of moonlit marble. She breathed slow, deep, even, and her eyes felt so wide and bright they could light up the whole sky. It was almost noon, but the air around us felt thick with the hush of a deep midnight, the kind where invisible dew hangs heavy on every leaf and makes the stars burn huge and low in the dark.
“Why are you following me?” she asked, her voice quiet but sharp, stern, like she was looking at a total stranger she’d never laid eyes on before.
“I’ve clung to nothing but the faint hope of seeing your eyes for months now,” I answered her. “How could I stop myself from chasing that?”
“You will not be spared,” she said, cold as frost. “I order you to stop right where you stand.”
“I won’t leave you until I’ve seen you safe somewhere,” I shot back.
“Then face the consequences,” she said, and turned to walk again, her feet gliding over the grass like she didn’t even touch it. But over her shoulder she cast one glance at me, and I froze in place like I’d been run clean through the chest with a steel spear. Her scorn wasn’t enough to break me—so she was trying to kill me with the sheer weight of her beauty. Desperation snapped the spell holding me rigid, and I sprinted to catch up to her.
“Have mercy on me!” I cried. She didn’t even acknowledge my words. I followed after her like a small, lost child whose mother was pretending to leave him behind in the woods. “I’ll be your servant, your slave, whatever you want,” I said, and reached out to rest my hand lightly on her arm.
She spun around like I’d burned her with a viper’s bite. I cowered back from the blaze of fury in her eyes, but I couldn’t tear my own gaze away. “Please,” I begged again. “Have pity on me.”
She didn’t say a word, just turned and kept walking.
I followed her all day. The sun climbed high overhead, hung motionless at its peak for what felt like hours, then slid slowly down the other side of the sky. She didn’t pause for a single breath. I didn’t stop walking either. She never turned her head, never slowed her pace for even a second. The sun dipped below the horizon, and night seeped up over the moor. I stayed right at her heels—if I lost sight of her for even a blink, I knew I’d never find her again.
We’d been trekking over thick, soft, springy grass all day, until abruptly she stopped, and sank face down into it. The last of the twilight light was still just bright enough to show me she was completely, utterly exhausted. I stood behind her, staring down at her still form for a long moment. Did I love her? I knew for a fact she wasn’t a good person. Did I hate her? I could no more leave her side than I could stop breathing.
I knelt down beside her. “Get away. Don’t you dare touch me,” she screamed. Her arms lay limp at her sides, like they’d turned to stone and lost all feeling. Then, all at once, they flew up and locked tight around my neck, rigid as the iron bands of a torture device. She hauled my face down to hers, and her lips pressed hard to my cheek. A sharp, stinging pain lanced through me, throbbing hot where her mouth touched my skin. I couldn’t move a single muscle, not even twitch a single hair on my head.
Slowly the pain faded. A heavy, dreamy weariness washed over me, laced with a soft, floating pleasure that dragged me down under into blackness, until I knew nothing at all.
I jolted awake all at once. The moon hung low above the horizon, but it cast no light, just sat there a small bright coin stuck in a wall of pure black. My cheek stung like I’d been pricked by a needle. I lifted my hand to it, and found a damp, warm spot on my skin. My neck ached too—there was another wet spot there, right where her arms had wrapped around me. I sighed, my chest heavy, and felt leaden with exhaustion. I dragged my eyes slowly around the dark—and realized where all the moon’s light had gone. It was wrapped around the lady, coating her in a shimmering, silver nimbus.
I staggered to my feet and stumbled toward her. “Down!” she snapped, the tone of her voice sharp as if she was barking an order at a disobedient stray dog. “Take one more step after me and I’ll make you regret it.”
“I will follow you,” I mumbled, forcing the words out through a fog of agony.
“Step through the gates of my city, and my people will stone you to death,” she warned. “They do not suffer beggars to trespass.”
I didn’t hear a single word she said. Weak as melted wax, half lost in a daze, I didn’t even feel my feet moving—but the gap between us kept shrinking. She stepped back once, lifted her left arm, and her clenched fist swung toward my forehead like a sledgehammer. The blow hit me harder than any iron hammer could, and I crumpled to the grass.
I jumped back to my feet seconds later, cold and soaked through, my mind crystal clear, every muscle thrumming with strength. Had that blow woken me up fully? There was no wound on my forehead, no lingering pain—so why was I soaking wet? I couldn’t have been lying there for long, the moon hadn’t climbed an inch higher in the sky.
The lady stood a few yards away, her back to me, doing something I couldn’t quite make out in the dim light. Then a sudden flash of white told me she’d stripped off all her clothes, standing bare and luminous in the dazed, hazy moonlight. She stood perfectly still for one beat—and pitched forward onto the grass. A streak of blinding white shot away from the spot, moving fast in a long, clean line. The moon burst into full brightness right that second, and I saw what the streak was: a long-bodied, predatory creature, loping in low, curved bounds across the grass, dark shadowy streaks rippling down its back like it was running along the edge of a wood and catching the dark shapes of passing leaves.
“Merciful God,” I breathed. “That terrible thing is speeding straight for the city, all wrapped up in night.” And for a second I thought I could hear the distant, rising scream of terror as the pale savage bounded over house tops, tearing through sleeping streets, rending and slaying everyone in its path.
I stood frozen, staring after it in horror, when a second large creature shot past me from behind, moving so fast it was almost silent, like a white arrow. It was purest white, and it was heading straight for the spot where the lady had fallen, the spot I thought her body still lay. My tongue stuck to the roof of my mouth. I lunged forward, chasing the beast, but in a single leap it put so much ground between us the spot I was running for was already far behind it.
It was a mercy I couldn’t call out, I thought dazedly. If she’d woken up, the monster would have been on her in a second. But when I finally reached the spot, there was no lady there. Nothing but the clothes she’d stripped off lay on the grass, dark and soft in the moonlight.
I stared after the second beast. It tore across the moor faster than the first one, moving in long, level skimming leaps, the very definition of perfect, wasteless speed. It followed exactly the same path the first creature had taken, and I watched it shrink smaller and smaller until it vanished into the hazy dark distance.
But where was the lady? Had the first beast snuck up on her silently while I was unconscious? I hadn’t heard a scream. There wasn’t enough time for it to have eaten her, either. Could it have snatched her up mid-run and carried her off to its den? It couldn’t have moved that fast if it was carrying a full grown woman. I would have seen the weight of her slung over its back. Horrible, twisting doubts began to gnaw at the edges of my mind. After searching every inch of the clearing until my feet ached and found no trace of her at all, I turned and set off after the two creatures, following their faint trail across the grass.

The fraying silver of this otherworld’s moon lit my path as I hurried, until a tattered bank of storm cloud swallowed it whole. Out of the sudden ash-gray dark, a pale shape burst forth: a woman in a shift bleached bone-white by constant exposure to the strange night, a swaddled infant clamped tight to her chest, hunched low as she ran. Her path ran parallel to my own, and she never spotted me, every line of her body thrumming with the raw, driven speed of one hunted past the edge of endurance, terror sharp enough to taste hanging thick around her.
“Something’s chasing her,” I breathed to myself. “One of the prowling horrors of this accursed night is at her heels.”
I knew calling out would only send her spiraling into worse panic. Instead, I stepped square into the wake of her footprints, planting myself to block whatever came after. For a heartbeat I watched her recede into the dim, then a soft, lightning-fast rush sounded behind me—before I could twist around, something bounded clean over my head, a hard heavy paw striking my forehead with a crack that sent me sprawling.
I was on my feet before my gown could even settle, blood trickling warm down my brow, but all I caught of my attacker was a flash of vanishing white slipping through the mist. I gave chase, the sticky blood crusting on my skin, but I had barely cleared half a dozen paces when a shriek tore through the night so raw and ragged it made the very air quiver. I ran faster, my gut tight with the certainty I was already too late.
A minute later, a low white shape came lurching toward me through moonlight dusted with fine vapour. For a second I thought it was a second hunter beast, moving slow, half crawling, in lurching, floundering leaps like a creature broken and in agony. I stepped off the path to wait, hand curling around the hilt of the small knife I carried.
When it drew near, I saw it was moving on three legs, its left forepaw held high, cradled away from the grass. Its pelt was shining bone-white patterned with dark oval spots, and a strange low rushing hum hung around it, like the sound of spring water spilling over long grass. As it hobbled past, I saw dark warm blood streaming from its lifted paw.
“Someone faster and braver than me wounded it,” I thought. But unaccountable pity welled up in my chest so sharp it ached—even if I’d held a double-bladed axe in both hands, I knew I could not have struck the suffering thing. It kept leaping in broken, hobbling bursts until it vanished into the dark, its blood still flowing in a thin soft torrent that seeped back through the grass right at my feet.
“At that rate, it’ll bleed out long before it finds another target,” I reasoned, and kept moving. The woman might still need help, and I wanted to find whoever had stepped in to defend her.
I spotted her not far off, seated on the grass, the child curled safe in her lap.
“Can I help you at all?” I called out soft and low.
She jolted so hard at the sound of my voice she nearly tipped upright to run. I dropped immediately to the ground, hands up where she could see them even through the dim. “You’ve nothing to fear from me. I was chasing the beast, and was so glad to see you’d found a protector faster than me! It just passed me by with its paw pouring blood—by now it’s nearly dead, for certain.”
“There’s little hope of that,” she said, her voice shaking so hard the words stuttered. “Do you not know who that beast belongs to?”
I’d had odd, creeping suspicions, but I told her I knew nothing of the brute, and asked where her mysterious champion had gone.
“What champion?” she said, confused. “I saw no one else at all.”
“Then how did you hurt the monster so badly?”
“I bashed its paw in with a sharp flint—hit it as hard as I could manage. Didn’t you hear it scream?”
“I thought that scream was yours!” I said, stunned. “You are the bravest woman I’ve ever met.”
“That was the leopardess,” she said. “I’ve never heard a beast make a sound like that, it was exactly the scream of a woman being tortured. My throat was frozen tight with fear, I couldn’t have made a sound even to save my baby. When I saw that horrid mouth inches from my darling’s soft little neck, I grabbed the first heavy stone I could reach and smashed her lame foot right in.”
“Tell me about the creature,” I said. “I’m a stranger to this land, I don’t know its ways.”
“You’ll learn fast enough if you’re heading for Bulika,” she warned. “I can never go back there, not ever.”
“I am heading to Bulika,” I told her. “I’m going to see the princess.”
“Steer clear. You’d do best to turn back now—though wait, perhaps you’re—no. They say the princess is a good, kind woman.”
I heard a small shift of fabric, and the moon was so swaddled in thick storm clouds by then I could barely make out the outline of her shape. I thought she was about to run from me again.
“I mean you no harm at all,” I said quickly. “What oath can I swear to make you believe me?”
“I can tell from the way you speak you’re not from Bulika’s people,” she said. “I’ll trust you. I’m not one of them either, else I’d never be able to trust anyone—they never trust a single soul. I wish I could see your face, but I like your voice. There, my little one is fast asleep. That foul beast never touched her. It was my baby she was hunting, right from the start. She would have torn me to pieces the second she got her jaws on the child, for daring to run off with it.” She stroked the baby’s soft hair as she spoke. “Some say the princess keeps two white leopardesses. I only ever seen this spotted one. Everyone in Bulika knows her. The second the princess hears word of a new baby born, she sends the beast straight to drink its blood. The child either dies right after, or grows up to wander the hills, empty and witless. I tried to run away with my baby before, but the princess was off on a journey, so I thought I could wait a little longer until I was strong enough for the road. But she must have taken the beast with her on her trip, and came riding back the very hour I left. She caught my trail fast. I heard that snuffling sniff-snuff right behind me, and I ran. Oh, how I ran. But my baby will not die. There is not a single mark on her skin.”
“Where are you taking her?” I asked.
“Somewhere no one from Bulika will ever find us,” she said.
“Why is the princess so cruel?”
“There’s an old prophecy that a child will be the death of her. That’s why they say she will never take a husband, no matter the offer.”
“But what will become of her whole kingdom, if she kills every last newborn baby?”
“She does not care a whit for her country. She sends her witches out to the villages, to teach the women spells to make their babies fade, to feed them horrid poisons that make their wombs barren. Some say she’s struck a bargain with the Shadows, to wipe the whole human race out entirely. Every night we all lie awake shivering, listening to the questing beasts pad past our huts. She can smell a new baby from miles away, and she’ll lie down at the door of the hut for days, waiting for a crack to slip in. There are old rhymes that are supposed to chase her away, but they don’t always work.”
She surged to her feet all at once, panicking. “I’m sitting here chattering, and that beast might have dragged itself home already! Its mistress will send the second one out to hunt us down before dawn!”
“I don’t think it will ever make it back to the palace,” I said, standing beside her. “Let me carry the baby for you, to make the run easier.”
She said nothing, only clamped the child tighter to her chest when I reached out to offer.
“I still can’t make sense of how that brute was bleeding so much,” I said, falling into step beside her.
“Take my advice,” she said, not looking at me. “Don’t go anywhere near the palace. At night you can hear sounds coming from it, like a crowd of dead people trying to scream, but their mouths are sewn shut so no sound can get out but a terrible muted gurgle.”
Then she bade me goodbye, sharp and abrupt, turning away without another word. It was clear she did not want my company any longer, so I stood still, watching her pale shape melt into the dark, until the soft sound of her footsteps over the damp grass faded away entirely, leaving nothing but the hum of the night wind, and the distant, unblinking glow of Bulika’s palace far off on the horizon.

I had long lost all sense of direction, wandering raw and frayed with helpless impatience, when my feet slipped straight into the path of the leopardess, wading through the trickle that dripped from her paw. The flow surged against my ankles with the weight and momentum of a small fast brook, and I stumbled back fast, a cold, unformed dread pricking at the back of my neck: I did not want to linger long enough to wonder whose blood this might be. But I did not turn away. I followed the soft gurgle, wading up the gentle grassy slope the current carved, for that sound would lead me straight to Bulika.

It did not take me long to start questioning what I was seeing. No leopard, no elephant, no prehistoric titan that ever walked my home world could bleed that much. You would need every artery gaping wide, and the creature’s whole living system pumping straight from lakes and forests to replenish the flow as fast as it poured out. This could not be blood. I dipped a finger in, and the shakiness in my chest loosened. It was nothing but water: a softly murmuring rivulet, with no proper channel, spreading thin over the blades of grass. Sweet as its song sounded, I dared not take a sip. I kept moving, straining for the first glimmer of distant light, the familiar trickle a comfort I had not known in months, so far removed from the thick, scalding viscous streams I had navigated before. Even just the dampness on my feet refreshed me so thoroughly I walked on without fatigue, until the dark around me thinned, and I knew the sun was just about to crest the horizon.

Minutes later, against the pearly pale aurora, I spotted them: the spired wall-towers of a city, so weathered they looked older than the first stone ever carved by human hands. I glanced down, searching for the brook, and found it gone. I had noticed its sound fading little by little until I’d stopped paying attention entirely. I looked back: the grass all along its path lay bent flat where the current had pushed through, a few tiny glimmering puddles scattered here and there, but nothing else. No trace of it at all stretched toward the city. The fountain that fed it must have stopped flowing right where I stood.

Around the walls stretched acres of gardens, heavy with strange knobbly vegetables I barely recognized a single one of. No blooms, no birds, no bees, no hint of any living animal at all. The gardens pressed almost to the base of the walls, separated only by massive mounds of gravel and rotting refuse heaped over the battlements and dumped down the sides. I walked to the nearest gate: it hung half-ajar, no locks, no bars, no guards pacing the entrance. Its hinges were so rusted and fused they could not swing an inch further open, nor click shut any tighter. I stepped through, and stared down a long, ancient cobblestoned street. Dead silent. Not a wisp of smoke from the windows, no footfall, no hint that anyone alive resided here. Had I stumbled on a long-abandoned necropolis? I turned right back out, trudged over the dust heaps, and crossed three more roads each leading to a separate gate. I would not re-enter until someone, anyone, was up and moving.

What was I even doing here? What did I hope to find? I held no delusions. I did not crave Bulika’s company—she had woken something sharp and terrible and suspicious in me, and any thought of friendship, let alone anything softer, was absurd, impossible between us. But her presence warped reality around her, pulling at some hidden thread in my bones, and I had to see her one more time. I had to stand in the middle of that strange pull, resist it, and pick it apart. I wanted to crack the riddle of her, the things that made no sense, to peer into a kind of existence no human imagination could ever dream up. But I knew I was being reckless. No man should court temptation just to sate his hunger for knowledge. Worse still: I was the one who had pulled her back from the edge of oblivion. I owed it to the gentle child beings called the Little Ones, whom I had come to protect, to make sure she never harmed them. I already learned she was their enemy. I had come here chasing answers about their fate, and got a glimmer of light, but I needed more. I needed to learn how to keep them safe.

At last, I heard faint movement from within the walls. I walked through the next gate, down a narrow street lined with towering, fog-windowed houses, until I reached a small square, and sat on the base of a broken pillar topped with a hideous carved bat-like gargoyle. Soon, townsfolk started sauntering past. I spoke to the first one, and got a rude glare, a snappish insult, and the man kept walking. I got up, wandering from street to street as the roads slowly filled with loitering idlers. No children anywhere, just as I expected. Near one of the gates, I ran into a knot of young men, brutal, coarse, bearing a terrifying resemblance to the bad giants I had crossed paths with before. They swarmed around me, stared, started shoving, jostling, then hurling pebbles and rotten scraps at me. I took it, did not fight back— I did not want to make enemies when I needed to stay here long enough to find Bulika. I appealed to passersby I thought looked a little kinder, but not one would stop for even a second. I looked ragged, poor, and in Bulika that was a crime as far as the locals were concerned. Poverty, deformity, sickness—all were taxed, and no law their princess had ever passed was more beloved than the ones that forced the poor to serve the wealthy. At last I broke into a run, bolted for the gate, and none of them chased me past the walls. Only one lumbering lout, sitting by the gate gnawing a hunk of bread, lifted a stone to throw after me, but in his stupid eagerness, he flung the bread instead. I snatched it off the ground, and the man was too cowardly to follow past the walls to get it back. All of them turned to cowards the second they stepped outside the city gates.

I trundled a few hundred yards away, flopped down in the long grass, ate the bread, and dropped asleep fast, the warm midday sun seeping into my bones to refill my spent strength. I woke when it was dark. The moon hung high, bright and familiar, like an old friend who recognized me. It was the same moon, I thought, that had watched me through the terrors of my very first night in this uncanny world. A cold wind gusted out through the city gate, carrying a thick, foul stench, but it did not chill me— I was still brimming with the sun’s stored warmth. I slipped back inside the city. The few people still out in the open were huddled in doorways, shivering, trying to escape that cutting wind. I was walking slowly down the long narrow street when something huge, white, bounded across my path in a single flash of moonlight, vanishing before I could make sense of it. I turned down the next alley, narrow enough that I had to squeeze through the gaps between the houses, chasing it, until I emerged into a wider street. There, across the road, half-hidden in shadow, was the creature— I spotted it now, it was the white leopardess— trotting close at the heels of what looked like a man. Every few steps, the man glanced over his shoulder at the beast, but he never spoke, never tried to shoo it away. When he stepped across a patch of full moonlight, I froze. He cast no shadow. He was a shadow. Flat, two-dimensional, yet somehow opaque, so black he blotted out anything that sat behind him completely, made it vanish. In the deep shadow, he was blacker than the dark around him. In the moonlight, he looked like a man who had drawn his own shadow up tight around his skin, so no trace of it lingered at his feet. The leopardess glided right at his heels, so close she looked like a white negative of his blackness, her own dark shadow sliding smooth across the stone at her side. When they stepped together out of the shadow and into the moonlit patch, the man’s shadow-body deepened to pitch black, and the leopardess blazed bright, almost glowing white.
