I was walking level with them on the opposite side of the street, my bare feet soft on the flat cobblestones. The leopardess never turned her head, never twitched an ear. But the shadow-man twisted his head to look at me— for a split second, I lost his profile, saw nothing but a sharp, thin upright line of him. That instant, the cold wind cut straight through my body. I shuddered from scalp to toe, and my heart bounced hard against the inside of my ribs, like a pebble rattling around inside a child’s toy rattle.

I veered off the main thoroughfare into a shadowed alley, ducking for shelter beneath a weathered archway hewn from Bulika’s signature pearl-stone. I halted just inside its mouth, gaze drifting out over the cobblestones glowing under the silver spill of the realm’s twin moonlights, every crack glinting with flecks of uncut star-gem.

The very next instant, a woman glided in after me, indigo silk cloak pulled tight over her shoulders. She spun to press her back to the arch’s inner wall, trembling so hard the silver thread stitched into her cuffs winked erratically, and stared back out into the alley behind us. Barely three heartbeats later, a massive xenoleopard darted across the mouth of the arch, its pelt the color of bleached bone, dappled with void-black spots that seemed to shift and ripple like ink in dark water.

The woman pressed herself flush to my side, and a sharp, warm pity swelled in my chest. I wrapped one arm around her shaking form.
“If the brute tries to come in here,” I said, “I will grapple it, and you run for whatever safe place you know.”
“Thank you,” she murmured, her voice thin as gem-dust silk.
“Have you seen it before?” I asked.
“Many times,” she answered, still trembling hard enough that her teeth chattered. “She is the princess’s personal pet. You are a stranger, or you would already know of her!”
“I am a stranger,” I confirmed. “But are the authorities so careless they let a beast like that run loose through the city?”
“She is kept locked in an iron cage, her jaws strapped in a heavy leather muzzle, her paws sheathed in thick gloves stitched from crocodile hide. She is chained fast, too—but she slips free constantly, and sucks the blood from any unguarded child she can catch. There are barely any mothers left in all Bulika, now.”

She burst into ragged sobs, hot tears tracking down her pale cheeks. “I wish I was safe inside my own home! The princess returned only last night, and the leopardess is already loose! How am I supposed to make it to my door? I know she’s hunting me—she’ll be curled right on my threshold, waiting for me!—I am a fool, rambling all this to a total stranger.”
“Not all strangers are cruel,” I told her. “The beast will have to tear through me first before it lays a claw on you, and by the time it is done with me, you will be safely inside your home. You are luckier than many, to have a house to return to—what a gale that is kicking up out there!”
“Escort me home safe, and I will give you shelter from the wind,” she offered. “But we must wait a little longer, to be sure the leopard has moved off.”

As we waited, I asked her question after question about her city. She told me the people of Bulika did nothing at all, save dig for precious stones in the vaults carved beneath their basements. They were unfathomably wealthy, and every single good they owned was crafted for them in other, distant towns.
“Why is that?” I asked.
“Because it is a disgrace to work,” she replied, like the answer was so obvious it did not need stating. “Every single person in Bulika knows that!”
I pressed her for how they maintained such vast wealth if no one in the city ever earned a single coin. She explained their ancestors had hoarded a massive stockpile of gems generations ago, and they never touched the core of that hoard. Whenever they needed currency, they just sold a small handful of uncut stones.
“But there must be people who fall into poverty here,” I said.
“I suppose that must be true,” she said, frowning like she had never considered the idea before. “But we never think of people like that. When a citizen falls poor, we erase them from all record, we forget they ever existed. That is how we keep our hoard intact. We intend to stay rich forever.”
“But one day, when you have dug up every last precious stone in the ground and sold them all,” I pointed out, “you will have to start spending your stored coin, and eventually you will have nothing left.”
“We have so many gems already hoarded, and so many more still buried in the bedrock, that day will never come,” she said, dismissive.
“Suppose a foreign army invaded your lands, and seized every last thing you own?”
“No foreign people would ever dare,” she laughed. “They are all horribly afraid of our princess. She is the one who keeps us safe, free, and infinitely rich!”

Every few sentences as she spoke, she would pause to glance over her shoulder, scanning the dark for the leopard. I asked her why her people held such violent hatred for strangers. She answered that the very presence of an outsider defiled the sacred purity of their city.
“How does that work?” I asked.
“Because we are more ancient and more noble than any other nation on the continent,” she said, chin lifting. “Which is why,” she added, “we have a law that all strangers must be expelled from the city before night falls.”
“How, then, do you plan to take me into your house?” I asked.
“I will make an exception for you,” she replied simply.
“Is there not a single place in the city built to take in travelers?”
“Any such place would be torn down stone by stone, and its owner burned alive at the moon temple,” she said. “How are we supposed to preserve our purity if we do not keep low, unblooded people at a proper distance? Dignity is such an incredibly delicate thing!”

She went on to tell me their princess had reigned for thousands of years, that she wielded power over the air and the water and the earth itself, and she was certain over fire as well. The princess could do anything she pleased, and answered to no one at all.
When at last she worked up the courage to risk the walk, we set off winding through narrow lanes and cramped, shadowed passages, and did not meet a single living creature on our way to her door. It sat on a wider, quieter street, wedged between two towering gem-facaded spires, at the top of a narrow, steep flight of stone stairs. She climbed slowly, and I followed right behind her.
Before we reached the top landing, however, she seemed to be seized by a sudden, overwhelming terror. She darted up the rest of the steps at a run, and I only made it to the top in time for her to slam the door hard in my face, the heavy iron deadbolt sliding home with a sharp, final click. I stood there stunned on the tiny landing, which was barely long enough between the opposing doors of the two spires for a full grown man to stretch out and lie down.
Weary to the bone, and not caring in the slightest that I was defiling Bulika’s precious purity with my presence, I settled into the meager shelter it offered to wait out the rest of the dark.

Moonlight spilled in silver ribbons down the rough stone stair to pool on the pitted street far below, where a sharp, unwholesome wind howled through alleyways thick with the stench of long-forgotten rot. Not a single breath of that gale crept into my sheltered nook above the landing, and I was just settling in to rest when my eyes flew open of their own accord. There, peeking over the uppermost step, was the luminous ivory head of the creature I’d seen trailing the faceless Shadow days before.
The moment our eyes locked, she froze, then began to back away, tail first, cautious as a trespasser. I sprang to my feet, and with no room to turn her broad, spotted-free pelt in the narrow stairwell, she tumbled head over hindquarters in a flurry of white paws, scrambled upright, and vanished down the steps before I could call out. I chased her all the way to the street, scanning the length of the moonlit lanes, but she was gone without a trace. I trundled back to my hard stone couch, my mind racing. Two predatory things roamed Bulika’s dark streets now: the marked shadow-beast, and this unblemished white leopardess. I cared little for the fates of the cruel, closed-off citizens of this city, but a child’s life was well worth risking my own ragged, borrowed life for. I resolved to stand watch by the hidden door for the rest of the night.
Before long, the latch of that door moved, slow as a whisper, so faint I almost thought I’d imagined it. I looked up to see the door ajar, and slipped inside soft as a shadow. The figure waiting behind it was not the widowed woman I’d helped days prior, but the muffled, hooded stranger I’d crossed paths with out in the glass dunes of the desert. She said not a single word, just led me a few steps to an empty stone-floored chamber worn smooth by centuries of foot traffic, and pointed to a woven indigo rug tossed on the floor. I wrapped myself in its thick wool, and lay down. She shut the chamber door, and I heard the outer entrance of the house open and click shut behind her.
The only light in the room seeped from the moon through a cracked high window, slashing silver streaks across the dark stone. I lay wide awake, and soon caught the sound of a stifled, distant moan. It dragged on for minutes, rising, until a newborn’s wail cut through it, followed by a shriek that curdled my blood. I launched myself up and bolted into the passage, and there she was: the white leopardess, jaws firm but gentle around the scruff of a squalling new baby, carrying it as easily as she would her own cub. I threw my full weight at her, driving my shoulder into her ribs, and forced her to drop the infant. It fell to the stone slabs with a piteous, breathless wail.
At the sound, the hooded woman appeared. She stepped clean over both of us, the beast and I, locked in our struggle in the narrow hall, picked up the crying child, and carried it away. She returned moments later, lifted me bodily off the leopardess’s back, opened the front door, and pushed me gently out onto the street. The great white cat was right on my heels.
For a heartbeat I thought she had betrayed me, handed me over to the beast to tear apart at her leisure. But I was not about to go down without a fight. I sprinted down the stairs, waiting for her to pounce on my back, but the attack never came. When I spun around to grapple her, she bounded clean over my head, and when I turned to face her again, she was crouched docile at my feet.
I stooped, and ran my hand over her soft, glowing white pelt. She responded by licking my bare feet with her rough, dry, warm tongue, and suddenly a well of tenderness rose up in my chest so thick it felt like it would spill over. She might still be treacherous, I knew, but if I turned away from every small show of kindness for fear it was a trick, I would never find that real, unfeigned love that had to exist somewhere in this strange half-waking world. I stood up, and she rose to her feet beside me, matching my height almost exactly, her purr low and steady.
A heavy, wet squelch echoed out across the street a few yards from us. I ran to the noise, and found a pulpy, mangled mass, just shape enough left to make out the body of a woman, thrown down from some high overhead window. I looked up, and spotted the faceless Shadow walking along the opposite side of the street, the white leopardess trotting obediently at his heel.
I followed, gaining on them, pleading silently to the cat, begging her that I knew she was not a free agent, that she was being forced. But the second I drew close enough to reach for her, she spun and flew at me with a hideous, guttural snarl that made me flinch back. Instantly, she turned and resumed her place behind the Shadow. I tried again, and the same thing happened, her eyes blazing like raw emeralds lit from within, and this time she snapped and sank her teeth into my forearm. I yelped in pain, and she looked back at me with a glance so clear no words were needed: Why would you make me do that?
I turned away, angry at my own foolishness for wasting so much time. Night or no night, I decided, I would march straight for the palace I had seen from the central square, perched high above the city’s core, ringed with battlements so thick it looked more a fortress than a royal home. But when I reached it, I found its fortifications just as abandoned and crumbling as the city’s walls. It was clear no sentry had manned them for hundreds of years. Its great iron gates hung wide open, the rocky chasm before it that had once held a moat was long dry, choked with rubble. The whole palace was so still it felt like the entire structure was asleep, the bright moonlight seeming almost rude and awake against the heavy hush. I crept around its walls like a stray outcast dog, until I found a small recess holding a weathered stone bench, tucked myself in out of the biting wind, and fell fast asleep despite the cold.
I woke to something leaping onto my chest, licking my face with the rough, warm tongue of a feline. “It is the white leopardess,” I thought, resigned. “She has come to drink my blood. What do I care? Fighting to defend it would cost me far more than giving it up.” I lay still, waiting for the sharp burn of her fangs, but no pain came. Instead, a slow, pleasant warmth began seeping through my frozen clothes, thawing the ice from my bones. She was stretched full length against my back, pressed as close as she could get, the heat of her body wrapping around me like a blanket, her breath soft and sweet against my neck, with none of the feral stink of a wild beast. I knew, with absolute certainty, that she meant me no harm. I turned over like a sleepy child, slung my arm over her, and sank into deep, dreamless unconsciousness.
When I began to rouse, I half-believed I was back in my own soft bed at home. I could almost smell the roses from my old garden drifting through the open window. I rubbed my eyes, and blinked the fuzz away. I was lying on bare cold stone, in the heart of that hateful, half-dead city of Bulika. I jumped off the bench. Had the leopardess truly been my bedfellow, or had I dreamed the whole thing? She had only just left, for the warm imprint of her body still clung to my clothes, sunk deep into my skin. I stepped out of the recess with a new hope, bright and unshakable even though I could not name its shape. One thing alone was clear: I had to find the princess. Surely I had some power over her, if not over this strange world. Had I not saved her life, and had she not prolonged her own life at the cost of mine? The thought gave me all the courage I needed to face her, no matter what terrible form she might take.

I made a full circuit of the ancient castle’s outer battlements before I circled back to its wide, unguarded gates, crossed the ragged, ravine-deep moat, and stepped into a flagstone court lined at regular intervals with towering, poplar-like trees whose trunks rose straight as spear shafts toward the hazy sky. At its center stood a specimen far taller than the rest, its upper boughs fanning out in a broad fronded crown that gave it the stark, otherworldly look of a wild palm. Peering between their massive shadowed stems, I caught glimpses of the palace itself: a long, low structure of a style entirely foreign to my world, its architecture carrying the faint, distinct echo of far-off Indian design. Lofty square towers jutted from each corner, and a single massive central dome rose from its roof to climb half the height of those towers. The main entrance, set dead center in the palace front, was a low, half-elliptical arch, and no guards, no servants, no living soul stood between me and its yawning, unlatched oak doors. I walked unchallenged straight inside.

The hall beyond was an elongated ellipse, dim and cool, and against one far wall stood a heavy barred cage. Curled inside it was a huge leopardess, head resting on her front paws, secured by a thick steel collar locked about her neck, her jaws muzzled with stitched leather and her pads swaddled in thick soft cloth. Her coat was pure white splotched with dark oval rosettes, her eyes wide and round with canoe-shaped slitted pupils and vivid glowing green irises. She watched me pass, but not a whisker twitched, not a claw shifted, not the tip of her tail (stretched rigid behind her like a bar of forged iron) moved a fraction of an inch. I could not have said for certain if she was a living creature or a masterfully crafted statue.

Two low, narrow passages branched off the far end of the vestibule. I picked the left one, and it quickly split into a maze of even tighter, irregular corridors, narrow enough that two people could barely squeeze past each other. At the tightest turn, a boy in the tattered livery of a palace page sprinted straight into my chest. He staggered back in naked terror, then scanned my unfamiliar face, puffed out his chest in a surge of insolent bravado, and demanded to know my business.
“To see the princess,” I told him.
“A likely tale!” he huffed. “Her highness hasn’t granted audiences to anyone all morning, not even me!”
I grabbed him by the scruff of his neck and shook him hard. “Take me to her this instant, or I will drag you through every corridor of this palace until I find her. She will hear exactly how her servants turn away uninvited guests.”
The boy gaped at me, then turned and pulled so hard at my wrist he moved like a blind man’s harnessed dog, leading me straight to a large steamy kitchen. A dozen listless servants loitered over cold hearths, their movements slow, their eyes half-lidded as if they had not properly woken in days. I braced for them to shout and fall on me to drive me out, but instead they froze, eyes blowing wide—not on me, but on something over my shoulder. Their faces bleached ghastly white the longer they stared.

I turned my head. The white leopardess stood at my heels, no cage, no muzzle, no padded paws, her green gaze fixed on the servants with a quiet menace that would have unsteadied the steeliest of hearts. One of the cooks, gathering his wits when he realized she made no move to lunge, started to stagger toward me. I released the page.
“Take me to the princess,” I said to him. “Tell her I wait audience.”
“Her highness will beat me within an inch of my life if I disturb her now, my lord,” he stammered.
“Tell her one who knows the white leech has come to see her.”
He paled further. “I dare not. She will kill me for bringing such a message.”
“You refuse?”
He darted one panicked glance at the great white leopardess standing warm and glowing against the dim kitchen stone, then turned and ran. The rest of the servants could not tear their eyes off her, rooted to the spot by unshakable fear. I turned to the graceful creature, who had dropped her muzzle to rest against my boot, white as fresh milk, a patch of warm light in the gloom of the room, and stooped to pat her head. She lifted her chin to look at me, and that small movement was enough to send every last servant scrambling for cover through the back doors. She rose onto her hind legs, rested her heavy soft paws on my shoulders, pricked her ears at a distant sound, and vanished around the corridor bend before I could even call out to her.

The cook I had sent to fetch the princess appeared in the doorway a moment later, bowing so low his forehead nearly brushed the floor. “Please follow me, my lord.”
My heart thudded hard against my ribs, bracing itself for the encounter I had chased across miles of this strange half-dead world. I followed him down winding, lightless passages until he pushed open a door and gestured me inside. The room beyond was so vast and dark I could not see its walls at all. A single small circle of faint light pooled on the floor, and everything beyond that circle was swallowed in absolute black. I tilted my head up, and far above, I spotted an oval aperture cut into the domed roof, the seams between blocks of polished black marble glinting faintly around its edge. The pool of light fell on the same black marble floor, perfectly fitted, and the entire elliptical wall of the space was hewn from the same stone, absorbing every scrap of light that touched it. I thought for a split second I caught a flicker of thin red lines crawling across the shadowed stone, but when I blinked to look closer, they were gone.

Without warning, a radiant form bloomed in the center of the darkness, casting soft gold light across every shadowed corner. She wore a robe of soft flowing white, and her hair spilled down her back in a cataract of jet black, as dark as the marble floor under her feet. Her eyes were a luminous, endless black, her arms and bare feet the color of warm polished ivory. She greeted me with the guileless, sweet smile of a girl barely just stepped over the threshold of womanhood.
A cold twist of dread knotted in my chest. This was nothing like the sharp, cruel woman who had struck me, scorned me, and left me bleeding by the banks of the hot stream. I stood frozen in the dark, staring at her, and she stared back into the shadows as if searching for my face. For a heartbeat she vanished entirely, and I thought she would refuse to acknowledge me at all—then her eyes blazed out of the dark directly into mine. She had spotted me, and crossed the room to meet me.
“You have found me at last,” she said, laying a light hand on my shoulder. “I knew you would.”
My whole frame quivered with warring feelings I had no name for: I was drawn to her utterly, and repelled in equal measure, neither sensation able to win out.
“You are shivering,” she said softly. “This cold stone room is no place for you. Come.”
I could not find words to speak; her beauty had struck me dumb, and her gentle sweetness held me frozen. She took my hand, pulled me into the pool of light, and I saw her face clearly for the first time.
“You have grown brown and sunburnt since last I saw you,” she said.
“I have barely been under a proper roof at all since you left me,” I replied.
“Whose was the roof you did stay under?” she asked, her tone light.
“I do not know the woman’s name. The one who tended me by the hot stream.”
She laughed, soft and low. “I would very much like to meet her. The instinct of hospitality runs very thin in my people.”
She kept hold of my hand, led me dozens of steps back through the dark to a thick curtain of black velvet, and drew it aside to reveal a broad flight of white marble stairs. She led me up them to a bright, sunlit chamber hung with silken tapestries.
“You must miss that hot flowing river terribly,” she said. “But there is a bath sunk in the marble floor in the corner, and there are no white leeches in its water. You will find a clean new wool garment waiting at the foot of your couch. When you are done, come down, and I will be waiting for you in the room to your left at the stair foot.”

I stood still long after she left, my chest tight with confused doubt. How could I possibly treat this sweet, sisterly woman as a thing of evil, when she had welcomed me so gently, fed me, offered me rest? When last we met she had struck me hard enough to make me bleed, and now she greeted me almost like a long-lost lover. She had reviled me, spat cruel words at me, and now she said she had known all along I would track her down. Did she know the doubts that gnawed at me? Could she possibly explain everything? Could I even bring myself to disbelieve her, if she did? The water in the great white bath was perfectly clear, sparkling like cut crystal, glowing so bright it looked almost celestial. I had not seen fresh clean water since I left my home, save for the hot stream, a few cups of water at the veiled woman’s cottage, and the muddy pools left behind the limping wounded leopardess. I plunged in.
The second the water closed over my skin, a strange, faint, delicate scent flooded my nostrils, something that made my skin prickle in a way I did not like. My old doubt flooded back, sharp and unyielding. Had she laced the bath water with some strange drug? Had she enchanted it? Why was there such abundant clean water here in the palace, when not a single drop could be found in the dead city stretching for miles outside its walls? I thought of the leopardess’ crushed, bleeding paw, and scrambled out of the bath before I could immerse myself further. I dried off quickly, pulled on the soft white wool robe embroidered along the hem and neckline, and headed down the stairs.

The room she had guided me to was perfectly round, hewn entirely from solid alabaster, with no windows at all. Light seeped through every inch of the walls, a soft pearly shimmer instead of harsh bright shine, and vague shadowy forms drifted across the dome and walls like loose rain clouds skittering over a pale grey-blue sky. The princess waited for me, wearing a stiffer robe stitched tight with argent rings, discs, rectangles and lozenges that made it look like a light suit of silver mail. It fell straight from her neck to hide her feet, its long open sleeves leaving her pale arms bare. On the ivory table at its center sat cakes, ripe golden fruit, a jug of frothy milk, a crystal carafe of pale rose wine, and a crusty white loaf.
“We do not kill to feed ourselves here,” she said. “But I think you will enjoy what I have to offer.”
I told her I could not have asked for anything finer. She settled on a velvet couch beside the table, and gestured for me to sit next to her. She poured me a bowl of milk, handed me the loaf, and bade me break off as large a piece as I liked, then filled two exquisitely carved silver goblets with the pale rose wine. “You have never tasted wine like this,” she promised.
I drank, and marveled: every wild flower from the slopes of Hybla and Hymettus must have lent their ghost to the honeyed, sun-warmed taste in my mouth.
“Now that you are rested and fed,” she went on, her voice soft and serious, “I will do my best to make myself clear to you. Our natures are so very different, it will not be easy. Your kind live to die: you ripen for a short while, then fade, and old age is a terror you all dread. My kind—there are very few of us—we live to keep living. Old age is our greatest desire, for the longer we exist, the closer we draw to perfect, endless ripening. I am not even fully ripe yet, and I have lived thousands of your mortal years. I never bothered to count exactly. Eternity is not something you can measure in numbers.
“So many suitors have sought me over the ages, and I have never loved a single one of them. They only wanted to own me, to enslave me, to hoard me like the rare gems my people hoard in their vaults. But when you found me by the stream, I found a real man. I tested you, and you withstood it. Your love was genuine—but it was not ideal, not the kind of love I crave. Pity is not love. What woman in any world would return true love for pity? Your love for me then was full of pity, and it was hateful to me. I knew if I showed you my gentle self, you would fall for me like all the rest, just to possess me, and I would not have that. I wanted a love that outlasts hopelessness, that outmeasures indifference, scorn, even hate. That is why I wore cruelty and ingratitude like a mask, why I left you there in the woods. I wanted you to follow me not out of pity, not out of any debt you thought you owed me, but for the sake of finding me, content with nothing but my presence, even my coldness. I failed the contest first. I yield to you. You found me.”
She looked at me, eyes soft with tears, and hid her face in her hands. But I had caught a sharp, glinting flash of calculation behind that tenderness, and I did not believe a single word of it. She had laid every trap to fascinate and enslave me, and she had nearly succeeded.
“Beautiful princess,” I said, “tell me how you came to be injured and helpless by that hot stream.”
“There are things I cannot explain to you yet,” she replied, “not until your love grows perfect enough to understand them. But I will answer any question you can ask. I set out to visit a far edge of my dominions, home to a savage dwarf people, strong and fierce, enemies to all law, all order, all progress. I did not fear them, I went alone, and I never thought to take any precautions. I did not know that the veiled woman who tended you by the stream, a mortal far weaker than I, had cast what you call a spell over its waters—no magic, just a natural force that operates in a realm mortals cannot perceive. I bounded across the stream, and the second my feet left the far bank, an indescribable cold flooded through every bone in my body. I dragged myself through the trees, unaware of anything else until I found you asleep, that horrible white leech fastened to your neck. I pulled it off you, and sucked the poison out of your wound. When you started to wake, I hid myself in the leaves.”
She stood, her eyes blazing brighter than any human eyes could ever blaze, and lifted her arms high over her head. “Everything you saved from death that day is yours,” she cried. “I will repay you as no woman in any world ever has. My power, my immortality, my beauty, all of it is yours.” She dropped to her knees beside me, rested her arms across my knees, and looked up at my face. For the first time I noticed a thick, clumsy leather glove covering her entire left hand. Behind it, I could see in my mind’s eye sharp claws and coarse dark fur, not soft human skin. Her other hand was perfect, as lovely as any hand could be, and I felt that if I let even a fraction of my guard drop, I would love her completely. I turned my eyes away before the temptation could take root.
A deafening, bone-shaking roar split the air. The alabaster walls trembled so hard I thought they would shatter into a thousand shards. The princess shuddered visibly.
“The wine was too strong for you,” she said, her voice trembling. “I should not have let you drink a full goblet. Go rest now. When you wake, you may ask me anything you like. I will walk you back to your room.”
She led me up the stairs ahead of her. “I do not wonder that roar startled you,” she said, with a forced little laugh. “It startled me, too, for a second. I thought for an instant the white leopardess had gotten loose. But that is impossible.”
I knew the roar had not come from her caged leopardess. It had been a warning, directed straight at me. As she left me at my chamber door with a bright, false smile, I caught the tight, anxious line around her mouth. She was lying.

I collapsed onto the soft bed, my mind reeling over every detail of the tale the princess had just shared. She had slipped, unguarded for a single reckless second, and that one careless line had solved the riddle of how I’d found her half-dead in the forest: the leopardess had bounded clear of the bank, the princess had crumpled into the mud, and the running stream had melted her self-cast glamour away. Her own admission of what she’d come to this realm to hunt laid bare the terrible peril the Little Ones were in at that very moment. I had saved the life of their single most feared, ravenous enemy.
I had barely landed on that horrifying conclusion before sleep dragged me under. The heady, sweet wine she’d served me was far from harmless, it seemed.
I woke to nightfall, a softly glowing lamp strung from the ceiling gilding every edge of the chamber in hazy, warm light. A thick, delicious languor wrapped around my bones, like I was drifting far out to sea on the bosom of a twilight ocean. Just existing felt like a gift. No ache, no weariness. I thought I must be dying, peaceful as I was—until a searing, sickening stab lanced straight through my heart. Then another, sharper still, so hot it made my vision swim. I couldn’t lift a hand to press to my chest, some invisible weight pinning my limbs fast. The pain ebbed, but my whole body lay paralyzed, my will screaming to move and not a single muscle obeying. Some hateful, hungry thing was feeding on my vitality, inches away.
I stopped fighting, lay still, and then felt a soft palm press down on my face, shoving my head deep into the pillow. The weight vanished from my chest. I blinked my eyes open.
The princess was standing on the bed right over me, staring off into the empty room like she was trapped in a waking dream. Her great dark eyes were clear, calm, her mouth curved in a line of sated hunger. She wiped a thin streak of red from her lips. She caught me staring, bent fast, and swiped the linen handkerchief in her hand across my eyes—its edge dragged like a cold knife blade, and I went blind for a few long, breathless seconds.
I heard the dull soft thud of a massive padded paw landing after a small jump. My sight cleared just in time to see the long, spotted tail of the great leopardess flick out of sight through the half-open door. I bolted after her. The creature was gone without a trace. I tore down the stairs and into the alabaster hall, where the high moon bleached the pale stone until the whole space glowed like the hollow interior of a moon rock. The princess was not there.
I had to find her. Only when she was in my line of sight could I defend myself. Left unguarded, asleep, I was nothing but tame prey for her to feed on, a human fountain for her ravenous, unquenchable thirst. She’d been kind to me only to make me easier to devour. I didn’t fear her while my eyes were open, but sooner or later I would blink, I would fall asleep, and she would creep in. She was everywhere I could not see, waiting in the dark gaps of every room, every secret hollow of sleep.
Outside the alabaster hall, the dark was absolute. I groped my way forward with hands and feet, until my fingers brushed a heavy velvet curtain. I shoved it aside and stepped into the Black Hall. A silent, massive assembly milled inside. I had no way to tell how I could see them: walls, floor, and ceiling were swallowed in a blackness deeper than the darkest moonless, starless midnight, but I could vaguely make out dozens of shifting shapes weaving through the void. My eyes felt fogged, no matter how hard I rubbed and stared, nothing snapped into focus. Form tangled with shadow, silence hummed with undefined motion, and the whole wide space throbbed with a dim, confused dance of creatures I recognized from past encounters. Now a woman with blazing golden eyes peered out from a grinning skull, now a armored rider trotted past on a skeleton horse, now one of the hideous burrowing tunnel phantoms slithered past my boots. No order, no rhythm, just shifting eddies of half-solid shapes and free-floating shadows that moved of their own unmoored will.
I searched every face for the princess, but she was nowhere to be found. Not a single being in the crowd paid me any mind as I wandered through the horde. At last I gave up, groped my way along the dark wall until my fingers found the curtained doorway leading to the vestibule. Moonlight spilled dimly over the space, and the leopardess’s cage was the stage of a silent, thrashing brawl: two forms, half human half beast, tangled so tight their limbs merged, writhing and wrestling in a vicious locked embrace.
Blink, and the leopardess was already outside the cage, padding calmly toward the open front door. I threw a glance over my shoulder as I ran after her, and there she was, still crouched motionless in the cage, exactly as I’d first found her. The moon hung round and high in the sky, the bodiless shadow I’d spotted the night before gliding through the trees toward the outer gate, and the real spotted leopardess followed close behind, swinging her tail slow.
I trailed them a short distance back, silent as the dark, and neither of the two glanced over their shoulders once. We passed through the open gate and down into the sleeping city, every stone bathed in pale moonlight that hummed with quiet, waiting expectation. The shadow headed straight for the staircase where I’d woken the night before, climbed it without pausing, the leopardess at his heels. I sprinted after them, and a shriek of raw, unutterable horror split the stillness. A second later, a heavy soft weight thudded to the pavement right at my feet: the crushed, blackened, unrecognizable but familiar body of the woman who had first led me home, then locked me out in the dark.
I stood frozen, and the spotted leopardess came bounding down the stairs, a tiny baby clamped gentle but firm in her jaws. I lunged to grab her before she could twist away at the stair foot, but in that exact instant, a streak of glowing silver shot across the moonlit pavement—my old friend the white leopardess, slamming full force into the spotted beast’s throat. The baby slipped from the leopardess’s jaws, I caught it mid-fall, and stood back to watch the battle unfold.
It was a wild, breathtaking sight: first one cat on top, then the other, both too absorbed to make any noise louder than a low rumbling growl, a sharp snarl of hate, a whimper of pain. Claws scrabbled madly for purchase on the smooth stone as they bit and dragged and heaved, fighting for every inch of ground. The spotted leopardess was larger, heavier, and I tensed to step in to aid my white-furred friend—until I saw that for all her speed and strength, the spotted beast had nothing on the white leopardess’s iron endurance. Not once did she loosen her death grip on the other’s throat. At last a howl of agony tore out of the spotted beast, rising in a ragged, mounting crescendo that shifted from feline snarl to a woman’s raw, screaming peak of unendurable pain.
The white leopardess relaxed her jaws. The spotted one staggered back, and rose upright on her hind legs, right in the silver moonlight. The dark spots that covered her pelt peeled off like swarms of fleeing moths, swirling across her pale skin, rushing for refuge in her dark eyes until they merged and vanished. Only a handful of straggling spots got tangled in her streaming dark hair, leaving her glowing bright as the moon after a horde of small wind-tossed clouds have cleared off its disc—save for a thin thread of blood trickling down the pale column of her throat from the white leopardess’s terrible bite wounds.
She turned away, took a few staggering steps with the wild, unearthly gait of Hecate the huntress, then the spots burst back over her skin, and she shifted at once back into leopard form, galloping off into the dark at a full stretch. The white leopardess turned to me next, sprang up to pry my arms gently open to catch the baby I nearly dropped in my shock, and flew off with the little one clutched safe in her jaws, racing for the outer gate.

I turned and followed the spotted leopardess, catching only one flash of her tawny, rosette-marked fur before she tore up the brow of the hill and vanished through the palace gate. When I stumbled into the entrance hall, the princess was just snatching her discarded robe off the stone floor, wrapping it tight around her shoulders. The blood from the wounds on her neck had clotted dark, crusted fast by the wind of her headlong flight. She spotted me first: a sharp flare of unmasked anger flickered across her face, and she snapped her head aside like she could not bear to look at me. Then she forced a wobbly, performative smile back onto her lips and turned to face me.
“I met with a trivial little accident,” she purred, smooth as spun honey. “I heard that cat-woman had snuck back into the city, so I went down to drive her off. But she brought one of her horrid abominations with her — it sprang straight for me, sank its claws in my neck before I could strike a single blow!” She shivered so hard her whole frame trembled, and I could not help pitying her, even though I knew every word was a lie. Her wounds were real, though, and her haggard, hunted expression reminded me exactly of how she had looked trapped in that dark cave days before. Guilt twisted my chest that I had let her fight that battle unaided, and I must have worn my compassion plain on my face.
“Child of folly!” she said, with that same wobbly false smile. “You’re not about to cry, are you? Wait for me right here. I’m stepping into the Black Hall for a moment, and you can fetch me something to dress these scratches.” But I stuck close on her heels. I did not dare let her vanish out of my sight.
The instant we crossed the Black Hall threshold, a low thrumming buzz hit my ears, as of hundreds of overlapping muffled voices. Slow wandering light seeped across the vast dark, roving from group to group, illuminating them one after another: cluster after cluster would glow bright for a handful of heartbeats, then sink back into the hazy gloom as the light drifted onward. So many of the scenes that flared to life were familiar to me — moments I had lived through, or watched unfold. And the princess was present in every single one of them, even though she stood right at my side, motionless, her head bowed to her chest. The skull-faced dancers stamped through the grass of the forest hall, and there was the princess peering in through the doorframe at them. The vicious brawl raged deep in the Evil Wood, and there was the princess, yelling to egg the fighters on.
The murmur rolled on, shapes and colours shifting like watercolour bleeding into paper, until the roving light finally settled on that sunken hollow out on the heath. There was the princess, pacing back and forth, snarling as she tried in vain to wrap the clinging mist around her own body. That was when I froze, stunned: the old librarian stepped up to her, stood staring at her for a long beat, and then she crumpled. Her limbs peeled away from her, her whole ghostly eidolon dissolved into thin air. A wild, ringing shriek echoed off the high vaulted ceiling, and the moment her double collapsed, the real princess standing like a statue at my side fell heavily to the stone floor, and went utterly still.
I turned on my heel immediately and walked out. Not again. I would never waste another breath trying to restore her.
I was still shaking so hard my teeth rattled as I stood beside the leopardess’s cage, and it hit me all at once: that shifting, buzzing dark in the Black Hall? We had been standing inside the princess’s own mind. I watched the spotted tip of the leopardess’s tail twitch once. I was still trying to calm my frayed nerves when the princess’s voice rang out right beside my ear.
“Come now,” she said, soft and sweet like nothing at all had happened. “I will show you exactly what I need you to get me for my scratches.” She led me out into the courtyard, and I followed in dazed, unresisting compliance. The moon hung near the zenith, and its silver glow shone brighter than the gold of any absent midday sun I had ever known. She led me through the orchard of trees, straight to the tallest one at the very centre of the grove. It was nothing like the rest: all its topmost branches curved inwards, knitting together to make a tight rounded crown that looked, from where we stood below, exactly like a giant fir cone. The princess stopped right under it, tilted her head back to stare up at its crown, half talking to herself.
“Right at the summit of that tree grows one tiny blossom. One touch of it would heal all my scratches instantly. I could turn myself into a dove for a moment to fetch it, but there’s a little venomous snake coiled in the leaves up there — its bite would be worse to a dove than a tiger’s bite would be to me! How I hate that cat-woman!” She spun around to face me, one of her softest, most persuasive smiles tugging at her lips. “Can you climb?” The smile vanished as fast as it had come, and her face crumpled into a look of raw, aching suffering. I ought to have left her there to suffer, but the way she pressed her wounded hand to her neck went straight to my heart.
I studied the tree: all up its thick trunk, there were rough knobby protrusions, leftover stumps of shed palm fronds, perfect hand and footholds. “I can climb that tree,” I answered.
“Not with bare feet!” she protested. I had run after the vanishing leopardess so fast I had left my sandals back in my chambers.
“It is no matter,” I said. “I have long gone barefoot.” I glanced up the trunk again, my gaze wandering all the way up until it vanished into the shadowed crown of leaves. The moon spilled silver across the rough bark in patches like river froth, and a soft gust of wind slipped through the top of the tree, making a sound as gentle as water falling into still water. I stepped forward to begin my ascent, and the princess grabbed my arm to stop me.
“I cannot let you attempt it with your feet bare!” she insisted. “A fall from the top would kill you!”
“So would a bite from the snake!” I laughed — and I will confess, I did not believe there was any snake up there at all.
“It would not hurt you, not the way it would hurt me,” she replied. “Wait a moment.” She tore the two wide embroidered silk borders off the front of her robe, dropped to one knee, and wrapped one strip around my left foot, the other around my right, tying them tight.
“You have left the ends hanging, princess!” I pointed out.
“I have nothing sharp to cut them off with,” she said. “But they are not long enough to get entangled.”
I turned back to the tree, and began to climb. The cold after sundown in Bulika was never as biting as the chill around the sexton’s cottage, but the higher I climbed, the colder I grew. I shivered hard, until I could no longer feel my hands or my feet. There was barely any wind, the branches did not sway at all, but as I drew near the summit, every single branch I put my foot on or grabbed hold of felt like it was about to snap the second I put pressure on it. When my head poked above the topmost layers of leaves, out into the bright open moonlight, to start searching for the tiny healing blossom, I was suddenly drenched from head to foot. The next instant, I was no longer in a tree at all. I was thrashing in storm-tossed water, heaved wildly about, sinking fast. Tossed up and down, side to side, rolled over and over, dragged deeper every time I caught a gasp of air, I was choking and gurgling, lungs burning, until I slammed down hard on solid ground.
“I told you so!” croaked a familiar cackling voice right in my ear.

I scrubbed stinging fountain water from my lids, and there he was: the raven, perched light as a living shadow on the chiselled lip of the massive stone basin I lay half-submerged in. Dawn spilled cold pale silver over his glossy iridescent plumage, and he stood perfectly still, head tilted, staring down at me where I lay propped on my elbows, my face just clear of the rippling surface. I recognized the place at once: this was the great central fountain my father had built decades before, set dead in the heart of the front lawn. High above my head, the thick, gunmetal-sheen stalk of its central jet hurled a roiling column of water a hundred feet skyward, bursting at the peak into a wide frothy blossom that drifted down in fine misty spray.

I was still smarting from his earlier rebuke. “You never told me any of this!” I snapped at him.
“I told you never to do a single thing anyone you distrusted asked you to,” he returned, his voice low and croaky, sharp as a shard of flint.
“Tch! No mortal man could be expected to hold that at the front of his mind every waking moment,” I protested.
“You will not be so quick to forget the cost of forgetting it, I promise you,” replied Mr. Raven, leaning over the basin edge to stretch out a dark clawed hand. I took it, and in a single weightless blink I was standing beside him on the grass, my clothes dripping cold water onto the blades, streaming small puddles at my feet.
“Change your clothes at once,” he commanded, shifting back to his raven form in a ripple of shadow. “Where you come from, a dousing might not matter—but even there this kind of misstep is rare, and here it brings no end of inconveniences.” He stalked toward the open front door of the house, every step stately and deliberate, like a royal procession on scaled feet.
“I don’t have much to change into,” I laughed, remembering I’d stripped off my outer robe entirely to climb the gnarled old tree the night before.
“Feels like centuries since I last moulted a single feather,” the raven rumbled over his shoulder as he crossed the threshold.

The house was wrapped in total quiet, no sound of stirring occupants. I trundled up to my old familiar room, fished out a soft wool dressing gown, and made my way back down to the library. I had barely stepped over the threshold when the librarian stepped out from the back closet, and I threw myself onto the velvet couch near the cold hearth. Mr. Raven dragged an oak chair to sit right at my side, and for two long still minutes neither of us spoke.

I broke the hush first. “What in the name of all that is unearthly does any of this mean?”
“Fair question,” he said, nodding his ebony head. “Nobody on any plane of existence knows what a thing *is*—a man can only ever learn what a thing *means*. And whether he learns that or not depends entirely on what he chooses to do with it.”
“I haven’t done much of anything with anything, yet,” I said.
“Not much, no—but you know that fact, and that is worth more than a lifetime of empty busywork. Most people run through their whole lives without ever figuring out that they’ve learned nothing, and done even less. At the very least, you’ve always wanted to be useful.”
“I did want to help the children—the precious Little Ones, that is,” I amended quickly.
“I know you did. But you set off on the wrong path entirely.”
“I didn’t know what the right path even was!”
“That is true enough—but you are still at fault for not finding it.”
“I’ll believe whatever you tell me, as soon as I can wrap my head around what it means.”
“If you had accepted our invitation when we first extended it, you would have known the right way long ago. When a man refuses to do the work right in front of him where he stands, he will wander half the universe looking for it, and never find it.”
“I certainly have wandered far enough, and got nowhere. I left the Little Ones so I could learn how to serve them better, and all I came back with was the knowledge of just how much danger they are in.”
“When you were with them, you were already exactly where you could help them. You left your work to go looking for it. It takes a very wise man to know when he ought to leave; even a fool can learn to turn back immediately, if he chooses.”
“Do you mean to tell me I could have done something to help the Little Ones, just by staying there with them?”
“Could you have taught them a single thing by running away from them?”
“No—but how was I supposed to teach them? I had no idea where to even start. Besides, they were far, far ahead of me in every way that matters!”
“That is no lie. But you were never meant to be a measuring rod to judge their worth. If they knew even a fraction of what you know, let alone what you could have learned, they would be leagues ahead of you, out of your line of sight entirely. But you saw it yourself, didn’t you? They weren’t growing. They grew so slowly they’d never even conceived of the idea that they *should* grow—they were even afraid of it. You never saw children that clung to childhood like it was a cage they refused to step out of, back in your old world, did you?”
“Wait a minute—surely I had no power to force them to grow?”
“You could have cleared away most of the things that were stopping them from growing, in the first place.”
“What were those barriers? I never figured them out. I thought, once, that maybe they just didn’t have enough water.”
“Of course it is! They don’t even have enough to cry with, poor things.”
“I would have done anything to keep them from ever needing to cry, to need that water for tears!”
“No doubt you would—that’s the exact folly of every well-meaning stupid philanthropist who ever lived. Why, Mr. Vane, if there was no weeping in your old world, it never would have been worth saving at all! You already guessed that water was what they needed. Why didn’t you dig them a well? Two wells, even?”
“It never crossed my mind!”
“Not even when you could hear the rumble of underground rivers, thrumming just under the earth at your feet?”
“I think it did, once. But I was so terrified of the giants, for their sakes. That’s why I put up with so much cruelty from those brutes myself.”
“You almost taught those noble little souls to be afraid of those stupid, greedy Bags. While they fed you, comforted you, worshipped you like some kind of hero, you spent all your time submitting to those bestial men, acting as their slave. You gave those darlings a hero that looked like a coward. You could hardly have done them a worse wrong. They gave you their whole hearts—you owed them your whole soul, in return. You could have turned all those Bags into hewers of wood and drawers of water for the Little Ones by now, if you’d stood your ground.”
“I fear every word you say is true, Mr. Raven. But I swear I served the giants out of love for the Little Ones, not out of cowardice. I was afraid more knowledge would hurt them, make them less innocent, less soft and beautiful.”
“They never gave you any reason to fear that, not for a single second.”
“Isn’t a little knowledge a dangerous thing, though?”
“That is one of your old world’s most beloved, persistent lies. Is all the greatest knowledge any man can ever hold nothing more than a little? And is it dangerous, for that? That silly fantasy that knowledge is some grand, dangerous power in itself makes even the tiniest bit of it far more perilous than a whole lifetime of ignorance. To know every single thing in the universe would not make you a great man.”
“At the very least, I acted out of love for them, not cowardice, when I served the giants.”
“Granted. But you ought to have served the Little Ones, not the giants. You ought to have given them that water they so badly needed. They would have worked out very quickly how to put the giants in their proper place, after that. You could have made those brutes cut down two thirds of their coarse, bitter wild fruit trees right then, to make space for the delicate little fruit trees the Little Ones tended. You lost your chance with the Lovers, Mr. Vane. You spent all your time speculating about them, instead of getting your hands dirty and helping them.”

I sat hunched, heavy with cold, crawling shame. Every word Mr. Raven had spoken was true: I had been no wise guardian, no kind neighbour to the gentle Little Ones.

The raven-faced librarian leaned forward, his voice sharp but not unkind. “You did the very creatures you sought to spare a deeper wrong. Slavery would have been progress for them—firm, gentle lessons, meted out with a switch hewn from their own fruit trees, would have taught them courage they never could have learned left unprotected. It would have been priceless.”

“I did not know they were all cowards!” I burst out.

“What difference does their cowardice make? Any man who builds his choices around exploiting another’s fear is a coward in his own core.” His brows drew tight, shadowed over old, deep worry. “I fear worse harm will come of your choices. At this rate, the Little Ones might have grown strong enough to fend off the princess on their own, never mind the giants—they laughed at the giants, for heaven’s sake! But now, through the tangled mess you wove with her—”

“I hate her!” I cried, the words tearing out of me.

“Did you ever let her know that?”

Silence swallowed me whole. I could not answer.

“Not even to her were you honest, unflinching. But hush—we were followed from the fountain, I am certain.”

“I saw no living thing,” I frowned, “except a scruffy, disreputable cat that bolted straight into the shrubbery when it spotted us.”

“It was a magnificent Persian—so soaked, draggled, and foul matted that it looked as wretched as it truly is, far worse than disreputable. There was a beautiful blue Persian that haunted my house long ago, that would flee screaming at the very sound of running water. Could it have been creeping after the goldfish in the fountain pool?”

“We shall find out,” the librarian said, rising to his feet. “I know cats of every breed that walks all the worlds, and there is a book in that closet that will unmask this creature once and for all, if I am not mistaken.”

He crossed to the closet door, pulled out the half-mutilated volume I had seen broken apart months before, and set it down on the table between us. I gaped: it was whole, entire, the vellum cover smooth, every page intact.

“Where was the other half?” I gasped.

“Sticking through the thin membrane of this world into my library, on the other side.”

I held my tongue. One more question would plunge me into a bottomless sea of unknowing, and I could tell there was no time to wander.

“Listen,” he said, opening the cover. “I am going to read a few stanzas. There is someone hidden in this room, I think, who will not enjoy a single line of what follows.”

He turned the aged parchment pages, stained dark with time, one sheet blotched two-thirds over with a dark, old fluid. He paused, scanning the pages for the right passage, and when he began to read, the language was one I had never heard before—yet every word sank into my skull, clear as water, impossible to write down in my own tongue, only capable of being approximated in the fragments that clung to my memory later:
> *But if I found a man that could believe
> In what he saw not, felt not, and yet knew,
> From him I should take substance, and receive
> Firmness and form relate to touch and view;
> Then should I clothe me in the likeness true
> Of that idea where his soul did cleave!*

He turned a leaf, and read again:
> *In me was every woman. I had power
> Over the soul of every living man,
> Such as no woman ever had in dower—
> Could what no woman ever could, or can;
> All women, I, the woman, still outran,
> Outsoared, outsank, outreigned, in hall or bower.*
> *For I, though me he neither saw nor heard,
> Nor with his hand could touch finger of mine,
> Although not once my breath had ever stirred
> A hair of him, could trammel brain and spine
> With rooted bonds which Death could not untwine—
> Or life, though hope were evermore deferred.*

He paused, flipped another page, and began once more:
> *For by his side I lay, a bodiless thing;
> I breathed not, saw not, felt not, only thought,
> And made him love me—with a hungering
> After he knew not what—if it was aught
> Or but a nameless something that was wrought
> By him out of himself; for I did sing
> A song that had no sound into his soul;
> I lay a heartless thing against his heart,
> Giving him nothing where he gave his whole
> Being to clothe me human, every part:
> That I at last into his sense might dart,
> Thus first into his living mind I stole.
> Ah, who was ever conquering Love but I!
> Who else did ever throne in heart of man!
> To visible being, with a gladsome cry
> Waking, life’s tremor through me throbbing ran!*

A sharp, repulsive feline wail tore through the still air of the room. I jolted upright, staring all around me, but no shape met my eyes. Mr. Raven flipped several more pages, and his voice went on, unshaken:
> *Sudden I woke, nor knew the ghastly fear
> That held me—not like serpent coiled about,
> But like a vapour moist, corrupt, and drear,
> Filling heart, soul, and breast and brain throughout;
> My being lay motionless in sickening doubt,
> Nor dared to ask how came the horror here.
> My past entire I knew, but not my now;
> I understood nor what I was, nor where;
> I knew what I had been: still on my brow
> I felt the touch of what no more was there!
> I was a fainting, dead, yet live Despair;
> A life that flouted life with mop and mow!
> That I was a queen I knew right well,
> And sometimes wore a splendour on my head
> Whose flashing even dead darkness could not quell—
> The like on neck and arms and girdle-stead;
> And men declared a light my closed eyes shed
> That killed the diamond in its silver cell.*

Again that ugly cry of feline pain sliced through the quiet. I scanned every corner, saw no movement, no form. Mr. Raven listened for a heartbeat, then turned more pages and resumed:
> *Hideously wet, my hair of golden hue
> Fouled my fair hands: to have it swiftly shorn
> I had given my rubies, all for me dug new—
> No eyes had seen, and such no waist had worn!
> For a draught of water from a drinking horn,
> For one blue breath, I had given my sapphires blue!
> Nay, I had given my opals for a smock,
> A peasant-maiden’s garment, coarse and clean:
> My shroud was rotting! Once I heard a cock
> Lustily crow upon the hillock green
> Over my coffin. Dulled by space between,
> Came back an answer like a ghostly mock.*

That bestial, guttural wail burst out a third time.
“Thought I heard some foul skulking thing in the room,” the librarian said, glancing casually around, before he turned more pages and kept reading:
> *For I had bathed in milk and honey-dew,
> In rain from roses shook, that ne’er touched earth,
> And ointed me with nard of amber hue;
> Never had spot me spotted from my birth,
> Or mole, or scar of hurt, or fret of dearth;
> Never one hair superfluous on me grew.
> Fleeing cold whiteness, I would sit alone—
> Not in the sun—I feared his bronzing light,
> But in his radiance back around me thrown
> By fulgent mirrors tempering his might;
> Thus bathing in a moon-bath not too bright,
> My skin I tinted slow to ivory tone.
> But now, all round was dark, dark all within!
> My eyes not even gave out a phantom-flash;
> My fingers sank in pulp through pulpy skin;
> My body lay death-weltered in a mash
> Of slimy horrors—*

A fearsome, unearthly yell shattered the verse. A huge white cat burst from the shadows, its fur clumped and clammy, its tail thick as an anchor cable, its eyes flaring green as polished chrysoprase, its claws tangling in the carpet so it stumbled and floundered straight for the chimney to escape. Quick as a striking bird, the librarian flung the vellum manuscript down between her and the hearth. She froze instantly, crouched low, eyes fixed fast on the open book. His voice continued, steady, as if he still read from the pages, his own gaze fixed unblinking on the creature:
> *Ah, the two worlds! so strangely are they one,
> And yet so measurelessly wide apart!
> Oh, had I lived the bodiless alone
> And from defiling sense held safe my heart,
> Then had I scaped the canker and the smart,
> Scaped life-in-death, scaped misery’s endless moan!*

At those words, a prolonged, shrieked howl of pure agony burst from the cat, so loud we both clapped our hands over our ears to muffle it. When the noise died away, Mr. Raven walked to the fireplace, picked up the manuscript, and stood between the creature and the chimney, pointing one finger at her for a long quiet moment. She lay perfectly motionless, pinned. He took a half-burned stick from the hearth, drew a strange glowing sigil on the floor, tucked the manuscript back in its closet shelf with a small, grim nod that seemed to say *we have you now*, and returned to stand over the cat, his voice low, solemn, no trace of the bumbling old librarian left.

“Lilith, when you crept here on your way to work your evil, you never dreamed you would walk right into my hands. Mr. Vane, when God created me, he did not make me from nothing, as the foolish say—he made me from his own endless, uncreated glory. He gave me an angel of splendour to be my wife. There she lies.” He nodded at the motionless cat. “Her first thought was power. She called being one with me, bearing children for the God that gave us life, slavery. She bore one child, then, swollen with the lie that she had created that child, she demanded I fall down and worship it. When she found I would love and honour her always, but never kneel to her, she spilled her own blood to escape me, fled to the horde of the aliens, and ensnared the great Shadow’s heart so thoroughly he became her slave, did every bidding, and crowned her queen of Hell. She knows her own state now, and I know it too. That one child of hers she fears and hates, and would kill, claiming a false right over the life God sent through her into the new world. She knows no more of true creation than a crystal that grows to its set shape, or a worm that splits in two to make two worms. She is the vilest of God’s creatures, feeding on the blood, lives, and souls of men. She consumes and slays, but she has no more power to destroy than she does to create.”

The cat lay still, its beryl eyes flaming, fixed on his gaze so it could not look away.
“Then God gave me a second wife—not an angel, but a woman—who is to her what pure light is to utter darkness.”

The cat let out a horrible screech, and began to swell, growing larger and larger by the second. At last, a spotted leopardess reared up, and her roar shook the very foundations of the house. I jolted to my feet. Mr. Raven did not twitch a single eyelid.
“That is only her jealousy speaking,” he said, calm as ever. “Jealousy of her own making, fruitless, foiled. Now she is my subject, my creature to command, the same woman who once would not have me for her husband. My beautiful Eve still lives, hoping immortally. The daughter she hates lives too, hidden far past her reach, and one day she will be the very thing Lilith counts as her destruction—for even Lilith shall be saved by the child she bore. She exults that my human wife once dragged both of us into despair, and bore a whole race of the wretched and lost. But my Eve repented, and is now more beautiful than any woman or angel that ever was, and her groaning, travailing world is the nursery of our Father’s children. I repented, too, and I am blessed. But you, Lilith, you have not repented yet. But you must. Tell me: is the great Shadow beautiful? Do you know how long your own beauty will last? Answer me, if you know.”

In that moment I knew. Mr. Raven was Adam—the old man, the new man, the first father of us all. The wife who tended the house of the dead was Eve, mother of every living soul, the lady of the New Jerusalem.

The leopardess reared, her spots flickering and shifting, fading away one by one until the princess stood before us, radiant, in her perfect, human form.
“I am beautiful. And I am immortal,” she said, the look of a goddess in her eyes.
“Like a bush that burns, and is entirely consumed,” Adam answered. “What is that under your right hand? Your arm is crossed over your bosom, your hand pressed hard to your side.”

A sharp, swift pain twisted her beautiful face for a heartbeat, and vanished.
“It is just a leftover leopard spot,” she said. “It will fade away with all the rest.”
“You are beautiful because God made you, but you are the slave of your own sin. Take your hand away from your side.”

Her hand fell limply to her side, and she stared him down, fierce and quailing, no surrender in her gaze. He looked at the faint dark mark on her flesh. “That spot is not on the leopard. It is inside the woman. It will not leave you until it has eaten all the way to your heart, and all your beauty flows out of you through that open wound.”

She glanced down, and shivered from head to toe.
“Lilith,” Adam said, his voice softening to tender, pleading, “hear me. Repent, and he who made you will wash you clean.”

Her hand flew back to her side, shaking. Her face went dark as storm clouds. The cry that tore out of her was the cry of a soul whose last spark of hope was dying, curdling into a howl. She writhed on the floor, shifting back into a spotted leopardess.
“The evil you planned to do, you will never accomplish, Lilith. Good, not evil, is the core of the universe. The war between them may stretch across uncounted ages, but it will end. What will become of you when Time dissolves into the dawn of the eternal morning? Repent, I beg of you. Repent, and be an angel of God once more.”

She stood upright again, a woman once more, and her voice was cold, hard as flint. “I will never repent. I will drink the blood of your child.”

My eyes were fixed on her, but when Adam spoke I turned to him. He towered over her, the line of his face transfigured, his voice thunder, terrible to hear. “Down! Or by the power given me, I will melt your very bones to ash.”

She flung herself to the floor, dwindling, shrinking, until she was once more a scruffy gray cat. Adam caught her up by the scruff of her neck, carried her to the closet, and tossed her inside. He drew the glowing sigil he had marked on the floor across the threshold of the door, locked it tight, and came back to my side once more, the worn old librarian, wiping furtive tears from his eyes.

The librarian’s knuckles bleached white where he curled them around the edge of his pitted oak desk, fine dust motes spinning in the unearthly silver glow leaking through the high, unglassed library windows. “We must be on our guard,” he said, his voice low enough that it barely disturbed the hush of the surrounding shelves. “Or she will outwit us again. She would befool the very elect.”
“How are we even supposed to guard against her?” I asked, the cold prickle of unease creeping up the back of my neck.
“Every way there is,” he answered. “She fears her own child, and that fear curdled long ago to unending hate. She is in this house right now, only passing through to destroy her. To her, the birth of any child is the death of the parent that sired them, every new generation a natural enemy of the one that came before. Her daughter looks to her like an open, gushing channel, draining away the immortality she has convinced herself is her birthright, self-contained and unassailable. To plug that channel, she has hunted the girl since almost the hour she was born, with unrelenting, unforgiving malice.
“But every one of her schemes to kill her backfired. Instead of wiping the child out, the hunt left a whole thriving colony of the Little Ones settled in the realm she claims as her personal fiefdom, and that girl is their heart, their head, their sheltering wings. My Eve ached for that child for centuries, longed to care for her as a mother tends her firstborn, but we were not yet fit to raise her. So we carried her deep into the uncharted liminal wilderness, and for ages we knew nothing of her fate. But she was fostered by forces older than our worlds, young winged beings for her playmates, and she never knew a single moment of care until she found a wailing abandoned half-human infant tangled in the brambles, and the dormant mother-heart inside her woke wide open.
“One by one she has gathered dozens more of the lost children since, and that heart of hers still has room to grow. Her family is her whole world, no children ever were loved or protected better. Her authority over them is absolute, but she does not even know she holds it: it never shows itself except in quiet watchfulness and gentle service. She has forgotten entirely the life she lived before the children, and truly believes she walked out of the woods the first of their little family.”
He leaned forward, his gaze sharp. “You saved the life of the one that wants them all dead. That means your life now belongs to her and every last one of the Little Ones. Lilith the princess was already halfway to their colony when the barrier stream struck her down with its own vengeance, and she would have dissolved back into unmade shadow forever if you had not dragged her from the water. You did, and if not for the lingering curse of the stream, she would have been raging through their homes long before now.
“But she found a loophole. She barred herself from our shared three-dimensional world eons ago, the day she destroyed her old mortal body—she could never re-enter it, not unless she made direct physical contact with someone who still belonged to that physical plane. You gave her that exact chance. She never had it once, in all her thousands of years of life. The lightest possible touch of her hand brushed one of your muffled boots every single step you climbed that path out of the stream, anchoring herself to you. Right now, she is locked in that small back chamber, waiting for the exact second no one is watching to slip free.”
“She can’t know anything about that transdimensional door! She can’t possibly know how to open it!” I blurted, but my chest felt tight, and my words were far more confident than my thudding heart.
“Hush, hush!” the librarian whispered, one hand lifted to silence me, his eyes sharp. “She can hear through anything. There is no wall, no gap between realities that blocks her senses. You must leave right now, make your way straight to my wife’s cottage. I will stay here to keep guard over her.”
“Let me go straight to the Little Ones instead!” I cried.
“Beware that urge, Mr. Vane. Go to my wife, and do exactly what she tells you.”
His advice did not make sense to me. Why waste time running to his wife when I could rush to defend the children? Even then I only half-believed him, enough to ask questions but not enough to obey without pushing back. “First tell me, Mr. Raven—why on earth did you lock her up in that exact closet? The night I ran from your house, I stumbled straight into that very same nook!”
“That closet is no closer to our cottage, and no farther from it, than any other point in this entire library,” he said simply.
I shook my head, too stubborn to accept what I could not wrap my head around. “Then how is it that just earlier, you pulled a whole book from that door, when all I saw and felt was a single half-page of it sticking through? The other half, you said, existed somewhere on your library shelf. When you put the book back, won’t the other half stick through the door again? Does that mean two parts of the same book can exist at the same time in two separate places? Can one half be in space, somewhere, and the other half out of space, nowhere at all?”
“I wish I could explain it to you,” he answered, a note of gentle regret in his voice. “But your mind was not built to parse this kind of reality. This phenomenon is not just inexplicable to you—the very nature of it is something you cannot yet even perceive. I do not fully understand it myself, either. But you live every single day with things you do not, and can not, understand. You think you grasp gravity, or sunlight, or the way water flows, but your ‘understanding’ of those things is nothing more than being used to them, so you do not feel surprise when they happen. You accept them not because you get how they work, but because you have no choice—they are there, and they touch your life whether you like it or not.
“The truth is, no human being fully understands anything at all. The moment you realize you do not understand, that is your first tottering, unsteady step—not toward instant comprehension, but toward the day you will finally be capable of understanding. You are not used to these liminal rules, so you do not pretend to know them. No one can force this knowledge on you. But I can, perhaps, help you learn to believe.”
He walked to the closet door, and let out a low, soft whistle, then stood silent, listening. A heartbeat later, I heard or thought I heard the faint whir of wings, and looked up to see a pure white dove perch for a single instant on the top of the shelves above the old oil portrait, then glide down to land on Mr. Raven’s shoulder, tucking its head against his cheek. I watched the two of them move their heads gently in quiet conversation, but I heard not a single word. When I blinked, the dove was gone, and he was walking back to his seat.
“Why did you whistle?” I asked. “Sound from our world can’t possibly carry through that door, can it?”
“You are exactly right,” he said. “I whistled so that you would see I called her. It was not the sound itself that reached her, but the meaning behind the whistle. There is not a single minute left to waste. You must go.”
“I will go right now,” I said, turning for the library door.
“You will sleep tonight at our roadside hostelry,” he said, not as a question, but in a tone of quiet, unshakable authority.
“My heart is with the children,” I protested. “But if you absolutely insist—”
“I do insist. You can do nothing to help them otherwise. I will walk you as far as the mirror chamber, and see you off properly.”
He rose to his feet. A thunderous crash shook the closet door. The leopardess that was Lilith had flung her full weight against it. I looked back at him, my throat tight.
“Come on,” he urged.
Before we made it to the library exit, a howling, demon-like yell echoed after us, mixed with the rasp of sharp claws scoring deep gouges into the solid oak. I hesitated, half-turning back.
“To leave her there all alone, bleeding from that terrible wound—” I murmured.
“Nothing will ever close that wound,” he said, with a soft, heavy sigh. “It is not a cut in her flesh, but a gash through her soul. Annihilation itself is no death to evil. Only good, growing in the exact space evil once occupied, can kill evil for good. An evil thing will carry its own malice forever, until the day it chooses to stop being evil. That is the only thing that can slay it.”
I held my peace until a new, splintering crack of splitting wood reached us. “What if she breaks free?” I cried.
“Hurry,” he said. “I will rush back the second you are through the mirror, and re-arrange the mirrors to block her path after you.”
We ran, and burst breathless into the wooden mirror chamber. Mr. Raven grabbed the hanging chains, adjusted the dimming hood, then lined the three mirrors up in their correct, reality-warping alignment, stepping next to me in front of the largest standing mirror. Through the glass, the familiar mountain ranges were already clearing from the mist.
Between us, tearing the space open with a shriek no human throat could make, the huge spotted bulk of the leopardess Lilith burst. She leaped through the mirror as if it were no more than an open window, and settled instantly into a low, swift, ground-eating gallop straight toward the Little Ones’ hidden colony.
I stared in horrified dismay, and sprang through the mirror after her. Adam followed me at a casual, unhurried pace.
“You do not need to run,” he called. “You will never overtake her. She has had thousands of years to learn every shortcut through this world.” He turned in the opposite direction from her fleeing shape. “She knows more hidden tricks of reality than I care to count. We will do what we can, no more.”
“I am not going to let her hurt those children,” I said, breaking into a run, but I slowed a moment later, sick in my stomach as I watched her spotted shape dwindle to a dot on the horizon. I turned back to him.
“Doubtless we will do what we can,” he agreed. “But my wife already warned Mara, the white guardian, and she will do her part. You must sleep first. You gave me your word.”
“I will not break it,” I said. “But surely action comes before rest right now! We can not waste time sleeping while the children are in danger.”
“A man can do nothing that he is not fit to do,” he said, nodding toward the skyline. “Look. Did I not tell you Mara would do her part?”
I followed his gaze, and saw a bright white spot moving fast at a sharp diagonal, cutting straight across the path Lilith was taking.
“There she is!” he cried. “The spotted leopardess is fierce, but the white one is far stronger.”
“I saw them fight once before,” I said. “It did not look like a decisive win for the white one.”
“How could eyes that have never once woken fully to this world tell who won? The princess never once admits she was beaten—she never will. But she fled that fight.
