One day soon she will finally admit that kicking against the goad of her own unacknowledged goodness is useless, and that is the day her new dawn will finally begin. Come on, come. He who is not yet strong enough to act for the good of the children must hurry to sleep, so he will be ready when the time for action comes.”

I stood rooted, watching the last pale glimmer of the white leopardess’s pelt melt into the haze of the far heath, then turned to follow my guide—my steps heavy with sharp reluctance. What place had sleep for me now? Surely reason wove its same unbroken thread through every world that ever was, and what logic could there be in curling up to slumber beside the dead, when the hour was calling every living soul to rise? Worse, no one would wake me. How could I be sure I would rouse early? That I would rouse at all? The sleepers in that strange house let dawn bleed into noon, noon bleed to dusk, never once stirring in their shadowed beds. I muttered half-strangled protests under my breath, but followed all the same, for I knew no other path to take.

The librarian walked on in utter silence, and I matched his quiet stride. Time and unmarked space slipped past us uncounted. The sun sank below the ragged line of the horizon, dusk seeped over the moor, and I tasted on the rising wind the spreading, bone-deep cold of the death-chamber. My heart sank lower with every step. Soon I lost sight of his lean, long-coated frame, and at last could no longer hear the soft swish of his passage through the heather stems. But in their place came the slow, heavy flap of raven wings, and at intervals, a firefly, or a glowing ghost-butterfly, would rise and drift through the rayless dark. By and by the moon lifted, slow and heavy, crawling across the far rim of the sky.

“You are tired, are you not, Mr. Vane?” the raven said, alighting on a lichen-crusted gravestone. “You must make the acquaintance of the horse that will carry you where you need to go at dawn.” He let out a strange, piercing whistle through his long black beak. A dark, moving spot bloomed on the face of the half-risen moon. The drumming of swift, soft hoofbeats rolled toward me, and in the space of a few breaths, the terrible steed thundered straight out of the moon’s very disc. His mane streamed behind him like the crest of a wave fighting a gale, torn loose in hoary sprays of frosty white, and the flick of his tail kept blinding the moon’s silver eye every time it lashed out. He stood near nineteen hands, huge of bone, skin stretched taut as parchment over hard, corded muscle—a steed even holy Death himself might choose to mount when he rode abroad to reap. The moon seemed to gaze at him in awe; in her eerie, wavering light he looked a living skeleton, its great joints loosely strung together. For all his terrific size, he moved with the weightless lightness of a winged moth. As he drew near, his speed slackened, mane and tail drifting softly around his frame as they settled.

I had always loved horses, loved every one I ever laid eyes on. I had never spent a penny save on them, never sold a horse that had once been mine. The sight of this mighty, terrible beast woke in me a sharp, hungry longing to possess him: pure greed, rank covetousness, an evil thing no matter what world you walked. I do not mean I would have stolen him, but that, blind to whatever purpose he was made for, I would have paid any price to call him mine. I laid my hands on him, stroking the prominent bones that humped under a hide so thin, smooth, and shiny as satin—so shiny the whole round shape of the moon was reflected clear on its surface. I fondled his sharp, pointed ears, whispered soft words into them, and breathed the warm breath of a living man straight into his flaring red nostrils. He breathed back into mine the hot, wild breath of a horse’s life, and in that instant, we loved one another. What eyes he had! Filmy pale blue like the eyes of the newly dead, with a smoldering coal of fire glowing deep behind each pupil. The raven, wings half unfurled, looked on, pleased at the way I fussed over his magnificent mount.

“That is well, be friends with him,” he croaked. “He will carry you all the swifter tomorrow! Now we must hurry home.”

My longing to ride him swelled to a fever pitch. “May I not mount him at once, Mr. Raven?” I cried.

“By all means!” he answered. “Mount, and ride him home.”

The horse bent his great head over my shoulder, nuzzling me with soft affection. I twisted my hands in his streaming mane and scrambled up onto his back, hauling myself up with the aid of the jutting bones of his shoulders. “He could outrun any leopard that ever walked the earth!” I exclaimed.

“Not at night, not by this road,” the raven corrected me. “The path is full of pitfalls. But come: what looks like loss now will be gain later. To wait is harder than to run, and its reward is far fuller. Go on, my son, straight to the cottage. I shall be there as soon as you. It will make my wife’s heart glad to see her son riding that horse.”

I sat silent, the horse stock still under me like a block of polished marble. “Why do you linger?” the raven asked.

“I long so much to ride after the leopardess,” I answered, “that I can barely hold myself back.”

“You have given your word.”

“My debt to the Little Ones, I confess, feels far heavier than my bond to you.”

“Yield to that temptation, and you will bring ruin down on them—and on yourself as well.”

“What do I care for my own fate? I love them, and love does no harm. I am going.”

The truth was, I had forgotten the children entirely, infatuated as I was with the strength of the great beast beneath me. Eyes glowed through the dark, and I knew Adam stood beside me in his true, human shape. I could hear the suppressed indignation in his voice, barely held back from bursting out. “Mr. Vane,” he said, “do you never wonder why you have not yet done a single thing worth doing?”

“Because I have been a fool,” I answered.

“Foolish in what way?”

“Everything.”

“Which of your acts do you count the most unwise?”

“Bringing the princess back to life. I should have left her to her just fate.”

“Nay, now you speak foolishly. You could not have done otherwise, not knowing she was evil! But you never brought anyone back to life. How could you, when you are dead yourself?”

“Dead?” I cried, stunned.

“Yes,” he said. “And you will remain dead, so long as you refuse to die.”

“More of your old riddles,” I scoffed.

“Be persuaded, come home with me,” he went on, his tone softening. “The most—nearly the only foolish thing you ever did, was to run from our dead.”

I pressed my heels to the horse’s lean ribs, and he shot forward like a sudden gust of wind. I patted his neck, and he wheeled sharp around, low to the ground like a cat chasing a scurrying mouse, his mane sweeping the tops of the heather as he leaned into the turn. I heard the raven’s wings beat behind me, five quick flaps, and he perched right on the horse’s forehead. The great beast checked instantly, ploughing up clods of turf with his hooves.

“Mr. Vane,” the raven croaked, “think what you are doing! Twice before misfortune has befallen you—once from fear, once from heedlessness. To break your word is far worse: it is a sin.”

“The Little Ones are in terrible danger, and I am the one who brought that danger down on them!” I cried. “But I swear I will not break my word to you. I will come back, and spend as many nights, days, years in your house as you wish.”

“I tell you once more, you will do them far more harm than good if you ride out tonight,” he insisted. But that false, rootless sense of power, the strength I had siphoned off from the great horse under me, had turned me stupid, deaf to all warning.

“Would you steal my last chance to make amends?” I shouted. “This time I will not shirk my duty! I am going, if I perish for it!”

“Go then, foolish boy!” he spat, anger sharp in his croak. “Take the horse, ride to failure. May it at last teach you humility!” He spread his wings and vanished into the dark.

Again I pressed my heels to the horse’s ribs. “After the spotted leopardess,” I breathed in his ear. He turned his great head back and forth, snuffling the air, then started off at a slow, uncertain walk. All at once he quickened his pace, broke into a trot, then a gallop, and in moments his speed was shattering. He seemed to see perfectly in the dark; he never stumbled, never faltered, never hesitated. I sat on his back as if perched on the ridge of a racing wave. I could feel every individual muscle shift and flex under me, his joints so elastic every movement melted seamless into the next, not a single jolt to unseat me. His speed grew until he flew rather than ran, the wind tearing past us like a tornado. We shot across the evil hollow like a bolt fired from a crossbow. No monster lifted its neck from the mud below; every creature there knew the thunder of those hooves passing overhead. We rushed up steep hills, shot down their far slopes, never swerving even when we charged across the jagged chasms of the riverbed, holding that same terrible, fierce gallop. The moon, halfway up the sky, gazed down with solemn, troubled face. Drunk on the power of my steed and the rush of my own life, I sat like a crowned king as I rode.

We were nearly at the center of the river’s tangled many channels, the horse clearing one, sometimes two gaps with every stride, gathering himself now for a great bounding leap, when the moon reached the highest point of her arch. Then came a wonder, and a terror: she began to descend, rolling like the nave of Fortune’s wheel bowled by the hands of the gods, faster and faster. Just like the moon I had known in my old world, this one bore a human face, and now the broad forehead, now the sharp chin turned to the sky as she rolled over and over. I stared aghast. The howling of wolves rose from the ravines far below. An ugly, cold fear began to creep into the hollows of my heart, my confidence draining away. The horse held his breakneck speed, ears pricked forward, flaring nostrils drinking deep of the wind his own passage had stirred up. But the moon jolted down the slope of the sky like an old, cracked chariot wheel, heavy with awful omen. At last she rolled over the edge of the horizon and vanished, taking every scrap of her light with her.

The mighty steed was in the middle of clearing a wide, shallow river channel when we were caught in the total net of that unbroken darkness. His head dropped. The momentum of his leap carried his bulk across to the far bank, but he crumpled into a heap the second he landed, and lay still where he fell. I clambered off, knelt beside him, ran my hands over every inch of his great frame. Not a single bone was broken—but he was a horse no more. I sat down on his motionless body, buried my face in my hands, and the unforgiving dark closed all around me.

The night turned sharp enough to shatter bone. Frost crept through my tunic, icing my eyelashes, as the frozen corpse of the horse beneath me leached every last scrap of my warmth away. The wolves’ howls coiled closer, soft padded paws whispering over jagged rock, hot rank panting thickening the air until I could taste it. Out of the absolute dark blazed a constellation of smoldering amber eyes, drawing tight in a half-circle around me. My hour had come. I staggered to my feet—only to realize I did not even have a stick to defend myself.

They rushed, jaws slavering, eyes alight with ravenous fury, black throats gaping to tear me apart. I stood still, hopeless, waiting for the first tooth to sink in. For a heartbeat they hesitated, noses dipping to snuffle at the dead horse, then lunged for me.

A blur of movement hit their flank so fast it barely made a sound, and a swarm of glowing pale green eyes launched themselves at the wolves, a cry bursting from them that was feebler than the wolves’ snarls but far more feral. I knew that cry instantly: cats, led by the massive gray tom I had crossed paths with before. I could not make out a single furred shape of him in the dark, but those eyes were unmistakeable— I knew exactly how broad his paws were, how long his claws, how his silver fur glinted when the sun hit it.

A roar of a battle exploded around me, its violence communicated only through snarls, yowls, the crunch of bone and the snick of claw through hide. I would have run, certain that whichever side won I would end up their next meal— but what was the point? I would have tripped and cracked my skull on a stone before I had taken three steps, and both wolves and cats could see and scent me clear as day in the unbroken dark.

All at once the wolves’ howls cut off mid-snarl, and the cats’ caterwauling spiked wilder. Then came the soft scrabble of retreating paws: the cats had won.

Sharp teeth sank into the flesh of my calves before I could blink, and the horde of felines spilled over me like a living waterfall, biting every exposed patch of skin they could reach, raking their claws across my arms and ribs. Scores of them clung to my frame, too many to shake free. I fought back madly, every finger coiled to crush, tearing them from my skin, choking at their necks to no avail— when I tried to fling them away they clung to my hands like limpets. I trampled them underfoot, jammed my fingers into their eyes, even bit down on the furred bodies that pressed to my face, but I could not rid myself of a single one. They never stopped finding new patches of skin to bite, hauling at my flesh with their horribly curved, pincer-like claws, hissing and spitting in my face— until I threw myself to the ground in despair, and they abandoned my body to dart for my eyes. The second I staggered back to my feet, they fell back from my face to tear at my legs again.

I broke into a run, blind to every turn, no longer caring where my feet carried me. The cats flowed around me in a living torrent, rubbing against my calves, leaping up to nudge my sides, but no longer biting to hurt. If I fell, which I did over and over, they waited for me to climb back to my feet. If I slowed from fear of tumbling down a gully, they nipped my ankles to spur me onward. For the entire wretched night they drove me forward, along a path far smoother than any I had traversed before, so I never stumbled into a chasm, and slipped past the edges of the Evil Wood without ever catching a glimpse of its twisted boughs.

When dawn bleached the edge of the sky, I was clear of the jagged stream channels, standing right on the border of the orchard valley. Overwhelmed with relief, I turned to try and make peace with my strange tormentors— but not a single cat was left in sight. I collapsed face first into the soft moss, and dropped into a dead sleep.

A hard boot to my ribs wrenched me awake. I was bound hand and foot, trussed tight with rough hemp, once more the captive of the giants. “Of course,” I laughed bitterly to myself. “Who else would I ever belong to?” A second kick silenced the sharp, self-mocking laugh, and after a few more well-placed prods from my captors, I finally managed to stagger upright.

Six giants loomed over me. They untied the rope that bound my ankles together, fixed a separate length of rope to each leg, and dragged me off. I stumbled along as best I could, but every so often they would yank both ropes at once, sending me sprawling onto the dirt, only to kick me back to my feet when I did not rise fast enough. They hauled me straight back to my old punishment labor, tied my leg ropes to a thick oak tree, freed my arms, and pressed the hateful sharp flint hammer back into my left hand. Then they flopped down on the grass nearby, pelting me with rotten fallen fruit and small stones, though they rarely landed a hit. If I could have wrenched free of my leg ties and grabbed the stout stick I spotted lying two yards away, I would have tackled all six of them at once. “But the Little Ones will come when night falls,” I told myself, and the thought softened the edge of my exhaustion.

I worked nonstop all day. When darkness fell, they tied my hands behind my back and left me fast secured to the tree. I slept fitfully, waking over and over from dreams of being curled in the middle of a great pile of laughing children.

When the sun rose, my giant enemies were back, bringing their usual kicks and their brutish roughhousing with them. It was nearly noon, and I was half fainting from hunger and fatigue, when I heard a sudden commotion in the brush, followed by a peal of that bell-clear laughter that I loved more than any sound in the world. I yelled out in joy and welcome.

Immediately a din exploded around me: the high trumpeting of baby elephants, the whinny of foals, the low bawl of calves, all coming from the throats of riders rather than the tiny beasts themselves. The Little Ones poured out of the undergrowth, mounted on shaggily miniature horses, palm-sized elephants, and soft fluffy young bears. The older boys and girls walked at their sides, and among them I spotted a young woman with a crowing baby cradled in her arms.

The giants scrambled lumbering to their feet, but a storm of sharp little stones pelted them the second they stood. The tiny horses charged at their ankles, the little bears stood up to hug them tight around the waist, the baby elephants coiled their trunks around the giants’ necks, dragged them to the ground, and trampled them just as roughly as the giants had once trampled other victims, a taste of their own medicine.

My ropes were cut in a heartbeat, and I found myself buried under a swarm of seemingly innumerable small, warm arms. I did not see hide nor hair of the giants after that. They made me sit down, and Lona stepped forward without a word, feeding me bite after bite of the juiciest red and gold orchard fruit I had ever tasted. The whole colony stood guard around me until I had eaten every last scrap they gave me.

Then they led forward two of their largest baby elephants, stood them side by side, hooked their trunks together, and tied their tails fast with twine. The gentle creatures could have shaken their tails free with a single twitch, and unhooked their trunks by simply letting them go slack— but they held perfectly still, clearly understanding they were meant to keep their broad backs pressed parallel. I climbed up, and laid myself in the soft hollow between their two backs. The clever elephants leaned gently against each other to counteract the weight that would have pushed them apart, making a perfectly smooth, warm litter for me. My feet dangled a little past their tails, and my head rested pillowed on one soft ear from each elephant.

A line of small children climbed up to sit along the back of each elephant to act as my bodyguard, the whole group arranged themselves into a cheerful procession, and we set off. I did not even try to guess where they were taking me, I surrendered entirely to their care, nearly as happy as they were. They chattered and laughed and pulled dozens of silly, glee-filled tricks at the start, but the second they noticed my eyelids drooping, they fell quiet as solemn judges.

I woke to a burst of bright, trilling music. The sun had set, dusk was falling fast, and we were moving through that ancient forest where they had first found the stray lost babies, the same forest that I had long suspected stretched unbroken from the orchard valley all the way to the banks of the hot healing stream. A tiny girl sat perched right next to my face, her small bare feet dangling inches from my nose, and looked down at me coaxingly for a long moment before she spoke, all the other Little Ones falling silent to hang on her words.

“We make a petisson for our king,” she said.
“What is it, my little darling?” I asked her.
“Shut your eyes for one minute,” she replied.
“Of course I will,” I said, closing my eyes tight.
“No no! Not before I tell you what to do!” she yelped, and I opened my eyes again. We talked and laughed together for another full hour before she suddenly cried out, “Close eyes now!”

I shut my lids and held them closed. The elephants stopped walking. I heard soft scurrying steps, a faint rustle of leaves, then a silence so thick in that strange world that I could almost hear it hum.
“Open your eyes!” twenty little voices shouted all at once from a little ways off. When I obeyed, not a single Little One was in sight— only the two elephants that bore me. I knew the children were impossibly quick at hiding, the giants had taught them that skill well, but when I sat up and scanned the open, shrub-less forest around me, I could not spot so much as a stray hand or peeking toe anywhere. I stared around in utter blank astonishment.

The sun had dipped fully below the treeline, and dark was creeping fast across the ground— but then thousands of birds started to sing all at once, right overhead. I laid back down to listen, certain if I left them to their game the hiders would soon come out again. The bird song swelled into a tiny, roaring storm of trilling calls. “The children must have a hand in this,” I thought to myself. “But how could they make the birds sing all at once like this?”

I glanced up at the boughs of the giant tree we had stopped under, and spotted a tiny flicker of movement among the leaves. I squinted harder. Pale little white spots popped out of the dark foliage, the bird song died down, and a gale of childish laughter rippled through the air. White faces popped out of every tree: the branches were full of them, hidden in nests they had built high up in the canopy. They climbed down in wild glee, dropping from bough to bough so fast I could barely believe they did not slip and fall. I climbed off my litter, and they swarmed around me instantly, pelting me with tiny soft pinecones in their giddy game. The two elephants ambled away slowly, stately, to find a spot to rest for the night.

“Tell me,” I said, once their wild laughter had died down a little, “how is it I never heard you sing like the birds before? Even when I guessed it was you making the noise, I could barely believe it.”
“Ah!” said one of the wildest little boys, bouncing on his heels, “we weren’t birds then! We were run-creatures, not fly-creatures! We hid in the bushes back then, but when we came to this part of the woods where there are no bushes, only big trees, we had to build nests up high. Once we built nests, we turned into birds, and when we were birds, we had to learn what birds do! We asked all the birds to teach us their songs, and they did, so now we are real birds. Come see my nest! It’s not big enough for the king to fit in, but it’s big enough for the king to watch me sit inside it!”

I told him I could not climb a tree in the dark without the sun to light the way, that I would try once the sun came up the next day. “Kings rarely grow wings, anyway,” I added.
“King! King!” another little one yelled, “None of us have those silly feathery things! Arms and legs are far better!”
“That’s true,” I laughed. “I can climb without wings— and carry straw in my mouth to build my own nest, too!”
“See? You know it!” he answered, then wandered off sucking his thumb. A moment later, I heard his voice calling down from his nest, hundreds of feet up in a massive ancient walnut tree. “Good night, king! I’m sleepy!” and I heard nothing more from him until he woke me at dawn the next day.

I settled down against the gnarled, mossy trunk of an old ash as dusk thickened, and one by one, or in soft chattering little groups, the children drifted away from my side to clamber up to their woven treetop nests. They were always heavy-limbed with tiredness at sundown, and bright as sparrow fledglings when they woke at dawn, equally happy to curl into sleep and throw themselves back into play the moment they opened their eyes. I, though my own bones ached from days of travel, lay wide awake: Lona had not come to bid me good night, and every instinct told me she would find me. The very second I had seen her again, I had been struck by how much she resembled Lilith the princess, and I knew at once she was the daughter Adam had told me of—but in Lona, the princess’s blinding, almost searing beauty was softened to something soft and childlike, and deepened with the quiet, warm weight of gentle motherhood. “She is busy tending the child of that fleeing woman I met on the road, no doubt,” I thought to myself, the same woman Lona had told me was not half the mother her baby deserved.

At last she came, sank down beside me, and for a long, soft moment said nothing, only stroked my face and my calloused hands in that wordless, overflowing delight that needs no speech. Then she began to tell me every thing that had befallen the Little Ones from the day I vanished from the orchard valley. The moon crept over the treeline as we talked, sending shivering streaks of silver through the leaves to gild her thoughtful face, where a faint, steady care sat so wrapped up in love it did not weigh her down, but glowed like light through stained glass. It was hard to fathom how such a tender, wise child-woman could be born of a being as fierce and terrible as the princess—though, as it turned out, she had not two parents, but three, and that third was the quiet magic of the forest itself. She pulled at my heart with every word that was most like my own deepest, unspoken hopes, and I loved her as a being who could only ever grow more fully a child, no matter how much wisdom she gained. I knew now I had carried that love with me the day I left her, and the hope of seeing her again had been the small, steady light that got me through every dark, hard hour of my journey. Every word she spoke went straight to my chest, and like a shard of unvarnished truth, left my heart cleaner than it had been a moment before.

She told me that the moment I left the orchard, the giants had stopped writing off their tiny neighbours as silly fairy tales, and their old vague dislike curdled into open, furious hostility. The Little Ones would stand right beside them, laughing, as the giants stamped and roared, tearing at bushes, enraged by what they thought they saw of the small folk’s traces. Before long, their anger turned cruel and practical: they began chopping down the fruit trees the Little Ones lived on, hacking at trunks until apples and pears and figs came crashing down to rot on the ground. That was when Lona, the mother of them all, set about finding a way to stop the destruction. She set the sharpest of the children to listen through the dark hours, and learned the giants were convinced I was hiding somewhere nearby, biding my time to creep up and kill them in their sleep. The only way to turn them from their path of destruction, she decided, was to make them truly believe the Little Ones had abandoned the land entirely.

They would move deep into the forest, far enough away that the giants rarely ventured, but close enough they could sneak back under cover of dark to harvest their ripe fruit. The only problem was that part of the forest had almost no underbrush for them to hide in. But if the birds could make homes there, Lona reasoned, so could they. The Little Ones already loved and understood every form of wild life, could learn every trick the forest’s creatures knew—so why could they not take shelter in the treetops, to escape both the cold and their giant enemies? Why, after nesting in low brush for so long, should they not climb to the lofty boughs, and build nests where there was no hollow to dig in the earth? Every single thing the birds could do, the Little Ones could learn to do, save fly.

She laid the plan before them, and the children cheered at once. They already climbed trees faster than squirrels, and had spent hours watching sparrows and thrushes weave their twig and moss nests. The great forest trees stretched higher toward the sky than any of the orchard trees the giants owned, their branches spreading wide and some even dipping down as if reaching out to invite the children to climb aboard. Half giddy, they chattered that in the very top of the tallest tree, they might even find that fabled bird that laid the tiny baby-eggs, sat on them until they were ripe, then tumbled them down to let the new little ones hatch free. They would build their sleep-houses high above the ground where no giant would ever see them—for none of those dull, heavy oafs ever bothered to crane their thick necks back and look up. The bad giants would be sure they had all fled the country, and the Little Ones could sneak down to gather their fruit when the giants were fast asleep. That was the plan, and the whole band of Lovers threw themselves into it with such joy that within days they were as much at home in the treetops as the birds themselves. Before long, the giants had convinced themselves they had frightened the small folk away entirely, forgot about the trees, and almost stopped believing the Little Ones had ever existed at all.

Lona asked me if I had noticed how many of the children had grown since I left. I admitted I had not, but I could well believe it. She assured me it was true, and said that the discovery that their minds had grown sharper and bolder alongside their bodies had softened the alarm she had first felt at the change. They crept down to the valley in the short twilight hours, when the giants would not step foot outside—for twilight was full darkness to their dull eyes, and they hated the moon so fiercely they would have snuffed her out if they could. But soon the children found that fruit picked after dark went sour before midday the next day, and a new idea sprung up: instead of pretending they had run away, why not make the bad giants themselves leave the land entirely?

They had already explored the full stretch of the forest, introduced themselves to every creature that lived there. They knew how strong, gentle, and wise the elephants were, how fast and steady the horses were, how calm and clever the bears could be. So they set out to befriend every one of them, greeting every beast as Brother or Sister at first, until soon each creature had a soft, special name all its own. It took a little longer to greet the bears that way, and longer still to greet the snakes—but just the other day, Lona had heard one tiny boy laugh and call “Ah, Sister Serpent!” to a snake that had nipped him when he played too roughly. Most of them would not touch caterpillars, only sit and watch them through every stage of their slow transformation, but the second the new butterfly unfurled its wings from its cocoon, all the children swarmed to greet it, calling it Sister Butterfly and congratulating it on its “Repentance”—the word they used for that sacred, beautiful metamorphosis, a second, brighter life.

One moonlit night, when they were on their way to pick fruit, they stumbled on a woman sitting huddled on the ground with a baby in her lap—the very woman I had met fleeing from the city of Bulika. The Little Ones mistook her for a giantess that had stolen one of their shared babies, and swarmed her all at once, beating her with soft tiny fists in a bewildering, overwhelming flurry. She would have run, but a boy threw himself down to wrap his arms around her ankles and hold her fast. When she got her wits back, she recognized the small folk she had come looking for, and held the baby out gently. Lona stepped forward and took the baby, tucking it warm and safe inside her own tunic. They noticed as she gave the baby up, she hugged it and kissed it exactly the way they all hugged and kissed the small ones, and realized she must be a good giantess, like the kind giant that had once been kind to them. So they piled her arms high with fruit, and lavished every sort of childish, silly attention on her. The woman had been lost, too afraid to go back to Bulika, for she knew the princess would find out she was the one who had lamed her favourite leopardess. Delighted by the small folk’s welcome, she decided to stay with them for a while: she would not have to worry about her baby, and she hoped she might find a way back to her husband, a rich man who hoarded gold and gems and was very rarely unkind to her.

What Lona did not know, and I have to piece together from the bits I learned in Bulika, was that the woman knew the old city legend: the princess lived in terror of a child fated to destroy her. No one in the city knew the terrible, dark rituals she used to preserve her unnatural youth and beauty—they only saw her growing more hostile to every new baby born, and assumed she could feel her doom creeping closer, even if no one dared to lift a hand against her. So the woman hatched a scheme of her own: if she could make the Little Ones see themselves as the ones fated to bring the princess down, she could use the small folk’s bravery to storm Bulika. The Bulikan people were cowards who were terrified of all animals, after all, and the Little Ones were bold and strong, with all the forest’s beasts on their side. Once the fighting broke out, she would slip away unseen, creep back to her own house and hide, and wait to see who won. If the children drove the giants out of the forest, she could fan their excitement, and push them to aim higher, at the city itself.

The Little Ones hated fighting, of course. They almost never quarrelled, never hurt any living thing if they could help it, and never wanted to suffer pain themselves. But they were curious, and easily won over to new games. The woman started by setting the smallest children to throw stones at a carved wooden mark, and soon every last child was obsessed with the new game, growing more and more skilled by the day. That skill was exactly what they used to rescue me: when they found me asleep, tied up and guarded by giants, they went back to the trees, held a quick council, then came back the next day with all their elephant and horse friends, overwhelmed the few giants watching over me, and carried me away. After the victory, the small boys were boasting like little jays, the older boys were quiet and proud, and the girls, though their eyes sparkled brighter than usual, were far less talkative than normal. The Bulika woman, no doubt, was more encouraged than ever.

We talked long into the night, mostly about the children’s growth, and what that strange, steady change might mean. I had long known Lona saw truth as clearly as a bird sees the open sky, but now I was astonished at how sharp and practical her wisdom was—though if I had been more of a child myself, I suppose I would not have wondered at all. Long before dawn, I heard a soft scrabbling and fluttering in the boughs above me. I propped myself up on my elbow, and watched dozens of the Little Ones climb down from their nests, vanish into the dark underbrush, until a few minutes later the forest went quiet again.

“What are they doing?” I asked Lona.

“They think the stupid giants are going to come search the woods for us,” she answered. “They’re out gathering stones to meet them. There are not many good throwing stones in this part of the forest, so they have to scatter far and wide to find enough. They will carry them back up to their nests, and hurl them at the giants the second they step within range. They know the giants will not come before morning, but if they do, this will be the start of a war to drive them out. One of us has to go, and the giants are the ones who will leave. We do not mean to kill them, you know—their skulls are so thick I do not think we could hurt one badly even if we tried. And it would hardly matter if one did die, anyway: they are barely alive at all. If a giant dropped dead, his giantess would forget he existed after three days.”

“But the children throw so well it could happen, could it not?” I asked.

“Wait till you see them,” she said, a faint, proud smile tugging at her lips. “I have not even told you the strangest thing that happened two nights ago. We had all come back from gathering fruit, and fallen asleep in our nests, when we were woken by the most horrid, snarling noise of beasts fighting. The moon was bright, and all our trees were suddenly full of wide, staring little eyes, watching two huge leopardesses tearing at each other. One was pure as driven snow, the other was covered in black spots, rolling over and over each other, biting and clawing. By the look of the spotted one’s back, she must have been halfway up a tree when the white leopardess jumped her. When I first saw them they were right under my own tree, and I climbed down to the lowest bough to get a better look. The children thought they were playing at first, cheered for one or the other, because we had never seen such great big cats before. But then their roars grew weaker, and we saw blood pouring down their flanks and throats, and I wished neither would be left alive enough to climb a tree. Before I could stop them, the whole crowd of children scurried down to the ground to pat them and try to comfort them. I climbed down fast to pull them back, but before I could reach them, the white leopardess stopped fighting, and let out such a hideous yell that the children flew back up into the trees like panicking birds. Before I could climb back up to my own nest, the two were at it again. The white one won, chased the spotted leopardess running into the woods, then came and laid down right at the foot of my tree. She got up every few minutes to pace, making sure the spotted one was not lurking nearby, and she stayed there the whole rest of the night. She was gone by dawn.”

“I know both of those leopards,” I told her. “The spotted one is Lilith’s cruel pet, who hates all the children and would kill every last one of them. The white one loves them. She only yelled to scare the children away, to keep them from getting hurt if the spotted one won. No one has any reason to fear her.”

By then the children were tramping back into the clearing, chattering loudly, no longer bothering to stay quiet now that they were openly at war with the giants, their pockets and arms crammed full of smooth, heavy throwing stones. They climbed back up to their nests, grunting a little under the weight of their burdens, and fell fast asleep the second they curled down. Lona climbed up to her own nest in the great ash tree, and I lay back down, sleeping all the easier for knowing the gentle white leopardess was somewhere in the woods, standing watch.

I woke not long after the sun rose, and lay still, thinking. Two hours later, the giants began to trudge into the clearing in straggling groups of three and four, until I counted more than a hundred of them. The children were still asleep, and if I called out I would draw all the giants’ eyes straight to us—so I stayed quiet, planning to keep out of sight as long as they did not find me. But soon one blundered right over my legs, tripped, fell, and stumbled back to his feet. I thought he would wander on, but he started patting at the bushes, searching. I jumped to my feet, and punched him dead in the middle of his huge, soft belly. The roar he let out was deafening, and it was the signal. A storm of stones hailed down from the treetops, not one of them touching me, not one of them missing the giant. He fell flat and lay still. More giants stumbled forward to see what the noise was, and the stone storm spread. Every dull, purblind giant that stepped within range of a garrisoned tree became a target for a dozen converging throws. In minutes, almost every giant was laid flat on the ground, and a jubilant, song-like cheer rose up from the tops of fifty trees.

The great elephants came lumbering over at the children’s call, and the little ones climbed down the trunks like monkeys, three or four to an elephant’s back. The elephants began to walk slowly over the giants lying on the ground, and when the oafs kept roaring and making a nuisance of themselves, the elephants gave them a few sharp, soft blows with their trunks to hush them. The giants lay there silent and motionless until night fell. The next morning, every last one of them was gone. They fled all the way to the far end of the orchard valley, and never dared set foot in the Little Ones’ forest ever again.

The echoes of their recent victory still hummed in the clearing when the exile from Bulika stepped forward to speak. She talked long of her home, of how its walls were more for show than defense, of the cruel, child-killing princess who ruled it, and of how the town’s people had long grown too cowardly to stand against her. Within days, the Little Ones chattered of nothing but Bulika—though not a one of them had any frame of reference for stone walls, cobblestones, or a settlement that stretched farther than the edges of the forest. It was then I first saw the shape of the woman’s design, even if I could not yet guess her full motive. The idea of seizing the city seized hold of Lona at once, and of me no less.

The children’s gifts and faculties were blooming so fast by this point I could spot no serious barrier to the enterprise’s success. I knew Lilith, that shapeshifting woman or leopardess, had one single vulnerable point: the doom foretold to her by the old prophecy of the land, tied to her own daughter. Between that curse hanging over her, the fear that gnawed at Bulika’s citizens, whatever risk the mission carried was worth taking. We could not fail. Once we held the city, this crowd of unruly, loving children would grow fast into a young, righteous people, ruling the wicked of Bulika with a firm, just hand and redeeming the whole broken nation. I will not pretend I had no personal ambition in the plan: it felt only right that Lona should take the throne that had been stolen from her mother, natural that she would make me her consort and chief minister. I would spend my whole life in her service, and with the Little Ones as the heart of our new state, we could build something nobler than any realm that had ever existed. I even harbored a silly, foolish little daydream of opening a gem trade between my old world and this one—something I would later be grateful was impossible, for it would have corrupted both.

I thought back to Adam’s old appeal, and suggested to Lona that we might seek out the hidden, life-giving water he had spoken of to speed the Little Ones’ growth. She judged it wiser to hold off. We knew nothing of what unforeseen hungers or changes that water might bring, and would far rather not unleash a new necessity on the children we did not understand. “They are perfect as they are,” she said. “Once we hold the city, we will search for water then.”

So we threw ourselves fully into preparations, parading the merry little companies over and over to drill them. Lona oversaw the commissariat, storing piles of dried fruit, foraging wild nuts, and coordinating hay harvests, while I drilled our small soldiers: teaching them to hurl stones true with slings, draw bows to the ear, and wield sharpened aloe spikes hardened as steel, fixed to long light shafts, that made even our youngest fighters a formidable force. The oldest girls carried no weapons for themselves: their only duty was to tend the toddlers too small to fight, and keep them safe out of harm’s way. The hardest part of training was teaching them to rally to their flag the instant the call sounded, no matter what caught their attention.

Lona herself had grown nearly to full womanhood, though she did not seem to notice. She was still the tallest of us all, her hair falling long down her back, and no trace of her soft childish beauty had faded in the least. When we had first met again after our long separation, she had set down the infant in her arms, wrapped her arms around my neck and clung silent to me, her face glowing bright with unspoken joy. Only when the baby whimpered did she spring back to tuck him safe against her chest. To see her tend to a stubborn, fussy, or irritable little one was to see the softest tenderness of a grandmother at work. It felt like I had known her for ages—for always, from before time had even begun. I could barely remember what my own mother had looked like, but whenever I pictured her, her face was Lona’s. Any sister or child I imagined, bore Lona’s features. My every thought turned to her: she was my heart’s true wife. She never sought me out unnecessarily, but she was always within earshot, and every choice I made I ran past her in my mind, rejoicing to see that she did her own work with quiet, unshakable independence, and was happiest of all at my side. She never neglected the smallest child for my sake, and my love for her only sharpened my sense of duty. Loving her and doing what was right were not the same, but they were inseparable. Her love flowed to me not in soft caresses, but in that perfect, unshakable recognition that I can only compare to the devotion of some divine, loyal creature. I never told her Lilith was her mother.

The forest teemed with birds whose iridescent plumage more than made up for the lack of flowers that could not grow without open water. Gathering piles of their shed feathers, I wove them in overlapping rows into a long, flowing mantle that fastened at the throat and waist, with slits for the arms. Lona sat and watched me work the whole two weeks it took, never asking what I was making, just waiting patiently to see the end result. When I set it on her shoulders, she first took it off and laid it at my feet out of quiet respect, then stroked the shimmering feathers, and carried it away with her. A few days later she came to me wearing the feather mantle, and handed me a tunic she had woven from the tough leaves of an evergreen shrub, strong as leather and patterned like scale armor. It was perfect for riding.

A troop of giant wild horses had wandered to the forest’s edge some weeks prior, and since they showed no fear of our strange small shapes, I had tamed the two finest for Lona and me. When Lona first looked down from the back of the great steed, after a lifetime of riding tiny forest ponies, her delight was plain to see, and the horse seemed as proud to bear her as she was to ride him. We exercised them every day until they obeyed our every signal instantly and feared nothing, and after that we rode them for every parade and drill.

The Bulika woman’s constant unshakable confidence kept my own hesitations at bay, even when the whole foolhardy enterprise seemed to loom too big. She swore the princess’s magic would have no power over the Little Ones, that our animal allies alone would outmatch any ragtag militia the city could scrape together, and that she herself could take on two grown men of Bulika with nothing but a stout stick. She confessed she was afraid of the leopardess Lilith—but I told her I was ready to face her. I still shrank from bringing all the very smallest children with us, and suggested Lona stay in the forest with the babies and the toddlers.

“I must come,” she said. “The sight of our gentle Little Ones will storm the hearts of Bulika’s women, especially the mothers. If there is any courage left in that city, it lives in the women.”

“You must not carry any children yourself,” I told her. “You will be needed everywhere.” She still had a habit of tucking one of the babies into the crook of her arm even on horseback.

“I cannot remember ever being without a child to care for,” she smiled, “but once we reach the city, I will do exactly as you ask.”

Her quiet, unwavering trust in a man who had failed her once shamed me. I had not started this movement, and I had no right to oppose it. I gave myself over entirely to her cause, ready to live or die by her side.

The open grassy plain that stretched between us and Bulika was rich with short grass, enough to feed our horses and the two milk cows we brought for the infants, but the elephants could not pluck the short grass with their thin, delicate trunks. We set all the Little Ones to cutting and bundling hay, enough that the elephants themselves could carry a several days’ supply, with extra foraging every time we halted. We stored nuts for our ursine allies, dried fruit for ourselves, and loaded our extra tamed horses and elephants with all the supplies we needed. We were ready to march.

Lona and I held one final full review, and I spoke to the crowd of bright, eager children. I told them they had not simply sprung out of nothing, as they liked to claim. They had all been stolen from Bulika when they were too small to remember, I said, their mothers sneaking them out of the city to save them from the cruel princess who killed every baby born. We were going to Bulika to find their mothers, and free them.

“There is danger ahead,” I warned them. “We may have to fight very hard to take the city.”

“We can fight! We are ready!” the boys roared.

“Mothers are worth fighting for,” I told them. “Only keep together, and obey your officers instantly. Strike hard, draw your arrows to the head, sling your stones straight and strong. Some of you may be hurt.”

None of them flinched. “We don’t mind!” they shouted.

“Some of you may even be killed,” I said.

“I don’t mind being killed!” yelled a small, bright boy riding a tiny bull that galloped and jumped like a racehorse. The whole crowd of children took up the cry.

Then Lona spoke, seated high on her great steed at my side, queen, mother, and sister to every one of them. “I would give my life to meet my mother,” she said. “If she wanted to kill me, I would let her. I would just kiss her, and then die.”

“Come on!” a girl shouted from the ranks. “We’re going home to our mothers!”

A sharp, cold pang pierced my heart. But I could not turn back. To retreat now would shatter the Little Ones, and leave them morally broken forever. There was no going back.

We set out at first pale dawn, strung across the wide plain like a living tapestry slung between unbroken blue sky and sun-dappled emerald grass. We planned to march all morning, rest through the heat of the afternoon, then advance under cover of dark, pause to sleep through the next full day, and push onward again in the short dim twilight of the Bulikan lowlands. The final leg of our journey we would time perfectly, so we slipped through the city gates at the very first break of dawn, before any guard had rubbed the sleep from their eyes.

It felt as if the whole forest had upped and migrated with us. Flocks of iridescent songbirds wheeled ahead, no doubt fully convinced they were the official vanguard of our procession, while clouds of swallowtails, darting dragonflies, and fuzzy bumbling wasps bobbed an arm’s length above our riders’ heads. A throng of four-footed forest folk – foxes, otters, frisky fawns – trotted at our heels, most peeling off once the dark deepened, but every last winged creature stuck with us all the way to the shadow of Bulika’s walls.

We halted at midday, and slept deep and dreamless through the afternoon. It was our first real long march, and not a single one of us was tired. When night fell, the sharp cool air pricked our cheeks and we moved faster still. Many of the Little Ones nodded off slouched against the shaggy fur of their riding beasts, or atop the thick piles of hay stacked on the elephants’ backs, and woke at sunrise bright and fresh, no one tumbling off their mount to hit the dust. Some rode shambling, broad-pawed bears that kept pace easily with the great elephants; others were mounted on fleet deer, and would have raced each other the whole length of the plains if I had not forbidden it. The children perched on the elephant hay stacks, unable to see their mounts far below, chattered to the beasts nonstop whenever they were awake. Once, when we halted to let the elephants feed, I heard a tiny boy tugging at a tuft of hay, leaning in earnest against his elephant’s trunk to murmur: “Nosy dear, I’m digging you out of the mountain, see? I’m almost all the way down! Be patient, yeah? Soon you’ll push your nose up to look for me, and then we’ll kiss just like good elephants do, we will!”

That same night, a raucous tumult burst out across the levels: elephant trumpets, horse whinnies, and the Little Ones mimicking every animal’s call so the noise rang for miles across the silent plains. Terrified we would give away our position before we were close enough to the gates, I quickly stilled the uproar.

Then one morning, the sun and Bulika rose together, as if the first light had birthed the whole jagged mass of city walls out of the bare earth. To the children, the fortifications looked like nothing but a great barren heap of rock, and when I told them the inside was crammed full of hollow stone nests, carved chambers stacked high to hold people, a cold dread settled over every small heart. It was the first real fear most of them had ever known, even the ones who’d lived long little lives out in the woods. How were they supposed to find soft, loving mothers in a cold, stony place like that? But they did not falter, for they trusted Lona – and me too, fool that I was, for little did I deserve that faith.

We rode through the echoing archway, the drumming of hooves, padding of paws, and thud of bare feet booming off the ancient vaulted pavement. The horses spooked at the sound of their own echoes, some skidding to a halt, others rearing and wheeling wildly, but we calmed them fast and pushed onward. The Little Ones shivered, going dead still as death, the three older girls clutching the swaddled infants they carried tight to their chests. Only the bears and the flitting butterflies showed no trace of fear. The woman we had rescued back in the woods wore a face tight with dark, heavy anxiety, and even I was not untouched by the general dread: the whole army of innocents was on my hands, my responsibility, my fault if harm came to them. I had dreamed of the coming kingdom of the Little Ones, where the cruel bad giants would serve, and the animals would be their loving, obedient friends – but I had been stupid enough never to learn to obey the quiet, gentle law that governed that good world. My own stubborn, untrusting willfulness had put me at the head of this procession of pure hearts, and I was no better than any selfish king in the world I had left behind, who only ever did what pleased himself. But Lona rode at my side, a child in body and therefore a truly free woman: calm, silent, watchful, not a flicker of fear crossing her face.

We were nearly to the very heart of the city before any of its inhabitants noticed we were there. Then window shutters began to creak open, and sleepy heads poked out. At first every face wore a dull, uncomprehending stare of astonishment, which curdled into unmasked terror the second they spotted the bears, elephants, and deer moving through the streets. Even through their fear, when they saw that their invaders were almost all small children, the women poured out of the houses into the streets, men trailing after them, all huddling tight against the walls, too afraid of the wild animals to step into the open middle of the road.

At length a five-year-old boy, half out of his mind searching for a mother, spotted a woman in the crowd whose face felt soft and safe, and launched himself off his antelope straight into her arms, clinging tight around her neck. She hugged him back, kissing his cheeks like he was her own long-lost child. Then a brute of a man loomed over her shoulder, and seized the boy by the scruff of his neck. Instantly an older girl drove her sharp wooden spear through his arm. He let out a savage howl, took two more stabs from other Little Ones, and fled screaming down the lane. “They are just bad giants,” Lona said, her eyes flashing like embers as she kicked her horse forward straight at an unusually tall brute who had worked up the nerve to bar her path with a heavy club. He dared not meet the full force of her horse’s charge, and scuttled aside, only to be knocked flat a second later by a hail of small stones. Another giant dodged my charger, stepped suddenly between me and the small boy riding behind me, snarling a curse whose rudeness alone I could parse, and swung a heavy hammer at the little boy’s pony, caving its skull in. Before he could strike again, the elephant behind me slammed its trunk down, knocking him flat, and trampled him so thoroughly he did not dare try to stand until hundreds of feet had walked over him and the whole army had moved past.

But the second Lona looked at the crowd of city women, a great shadow of dismay clouded her face. Hardly a single one of them was pleasant to look upon. Were her darlings supposed to find good mothers among these hard, cruel faces? We barely halted in the central square when two girls rode up in breathless haste, with tidings that two small boys had been snatched and hurried away by a group of the city’s women. We wheeled around at once, and that was when we discovered that the woman we had befriended back in the woods had vanished, taking her baby with her.

At that exact moment, a great white leopardess came bounding down the narrow lane that led from the square straight to the palace. The Little Ones had not forgotten the brutal fight between the two leopardesses in the forest, a few flinched, their ranks wavering – but they remembered the order I had given them, and stood fast. We paused to see what would happen next, when little Odu – the swiftest, bravest of the Little Ones, who had heard me speak once of the white leopardess’s gentle nature – leaped off the back of his shambling bear, who lumbered loyally after him, and ran straight to meet her. The leopardess, to avoid knocking him flying, skidded to a halt so fast she rolled head over paws across the cobblestones, and when she righted herself, the grinning small boy was perched right on her back. No Bulikan who saw that, who lived in daily terror of that leopard, could doubt our victory.

Confident the whole city’s will to resist was broken, we rode onward. When we pulled up outside the house our guides pointed to, a sharp scream cut the air. I sprang down from my horse and thundered my fists against the locked door. My great war horse nudged me out of the way with his nose, turned, and began battering the door with his hooves, until Odu trotted up on the leopardess. The horse froze, trembling, but the leopardess had heard the scream too, and forgetting the child on her back, hurled herself full force at the door. Odu was dashed against the wood and fell senseless. Before I could reach him, Lona had him swept up in her arms, and as soon as he blinked awake, set him back on his loyal bear, who had never left his side.

The leopardess hit the door a third time, and it splintered open. She darted inside before any of us could follow. We rushed in after her, but she had already vanished. We tore up the stairs, searched every room in the house, and found no one. Darting back down, we spotted a low hidden door under the staircase, and ducked through into a twisting labyrinth of excavated tunnels running under the foundations. We had not gone far when the white leopardess came trotting back, the missing snatched boy draped safe across her back. He told us the woman he had thought was his mother had thrown him down the dark hole, saying she would feed him to the leopardess – but the good leopardess had climbed right down after him and pulled him out.

We went next door easily, searching for the other missing boy, but we were too late. One of the savage giants had already killed the small captive. It consoled Lona, though, to hear which boy it was: she had long suspected he was fated to grow up into a bad giant, the worst possible fate that could befall any Little One, and death had saved him from that dark end. The leopardess sprang on his murderer, seized him by the throat, and dragged him out into the street, following Lona like a great cat carrying an overgrown rat in her jaws.

“Let us leave this horrible place,” Lona said. “There are no mothers here. This people is not worth delivering.” The leopardess dropped her burden, and charged into the panicking crowd, tearing back and forth wherever the Bulikans were packed thickest. The city’s enslaved residents screamed and ran, tripping over each other, piling up in heaps on the cobblestones.

When we got back to our army, we found it exactly as we had left it, standing in perfect order, ready for anything. But I was far from easy. The princess of Bulika had not shown her face once, and we had no idea what dark plot she might be weaving. We would have to keep watch and ward all through the night. The Little Ones were such hardy, resilient creatures they could rest anywhere: we told them to lie down right there in the square, curled up against their animals, and sleep until we called them. In one moment they were all settled, and in the next the whole square hummed with that soft, unique sound of sleeping Little Ones, like water trickling over smooth grass, or a warm summer wind sifting through oak leaves. Their animals slept more lightly, ears twitching, always half awake, ready to rouse at the first sign of trouble. The older boys and girls patrolled soft and silent through the dreaming multitude. All was still. The whole wicked city seemed at rest, hiding all its worst secrets in the dark.

Lona’s revulsion for the city’s hollow, half-feral denizens—its women most of all—curdled to the point she begged we flee the blighted place at once. I, though, burned with the certainty that retreat would be a willful failure when victory hung within reach: far worse, running would shatter the fragile courage of the Little Ones, leaving them exposed to far greater peril. If we turned tail, the princess would never let us escape unassailed. If we marched to face her, the old prophecy we’d carried across the wastes hummed with new, living hope. The ancient law of the realm said mother and daughter must meet. It was possible Lona’s soft, radiant goodness might crack Lilith’s stony, cruel heart of its own accord. If she moved to strike violence, I would stand between them. If no other power could reach her, I was ready, for Lona’s sake, to drive my blow straight into that fateful closed palm the tales spoke of, the one sealed to bring about her doom. I knew her end was written. It seemed all but certain her fate would unfold through us, here and now. I did not breathe a word to Lona of the blood tie between her and the tyrant, only laid out my reasoning plainly. She agreed at once to walk with me to the obsidian palace.

From the peak of its tallest spire at dawn, while the whole city still slept, Lilith had spotted the approach of the Little Ones’ camp across the plain. The sight woke a wild, overpowering terror in her: she had failed to destroy them, and they were coming for her. The prophecy was about to come true. When she shook off her paralysis, she stumbled down to the Black Hall, the elliptical chamber hewn from pure, absolute dark marble, and seated herself at its northern focal point, directly beneath the oculus cut into the domed roof above. She had to think. For Lilith, “thinking” meant wrapping herself in the delusion of her own perfection, not as she was, but as she chose to see herself. To feed that illusion, she had hung a polished mirror in the shadows just above her head, invisible in the hall’s perpetual dim, positioned to catch the full light of the meridian sun and reflect its rays straight onto her own form. She waited for the sun to climb high enough to show her the glorious vision of her own beauty she craved. A dozen skittering shadow-things drifted through the dark around her, but she turned her inner eye away from every last one of them. Her personal shadow, the being that followed her every footstep through the realm, hovered right beneath the mirror, but wrapped in self-adoration, she did not see him at all.

By the time the city fell, its inhabitants cowering in their huts too afraid to even scream, the Little Ones and their great war horses and tiny riding elephants were encamped in the central square. The midday sun spilled over her, and for a few short minutes she drank in the sight of herself, radiant and unblemished, in the mirror. The vision faded, but she did not move. Night fell, and the mirror filled with inky darkness, yet she still sat motionless. Shadows swarmed thick through the halls of the palace, her servants shivering so hard their teeth chattered, too afraid of the Little Ones’ war beasts to dare flee. All night long the princess sat fixed to her throne. She had to see her beauty again. She had to force her delusions to hold. But her courage and her will had tired of her, and would stay no longer.

At dawn, we chose twelve of the tallest, bravest of the boy Little Ones to ride with us to the palace. We mounted our shaggy, great war horses, and the smaller boys rode their little ponies and young elephants. The princess still sat waiting for the sun to grant her the joy of her own glorified reflection. The tide of light crept up the shore of the sky, but not a single ray could pierce the Black Hall’s gloom until the sun stood directly overhead. It rose to meet our eyes as we climbed the palace’s steep marble steps, climbing higher still to the top of the great hall’s dome. It peered through the oculus, and sudden, blinding radiance flooded the room. Lilith caught sight of herself in the mirror, and sprang to her feet with a shriek of despair. The cursed brand, the great black spot that covered half her side, blazed as dark as the marble around her, impossible to hide. She clutched her silk robe tight to her chest and fell back against her throne. Her loyal shadow glided out of the hall, and she watched him go.

We found the palace gates hanging open as always, passed through the grove of petrified paved trees, up to the palace door, and stepped into the vestibule. There, locked in an iron cage, lay the spotted leopardess, her guard beast, still as a corpse, eyes closed. The Little Ones paused a moment to stare. She reared up with a roar, slamming against the cage bars. The horses reared and plunged; the elephants stumbled back a step. The next instant she collapsed flat on her back, writhed in short, quivering spasms, and went motionless.

We rode straight into the great hall. The princess still leaned back in her shaft of sunlight, and the echo of hoofs on stone drifted to her ears from the courtyard. She started, listened, and shook—no sound like that had ever echoed in her palace before. She pressed a hand to her branded side and gasped. The trampling drew closer, crossed the threshold of the hall itself, and shapes that were not her skittering shadows advanced through the dark. For us, there was no gloom where she sat: a single, blazing point of unearthly light, a glorious woman centered in all the blackness. Lona threw herself off her horse before it had even stopped moving, and bounded toward her. I sprang down right after her. “Mother! Mother!” she cried, and her clear, lovely voice bounced off the dome’s high walls.

The princess shivered. Her face twisted almost black with hate, her eyebrows knitting tight together over her forehead. She rose to her feet. “Mother! Mother!” Lona cried again, leaping up onto the dais and flinging her arms around the princess’s waist. One step more and I would have reached them. In that split, terrible second, I saw Lona lifted high over Lilith’s head, and dashed hard against the cold marble floor. The sound of her fall was wet, and horrible. She landed limp at my feet.

Lilith sank back into her throne with the smile of a demoness. I dropped to my knees beside Lona, lifted her from the stones, and pressed her tight to my chest. Burning with indignant hate, I glanced up at the princess, and she answered me with her sweetest, most innocent smile. I would have lunged for her, wrapped my hands around her throat and strangled the life out of her, but my love for the child in my arms was stronger than any hate I could feel for the mother. I held my precious burden tighter. Her arms hung limp and helpless. Her blood seeped between my fingers, dripping onto the floor in soft, slow little plashes.

The scent of blood hit the horses first: mine reared, wild-eyed and shivering, and bolted blindly down the dark hall, the rest of the horses after it. They never saw the far black wall, and crashed against it to shatter themselves to pieces. The elephants lumbered up to the foot of the dais and stopped, trumpeting wildly. The Little Ones clambered up onto the raised platform, frozen in horror. The princess lay back in her seat, her face as gray as a corpse’s, only her eyes alive, burning wickedly bright. She had withered back to the gaunt, wasted thing I had first found wandering in the woods, the brand on her side blazed dark as if a great hot hand had been pressed straight through her flesh.

But Lona saw nothing. I saw nothing but Lona. “Mother! Mother!” she sighed, and her breathing stopped.

I carried her out into the courtyard, where the sun shone down on her pale white face, and the faint, pitiful ghost of a smile curved her lips. Her head lolled back. She was dead as the dry dust of the realm. I forgot the Little Ones, forgot the murdering princess, even forgot the body I held in my arms, and wandered away, searching for the Lona that was no longer there. The doors and windows of the city were crowded with the half-brute denizens, jeering at me but too afraid to speak a word, for they saw the white leopardess padding right at my heel, head hung low. I kicked her away hard. She hesitated a moment, then followed me again.

I reached the square. The Little Ones’ army was gone. The empty space jolted me back to my senses. Where were the Little Ones? Her Little Ones? I had lost them. I staggered, helpless, over to the great stone pillar at the square’s center, and sank down on its base. As I sat staring at Lona’s still face, for a split second she smiled a living, warm little smile. I never once thought it was an illusion. I knew what it meant. I would see her alive again. It was not her that was lost. It was me, and she would find me.

I stood up to go after the Little Ones, and turned instinctively toward the gate we had entered through. I glanced over my shoulder, and the leopardess was gone. The street was filling fast with a fierce, rabid crowd of the city’s inhabitants. They saw me burdened with my dead, and for a while dared not attack. But by the time I neared the gate, their courage had returned. The women began shoving at me, and I held on, heedless. A man lunged and pushed hard against my sacred burden, and I kicked him so hard he went flying, howling. The crowd surged closer, and fearing they would defile the girl who could no longer feel harm, I clasped my treasure tighter and freed my right arm to fight.

At that exact moment, a commotion burst out behind me. The crowd split apart, and through the gap came the Little Ones I had thought lost in the palace. Ten of them rode four elephants, and on the other two elephants lay the princess, bound hand and foot, completely still save for her eyes rolling wildly in their ghastly sockets. The last two Little Ones rode behind her on Lona’s own horse. Every few steps, the wise great beasts bearing her curled their trunks behind them to test that her binding cords held fast. I walked on ahead of the small procession, and carried Lona out of the city.

What a bitter end to all the bright hopes I had carried when I stepped foot in that evil place. We had captured the tyrant princess at last, but we had lost our all-beloved queen. My life was bare. My heart was empty.

A soft, rippling murmur of delight from my companions tore me out of the fog of my own heavy grief: they had spied the rest of their scattered kin far across the meadow. The two riders mounted on Lona’s horse spurred ahead at once to meet them, and the distant group lifted their voices in a wavering, overjoyed shout that died sharp in their throats before it could fully carry. As we drew nearer, the sound of their sobs drifted to us, thin and fragile, like tiny foaming billows breaking against a cold stone shore.

When I stepped into their circle, I saw some unnameable, terrible harm had befallen them. No ordinary grief could have carved that gaunt, hollow haggardness across their childish faces, as if something had gnawed at the edges of their very souls. A handful of them drifted slowly toward me, arms outstretched to take Lona’s weight from my own. I yielded her up to them, and the tender, hopeless softness of the smile they gave as they received her made my throat tighten, pity swelling in my chest even as my own heart felt hollowed out. They wept over their mother-queen in vain; they whispered over and over for her to wake, to recognize their love, to speak a single word back to them, but nothing came. They kissed her hands, stroked her hair, and bore her along as gently as they could, as many as could fit tucking their arms under her body to cradle her, the rest pressing in close around the bearers to walk beside her. They laid her down in a patch of thick, soft, sun-warmed grass, and every last one of the Little Ones huddled around her, sobbing quiet, shaking little sobs.

The elephants stood just outside the crowd of small bodies, and I lingered near them, staring past the tops of dozens of small heads to fix my gaze on Lona’s still face. The two Little Ones closest to me caught sight of the chained princess a little ways off, and stared at her, trembling where they stood. Odu was the first to speak, his voice barely a breath.
“I’ve seen that woman before,” he whispered to the friend at his side. “That’s the one that fought the white leopardess the night all that yelling woke us up!”
“Silly,” his friend huffed back. “That was a wild beast, all covered in spots!”
“Look at her eyes,” Odu insisted. The other took a cautious step forward, and Odu wrenched him back hard, shrinking away even as he seemed magnetized to the hate-crazed, longing look in her gaze. “Don’t get close! She’d gobble you up in a heartbeat! That’s her shadow that chased us, isn’t it? She’s the wicked princess!”
“That can’t be right,” the other protested. “Everyone said the princess was beautiful.”
“It is her, exactly as you say,” I cut in. “Wickedness has warped her until no trace of the beauty she once bore remains.”
She heard me, and the venom that flashed across her face made my skin prickle.
“I… it was very wrong of me to run away,” Odu said suddenly, quiet and thoughtful.
“Why did you run?” I asked him. “I expected to find you right where I left you.”
He didn’t answer at once. “I don’t know what made me run,” another Little One piped up behind him. “I was just… scared, all at once.”
“It was a man that came down the hill from the palace,” a third added.
“What about him frightened you?”
“I don’t know.”
“He wasn’t a man,” Odu corrected. “He was a shadow. He had no thickness to him at all.”
“Tell me every detail you can about him.”
“He came down the hill jet black, walking like a bad giant, but spread flat across the grass, no height, no form, nothing but pure blackness. We were scared the second we saw him, but we didn’t run at first. We stood still and watched him, and he kept coming closer like he meant to walk right over the top of us. Before he got to where we stood, he started spreading, and spreading, growing bigger and bigger until his edges vanished over the horizon, and we couldn’t see where he ended. And then he was upon us.”
“What do you mean, he was upon you?”
“He was all that blackness seeping between every one of us, so we couldn’t see each other at all. And then he was inside us.”
“How did you know he was inside you?”
“I didn’t feel like me anymore. I didn’t feel like Odu. I wanted to tear Sozo to pieces— not the real me, not deep down, but some ugly other thing in my chest made me want to.” He spun around and hugged Sozo tight, sobbing into his friend’s shoulder. “It wasn’t me, Sozo! Deep down it was always Odu, loving you! The real Odu fought back, and knocked the naughty thing away. I felt sick, like I’d have to kill myself to claw all that black out of my bones. Then I heard a horrible laugh that had heard every ugly thought the shadow put in my head, and it made the whole air tremble around me. Next thing I knew I was running, as fast as my legs could carry me, and all the rest were running too. I wanted to stop, but I didn’t even think of stopping until I was all the way out the gate, out in the tall grass. That was when the real Odu came back, and I knew I’d run from something that tried to steal my skin and pretend it was me. It was the shadow that hated you, Sozo, not me! And I know now I never should have run away! But I didn’t even know what I was doing until it was done. My legs did it, I think—they got scared, forgot they belonged to me, and just ran off. Naughty legs! There, and there!” He gave each of his calves a playful, indignant kick to scold them.
“Where did the shadow go after that?” I asked.
“I don’t know,” he said. “I expect he went back home, to the dark of the night where the moon never shines.”

I felt a sudden, sharp ache of longing for Lona’s spirit, wondering where it had fled to. I dropped to the grass, lifted her still-warm, slack body into my lap, and whispered right against her ear. “Where are you, Lona? I love you.” Her lips didn’t stir. They weren’t quite cold yet, and I kissed them gently before laying her back down on the soft grass. I posted a small, trusted guard over her, then rose to organize the camp’s safety for the coming night. Before the sun sank below the treeline, I set a separate watch over the chained princess at the edge of the camp, and positioned sentinels all around the perimeter. I told the rest of our company they could rest easy, I would walk the patrol the whole night through. They collapsed onto the grass and fell asleep in seconds, heavy, dreamless sleep claiming them at once.

When the silver moon climbed high enough to spill pale light across the meadow, I caught a flash of bright white fur through the dark: it was the white leopardess. She glided silent as a ghost around the entire sleeping camp, and I watched her slip back and forth three times, moving between the chained princess and the huddled, sleeping Little Ones, a wordless, living barrier between the two groups. At that, I dismissed the rest of the watch to lie down and rest with their kin, then stretched myself out right beside Lona’s body, to keep vigil through the long dark.

Dawn spilled pale and thin over the barren moors the next morning, and we set out at once, pushing hard for the shadowed line of the forest that hung on the horizon. I rode Lona’s dappled mare, her cold, still form swathed in her own wool cloak laid across the saddle before me. I would lay her at her father’s feet: he would give her a place on the soft couches of his dead chamber, I told myself. If he refused her, for she had not returned to him of her own will, I would watch over her body in the desert until it melted back into the dust. But I knew deep in my bones she had died long before this, and he would not turn her away. The thought of humbling myself before him, of confessing every wrong I had done, coiled bitter in my throat.

And Lilith? I had to bring her to Adam too. I had failed. I had no power to wring a single spark of repentance from her. I had no right to slay her, and far less right to loose such a force of hungry, unappeasable hate loose across the world. I was not worthy to be her jailor for all eternity. Again and again along the road I offered her food and water, and every time she only answered with a searing look of ravenous, unblinking malice.
