Her fiery eyes rolled restlessly in her skull, never closing, not for a moment of rest, until we stepped across the banks of the steaming hot stream. After that they stayed shut, sealed tight, until we stood at the threshold of the House of Bitterness.

One evening as we made camp for the night, a tiny barefoot girl wandered over to where Lilith lay bound on her pallet. I sprinted to stop her, but before I could reach them, the child had pressed a wild berry to the princess’s lips, and let out a sharp, wailing cry of pain. “Please, king,” she whimpered, holding out her pricked, oozing finger, “suck it quick. The bad giantess bit a hole right through it!” I drew the tiny wound clean, and a minute later she was already reaching for a second berry, ready to try again. This time she snatched her hand away fast, and the fruit dropped unharmed to the grass. The little one’s name was Luva.

The next day we crossed the hot stream, and the Little Ones burst into peals of glee, bouncing and cheering to be back on the soil of their home woods. But their nest-village was still miles off, and that day we traveled no further than the ivy-covered hall draped in heavy purple grape clusters I had spotted on my earlier journey. The moment the children caught sight of the fruit they swarmed it, eating greedily, and within minutes every last one of them was fast asleep on the mossy stone floor and in the fern thickets outside. Hoping to watch the ghost dance play out again, certain the Little Ones would sleep straight through the night, I had them leave a wide empty circle clear in the center of the hall. I laid down myself, Lona’s form soft at my side, but I did not sleep.

Darkness fell, and the shadowy company of dancers materialized as if they had stepped out of the very stone walls. I was lingering on the horrid thought that they would dance this hollow, ghostly waltz for all eternity, that my own stiff-necked stubbornness might one day condemn me to join them, when the Little Ones’ eyes all flew open at once. They sprang to their feet wide awake, and every single one of them latched on to the nearest cold, shadowy hand, bounding and skipping along in time to the slow, waltzing rhythm. The spectres did not flinch. They welcomed the children, it seemed—they had long been wandering the path back to their own lost childhood, after all. Their innocent, unruly gambols must have stoked a faint spark of warmth in those weary souls, robbed of their present and with no future but shadow left to them. The children played a hundred merry, never rude pranks, and if they stumbled every now and then and jostled the dance’s rhythm off beat, the hollow spectres, with no mouths left to smile, showed not a flicker of annoyance.

Just before the first pale streak of dawn touched the sky, I froze. There in the doorway stood the skeleton-princess, Lilith, her eyes wide open and blazing, the cursed black mark eating dark into the flesh of her side. She stood motionless a moment, then glided across the stone floor as if drawn to the dancing circle, ready to join. I leaped to my feet with a shout. The Little Ones cried out in sharp, repulsed fear, and all the faint ghostly lights winked out. The low wan moon slanted in through the ivy-framed windows, and I saw the children huddled clinging to each other. The spectres were gone, vanished into thin air. Lilith too had disappeared. I darted back to her pallet, and there she lay, eyes closed, as if she had never moved an inch. When I returned to the hall, the Little Ones were already curling up on the floor, falling back asleep.

We set out again at sunrise, and a little way down the path we spotted two skeletons wandering slowly through a bramble thicket. The Little Ones broke their ranks and ran to greet them. I followed, and even though I now walked free of splints and bandages, I recognized the pair I had seen wandering these woods weeks before. The children made friends with them at once, clinging to their bony arms and stroking the long, smooth bones of their fingers. It was plain the two poor creatures welcomed the attention. Their shared loss of flesh, of everything they had ever known, had drawn them close; the erasure of their old lives had been the start of a strange new one. When the children saw they held handfuls of healing herbs, searching for more to rub into their dry bones (what other nourishment could a being so rudimentary need?), they examined the herbs they held, gathered armfuls of the same, and filled the empty bony hands the skeletons held out to receive them. They said their goodbyes, promising to visit again, and set off chattering to each other about how they never knew such nice, gentle people lived in the woods.

When we reached the nest-village, I stayed one night there to watch the children settle back into their tree-top homes. Lona still looked as if she had just moments since drawn her last breath, and there was no rush to move her. The princess had not eaten a single bite since we set out, and her eyes stayed sealed shut; fearing she would waste away entirely before we reached our destination, I crept to her pallet in the dark of night and laid my bare arm across her lips. She bit down so fiercely the world went black at the edges. I do not remember how I tore away from her, but I came to my senses lying out of her reach, the sun already peeking over the treetops.

I set off immediately after dawn. I chose twelve of the Little Ones—not the biggest or strongest, but the sweetest, merriest of the lot. I seated six of them each on the back of one of six elephants, and took two more of the great gentle “Clumsies” the children loved to bear Lilith’s pallet. I still rode Lona’s mare, her wrapped body held safe before me. I took the straightest path I knew, across the left branch of the dead riverbed, heading for the House of Bitterness. I hoped Mara would show me how to cross the broader, rougher branch of the dead river without harming the elephants, and how to skirt the monstrous basin full of horrors I dreaded even more, for the children’s sake.

We endured one terrible night on the road, the third one, camped in the open desert between the two branches of the dead river. We set the elephants to rest in a sheltered hollow, and laid Lilith’s pallet down between them on the sand. She looked fully dead, but I knew she was not. I laid myself down a little way off, Lona’s body at my other side, keeping watch over both my dead love and the living monster. The moon hung half-way down the west, pale and thoughtful, dappling the sand with soft shadows. Of a sudden it was eclipsed. It stayed visible, but shed no light; a thin, diaphanous dark film stretched across its face, and it looked troubled. The film shifted just a little, and I saw its jagged, torn edge against the moon’s clear silver— the hooked outline of a great bat-like wing. A cold wind blew, one that stung like burning frost, and Lilith was on top of me. Her hands were still bound, but she tore the cloak Lona had woven for me right off my shoulder with her teeth, and sank them deep into my bare flesh. I lay paralyzed. I could feel my life force draining out of me, flowing straight into her, until I remembered the word Mara had taught me. I struck her hard across her bound wrist. She threw back her head and let out a gurgling, unhuman shriek, and I felt her whole body convulse. I flung her off me and sprang to my feet. She was on her knees, rocking back and forth. A second blast of that burning cold wind swept over us, the moon shone bright and clear again, and I saw her face—gaunt, ghastly, smeared red with my blood.

“Down, devil!” I shouted. “Where are you taking me?” she asked, her voice a dull sepulchral echo. “To your first husband,” I answered. “He will kill me!” she moaned. “At least he will take you off my hands.” “Give me my daughter,” she screamed suddenly, grinding her teeth. “Never! Your doom is upon you at last.” “Loose my hands for pity’s sake,” she groaned. “The cords have sunk right into my flesh, I’m in agony.” “I dare not. Lie down,” I said. She fell onto the sand like a heavy log. The rest of the night passed in perfect peace, and by dawn she looked dead again.

Before evening we caught sight of the House of Bitterness on the rise ahead. A moment later one of the elephants ambled up beside my horse, and the Little One riding its neck whispered in my ear. “Please, king, you are not going to that place? That’s where the cat-woman lives!” “Indeed I am,” I answered. “We are spending the night there.” “Oh, please don’t! Nobody ever sees her, the giants say—she has no face! Her head is nothing but back and sides, all round.” “She hides her face from people who are dull and discontented,” I told him. “I met her before. I spent a whole night at her house, and she is good.” “But what if her claws are folded up inside the cushions of her feet, and you never noticed them?” “What if I have claws hidden in my toes?” I teased. “Oh no, you are good, that can’t be!” “Then why do you believe the bad giants’ lies about the lady, when you know they lie about everything else?” The children fell quiet, then one of the little girls spoke up. “If you say she is good, then we believe you. But I don’t want scratches in the dark!” “That is why we have come,” I said. “The princess is her friend. A real friend gives you what you need, not just what you want. The princess sorely needs the kind of hard, firm kindness no one else can give her. If any of you are afraid, you can go home right now. I will not be angry. But I cannot bring anyone with me who believes the giants more than me, or who calls a good lady rude names.” “There she is! In the doorway waiting for us!” one of the children cried, and clapped his hands over his eyes. “Her face is covered, but she’s ugly!” “She has the most beautiful face I have ever seen,” I told them. “As beautiful as Lona’s, hidden under that veil.” Mara stood in the doorway, dressed and swathed all in soft white, waiting for us. “At last!” she said, stepping forward. “Lilith’s hour has been so long on the way, but it has come. Thousands of years I have waited here, and I did not wait in vain.” She came to me, took Lona’s body gently from my arms, carried it into the house, then came back for Lilith. The princess shuddered, but did not fight. The elephants and Clumsies laid down by the door, and we followed Mara inside. She laid Lilith on a rough wooden settle along the wall, cut her bonds, and turned to face us. “Mr. Vane, and all you Little Ones, thank you,” she said. “This woman would never yield to soft, gentle kindness. Hard love is all that is left for her now. I will do everything I can to make her repent.” The tender-hearted Little Ones began to sob softly. “Will you hurt her very much?” the little girl who had spoken earlier asked, slipping her warm hand into mine. “I am afraid I must,” Mara answered. “It would be far crueler to hurt her too little. The work would just have to be done all over again, later, and far worse.” She bent, lifted the small boy Odu up in her arms, turned her back to the rest of us, and drew her veil down from her face, holding him at arm’s length so he could see. I saw the look that spread across his small face, as if he was gazing in a mirror. His mouth fell open, then a look of divine wonder lit up his features, melting into unutterable delight. He stared entranced for a full minute, then Mara set him down. He stood for a moment longer, lost in that vision of bliss he could not name, then ran back to us with the glowing face of a prophet. Mara pulled her veil back into place, and turned to the rest of the children. “Go eat and drink before you sleep,” she told them, “you have had a very long journey.” She set out her house’s hard, dry bread and a jug of cold well water. The children had never seen bread, never tasted water, but they ate it without a single complaint, sipping the water one by one and looking up with faces bright with astonished joy. Then she herded the smallest of them ahead of her, and they all trooped after her to the garret, where she laid them down to sleep on soft piled straw, tucking each one in with her own gentle hands.

The children passed a night tangled in unrest, and carried a wild, unshakable tale of its terrors out into the dawn. No one could untangle what bled over between their waking fear and the nightmares that dragged them under: awake or asleep, no moment slipped free of that lurking dread. All night long, something moved in the walls of the cottage—silent, terrible, locked away from their knowing. No sound ever rose to wake them; the darkness and the silence were one, and that silence itself was the terror. They swore a wind that shook the whole house like a shivering horse’s pelt filled every empty space, yet it made not a single moan in their chamber, and passed like a sob that never found a voice. They slipped into sleep, then jolted wide awake certain the house was flooding with the same sweet water they had drunk at Mara’s table: it rose from the foundations, swelled higher and higher, until the garret brimmed up to the very roof. It made no more sound than that silent wind, and when it receded they woke dry, warm, and shivering.
The next time they opened their eyes, every inch of air inside and outside the cottage was thrumming with cats. They swarmed up the walls, across the floors, over the covers of their bed, and the children felt their sharp tiny claws pricking through the soft nightgowns Lady Mara had dressed them in—yet not a single scratch marked any of their skin when the sun came up. Through the whole dark, the only sound that broke was a far-off, guttural howl, the cry of the enormous ancient great-grandmother cat that roamed the desert. The children were sure she was calling her brood, for that instant every last cat went still, and the silence snapped back over them again. They fell fast asleep not long after, and did not wake until the first pink of sunrise streaked the sky.

That was the children’s story. But I never left the side of the veiled Mara and the princess Lilith through the whole long night. Some of what unfolded I saw with my own eyes; more I felt thrumming in my bones; and there were layers of it no eye could ever pierce, no heart could ever fully grasp.
The second Mara herded the children out of the room, I spotted the white leopardess we had thought we left far behind us, cowering in the furthest corner, every muscle locked in mortal terror of what she knew was coming. A lamp stood atop the high chimney-piece, and at times the room swam with long, wavering lamp-shadows, at others with vague, cloudy unbodied forms drifting at the edges of sight. Lilith lay motionless on the settle pushed against the wall, as if she had not moved a finger or shifted an inch in hours. It was a waiting thick with fear.
When Mara stepped back into the room, she dragged the settle bearing Lilith’s prone body to the very center of the chamber, then sat across from me on the opposite side of the hearth, where a small, low fire burned between us. Something terrible was drawing nearer. The cloudy presences flickered and shook. A silvery creature shaped like a slowworm crawled out from their mist, crossed the clay floor slow as molasses, and slipped straight into the heart of the fire. We all sat motionless. The hours dragged on, midnight creeping closer, and still no change came. The night was so still not a rustle stirred the flame, not a crack split a board or beam. Now and then I felt a slow, heavy heave—but I could not tell if it rippled through the earth, the air, the dark waters under the ground, my own body, or my soul. A dreadful weight of coming judgment settled over me, but I felt no fear: I had ceased to care for anything but the thing that had to be done.
Then midnight struck.
The veiled woman rose, turned toward the settle, and slowly unwound the long swathes of cloth that hid her face. They pooled soft on the floor, and she stepped over them. Lilith’s feet pointed toward the hearth; Mara went to the head of the settle, turned, and stood behind it. That was when I saw her face. It was lovely beyond words—pale, sorrowful, sorrow rooted so deep in her heart and soul it could never curdle into unhappiness. Great tears rolled down her cheeks, which she wiped away with the hem of her robe. Her face settled into a perfect stillness, and she wept no more. But for the pity written in every line of her expression, she would have looked stern, almost unyielding. She laid her hand on Lilith’s hair, which grew low on her sallow forehead, stooped, and breathed on that cold brow. The withered body shuddered.
“Will you turn away from the wicked things you have done so long?” Mara asked, her voice soft as a lullaby.
Lilith did not answer. Mara repeated the question, same gentle, inviting tone. Still no sign that she had heard. She spoke the words a third time. Then the seeming corpse parted her lips, and words came that were not shaped of sound at all, but settled clear and whole in my mind:
“I will not. I will be myself, and no other.”
“Alas, you are another now, not your true self. Will you not be restored to the one you were made to be?”
“I will be what I choose to call myself now.”
“If you were made whole again, would you not make what amends you can for all the misery you have caused?”
“I will act after my own nature.”
“You do not know your own nature. Your nature is good. You only choose evil now.”
“I will do as my Self pleases—as my Self desires.”
“You will do as the Shadow overshadowing your true Self moves you to do.”
“I will do whatever I will to do.”
“You have killed your own daughter, Lilith.”
“I have killed thousands. She was mine to take.”
“She was never yours, any more than you are another’s.”
“I belong to no one but myself. My daughter is mine.”
“Then alas—your hour has come.”
“I do not care. I am what I am. No one can take myself from me.”
“You are not the Self you imagine yourself to be.”
“So long as I feel myself to be what it pleases me to think I am, I do not care. My own thought makes me me. My own idea of myself is me. No other will make me!”
“But another made you, and can compel you to see what you have made of yourself. Soon you will no longer be able to pretend to yourself that you are anything but what He sees you. You will not much longer find satisfaction in your own idea of who you are. You feel that change coming now, do you not?”
“No one ever made me. I defy that Power to unmake me, a free woman! You are His slave, and I defy you! You may be able to torture me, but you will never force me to do anything against my will!”
“Such compulsion would be worthless. But there is a light that runs deeper than the will, that lights up the dark behind it. That light can change your will, make it truly yours—no longer the Shadow’s. The creating Will can pour itself into the created, and redeem it.”
“That light will never enter me. I hate it! Begone, slave!”
“I am no slave. I love that light, and will alongside the deeper will that made mine. There is no slave but the creature that wills against its creator. Who is a slave but she who cries ‘I am free’ yet cannot stop existing?”
“You speak foolishness from a cowering heart! You think I am in your power. I defy you. I hold myself against you. You cannot change what I choose to be. I will never be what you say I am!”
“I am sorry. You must suffer.”
“—But be free?”
“She alone is free who would set others free. She who would enslave does not love freedom. She is a slave herself. Every life, every will, every heart that ever crossed your path you tried to subdue. You are the slave of every slave you ever made—so deep a slave you do not even know it. Now see your own self.”
Mara stepped back two paces from the settle. A soundless presence, like the roar of a thousand unseen flames, filled every corner of the house—the same force that had felt to the children like that silent wind. I turned involuntarily to the hearth, and saw the fire was nothing but a still, small, motionless glow. The silvery slowworm crawled out of it now, white-hot, bright as incandescent silver, the living beating heart of primal fire. It crept slow across the floor, toward the settle, then up its wooden legs, and laid itself at Lilith’s bare bony feet, as if reluctant to go further. No pain crossed her face. The wood of the settle did not scorch where it lay. It crept slow up her robe, to her bosom, and vanished into the folds. Lilith’s face stayed stone-calm, eyelids closed as if over dead eyes. For long minutes nothing happened.
Then fine dewdrops beaded on her dry parchment skin, swelled to the size of seed pearls, ran together, and poured down her face in streams. I lunged forward to snatch the glowing worm from her withered chest and crush it under my foot, but Mara stepped between us, and drew open the edges of her robe. There was no serpent there, no searing burn mark. The creature had slipped straight through the center of the black mark on her chest, piercing through joints and marrow, straight to the secret core of her heart. Lilith gave one great writhing shudder.
“She is seeing herself,” Mara said, laying a hand on my arm to draw me three paces back from the settle.
All at once Lilith arched her whole body upward like a bow, then sprang to her feet, standing straight. The horror on her face made me tremble, terrified she would open her eyes and the sight of them would shatter me. Her bosom heaved but no breath came out. Her hair hung heavy, dripping with the sweat of her torment, then stood out from her head, sparking, then fell again to pour that bitter sweat on the floor. I would have thrown my arms around her, but Mara stopped me.
“You cannot go near her,” she said. “She is far, far away from us, lost in the hell of her own unvarnished self-consciousness. The central fire of the universe is flooding her now with the full knowledge of good and evil, the full knowledge of exactly what she is. She sees now the good she never became, and the evil she chose. She knows she herself is the fire she is burning in, though she does not yet know the Light of Life is the heart of that same fire. Her torment is that she cannot escape being what she has made herself. Do not fear for her. She is not forsaken. No gentler path to healing was left. Wait and watch.”
I could not tell if five minutes or five years passed before she finally flung herself face down on the floor. Mara went to stand over her, and large tears fell from her eyes onto the woman who had never once wept of her own will.
“Will you change your way?” Mara asked at last.
“Why did He make me like this?” Lilith gasped. “I would have made myself so different! I am glad He made me and not I myself! He is the only one to blame for what I am! He meant me to know how worthless I am, to be miserable! I will not be made any longer!”
“Then unmake yourself,” Mara said softly.
“Alas, I cannot! You know it, you mock me! I have agonized a thousand times to cease to exist, but that tyrant forces me to stay! I curse Him! Now let Him kill me!” The words came in gushes, like water spouting from a dying fountain.
“If He had not made you at all,” Mara said, slow and gentle, “you would not even have the power to hate Him. But He did not make you like this. You made yourself what you are. Take heart. He can remake you.”
“I will not be remade!”
“He will not change you. He will only restore you to what you were at the first.”
“I will not be anything He made.”
“Are you not willing to set right all the wrong you have done?”
She lay silent, and the edge of her suffering seemed to soften.
“If you are willing, climb back onto the settle.”
“I will not,” she forced out, through clenched teeth.
A wind woke inside the cottage, blowing without sound, without weight, and a silent water began to rise that had no lap to its ripples, no sob in its swell. It was cold, but it did not numb. It touched no one of my senses, but I knew it was rising. I watched it lift Lilith, float her, unable to resist, and set her soft on the settle before it sank swiftly away.
The strife in her soul began anew, thoughts of accusation and excuse warring fiercer than before. Lilith’s soul lay bare to the torture of that pure penetrating inner light. She began to moan, to sigh deep ragged sighs, then murmur as if talking to a split, divided part of herself. Her old queendom was no longer whole; it was split against itself. One moment she exulted as if she had defeated her worst enemy, and wept; the next she writhed as if caught in the embrace of a friend she hated, and laughed like a demon. At last she began to tell a story of herself, in a language so strange and full of shadowy forms I could only catch fragments here and there. It felt like the oldest, first form of a language I knew well, and the memories it wove belonged to old half-forgotten dreams I had once had, that I never could quite recall. Bits of it touched on the scattered pages Adam had read from the broken manuscript, and referenced forces, vices, old influences I had no name for. When she fell silent, her hair sparked and dripped with that tormented sweat again.
I gave Mara a pleading look.
“Those are not the tears of repentance,” she said. “True tears gather in the eyes. These are far more bitter, and not half so healing. Self-loathing is not sorrow. But it is a step on the road home. When the prodigal son falls into his father’s arms, he forgets the self he loathes. It will be the same for her.”
She stepped closer. “Will you restore everything you wrongfully took?”
“I have taken nothing,” Lilith forced out through her pain, “that I did not have the right to take. My power to take it was proof of my right.”
Mara stepped back. Gradually I became aware of an invisible darkness, more terrible than anything we had faced that night. A horrible positive Nothingness, a living Negation, folded around Lilith. The edge of its not-being brushed me, and for one ghastly instant I stood alone with Absolute Death. It was not the absence of everything I felt, but the presence of Nothing.
Lilith hurled herself from the settle to the floor with a great, bitter scream. It was the recoil of all Being from total Annihilation.
“For pity’s sake,” she shrieked, “tear my heart out—but let me live!”
At once a perfect calm fell over her, over all of us, soft as a summer night. Suffering had almost filled the brim of her life’s cup, and some unseen hand had tipped it empty. She raised her head, half rose, and looked around. For a moment she stood straight, with the air of a conqueror: she thought she had won. She had faced every spiritual foe, and beaten them. She raised her withered arm above her head, a song of unholy triumph rising in her throat—when her eyes froze wide in a ghastly stare.
What was she seeing?
I looked, and saw. Cast from an unseen mirror in heaven, two figures stood before her: the reflection of the twisted, withered thing she had made of herself, and beside it a form of blinding, splendent beauty—the self she had been created to be. She trembled, and sank helpless back to the floor. She knew both now.
The rest of the night she lay completely motionless. When the gray dawn seeped into the room, she rose, turned to Mara, and spoke with a strange prideful humility.
“You have conquered. Let me go into the wilderness and bewail myself.”
Mara saw her submission was not feigned—but it was not yet real. She regarded her a long moment, and said, “Then start by setting right the first wrong you did.”
“I do not know how,” Lilith said, and there was fear in her face, as if she already knew what the answer would be.
“Open your hand. Let the thing you have been clutching tight for all these years go.”
A fierce refusal fought to burst out of her, but she held it prisoner.
“I cannot. I no longer have the power. Open it for me.” She held out her hand, which looked more like a clawed paw than a human hand. It was obvious she could not force it open.
Mara did not even glance down at it. “You must open it yourself,” she said, quiet and steady.
“I told you I cannot!”
“You can, if you will. Not all at once, but with slow, persistent effort. What you did with it, you do not yet truly wish undone—you do not yet intend to undo it.”
“You think that, no doubt,” Lilith snapped, a flash of her old insolence breaking through, “but I know for a fact I cannot open my hand!”
“I know you better than you know yourself. I know you can. You have already opened it just a little, many times before. It will hurt, it will be hard, but you can pry it all the way open. At worst, you could beat it open until the fist unclenches. I beg you: gather your strength, and open it wide.”
“I will not waste effort on something I know is impossible. That would be the act of a fool.”
“Which you have played your whole life! Oh, how hard you are to teach!”
Defiance flooded back onto her face. She turned her back on Mara. “I know what you have been tormenting me for! You have not succeeded, and you never will. You will find I am far stronger than you think. I will still be mistress of myself! I am still what I have always known myself to be—queen of Hell, and mistress of all worlds!”
Then the most fearful thing of all unfolded. I did not know what it was then. I only knew I could never have imagined

The Mother of Sorrows pushed herself to her feet, drew her veil tight over her face, and glided off to rouse the Little Ones. They slept so still they might have lain unmoved through a hundred cycles of the long twilight, but the second her voice brushed the edges of their dreams, they sprang upright, bright and sharp as if they had been newly spun out of starlight. Chattering soft, merry nonsense, they pattered down the stairs after her, until she led them to the chamber where Princess Lilith lay, tears still leaking slow down her cheeks as she slept.
Their bright, laughing faces smoothed over into quiet wonder. They glanced from the princess’s face out at the streaking rain lashing the window, then back to her glistening cheeks.
“The sky is falling apart!” one piped up.
“The white juice is running out of the princess!” another breathed, awed.
“Are they rivers?” Odu asked, leaning in to stare at the tiny, glimmering streams carving paths down her hollow cheekbones.
“Aye,” Mara answered. “The most wonderful rivers in all the worlds.”
“But I thought rivers were bigger! They rush and roar like a whole crowd of Little Ones yelling games!” Odu protested, glancing over at me, the only one who’d ever told him tales of waterways far from the desert.
“Look out at the rivers falling from the sky,” Mara said, nodding to the downpour. “They’re coming down to wake all the hidden waters under the earth. Soon rivers will run everywhere, loud and giddy as a hundred thousand happy children. How glad they’ll make you, little ones! You’ve never seen them, you don’t know how alive water is.”
“The sky is glad the princess is finally good,” Odu said. “That’s what the rain is.”
“Are her tears the princess’s gladness?” Luva asked. “They’re not juice, they’re not red.”
“They’re the juice hidden deep inside the juice,” Mara told her.
Odu poked his own eye with one finger, stared at the wet tip of it, and shook his head.
“The princess won’t bite anyone now, will she?” Luva said.
“No,” Mara said. “She never will again. Now we have to take her closer to her real home.”
“Is that place a nest?” Sozo asked.
“A very big nest, yes. But we stop somewhere first before we get there.”
“What place?”
“It’s the largest room in all this world. But I think it’s filling up, soon it’ll be too full of little nests to hold anything else. Go get your clumsies—your riding packs.”
“Are there cats there?”
“Not one. The nests are stuffed so full of lovely dreams no cat could ever squeeze through the doors.”
“We’ll be ready in one minute!” Odu yelped, and darted out the door, the rest of the boys tumbling after him, all except Luva.
Lilith was awake now, listening, a sad, faint smile on her face.
“Her rivers are running so fast,” Luva said, rooted at her side, unable to look away from the princess’s face. “Her whole robe is soaked, the clumsies won’t like sitting on it!”
“They won’t mind,” Mara said. “Those tears are so clean they scrub the whole world bright.”
I’d fallen asleep by the hearth, but I’d been awake and listening for some time, and now I stood up.
“Time to mount, Mr. Vane,” our hostess said.
“Tell me—isn’t there a way to avoid the monster channels and their den entirely?” I asked.
“There’s an easy, safe path across the dried riverbed I can show you,” she answered. “But you will have to pass through the horde of the monsters once more.”
“I fear for the children,” I said.
“Fear won’t come within a mile of them,” she promised.
We stepped out of the cottage. The great elephants and our horses were waiting, huddled quiet by the door. Odu was already perched on the neck of one of the two elephants that would carry Lilith between them. I swung up onto Lona’s horse, and Mara handed Lona’s soft, sleeping body up to me to cradle in my arms. When Mara came back out with Lilith, a little cry of delight burst from the children: the princess wasn’t wrapped in any shroud, her face was bare, and she was more beautiful than any of them had ever seen. They stared, entranced, and forgot all about taking her from Mara, but the elephants lifted her gently with their trunks—one curled around her waist, one around her knees—and between Mara’s guiding hands, laid her stretched out across the padded platform strung between their backs.
“Why does the princess have to leave?” a small boy asked. “She could stay here and keep being good!”
“She both wants to go and doesn’t want to go. We’re helping her,” Mara said. “She couldn’t stay good here. We’re taking her where she’ll get the help she needs to pry open that hand she’s kept clenched shut for a thousand years.”
“That long? Then she’s learned to do without it, why open it now?”
“Because her fist is closed tight around something that was never hers to keep.”
“Lady Mara, can we have some of your extra-dry bread before we leave?” Luva asked.
Mara smiled, and brought out four whole loaves and a massive jug of cold water.
“We’ll eat it while we walk,” they said, but they drank the water first, gurgling it down with glee.
“I think this must be elephant-juice,” one of them announced. “It’s making me so strong I could run all the way to the sky rivers.”
We set out, the Lady of Sorrows walking at our head, brighter than the sun, her white leopardess padding close at her heels. I thought she was only going to lead us to the cross-path across the channels, but soon it was clear she was coming all the way with us. I would have dismounted to offer her my horse, but she waved me off.
“I carry no burden,” she said. “I’ll walk with the children.”
It was the softest, fairest morning I’d ever seen. The sun blazed bright, the wind blew sweet as honey, but they couldn’t wake the dead desert to life—there was not a drop of water anywhere in the sand. We crossed the rutted channels without a single misstep, the Little Ones gamboling around Mara the whole way, and we didn’t reach the crest of the ridge overlooking the monster burrow until the sun was already slipping below the horizon. I made the Little Ones climb up onto their elephants’ necks then, because the moon might rise late, and I couldn’t quite shake my worry for them. The Lady of Sorrows led the way at my side, the two elephants carrying Lilith at the center of our little procession, the leopardess bringing up the rear. Just as we reached the edge of that cursed shallow basin, the moon peeked over the horizon, lighting up the flat, still ground in front of us like polished glass.
Mara stepped first into the basin. Her footsteps, and the hooves of my horse, made no ripple, drew no stir from the earth below. But the second the two elephants carrying Lilith stepped onto that seemingly solid ground, the whole surface began to heave and boil. The entire hellish brood of that accursed nest woke screaming. Monsters burst up from every side: long snaking necks, jaws full of needle teeth, writhing knotty tentacles by the hundreds, all surging straight for Lilith. She lay rigid, frozen in a paroxysm of terror, not daring to twitch a single finger. I doubt the horde even saw the Little Ones. Not one of the abominations touched a child. Not one filthy claw or slavering maw crossed the living rampart Mara had drawn around the princess.
“Little Ones, keep your elephants pressed tight around the princess!” I called. “Be brave, they won’t hurt you.”
“What won’t hurt us? We don’t know what we’re supposed to be brave about!” they called back, and I realized with a jolt that they couldn’t see a single one of the horrors writhing all around us.
“Never mind that, then,” I told them. “Just stay close.”
They were armored in their innocent blindness. What they could never perceive could never harm them. The hideous things I saw that night no words could ever describe.
Mara was a few paces ahead of me when a bodiless, bouncing head popped out of the dirt right between us. The white leopardess lunged out from under the elephants to tear it apart, but it twisted its face into a loathsome grimace, howled, spun in a full circle, and dove back into the dirt before she could reach it. With Lona’s soft, sleeping weight warm in my arms, the taint of death clinging to me kept me from fear, and I watched the whole horde unflinching. Mara walked on before me, the leopardess now padding close at her heels, shivering, for the air had turned bitter cold, when the ground to my left began to heave. A low wave of living earth slithered toward us, rising higher and higher, until a horrific head with fleshy tubes wriggling where its hair should be slid out of it, gaped a great oval maw, and snapped at me. The leopardess lunged, but bit nothing but empty air. A second later, a giant snake head shot up right under our feet, eyes blazing with a sick, watery light. The leopardess attacked it, and came away with nothing in her jaws. She charged a third monster with the same ferocity, and failed again, then fell back sullen, no longer wasting her fury on the phantoms.
I understood the peril we were in, and kicked my horse to speed the crossing—all the more so when I saw the moon acting strange. It would rise a little, then dip back down a full breadth of its own size, the arc of its path so low it looked like it was going to fall right out of the sky. We were almost across, when a long neck rose up between us and the far bank, topped with what looked like the bloom of some Stygian lily: the head of a rotting corpse, mouth half open and crammed full of sharp canine teeth. I rode on. It retreated, then slid aside. Mara stepped out onto the solid far shore, but the leopardess between us, roused one last time, turned and lunged straight for the monster’s throat.
I stayed where I was, making sure the elephants, the children, and Lilith all made it safe to the bank. When I turned back to look for the leopardess, the moon dropped entirely. For one split second I saw the great white cat and the snakemonster thrashing tangled in a cloud of dust, then absolute darkness swallowed them. My horse reared, trembling, and bounded after the elephants in three long leaps. As I caught up with them, a shapeless, quivering mass of jelly dropped right onto Lilith’s chest. A white dove fell out of the dark right after it, stabbing the jelly over and over with its beak. The thing gave a squelching, sucking whimper, and fell off her onto the ground.
Then I heard a woman’s voice talking to Mara, and I recognized it instantly.
“I fear she is dead,” Mara said.
“I will send to find her,” the Mother answered. “But why would you fear for her, or any soul? Death cannot harm one who dies doing the work they were sent to do.”
“I will miss her sorely. She is good, and wise. But I would not have her live past her appointed hour.”
“She went down with the wicked, and she will rise with the righteous. We will see her again before long.”
“Mother,” I called, though I could not see her in the dark, “we have come to you many, and most of us are Little Ones. Will you have room for all of us?”
“Every one of you is welcome,” she answered. “Sooner or later all will be Little Ones, for every living thing must sleep in my house. It goes well for those who come to sleep young, and willing. My husband is even now making up Lilith’s couch. She is not young, nor quite willing, but it is a good thing indeed that she has come at last.”
Their voices faded as Mara and her mother walked off together into the dark. But we saw a faint glimmer of light bobbing far ahead, and we stumbled across the moor toward it. Adam stood in the cottage door holding a candle, waving to guide us in, talking to Eve, who was behind him setting bread and wine out on the table.
“Happy children,” I heard her say, “to have already looked on the face of my daughter! Surely her face is the fairest in all the great worlds.”
When we reached the door, Adam welcomed us almost gaily. He set the candle down on the threshold and walked over to the elephants, meaning to carry Lilith inside himself, but she pushed him away, shoved the two elephants apart, and stood straight up between them. The elephants stepped back, leaving her face to face with the husband she had once abandoned. She looked gaunt, unlovely, ashamed—but not submissive. He looked at her, and a welcome shone so bright in his eyes it cut right through their usual severity.
“We have waited a very long time for you, Lilith,” he said.
She said nothing. Eve and her daughter stepped to the door.
“The mortal enemy of my children!” Eve murmured, radiant in her soft beauty.
“Your children are no longer in any danger from her,” Mara said. “She has turned away from evil.”
“Do not trust her too quickly, Mara,” her mother said. “She has deceived thousands.”
“But you will show her the mirror of the Law of Liberty, mother, so she can step inside it and stay there? She agrees to open her hand and give back what she stole. Will not the great Father welcome her home to inheritance with all his other children?”
“I do not know Him,” Lilith whispered, her voice thin with fear and doubt.
“That is why you are so miserable,” Adam said.
“I will go back where I came from!” she cried, wringing her hands, and turned to run.
“That is exactly where I want you to go—back to Him you came from,” Adam said. “When you were in your agony, didn’t you cry out for Him?”
“I cried out for Death! To escape Him, and you!”
“Death is already on his way to lead you to Him. You know nothing of Death, or the Life that lives inside Death. Both are your friends. I am dead, and I want you to die too, because I live, and I love you. You are tired, and heavy laden. Are you not ashamed at last? Has the thing you corrupted become a loathsome weight to you? Would you go on living in that disgrace forever? You can never stop existing. Will you not be restored, and at last *be* fully alive?”
She stood silent, her head bowed.
“Father,” Mara said, “pick her up in your arms, carry her to her couch. There she will open her hand, and die into life.”
“I will walk,” the princess said.
Adam turned and led the way. Lilith stumbled slowly after him into the cottage. Eve came to me where I sat on my horse, Lona still in my arms, reached up, and took the little girl from me to carry her inside. I dismounted, and the Little Ones clambered down off their elephants. The horse and the great elephants stood shivering, until Mara went to each of them, patting their necks, stroking their flanks. One by one they sank to the floor and fell asleep. Mara led us into the cottage, and gave the Little Ones bread and wine from the table. Adam and Lilith stood silent by the far wall. Eve came back out of the chamber of death where she had laid Lona down, and offered bread and wine to the princess.
“Your beauty slays me. I do not want food. I want death,” Lilith said, and turned away from her.
“This food will help you die,” Eve said. But Lilith would not taste a bite.
“If you will not eat or drink, Lilith,” Adam said, “come see the place I have prepared for you to lie in peace.”
He led her through the door that opened into the House of Death. She followed, quiet at last—until her foot crossed the threshold, and she wrenched it back, pressing a hand to her chest, as if some cold immortal thing had stabbed right through her. A wild blast howled down on the roof, then died away to a soft moan. She was ghastly pale, shaking with terror.
“It is him,” her lips formed, no sound coming out. I read the words.
“Who, princess?” I whispered.
“The great Shadow,” she breathed.
“He cannot come in here,” Adam said. “He can hurt no one here. Even he is under my authority.”
“Are all the children in the house safe?” Lilith asked, and at that one word, Eve’s heart softened completely to her.
“He has never dared touch a child,” Eve said. “And you never hurt a child, either. Your own daughter you only sent to a beautiful sleep—she was already long dead when you struck her. Now Death will be your atonement. You will sleep side by side, and wake together.”
“Wife,” Adam said, “let us tuck all the children into their beds first, so she can see they are safe.” He turned back to fetch them.
The second he was gone, the princess dropped to her knees before Eve, clinging to her knees. “Beautiful Eve, please make your husband kill me. He will listen to you. I want to, but I cannot pry my hand open. I cannot do it.”
“You cannot truly die until you open it. Killing you won’t help you,” Eve said. “No one but the Shadow can kill you, and anyone he kills never knows they are dead. They live to do his will, and think they are acting for themselves.”
“Then show me my grave. I am so tired I cannot live another minute. I have to go to the Shadow—even though I do not want to!” She could not understand. She struggled to stand, and collapsed at Eve’s feet. Eve lifted her up, and carried her deeper into the dark chamber.
I followed Adam, Mara, and the Little Ones inside the great House of Death. We passed Eve carrying Lilith, and went farther still.
“You will never go to the Shadow,” we heard Eve say behind us. “Right now his head

The hush of the death-hall hung soft around me, silvered by the faint luminescence of the still forms resting on their couches, when I turned to Eve, my voice raw and worn thin of all its old stubborn pride. “Mother, the couch next to Lona sits empty. I know I am unworthy of even a place here, but may I not sleep this night in your chamber with my dead? Will you pardon both my cowardice and the foolish self-confidence that led me astray, and take me in? I give myself up. I am sick of the weight of my own soul, and I would fain sleep that deep, unbroken rest.”

“The couch beside Lona was prepared for you long before you spoke the wish,” she answered, her smile soft as moonlit moss. “But there is a work waiting to be done before you may rest.”

“I am ready,” I said at once.
Her smile widened, warm and teasing. “How do you know you are equal to the task you do not even know?”
“Because you require it of me,” I replied. “What is it?”

She turned to Adam, his face lined with the weight of uncounted eons of life and loss. “Is he forgiven, husband?”
“From the very core of my heart.”
“Then tell him what he must do.”

Adam turned to Mara, where she sat quiet, her hands folded in her lap. “Give me that hand, my child,” he said tenderly, closing his calloused, soil-stained fingers around hers. He rose, nodding toward me. “Come, we will go to the cottage, and there I will instruct you.”
As we walked the short path, a sudden storm blast tore through the air, loud with the thunder of vast, leathery wings beating against the hall roof, before it died away in a low, keening moan like the cry of a wounded giant. When the cottage door of rough-hewn oak clicked shut behind us, he seated himself on a weathered wooden stool, the scent of turned earth and dried rose cuttings clinging to every beam.
“You will remember the dry rock you climbed after leaving Mara’s house,” he said, “the one pocked with the worn grooves of a cataract that ceased to flow before the first human heart ever beat? Climb that rock now, and step deep into the sandy desert beyond. Do not go more than a dozen paces before you lie down and press your ear to the sand. If you hear the faint murmur of water beneath, go a little farther and listen again. If the sound follows you, you are on the right path. Every few yards, stop, lie flat, and hearken. If the murmur fades entirely at any point, you have wandered off course: turn your head and listen in every direction until you pick the sound up again. Keep to that thrumming undercurrent, never retrace your steps, and the sound will grow louder and louder until it hums in your very bones. That is the spot you seek. Dig with the spade I will give you, dig until you break through to damp, living sand. Lay the hand there, cover it smooth to the level of the desert, and come home.
“Take heed, and carry the hand with infinite care. Never set it down, no matter how safe the place may seem. Let no other living thing touch it. Do not stop, nor turn aside for any obstacle that tries to bar your way. Never look behind you. Speak to no one, answer no call, walk straight onward. The night is still pitch dark, and dawn is hours away, but you must set out at once.”
He pressed the wrapped, warm weight of the hand into my palm, then fetched a dented iron spade, its wooden handle worn smooth from decades of use. “This is my gardening spade,” he said. “With it I have coaxed a thousand small, lovely things out of the dark dirt to meet the sun.”

I stepped out into the night. The cold bit through my thin tunic, the dark so thick I could not see my own outstretched hand. The path to the cataract crag was treacherous even at high sun, lined with sharp heather and loose jagged rocks, and I feared a single misstep would break my ankle before I had gone a hundred paces. But the very moment I took my first stride, a faint cool bioluminescent blue light seeped up from the loam beneath my feet, illuminating every crack and stone before my next step fell. I crossed the heath without a single stumble. The bad burrow of the shadow-things lay utterly still, not a ripple marring its surface, not a snarling head rising to snap at my heels as I crossed. Then the moon peeked over the crag edge, silvering the rest of the path before me. Toward early dawn I was nearly across the dry, empty channels of the old riverbed, and not far, I judged, from Mara’s old cottage.

The moon hung low, nearly touching the horizon, the sun still hours from rising, when I saw a figure wrapped head to toe in a veil of glowing moonlit mist, blocking the narrow path between two granite outcrops. I kept walking as if I saw nothing. The veil fell away.
“Have you forgotten me already?” Lilith said, her voice honeyed, sharp under the sweetness.
I did not hesitate, did not answer, walked straight onward.
“You meant to leave me rotting in that horrible sepulchre, did you not?” she snarled. “Do you not yet understand that I can go wherever I please? Take my hand. I am alive, just as you are.”
I was half a breath from saying “Give me your left hand,” to test her, but bit my tongue and held my peace, stepping steadily forward.
“Give me my hand,” she shrieked so loud the rocks rang with the sound, “or I will tear you to pieces. You are mine!”
She launched herself at me. I shuddered from crown to toe, but did not falter. She passed right through me like cold smoke, and she was gone.

A moment later, a full phalanx of armed men, their armour glinting even in the dim moonlight, marched in measured tread along the path, filling it edge to edge. I walked straight through their ranks, and could not tell if they stepped aside to let me pass, or if they were never solid to begin with. They turned, and followed me. I heard their heavy bootfalls thudding right at my heels, the clank of their armour ringing in my ears, but I never once glanced over my shoulder. Little by little, the sound of their steps and the clash of their metal died away to nothing.

A little farther on, the path fell into deep shadow, and I saw a woman muffled in a wool shawl, seated right across the narrowest stretch of track so that there was no way to go around her.
“Ah,” she said, her voice soft and warm, like the memory of a loving home. “You are come at last! I have waited here more than an hour for you. You have done so well. Your trial is over. My father Adam sent me to meet you, so you might rest a little on the way. Give me the charge you carry, lay your head in my lap, and I will watch over both until the sun is well risen. I am not always bitterness, not to every man.”
Her words were a temptation sharper than any I had yet faced. I was so weary my legs were shaking under me, and it sounded so real. What if I was being a fool, trampling over the Lady of Sorrow herself for the sake of following the letter of Adam’s command? My heart lurched so hard I thought it would burst my ribs. But my will hardened against my own soft longing. I bit down on my tongue to stop any unwise reply, and kept walking. If Adam had truly sent her, he would not blame me for clinging to the order he gave. And Mara, the real Mara, would never love me less for refusing to let even her face turn me from the task.
When I was a foot from her, she pulled the shawl from her face. Her beauty was greater than any I had ever seen, but those eyes were not Mara’s. No illusion could ever hold the quiet, tender grief that lived in Mara’s gaze. I stepped forward, and my foot found empty air. The phantom was no more solid than smoke.

Then a great towering Shadow stepped in front of me, blocking the whole path. I thought he wore a war helmet at first, but as I drew nearer I saw the “helmet” was his own head, twisted and distorted so it bore only a faint, warped resemblance to a human face. A gust of wind hit me, dank and rotting, stinking like the inside of a charnel house. My knees went weak, all firmness fled my joints, and I trembled so hard I nearly collapsed in a helpless heap. It felt not as if I passed through him, but as if he passed through me. For one blinding, terrible second, I knew exactly what it felt like to be one of the damned, trapped in endless, cold dark.
Then a soft wind blew, warm as the very first breath of spring after a thousand years of winter, and the pale pink glow of dawn spilled over the dunes ahead of me.

My path led me past Mara’s cottage. Its door stood propped wide open, and on the wooden table inside I saw a crusty loaf of bread and a pitcher of clear, cool water. No wailing echoed from its hollow rooms, no snarls of the lost rose from its corners. I reached the great precipice that bore the worn marks of the vanished cataract, climbed its smooth, water-worn face, and stepped out into the endless expanse of the desert.
I stopped over and over, pressing my ear to the shifting sand, hunting for the faint murmur of trapped water, until I found the spot where the thrumming was loud enough to hum through my teeth. I slung the hand I carried around my neck to keep it safe, and began to dig. The sand was loose, so I had to carve a wide pit, and the work dragged on for what felt like hours, until at last I hauled up a spadeful of dark, damp sand. I set the spade down on the pit’s edge, laid the hand gently in the hollow, and saw little beads of fresh water already seeping out from between its fingers. I scrambled out of the hole, and shoveled the sand back in until the desert surface was smooth and unmarked, no trace of the pit left behind. Utterly fatigued, I dropped beside the spot, and fell fast asleep.

When I stirred awake, dark, damp earth clung to my sleeves, and the footpath I’d traced back toward the hidden grave was dissolving beneath me into sucking quicksand. The long-dormant ancient river had burst its banks, its waters swelling and shoving at the land it had once buried beneath its tides. Soon it would roar over the precipice ahead, splitting into two raging falls: one branch would pour back over the sunken orchard valley to drown it all over again, while the other would surge straight for the horde of ravenous, unthinking monsters to sweep them away, leaving the twisted Evil Wood stranded as a lone isle between the two floods. I did not linger. I set off at once for the place the others had sent me to find.
When I reached the cliff edge, I took the narrow trail between the two oncoming river branches, for I could not bear to pass without stopping at Mara’s cottage, in case she had returned at last. I ached to see her face one more time before I laid down to my long sleep, and now I knew every safe crossing of the channels, even if the rising waters outpaced me and filled them to the banks. But when I reached the cottage, it was just as I’d left it: the door hung propped open, the crust of bread and bowl of water still sat on the wooden table, and deep, unbroken silence wrapped the place inside and out. I called her name loud at the threshold, but no voice answered, so I turned and went on my way.
A short distance further, I found a gray-haired old man huddled on the sand, his shoulders heaving with sobs.
“What ails you, sir?” I asked him. “Are you left all alone?”
“I weep,” he choked out, “because she will not let me die. I went to the House of Death, and its mistress turned me away, even old as I am. If you know her, I beg you, intercede for me.”
“I cannot do that,” I told him. “She turns no one away that it is lawful for her to receive.”
“How would you know that?” he snapped. “You have never sought death! You are far too young to want it.”
“I fear your words say more than you know: if you were young again, you would not want it either.”
“Indeed I would not! And I am certain you do not want to die.”
“I may not be old enough to long for death, but I am young enough to long to truly live. That is why I am on my way now to see if she will take me in at last. You wish to die only because you do not care to live. She cannot open her door to you, for no one can truly die unless they have first learned what it means to crave life with every part of them.”
“Mock not a friendless old man with your riddles, boy!”
“Did not the Mother herself tell you something very much like that, when you came to her?”
“I think she did, but I paid no mind to her excuses.”
“Then it is plain you have not yet learned how to die, and I grieve for you. I would have been the same once, if not for the Lady of Sorrow. I may be young, but I have wept enough tears to soften stone. Let me give you counsel: go to her cottage over yonder. She is not there now, but her door stands open, and bread and water wait for you on her table. Go in, sit down, eat the bread, drink the water, and wait for her to come. She is true, and her wisdom is greater than any you have known.”
He fell to weeping all the harder, and I left him there. I feared he would not heed my words, but I knew Mara would find him soon enough.
The sun had set, and the moon had not yet risen, when I reached the territory of the monsters. The whole expanse was dead still as a carved stone, until I crossed its full breadth. Only then did a roar of churning waters rise behind me, followed by a great, deafening cry, but I did not turn my head.
By the time I neared the House of Death, the cold had sharpened into something tangible, and the darkness grew so thick I could almost press it between my fingers. The cold and the dark were one and the same, sinking straight into my bones. But Eve’s single candle glowed soft in the cottage window, guiding my steps, and kept the frost and murk from touching my heart. The door stood open, and the small front room was empty. I sat down on a bench, hit with a desolation I had never once felt through all my wanderings. Thousands of the sleeping lay only a little ways away in the back chamber, but not one of them was truly with me. I was the one who was still awake, while they slumbered, and no matter if I was alive or dead, some uncrossable rift divided us. They lived, but I was not dead enough yet to know their life, and doubt crept in unbidden. No helpers waited to lay me down beside them, and I was adrift in a loneliness I could not have imagined before that moment.
I chided myself, telling myself the solitude was only a trick of perception: I was awake, they slept, that was all. They were still right there, and soon, very soon, I would join them. I dropped Adam’s old iron spade at my feet, and its dull thud against the clay floor echoed all the way back to the chamber of sleep. A silly, childish little terror seized me, and I stared frozen at the low, coffin-shaped door leading to the sleeping hall. But father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara would be here soon, I told myself. Then I would welcome the cold world, and all the pale, quiet neighbours lying at rest. My fear melted, and I loved all the sleeping dead, just then.
Something stirred in the back room. It was no sound I knew, not a noise that travelled through air, but a soft, distant vibration that hummed through every thread of my soul. I did not need to hear it to know it was coming, drawing nearer, and a tiny, fragile hope woke in the middle of my utter desolation. The noiseless thrill reached the coffin door, and bloomed at last into a soft sound that brushed my ear. The door began to creak open slow, its hinges making a low, soft groan.
A face peeked through the narrow gap. It was Lona. Her eyes were closed, but her face was turned straight to me, and it felt as if she saw me clear as day. Her skin was pale as Eve’s, pale as Mara’s, but it did not glow like theirs did. Her voice came soft, like a sleepy night wind sifting through long grass.
“Are you coming, king?” she murmured. “I cannot rest until you are with me, gliding down the river to the great sea, and the beautiful dream-land. The sleep is full of all sorts of lovely things. Come see them.”
“Ah, my darling!” I cried. “I had no idea! I thought you were dead!”
She slumped against my chest, cold as ice frozen to marble. Her thin, white arms curled feebly around my neck, and she sighed.
“Carry me back to my bed, king,” she said. “I want to sleep. The warm air hurts, just a little. Lay me down, and cover me from it. Your bed is right next to mine. I will see you when I wake.”
I carried her into the sleeping hall, holding her tight lest she dissolve right out of my arms. She was already fast asleep by the time I laid her on her couch, and I threw myself down on the empty pallet beside her, happier than any man on the eve of his wedding day.
“Come, sweet cold,” I whispered. “Still my heart speedily.”
But instead of the frost, a soft golden light spilled across the room, and I saw Adam walking toward me. He carried no candle, but he glowed from within. He paused at the edge of Lona’s couch, looked down at her with a gentle, questioning smile, then greeted me across her sleeping form.
“We were up on the top of the hill, listening to the waters make their way down,” he said. “They will flood the monster’s den by midnight. Why did you not wait for us to come back?”
“My child could not sleep,” I answered. “She is fast asleep now.”
“She was asleep the whole time,” he said.
“But she was awake when she came to me,” I protested.
“She was dreaming of you, and wandered out of her trance to find you. You did not notice her eyes were closed the whole time, did you?”
“I realize now I did see that, but I paid it no mind.”
“If you had stopped to look back, you would have seen her asleep on her couch the whole time, and she would have vanished out of your arms.”
“That would have been terrible,” I said.
“It might feel strange to think about, but it would not have troubled you once you saw it. Your time to sleep is not quite here. You must have food first, before you lay down for good.”
“I should not have gotten into bed without your leave,” I laughed, trying to push myself up, but I found my own weight was too heavy to shift.
“There is no need for that. We will tend to you right here,” he said, stepping to my side. He bent over me, and breathed warm on my heart. At once, all the deep, bitter cold vanished, and I was warm all the way through.
Then I heard a voice I knew was the Mother’s. She sang, soft and low and sweet, and I thought she sat right beside my bed in the dark, but before her song ended, her voice soared higher and higher, climbing far above the flight of larks, higher than any human heart had ever reached. I heard every word, but only a few stayed with me:
“Many a wrong, and its curing song;
Many a road, and many an inn;
Room to roam, but only one home
For all the world to win!”
I knew I had heard that song, somewhere, a long time before.
Then Adam, Eve, and Mara all came to my couch together, carrying bread and wine, and I sat up to eat and drink with them. “You are all so good, to receive me like this,” I said, my throat tight. “I am so sorry for all the wandering, all the mistakes.”
“We always knew you would come back,” Eve said, smiling.
“How could you be sure?” I asked her.
“Because it is my job!” Mara put in, grinning. “I was born to look after all my lost, wandering brothers and sisters.”
“Every creature, no matter how far they roam, must eventually yield and lie down,” Adam said. “They were made for freedom, and no one should ever be left a slave, trapped in their half-life.”
“Will it not take so long for all the rest to find their way here?” I said.
“There is no early or late in this house,” he told me. “True time only begins the moment a man lays himself down to rest. People do not come home fast, but the women come faster. A wide, barren desert stretches between the man who lies down trying to die, and the man who lies down ready to live. The first may run all he wants, but here, there is no rush. To all of us who waited for you, you were coming home the whole time. We knew Mara would find you, and you would make your way here eventually.”
“How long has it been since my father laid down to sleep?” I asked.
“I told you years do not matter here,” Adam said. “We do not count them. Your father will wake when his morning comes. Your mother, the one lying on the couch right next to you, the one with the wounded hand, will wake long, long before your own morning comes.”
“Oh, so that *is* her!” I exclaimed.
“It is. She will grow younger and younger, until she reaches the full height of her perfect womanhood, a splendour no one can even imagine. Then she will open her eyes, see her husband on one side and her son on the other, and rise to go to a Father and a brother that are more to her than we can ever be. It is a sight that makes even her Maker glad, to see that woman wake. He sees the travail of his soul, and he is satisfied.”
He held up his candle, so the light fell soft on her sleeping, beautiful face. I lay back, and blissful drowsiness wrapped around my limbs. I felt them drape the soft white garment of the dead over my shoulders. The whole chamber glowed pale, all the sleeping faces lit with that soft inner light, and I forgot everything else. I drifted off into the deep, long rest, and never even knew that I had fallen asleep.

I drifted into consciousness of my own existence, and of a cold so deep, so infinite it felt like a living, gentle embrace. I was flooded with a bliss sharper and softer than any I could have imagined in my earthly life, a blessing I know my fumbling, waking heart can no longer fully recall. Warmth was a faint, half-forgotten memory, no more appealing than a faded dream of summer rain to a man who has never known anything but the hush of snow. The cold had smoothed every sharp edge of care, dissolved every lingering ache, folded every old sorrow into itself until nothing remained but soft, bright expectancy. Not softened, not comforted—sorrow had been swallowed whole, for the life drawing near would restore every good, every lovely thing a hundredfold. I lay still, breathing the damp, rich scent of Earth’s dark, fertile bosom, aware of the slumbering souls of primroses, daisies, snowdrops, curled patient beneath the frost waiting for spring. There are no words to do justice to that frozen, half-aware sleep: no need to stand, no need to strive, only to stretch out and rest. No tongue can tell how cold I was, and every breath drew me deeper into that cold, and every breath made me welcome it more. I grew ever less conscious of my small, individual self, ever more wrapped in a bliss no mind could map, unasked, unearned, mine simply because I existed, and my existence was rooted in a Will that dwelt at the core of mine.

Then the dreams came, crowding fast one after another.
I lay naked on the wind-scoured peak of a snow mountain, white mist billowing below me like a storm-tossed sea. The cold moon hung level with me, and above us both stretched a sky even colder, where the moon and I dwelt as equals. I was Adam, new-formed from the loam, waiting for God to lean close and breathe the first spark of life into my nostrils.
Then I was not Adam at all, but a small child curled in the bosom of a mother whose skin glowed with a whiteness no earthly light could match. Then I was a youth on a white stallion, leaping from cloud to cloud across a blue heaven, riding calm and unhurried toward some unnameable blessed end. For centuries I dreamed—for chiliads? Or was it all one single, unbroken night? Time had no hold on me then: I was far deeper in the land of thought than any mapped seven dimensions, any imagined ten senses. I think I was right where I still am, in the very heart of God.
I drifted through dim, eon-long cycles slumbering at the center of a melting glacier. The spectral moon drew nearer and nearer, the wind’s low song and the roar of a new-born torrent swelling in my ears. I lay still and listened: wind, water, moon, all three sang a hymn of quiet waiting, for redemption was drawing close. Those cycles might have lasted a million years, they might have been one single pregnant second heavy with all eternity—there was no way to tell the difference.

Then, without breaking even a thread of my quiet bliss, every wrong I had ever done rose up around me, stretching far back past the edges of my mortal memory right to the present moment. In every memory of every harm I had caused, my fully awake, conscious self was there: confessing, abjuring, grieving, making full atonement one by one to every soul I had ever hurt, ever offended, ever made carry an unneeded troubled thought. Every human I had wronged was unspeakably dear to me now, and I bowed low before each, fighting to cast the lingering weight of my offence out between us. I wept at the feet of the mother whose gentle commands I had ignored as a boy; I stood burning with bitter shame before my father, confessing the two small lies I had told him, then forgotten for decades, until they had festered in my chest until I could finally lay them at his feet. I was the eager, willing slave of every person I had ever wronged. I dreamed up a thousand grand services to make it right: for one I would build a house such as no hand had ever raised from the earth, for another I would breed horses swifter and more beautiful than any that had ever run across any world. For a third I would grow a garden no spring had ever seen, strung with still, glassy pools and alive with the glint of running water. I would write songs that made hearts swell until they ached, and tales that made listeners glow with unnameable fire. I would twist the raw forces of the universe into new channels of invention that would make them laugh out loud with the joy of unending wonder. Love owned me entirely. Love was my life. Love was, as it was for the One that made me, all in all.

Suddenly I was wrapped in a solid, lightless blackness so thick even the faint ghost of light that lingers in the back of closed eyes could not cast a single imagined glimmer. My heart feared nothing, hoped infinitely, and I was still at peace. I lay there wondering what the first light would look like when it came, what new creation it would drag in with it—when, all at once, without any conscious choice, I sat bolt upright and stared around.
The moon was slanting through the low, horizontal, crypt-like windows of the death chamber, her long pale light streaking across the stone floor. For one wild second I thought it fell across the fallen, ripening sheaves of the Great Husbandman’s harvest. But no. That harvest was gone. Not a single sacred sheaf remained. My dead were all gone. I was alone.

There were depths of desolation in that empty room I had never guessed existed. Had the ripening dead ever been real? Had I only dreamed them, and their soft, quiet loveliness? But then why these cold stone walls? Why these empty couches lined up along the stone? No—they had all risen. They were all out in the new eternal day, and they had forgotten me. They had left me behind, all alone. The tomb was ten thousand times more terrible now its inhabitants were gone. The quiet breathing of the sleepers had wrapped me in their blissful peace before; now I had no friend, no lover left near me. I sat frozen, horror-stricken, for a long minute. Once I had been alone with the moon on a mountain top high in the sky; now I was alone with her inside a vast empty cenotaph, and even she stared wildly about, searching for her own dead with a ghastly, hollow gaze. I sprang to my feet and staggered out of that terrible place.

The cottage was empty. I stumbled out into the night. The moon was gone. Even as I crossed the threshold of the death chamber, a thick wall of cloud had risen up to cover her. But a broad, shimmering glow stretched far across the heath, tangled with a ghostly, murmuring music as if the hidden moon was raining down light that plashed and splashed against the earth as it fell. I ran stumbling across the moor until I found a small, lovely lake fringed with reeds and rushes. Behind the cloud the moon gazed down on the quiet, glass-clear water, still as polished glass. But the soft, musical murmur went on, filling the air, drawing me further onward. I circled the edge of the mere and climbed the low line of hills beyond.

The sight that met my eyes took my breath away. The whole expanse I had once tramped across a hundred times on hot, aching feet, crisscrossed with the deep scored ruts and ravines of the long dry dead riverbed, was now alive with streams, torrents, glassy pools—a river deep and wide, surging full from end to end. The moon flashed bright on the crests of the waves, and the water flashed back its own white sparks, breaking everywhere where it crashed against the rocks along its bed. A great jubilant song rose from its bosom, the song of new-born, unshackled liberty. I stood staring for a long moment, and my own heart began to exult. My life had not been all a waste. I had helped set this river free. My dead were not lost. I only had to follow the water to find them. I would walk and walk and walk, following it wherever it led, until I reached the place they had gone ahead to meet. Our reunion might be thousands of years away, but at last—at long last—I would hold them again. What other reason could the floods have to clap their hands for joy? I hurried down the slope of the hill. My pilgrimage had begun. I did not know which way to turn my steps, but I would go on moving until I found my living dead.

A wide, swift torrent ran at the foot of the hills. I rushed into it, and it did not drag me under. I waded through it. The next stream I sprang across. The third I swam, and the next I waded again. I stopped every few paces to stare at the endless, flashing loveliness of the flowing water, to listen to the thousands of tiny overlapping threads of music that rose from it. Every now and then a clear, perfect melody would seem to pull itself free of the soft, dulcet roar, only to melt back into the unified, thunderous song. At moments the whole world of moving water would wash over me, not to drown me with the force of its seaward rush, but with the enormity of a silence so deep it had turned itself into sound.

As I stood lost in that delight, a hand rested light on my shoulder. I turned. A man stood before me in the prime of his strength, so beautiful he looked as if he had just stepped straight from the glad heart of the Creator, young as the One who never grows old. I stared. It was Adam. He stood tall and grand, wrapped in a flowing white robe, strands of moonlight woven through his hair.

“Father!” I cried. “Where is she? Where are all the dead? Did the great resurrection come and go while I slept? The terror of my loneliness was too heavy to bear. I could not rest without my dead, so I ran from that empty desolate chamber. Where must I go to find them?”

“You are mistaken, my son,” he answered. The soft breath of his voice was pure consolation. “You are still in the chamber of death, still lying on your couch, asleep and dreaming, with all the dead sleeping around you.”

“Alas, how am I ever supposed to know that while I am dreaming? The very best dream feels more real than waking truth.”

“When you are fully dead, you will never dream a false dream. A soul that is true cannot generate anything that is not true, and nothing false can ever enter it.”

“But father,” I faltered, “how am I supposed to tell the difference between true and false when both feel exactly equally real?”

“Do you not understand?” he returned, with a smile gentle enough to slay every sorrow of every one of his children. “You cannot perfectly tell true from false while you are not yet quite dead—nor will you ever need to, once you are quite dead, which is to say, fully alive. For then the false will never come near you at all. Believe me, at this very moment you are lying on your bed in the house of death.”

“I am trying so hard to believe you, father. I do believe you, even if I cannot see or feel the truth of what you say.”

“You are not to blame for that. And because even in a dream you choose to believe me, I will help you. Reach out your left hand, open your palm, and close it gently. You will clasp the hand of your Lona, who lies asleep right where you are, while you dream you are awake.”

I stretched my hand out. It closed around Lona’s hand—firm, soft, deathless, warm.

“But father,” I cried, “she is warm!”

“Your hand is just as warm to her. Cold is a thing that does not exist in our country. Neither of you have reached the fields of home yet, but you are both alive, and warm, and whole to each other.”

My heart flooded with joy. But a sharp, stinging doubt pricked at me a second later. “Father,” I said, “forgive me—how do I know for certain that this, too, is not just part of this lovely dream I am walking through with you right now?”

“You doubt because you love the truth. There are people who would happily believe the whole of life is nothing but a phantasm, if it meant they could live forever in a world of pleasant dreams. You are not one of those people. Be content for a little while to not know for certain. The hour will come, and it will not be long now, when, being true, you will look upon the very face of truth itself, and doubt will be dead forever. By then you will barely even remember what those false phantoms looked like. You will know things you cannot even begin to dream right now. You have not yet looked the Truth in the face—you have only ever seen it dimly, as if through a cloud.
