That thing you have never seen save in a dark mirror, that can never be known except when its own innate splendor shines straight into pure, unclouded eyes—you cannot help but doubt it now, and you are blameless for that doubt, until the day you stand face to face with it and can no longer doubt at all. But to anyone who has even once seen a shadow of the truth, and even when that shadow is gone, strives to obey it anyway— to that one the real vision, the Truth Himself, will come, and He will never leave, but abide with them forever.”

“I think I see, father,” I said. “I think I understand.”

“Then remember. Hold fast to what you have seen. Hard trials still await you, of a weight and a kind you cannot now imagine. Recall what these things looked like to you, what they meant to you. Hold fast to what you will see in the dreams that come next. Truth is all in all, and the truth of every thing is both hidden and revealed in the shape it takes when it appears before you.”

“How can that be, father?” I said, lifting my eyes to ask the question, for I had been listening with my head bowed, aware of nothing but the sound of his voice.

He was gone. There was nothing in my ears but the roaring silence of the swift-flowing waters. I stretched out my hands to search for him, but no warm answering touch met my fingers. I was alone again, adrift in the land of dreams. I knew I looked wide awake, but I believed I was still dreaming, because Adam had told me so. But even in a dream, you cannot just sit still and refuse to move until the dream grows bored and fades away. I picked up my wandering and walked onward.

I crossed a dozen more channels until I reached a wide stretch of bare rock. There, dreaming I was tired, I lay down and ached to wake up. I was just about to stand and resume my journey when I saw I was lying right beside a dark pit cut straight down into the rock, its mouth shaped exactly like the mouth of a grave. It was deep, pitch black, no bottom visible at all. I remembered the trick I had figured out as a small child, in my childhood dreams: a fall would always wake me up. Whenever I wanted a dream to end, I would climb to some high place and throw myself off, to wake safe in my bed. With one last glance at the soft peaceful sky and one at the rushing waters, I rolled myself right over the edge of the pit.

Consciousness left me for a heartbeat. When it returned, I was standing in the garret of my own house, in the tiny wooden chamber that held the hooded chair and the magic mirror. An unspeakable, blank, bleak despair flooded into me. There was an impassable abyss between me and my Lona now. A distance no chain could ever measure. Space, time, the whole gulf between different modes of being, stood around me like unscalable walls of adamant, locking me away from her. It was true I could still try to pass back through the light-door, and make the journey back to the house of the dead—all that lay between me and that chamber was a wide stretch of heath, and the pale starry night that separated me from the sun, the only thing that could open the mirror door for me, and which was now all the way on the other side of the world. But an immeasurably wider gulf lay between us now: she was still asleep, and I was wide awake. I was no longer worthy to share that holy sleep with her, and I could never hope to wake up beside her ever again. I had done wrong. I had run away from my dream. The dream was no more of my making than my life itself. I should have stayed and seen it through to the end. By fleeing it, I had left the whole holy sleep behind me.

I would go find Adam, tell him the truth, and bow to whatever decree he chose to lay on me. I crept to my bedroom, threw myself on my bed, and passed a completely dreamless night.

I rose the next morning and dragged myself listlessly toward the library. I met no one on the way. The whole house felt dead. I sat down with a book to wait for noon, and could not make a single sentence make sense. The mutilated manuscript on the masked door drifted into my view, and the sight of it turned my stomach. What did the princess and all her devilry matter to me now? I stood up and looked out the window. It was a brilliant, bright morning. The great fountain shot high into the air, then fell roaring back to its basin, the sun sitting right in its feathery crest. Not a single bird sang. Not a single living thing was visible. No raven, no librarian came near me. The whole world was dead around me. I picked up another book, sat down, and kept waiting.

Noon drew near. I climbed the stairs up to the shadowy, silent roof. I shut the door of the little wooden chamber behind me, and turned to open the door out of my dreary world. I stepped before the mirror with a heart of stone. No matter what I did, nothing worked. I pulled the chains, adjusted the hood over and over, rearranged the mirrors a dozen different ways. Nothing happened. I waited and waited to give the magic time to wake up, but it never came. The mirror stood blank, its dim old depths holding nothing but the reflection of the opposite mirror, and my own haggard, worn face staring back.

I trundled back down to the library. All the books there were hateful to me, because I had once loved them so deeply. That whole long night I lay awake from sundown to sunrise, and the next day I tried the magic door

On my fourth night in the liminal house of rest, I slipped past the last thin veil of mortal dream, and woke truly.
All around me hung soft, unbroken dark—no shadow, no void, just the heavy, nurturant dark of a womb about to quicken—and I knew at once I was no longer trapped in the looping nightmares of my old life. Every moment of slumber I’d known since arriving here had been a dream, and this, at last, was waking. My heart leaped so hard it ached, and I breathed a quiet “At last” into the hush.
When I turned my head, Lona stood beside my couch, waiting for me. I had never lost her. I had only lost the sight of her, for a little while. I never needed to grieve her so bitterly. The dark that wrapped the rest of the room could not touch her: her eyes glowed with the same quiet radiance that clung to the Mother, her whole face luminous, and her stitched grave-clothes thrumming with the light of a body reborn to resurrection, white as star-frost, glistering from every thread. She had fallen asleep a girl, cut down before her prime; she woke a woman, full and soft and ripe with the unshakable loveliness of life unbound from mortal decay. I folded her into my arms, and the warm, solid thud of her pulse against mine told me I was alive at last, alive for real.
“I woke first,” she said, smiling half in wonder.
“You did, my love,” I laughed back. “And you woke me.”
“I only looked at you and waited,” she answered.
Then a candle of condensed starlight drifted toward us through the dark, and a moment later Adam, Eve, and Mara stepped into the glow. They greeted us with a quiet, unhurried good-morning, no fanfare, no cheers—they had tended hundreds of these wakings, and knew the hush was holy.
“I hope your stretch of unlit rest was gentle,” the Mother said.
“Not very, for a long while,” I told her, “but this waking is more heavenly than anything I ever dared dream of.”
“It is only just begun,” she replied, her smile soft. “You are hardly even awake yet.”
“He is at least clothed upon with Death,” Adam put in, drawing close, “the radiant garment that makes all true life possible.” He embraced Lona, his child, then slung an arm around my shoulders, glanced for a long quiet moment at the still-sleeping princess on the far couch, and patted the great leopardess curled at her feet. “We will meet the two of you again, before long,” he said, looking first at Lona, then at me.
“Have we to die a second time?” I asked, a flicker of old mortal fear pricking at me.
Adam laughed, a warm sound like shifting bedrock, and his smile held the same light as the Mother’s. “No. You have died *into* life, and you will die no more. All you have to do now is stay dead—dead to the hollow, greedy old hungers of your mortal lives. Once you pass through the kind of dying we do here, all suffering, all decay, all cycles of unmaking are over. Now you have only to live, and live with every blessed part of you. The more fully you live, the stronger you grow to live still more.”
“But will I not grow weary, living so hard?” I said. “What if I cease to strive with all my might?”
“It needs only the will, and the strength is already there,” the Mother said. “Pure life holds no weakness to tire out. The great central Life keeps generating every spark of ours, endlessly. Those who refuse to surrender their old mortal selves die many times, die constantly, dig themselves deeper and deeper into shadow, never done with unmaking. But here? All is upward motion, and love, and gladness that never runs thin.” She fell silent, and the look she gave me said we were family, no goodbyes could ever exist between us, no distance.
Mara leaned in and kissed my forehead, grinning bright. “I told you, brother, all would be well. The next time you meet someone sunk in grief, tell them this: what will be well, is even now well.” A tiny sigh escaped her, and I heard the unspoken line beneath it: *But most of them will not believe you*. “You know who I am now, don’t you?” she finished, her smile glowing like her mother’s.
“I know you,” I said. “You are the voice crying in the wilderness long before the Baptist ever walked the earth. You are the shepherd who chases lost, wandering sheep home before the shadows rise and the dark closes in.”
“My work will end one day,” she said, “and then I will share in the gladness of the great Shepherd who sent me.”
“All through the long night, the dawn has been close at hand,” Adam said.
I tilted my head. “What is that beating of huge wings I hear, far off?”
“The Shadow is hovering,” Adam replied. “He thinks there is one here that belongs to him. But once anyone has belonged to us, they can never be his again.”
I turned to look for my mortal parents, to kiss them before we left—only to find their couches empty, save for the Little Ones who had crept into them, bold with childlike love, claiming the empty beds for their own. For a single heartbeat, that old awful nightmare of total desolation, of being trapped forever alone in an empty dark house, crashed over me, and I flinched aside.
“What is it, my heart?” Lona asked, her hand on my arm.
“Their empty places frightened me,” I said.
“They rose and left long ago,” Adam said. “They kissed you while you were still half tangled in your old mortal dreams, and whispered ‘come soon’ before they slipped away.”
“And I neither felt nor heard them,” I murmured, stunned.
“How could you, when you were still half lost in that dreary old house you remembered? You thought the dark place had gotten you again. Now go out, and find them. As for you, child,” he added, turning to Lona, “your parents will come and find you soon enough.”
The hour of our departure was at hand. Lona walked to the couch of the woman who had slain her, leaned down to press a soft, forgiving kiss to her still-sleeping cheek, then stepped into Adam’s waiting embrace.
“That kiss will draw her home, my Lona,” Adam said. “Nothing is stronger than a gift of unasked love.”
“Who were her birth parents?” Lona asked.
“My father,” Adam answered, “is her father too.”
Lona turned and laced her fingers through mine. We knelt together, humbly, to thank the three of them for guiding us through the little death that had woken us. They leaned over us, and breathed on us, warm as sunlight through spring leaves.
“Hark,” Adam said. “I hear the sun.”
I listened. It was not the tiny sun I had known on Earth. It rushed toward us with the roar of ten thousand wings, the thunder of a molten, flaming world millions of miles wide, its building swell a crescendo chord of a hundred overlapping harmonies. The three of them looked at each other and smiled, and that smile drifted off them like a three-petaled glowing flower, their family’s morning offering of thanksgiving, rising heavenward. Light spread from their mouths, their faces, seeping through their skin and shining through their garments. Before I could even speak the words *they are changing*, Adam and Eve stood before me in the full splendor of resurrection angels, and Mara was the Magdalene, bright and radiant, the first witness at the empty tomb. Adam’s face blazed like lightning, and Eve held a linen napkin that shed flakes of splendor across every surface of the room. A wind began to moan in slow, pulsing gusts.
“You hear his wings now,” Adam said. I knew he did not mean the wings of the morning. “The great Shadow is stirring to leave. Wretched creature—he is trapped entirely inside his own empty self, and he can never rest.”
“But is there not something deeper in him, something worth saving?” I asked.
“A shadow cannot exist without something solid standing in the light behind it,” he answered. He tilted his head, listening, then grinned wide, glowing. “Hark to the golden cock! For millions of years, he has stood silent and motionless on the face of the universe’s great clock. Now at last he flutters his wings. Now he will crow. Men on every world will hear his cry in their bones, at intervals, until the dawn of the eternal day.”
I listened. Far away, buried in the heart of an eon’s worth of silence, I heard the clear, jubilant outcry of his golden throat. It hurled defiance at death and all the dark, sang of infinite hope, of the calm that was coming. It was the expectation of every living creature that had ever suffered, finding a voice at last. It was the cry of a chaos that would one day be a perfect kingdom.
Then a great rush of beating wings echoed through the whole house.
“The black bat is flown!” Mara cried, joy bright in her voice.
“Amen, golden cock, bird of God!” Adam shouted, and his voice boomed through the silent house, rising up into the open sky.
At that word Amen, the Little Ones sprang up off their beds like doves of silver rising from a pile of broken potsherds, all on their knees instantly, yelling at the top of their lungs. “Crow! Crow again, golden cock!” They had seen and heard him, even in their dreams. Then each turned to their sleeping bedfellow, gazed at them with soft, loving eyes, kissed the silent companion beside them, and leaped from the couch. The two Little Ones who had curled up in my parents’ empty beds stared blank and sad for a moment at the empty couches, then slid to the floor and fell into each other’s arms, as if only seeing each other’s wide awake faces could prove they were not still dreaming. When they spotted Lona, they ran toward her, radiant with bliss, to wrap her in a group hug.
Odu caught sight of the leopardess curled at the sleeping princess’s feet, and bounded over to her. He threw an arm over the great cat’s sleeping head, hugging and kissing her fur. “Wake up, wake up, darling!” he cried. “It is time to wake!”
The leopardess did not stir.
“She has slept herself cold!” he said to Mara, looking up at her with wide, confused, imploring eyes.
“She is waiting for the princess to wake, my child,” Mara told him.
Odu glanced over at the couch of the princess, and spotted two of his friends, Peter and Crispy, still fast asleep beside her. He ran at them, shaking them by the shoulders. “Wake up! Wake up!” he cried, tugging at their arms, first one, then the other. Soon his face fell, and he turned to me, eyes misty with tears. “They will not wake. Why are they so cold?”
“They are waiting for the princess too,” I told him.
He stretched out a hand to touch her cheek. “She is cold too! What is wrong?” he asked, looking around in dismay.
Adam knelt down in front of him. “Her wake is not ripe yet. She is busy forgetting all the sharp, hurtful things of her old life. When she has forgotten enough, then she will remember all the good things she ought to keep, and then she will wake fast.”
“Remember?”
“Yes. Not all at once, though.”
“But the golden cock already crowed!” Odu argued, and threw himself back at his sleeping friends. “Peter! Peter! Crispy! Wake up, Peter! Wake up, Crispy! We are all awake but you two! The golden cock crowed so loud! The sun is awake and coming! Oh, why won’t you wake?”
But Peter would not wake, neither would Crispy, and Odu burst out crying at last.
“Let them sleep, little one,” Adam said, stroking his hair. “You would not want the princess to wake up and find no one waiting for her, would you? They are perfectly happy. So is the leopardess.”
Odu wiped his tears, as if he had been doing it his whole life, though these were the first real tears he had ever had—soon to be wiped away forever, never to return.
We followed Eve out to the cottage. She offered us no bread, no wine, only stood glowing, the whole shape of her radiating gentle invitation for us to go onward. We never said a word of farewell; none was needed. We stepped out the door, to find our old horse and the three great elephants waiting for us, as we had left them. We were too full of unutterable joy to climb onto their backs, and so they followed at our heels, soft and steady, as we set off toward the rising sun.

Darkness had unspooled and vanished long before we noticed its passing. We trod soft heather underfoot in a pale, honeyed twilight, and the air we breathed did not just carry the scent of coming spring—it was the spring, warm and alive against our throats. A wonder had unmade the old world around us… or perhaps the far greater miracle was that the old, veiled world had unmade itself inside us. There was no harsh sun, no sharp moon glow spilling from the sky to carve shadows, yet every heather frond, every tiny sapling, every single blade of grass blazed clear as if lit from within: it was the same light that had burned unconsumed in the desert bush Moses had knelt before, seeping out of every growing thing, or else pouring out of our own eyes to meet them. No thing cast a shadow. All living things traded tiny, wispy sparks of light between them, no hoarding, no hiding.

Every plant showed me not just its shape, not just its hue, but the core idea that hummed inside it—the informing, living thought that made it what it was, that breathed it into existence. My bare feet ached to press into the earth beneath every stem I stepped over, to greet each small life as I passed. The world and my own being, its life and mine, were stitched together at last. The microcosm of my soul and the wide macrocosm of all creation had finally been reconciled, finally slipped into perfect harmony. I lived in every fern, every bee, every breeze that brushed my cheek; every small life seeped into me and lived there too. To be aware of a thing was to know its life and mine all at once, to know where we had both come from, to know where we belonged at home— to know that every one of us is what we are, because Another, the Source, is what He is.

Sense after sense I had never known I possessed, long asleep, woke one by one, layer after layer: indescribable, these new senses, for no old words, no old metaphors, had ever been forged to name them. My whole conscious being stretched wider and wider, ever making space to receive, as a hundred new doors swung open at once to let the world pour in. When a soft gust tangled in a heather bush and set its purple bells ringing, I felt the joy of the bells as my own, the joy of the breeze that stirred them to that bright tin-tinning chime, the joy of the soul that drank all those small joys in all at once. My being was a great, quiet hall, and every glad thing I passed came in to feast there. I was a calm, endless ocean, and the groundswell of one great, living joy kept lifting new waves to the surface—yet it was all the same eternal joy, wearing a hundred thousand different lovely faces. Life was a cosmic holiday.

I understood then that life and truth were one. Pure, unfiltered life is bliss in its very core; any state of being that is not bliss is not real life at all, but life-in-death. Every breath I drew of the soft dark wind that blew where it willed slipped out of me as a sigh of pure thanksgiving. At last I was. I lived, and nothing could ever touch this life of mine. My beloved Lona walked at my side, and we were heading home, to the Father. This much had been given to us before the very first sun rose on our new freedom: what glories, then, would that eternal day bring when it finally broke?

We reached the ragged, gutted hollow where the old earth’s monsters had once wallowed in filth, and as my dream had shown me long before, it was a glass-smooth, lovely lake now. I leaned over to gaze into its pellucid depths. A great whirlpool had torn out all the rotting soil those abominations had burrowed in, and the whole horrid brood lay visible on the lake bed. A dim, soft green light seeped through the crystalline water, laying bare every hideous form below. They coiled in spires, folded over on themselves in greasy layers, knotted tight, or stretched long and slack in motionless heaps—ghoulish, blighted shapes more monstrous than any fevered imagination of a wine-drunk, exhausted poet could ever conjure. No diver who had ever plunged into the churning Maelstrom had seen horrors to match these: tentacles twisting in writhing knots, bloated bulging flesh, glaring squid-ink eyes of utter wrongness, would have looked gentle, innocent beside these walking incarnations of hate—each of their heads a wicked, bloated flower that burst from an abominable stalk to perfect every last shred of its evil purpose.

Not one of them stirred as we passed. But they were not dead. So long as men and women with unwholesome, twisted minds walk the worlds, that lake will never empty of its loathsome population.

But listen— the sun’s herald, the auroral wind, was blowing soft, clear notes on a trumpet to announce his arrival. The great minister of the human tabernacle was drawing near! Heaped before his prow was a great wave of rippling crimson and liquid gold, and he rushed upward through the sky as if he had just been launched from his Maker’s outstretched hand into the vast upper sea. He paused, hanging high, and looked down on the world. He was a storm of burning, white-hot molten metal, and he was no more than a single coal plucked from the altar of the Father’s never-ending sacrifice for His children.

Look—every small flower straightens its stalk, lifts its thin neck, and stands with outstretched head, waiting. Something more than the sun, greater than mere light, is coming, coming. It does not matter that it takes a long time on the road: it is no less certain for that. What do a day, a year, ten thousand years matter to Life Himself, to Love Himself? He is coming, coming, and all of humanity are stretching their necks out to see Him arrive. Every morning they will stretch out thus, every evening they will droop and rest, and wait—until He comes. Is this no more than a vision spun out of thin air? When He arrives, will He find them still watching, still waiting?

It was a glorious resurrection morning. The whole long night before had been spent preparing for it. The Little Ones gamboled ahead of us, laughing, and the great gentle beasts followed close behind. Butterflies fluttered, dragonflies darted around our heads, a swirling cloud of colour and quick flashes of light: now they descended on us like a snowstorm made of rainbow flakes, now they rose back into the damp air like a rolling vapour woven out of embodied scents. It was a summer day more true to itself, more ideal, than any human who had not passed through the veil of death could ever have found in any world.

I walked the new earth, under the new heaven, and they were the same old world I had always known—save that now they had opened their minds to me, and I could see right into their hearts. Now the soul of every thing I met came out to greet me, to make friends, to tell me we all came from the same Source, all strove for the same end. They told me I was heading to the One they dwelled with always, the One they were always meant to be: they said they were lightnings that took shape the very moment they flashed out from Him to His creation. The dark crags drank the falling light like sponges, the whole wide world soaked up the sun’s rays, and breathed out new, living life. Lona and I were two bright fires of joy, walking side by side. Earth breathed its sweet, incense-like smoke up toward heaven; we breathed our longing desires homeward, to the Father. That pure, unbroken gratitude was the very shape of our consciousness, in that moment.

We reached the old river channels that had once been dry, dust-choked, weary waste, and now they ran and flashed and foamed with living water that shouted for joy as it moved. As far as the eye could see, it was one rushing, roaring, dashing river of water made vocal by the rocks it tumbled over. We did not cross it. We walked in glory and joy up its right bank, until we reached the great cataract at the foot of the old sandy desert, where the water roared and swirled and dropped sheer over the edge, splitting into two wide branches. We climbed the steep height above the falls, and found no desert at all. Grass spread out in soft plains on either side, and the deep, wide, silent river flowed full to its banks between grassy edges.

It was then that the Little Ones first saw the blazing glory of God made manifest in that limpid, flowing water. Instinctively they plunged in and swam, and the great beasts followed after them. The old desert had rejoiced, and blossomed like a rose. Wide forests had sprung up where there had only been sand, their undergrowth thick with flowering shrubs full of singing birds. Every thicket gave birth to a small rivulet, and every rivulet sang its own water-song as it flowed. There was no trace left of the place where the cursed buried hand had once rested. Further and still further beyond, the great river rolled in full, unbroken volume from the far horizon.

We traveled up and up, now along soft grassy banks, now through groves of gracious, sweet-smelling trees. The grass grew sweeter under our feet, its flowers more lovely and more varied the further we went; the trees grew taller, grander, and the wind that stirred their boughs grew fuller of soft, warm messages for us. At last we came to a forest where the trees were greater, grander, more beautiful than any we had seen yet. Their thick, living trunks held up a high, arched canopy so dense that hardly a single sunbeam slipped through the leaves and blossoms. The Little Ones climbed up into the bough rafters of that aerial vault, scrambling and leaping through that blooming world of branches, shouting down to the unseen elephants that wandered below, and hearing the great beasts trumpet their answers back. Lona understood every word of those conversations, while I could only fumble and guess at half their meaning. The Little Ones chased the squirrels, and the squirrels, playful and mischievous, drew them on—always, in the end, pausing to let themselves be caught and petted. Often some bird, gorgeous in plumage and song, would light on one of the children’s outstretched hands, sing a bright little song about all the good things that were coming, and then fly away. There was not a single monkey, of any kind, anywhere to be seen.

Lona and I picked our way along sun-dappled ground beneath the world-spanning canopy when a great, ringing shout tore down through the leaves above us. Moments later, the Little Ones came tumbling out of the foliage one by one, cheeks flushed, gowns smudged with sap, all chattering over each other to share their news: the bravest of them had scrambled to the very crown of a tree taller than any that had stretched skyward before it, and there, far across the rolling plain, had spied something strange clinging to the flank of a lone, towering mountain. That peak, they swore, climbed so high it pierced the very heart of the sky, which had bunched thick and heavy to hold it down, snapping its summit clean off. “It might be a city,” they bubbled, “but it looks nothing at all like Bulika.”

I scaled the colossus of a tree myself to see, and there it was: a city climbing upward into banks of hazy blue cloud, until I could no longer tell where mountain crag ended, sky began, and the gilded spires of palaces dissolved into mist. Cloud and stone and sky, precipice and domed roof wove together into a glittering chaos of broken shadow and shifting light.
I climbed back down, the Little Ones clinging to my heels, and we all sped forward faster than we had ever walked before. Their merriment only swelled as they went, darting ahead to lead the way, never once glancing over their shoulders. The river at our side grew more lovely with every step, until I realized I had never once seen real, living water in all my days on Earth. No lake, no stream I had known back there had ever been more than a faint, pale copy of this.

Before long, we could spot the city from the flat of the plain, half-shrouded in its blue cloud wrappings. But other clouds were coiling now around a spire so tall I could not tell if it was masonry or raw bedrock, jabbing up above the city, almost to the mountain’s crest. Gray, deep charcoal, soft bruised purple, they writhed in conflicting, swirling motions, tossing foamy plumes of vapor up into the air, while pockets of them spun like living whirlpools.
At last, a blinding flash burst forth, and for a heartbeat it danced across the hair and faces of the Little Ones running before us. Blinding darkness closed in after it, but through the hush we heard their voices, low and thrumming with unnameable delight.
“Did you see?”
“I saw!”
“What did you see?”
“The most beautiful man that ever was!”
“I heard him speak!”
“I didn’t! What did he say?”
The smallest, softest voice piped up then: Luva, the tiniest of the Little Ones, lisping around a gasp of joy. “He said, ‘All of you are mine, little ones: come along!’”

I had seen the lightning, but heard no voice. Lona, though, had seen and heard exactly what the children had. A second flash flared, and my eyes, if not my ears, were opened. That great, quivering sheet of light was stitched together from angel faces, blazing bright for a heartbeat before they winked out of sight.
A third flash burst, and its radiance was human, warm and familiar. “I see my mother!” I cried out.
“I see hundreds of mothers!” Luva chirped.
Once more the cloud blazed, and every kind of living creature filled that glow: horses, elephants, lions, hounds, beasts far more gentle and splendid than any I had ever imagined. And the birds! Great wide-winged ones whose feathers held every colour of sunset and rainbow, each plume glowing on its own; tiny songbirds whose feathers sparkled as if dusted with every precious stone the Earth had ever hoarded deep in its crust. Silver cranes, crimson flamingoes, opal-pale pigeons, peacocks draped in gold and emerald and sapphire light, jewel-bright hummingbirds, great flocks of butterfly wings the size of sails, lithe graceful creeping things—all of it, every one, alive in that single heavenly burst of light.
“Serpents turn into birds here,” Lona said softly, wonder in her tone, “just the way caterpillars used to turn into butterflies back home.”
“I saw my white pony,” I said, suddenly breathless, “the one that died when I was seven. I never needed to grieve so hard. All I had to do was wait a little longer.”

There was no thunder, no crack of clap or roll to follow the flashes. Instead, a soft, sweet rain began to fall, wrapping the air in a cool, gentle caress. We breathed deep, and stepped forward with strides that felt stronger, lighter, than any we had taken before. Every falling droplet glowed with the light of every gem that had ever woken under the Earth, and a rainbow so vast it spanned the whole width of the city arched overhead.
The blue clouds thickened, the rain poured down in torrents, and the children shrieked with joy and ran, darting so fast we had to hustle to keep them in sight. Beside us, the river rolled on silent and radiant, brim-full to the edge of its soft, yielding banks. There was no rock, no shingle, no sand beneath its waters: it flowed over thick, lush grass dotted thick with primroses and daisies, crocuses and narcissi, pimpernels and anemones, a whole host of star-shaped blooms glowing bright and clear through the luminous water. The river did not turn murky from the rain, did not tinge a hint of yellow or brown. Its clear, luminous mass shone with the pale, sea-green gleam that rose up from its dainty, living bed.

As we drew closer to the mountain, we saw that the river sprung straight from its very peak, rushing in full, frothing volume straight through the city’s main street. It cascaded down toward the gate over a wide, deep stair hewn from porphyry and serpentine that ran all the way to the mountain’s foot. When we reached it, we found shallower steps cut into both banks, leading up to the gate and along the ascending street.
Without pausing for even a breath, the Little Ones sprinted straight up the stairs to the gate, which stood wide open, unbarred. On the landing just outside sat the portress, a dark-faced woman angel leaning her shadowed brow on an idle hand. The children swarmed her in a wave, showering her with hugs and kisses, and before she could even stand to greet them, they had taken heaven itself by surprise, vanishing into the city still running up the stairs right beside the roaring torrent.

A great, towering angel came down with a retinue of shining ones to meet and welcome them, but the Little Ones dodged around all of them gaily, still scrambling higher. A group of woman angels descended on them in a merry flurry, though, and in a moment the children were wrapped safe in heavenly arms. The radiant beings carried them away, and I saw them no more.
“Ah,” the mighty angel said, as he kept walking down to meet Lona and me, who were nearly at the gate now, within easy hearing, “that is perfect! These are soldiers who would storm heaven itself to claim their home. I have heard word that a horde of black bats lurks on the frontiers. These little ones will make short work of such vermin!” He spotted the horse and elephants we had seen earlier clambering up the stair behind us. “Take those creatures to the royal stables,” he bade two of his attendants, “tend them well, then turn them loose in the king’s forest.”
“Welcome home,” he said to us, bending low with the softest, sweetest smile I had ever seen. He turned at once to lead the way higher. The scales of his armour flashed like flecks of living lightning.

I had no words for the feeling that swelled in my chest, to be welcomed so by the very stewards of heaven. Every longing I had ever held, every unspoken want I had not even dared name, must surely be waiting for me just a little further up the path. We paused for a moment at the gate, where the radiant river roared past us out of the mountain. I do not know what far-off quarries yielded the stones that made this city, but I could spot the prototypes of every gem I had ever loved on Earth, and they were far more beautiful than any earthly copy: these were living stones. In each one I saw not just the idea that had shaped it, but the mind that had thought it, the maker who had breathed it into being, the hand that had sent it out into the world. Nothing in this kingdom was dead. Nothing was merely an object.

We climbed up through the city, and passed out the far side of it. There was no wall on the upper edge, only a massive jumble of broken rock sloping upward like the moraine of an eternal, never-melting glacier, and through the gaps between the boulders, the river of life billowed outward. At the very top of the slope I could just make out three or four massive steps of a grand stair, vanishing into a cloud as white as fresh-fallen snow. Above those steps, not with my physical eyes but with the sight of my soul, I glimpsed what looked like an ancient, grand throne: the seat of the Ancient of Days. Over, under, and between those steps, the river of living water bubbled forth, endless, forever new-born.

The great angel could go no further. We must climb those rocks alone. My heart thudding with equal parts hope and hungry desire, I squeezed Lona’s hand tight, and we began to climb. Soon we let go of each other, so we could use our hands as well as our feet to scramble up the huge, uneven stones.
At last we drew near the cloud, which hung down over the top steps like the hem of a king’s garment. We passed through its soft, fringing edges, and stepped into its deep, billowing folds. A hand, warm and strong, wrapped around mine, and drew me toward a little door with a golden lock. The door swung open, the hand let go of mine, and gave me a soft, gentle push through. I spun around fast, just in time to see the cover of a great, massive book click shut behind me.
I stood alone, back in my own library.

I still have not traced Lona through the mist-shrouded borderlands, but Mara walks at my side more often than not now. She has unspooled dozens of quiet, secret truths for me already, and every breath we take together unfurls a new lesson I had no name for before.

Late at night, when the edges of my study blur soft and indistinct, I catch myself wondering: could that last supposed waking— the moment I stumbled back through my old front door to find my books stacked on their shelves, the kettle still humming half-forgotten on the hob—have been nothing but another layer of the dream? Am I still curled in that cold stone chamber of slumbering un-life, my eyes sealed shut, not yet ripened enough to shed the last of my dream-flesh and rise into the true world beyond? Or did I fail to surrender fully to that long, slow sleep, clinging to a thread of stubborn waking awareness that hauled me back far too soon, before the work of that place was done?

If that waking was itself only a dream, it must surely be a dream pointing toward a still finer, truer waking waiting just over the horizon. I cannot be the plaything of some hollow, false vision. No dream that thrums with that much warmth and beauty could hold nothing but a lie at its core.

When the dark gnaws at my certainty, I cry out: “Could God Himself, the forger of stars and worlds, craft something as soft and perfect as the things I dreamed?”
And Hope answers back, unshaken: “Then where did that dream come from in the first place?”
“From the dark hollow of my own mind, surfacing into the light of my awareness,” I protest. “My brain was its mother, and the fever singing in my blood its father.”
Hope laughs soft then. “Say rather your brain was a violin, strung with threads of the world unseen, and that fever in your blood was the bow that drew the song out of it. But who carved the violin? Who guided that bow across every string so perfectly to pull that exact melody free? Who set every songbird perched on its bough in the great Tree of Life, and startled each one to sing its own note in its own time? Where did that wild, unchained fantasia come from? And where did the life that danced to its tune originate? Did you, all alone in the dark of your own unconscious, whisper ‘Let beauty exist. Let truth shine bright’—and did it happen, just like that?”

Humans dream, and we hunger after the things we see in those dreams. The Other, the one that breathes life into all worlds, broods, wills, and makes those half-glimpsed things real. If you dream a dream that is only yours, small and self-spun, you become its toy, caught in its endless loops. But if the dream was given to you by that Other? That same power is more than capable of making it come true.

I have never gone looking for that cursed mirror again. The hand that reached through it to hurl me back to this side gave me a warning I will not ignore. I will never try to leave through that door again. “All the days of my appointed time will I wait till my change come.”

Sometimes when I glance at the spines of my old books lined on the shelf, they ripple, as if an invisible wind is blowing straight through their solid paper and leather, as if a crack is opening between worlds right there, ready to spill something new and unnameable into my study. Other times when I walk the roads outside, the whole sky and earth, the grass under my boots and the boughs of the oak trees, shake for a heartbeat, like they are about to dissolve entirely and show me the face of the reality hiding beneath them. Then the tremor passes, and they settle back, looking exactly like the familiar, ordinary world I have always known.

At odd moments, I catch faint whisperings curling around my ears, like people who loved me are standing just out of sight, murmuring about me. But the second I try to make out their words, the whispers cut off, and all around me is dead quiet. I cannot tell if these things are blooming in my own brain, or slipping through the thin veil from the other side. I do not chase them. I do not fight them. They come, and I let them go.

Strange, half-remembered memories drift through me in broad daylight, too, too fuzzy to pin down, like faces seen through a fogged window from a past I cannot quite place. I do not sleep to dream anymore, not the way I used to. For all I know, the more awake I think I am, the deeper I am sunk into the grandest dream of all. But when I finally wake for good, into that greater life that holds this small one like a mother holds her child against her chest, I will know. There will be no more doubt.

I wait. Asleep, or awake, I wait.

Novalis says, “Our life is no dream, but it should and will perhaps become one.”

**Chapter 45: *Tin tin sonando con sì dolce nota Che ‘l ben disposto spirito d’ amor turge.* DEL PARADISO, x. 142.
***Chapter 46: *Oma’ vedrai di sì fatti uficiali.* Del Purgatorio, ii. 30.
